PREFACE

This Guide is intended for those who have access to the UNIX* text-formatting programs
nroff and troff and would like to use them to their full potential. It has been designed as a
combination tutorial and reference manual: the former for those who need a better understanding of the programs, the latter for those who need to locate specific commands for
specific functions as quickly and easily as possible.
As a tutorial, the book takes the various procedures one step at a time, and eventually
builds up to practical, durable, and comprehensible instructions. A lengthy introduction
describes commands, macros, conditionals, environments, and diversions. This is followed by a
functional dictionary of basic commands. Finally, there is a macro chapter, divided into
independent sections addressing the most common tasks: footnotes, equations, tables. Each of
these sections begins with a consideration of the fundamentals, then explains various solutions, ending with those in the macro packages. Both nroff and troff have been aided enormously by a number of these packages, which were created at Bell Labs and the University of
California, Berkeley; the Guide reviews two of the most popular of them, -me and -ms. Moreover, since the packages can be manipulated, the sections devote space to a description of the
inner workings of each macro. Throughout, detailed explanation has been an overriding principle, and in consequence the preliminary material may seem formidable; it is certainly possible to format text without knowing any of it, but I hope that curiosity at least will tempt
some to peruse it.

As a reference manual, the Guide is to be used from the back, through the glossary,
index, and the extensive appendixes; supplementing alphabetical lists of commands

are lists

arranged by function. This arises in response to the often-heard criticism of some documentation, that you have to already know what the command is in order to find it.
I have decided to rely closely on the vocabulary used in the various Bell Laboratories
manuals and tutorials. Though these documents were not written for the general public, they
are often disseminated widely and photocopied into invisibility , and it is important to maintain a close tie with them. Given their original purpose as in-house documentation, it is a
pleasant surprise that they communicate

so well, and they often demonstrate a frankness and

humor that are unfortunately rare in computer publications. No system is perfect, including
UNIX; but, as I will have many opportunities to repeat, because of a flexibility in design you
can accomplish a great deal on it, and that is a telling point.

The Guide assumes some familiarity with UNIX systems functions and some acquaintance with the editors, preferably ex.

This does not exclude ed, edit, or vi, but many of the

global commands discussed in the following pages are possible only in ex.
In a book of this sort, a certain number of arbitrary conventions are necessary, even
though the only virtue of these conventions may be their consistency. Within the text, there
are three levels of notation: 1) all UNIX system commands are printed in boldface (nroff,
* UNIX

is a trademark of AT&T

Bell Laboratories.

x1

xii

grep, vi); 2) all nroff/troff commands and requests are placed within double quotes
(‘pl 11:1”); and all names of macros, strings, and registers appear in single quotes (macro Xx);
Examples of command sequences that you would type into the system are set off and
indented from the text itself. All such material should be entered exactly as it has been
presented. For those examples which demonstrate UNIX system commands, a dollar sign ($)
represents the shell prompt, and <cr> indicates a line you have entered and terminated by
pressing the carriage return key:
$ date<ecr>
Mon May 19 20:02:49 PDT 1986

$
Some UNIX versions use the percent sign (%) as a prompt, and in these cases the results
should be identical:
% date<er>
Mon May 19 20:02:49 PDT
%

1986

The prompt for the UNIX editors is, as always, the colon (:).
Since I needed texts for my discussion, I chose documents from the life of Abraham Lincoln on the principle that they were interesting and illuminating. Some of the names are
obscure enough, but worth reviving, if only because their bearers are our ancestors. What
they would have felt about all of this is difficult to know, but Lincoln himself probably would
have been sympathetic.

1. INTRODUCTION

Characterizing the System
The following Introduction will acquaint you with the UNIX formatters
nroff and troff in their essential particulars: how you use them and what they
are like. It should be stated from the outset that, along with other UNIX programs, nroff and troff share that overriding characteristic of the system, a
clumsy and powerful adaptability. UNIX enthusiasts are fond of saying that
you can do anything on it; they do not of course mention the cost in time and
energy doing it, but they do have a point.

This preliminary section describes some of the ramifications of “‘anything.” That in turn should fortify you for reading subsequent sections in the
Introduction: preparing the text; conventions (the subsection on the use of the
backslash [‘\’] should be read with strong coffee); units of measurement; internal mathematics;

definitions (number registers, strings, macros); and pro-

cedures for invoking nroff and troff.
UNIX formatting functions in two distinct stages: first, the user enters a
series of commands within the document; then, one of the two programs, nroff
or troff, generates a formatted version of the original file. Because of this
‘batch’ approach, it is often necessary to review the formatted version on the
terminal screen, then make the appropriate corrections before sending the final

copy to a printer.

This is quite unlike more sophisticated systems that compose

the pages automatically, almost as soon as they are written. Naturally this
represents an advantage in speed and convenience, though I believe it has little
effect on full production.

Both nroff and troff derive from roff, which in turn arose from runoff, a
program developed on the MIT CTSS System. roff and runoff are still kicking
around, and many of their basic commands reappear in nroff and troff. In this
Guide, I often refer to nroff and troff collectively as ‘the roffs’; I do not mean

Introduction

2 Characterizing the System

to include either roff or runoff in this. Both nroff and troff were developed in
the mid-seventies by Joseph Ossanna, who was killed in an accident in 1977.
Since then, the programs have served Bell Labs and a large number of
academic institutions well, and in the past few years have spread to private
business.
nroff produces typewritten documents; troff is the typesetting program.
It is an important advantage to them that they share common commands, like
those that determine the length of a line of formatted text or the length of a
page. It is possible, then, to create a typewritten version of a file and then produce a typeset version without any changes. Those commands that are
appropriate for printing (for example, size of type, spacing between lines) are
ignored by nroff. The two programs were originally created to generate internal memoranda and patent applications, and the pragmatic demands of this
work may have determined their essential characteristics as formatters,
characteristics that can be described as a blend of inconvenience and efficiency.

Macro Packages: -ms and -me
In most installations it is usual to invoke nroff with -me, -ms, or -mm,

independent packages that define a number of related commands in clusters
called ‘macros.’ These packages offer a number of advantages to the user who
does not want to get involved in complicated formatting. Though no package
can ever anticipate all needs, most users find them extremely handy.

The two packages that are generally available in UNIX are -me and -ms;
-mx can occasionally be found, and -mm has become popular recently. The last
three, developed at Bell Laboratories from the late seventies on, show strong
family resemblances, going back to the original -ms package created by
Michael E. Lesk.! -me, the product of Eric Allman at Berkeley” can be visualized as an alternative version of -ms, written especially to meet the needs for a
thesis format at Berkeley, and offering the user a wonderfully bewildering
choice of options.
The differences among the packages are more apparent than real. Macros
in -me are usually lower-case, with a regularity in designating internal registers and commands that makes the package extremely comprehensible. And,
unless someone

has been stupid about conserving disk space, there are exten-

sive and enlightening comments throughout the package. -ms and its cousins
are, on the other hand, a little more inflexible and tend to be more cavalier
about the time they take to format a text, particularly with the cover-page
macros. But a comparison is in fact meaningless, because any feature of -me
can be adapted into -ms, and of course vice versa. For example, the automatic
indexing macro in -me is missing from -ms, and for this reason a number of
users swear they'll never part from -me. However, the macro can be successfully adapted into -ms with only a few changes, and a number of local versions
of -ms have done this very thing.

1Typing Documents
13, 1978.

on the UNIX System Using the -ms Macros with Troff and Nroff.

Bell Technical

Report. November

2._ME Reference Manual (Release 1.1/20). Electronics Research Laboratory, University of California, Berkeley. June 4,
1979; and Writing Papers with Nroff Using -ME. Electronics Research Laboratory, University of California, Berkeley.

April 29, 1979.

Introduction

Producing the Source File 3

All packages will be found in a directory called /usr/lib/tmac, and they
can be examined from your own directory, or even copied into your directory if
you wish to experiment with them. Though most users would not need to, it is
an opportunity that the system offers and certainly is recommended for those
who are stymied by a particular feature in the package they are using.

Producing the Source File
In order to format a document, nroff and troff rely on commands inter-

spersed throughout the text file. Commands

(or ‘requests’ or ‘parameters’) are

instructions entered into the text that direct nroff or troff to print it in a certain way. They are something like marginal notes in a rough draft, directing a
typist to double-space the text or begin a new page at the end of a chapter;
naturally, no one would include such notes in the final copy, but they are physically present and sometimes prominent in the source draft. In the same way,
nroff/troff commands might occupy considerable space in a text that needs a
complicated format (titles, indented paragraphs, columns, headings); though
the source document may then look extremely forbidding, in fact each request
is intelligible and useful.
Parameters are inserted by means of one of the UNIX editors led, edit,
ex, or vil, which is almost certainly the way in which you originally entered
the text, or will enter it simultaneously with the commands. Let us begin,
then, with the editor, requested after the UNIX system prompt ‘%’ or ‘$, and
followed by the file name and a carriage return:

$ ex article<er>?®
To this the system responds, as usual,
"article" 300 lines, 14567 characters

or

"article" no such file or directory

In either case, you will be entering specific instructions in addition to the
straightforward prose of the document, and these instructions can be as powerful as any computer command. If you wish your final draft to be double-spaced,
for example, you would insert the single command “.ls 2” |for “line spacing =2”|
on a line all by itself before the text begins. In the following example of editing a source file, a line of text has already been entered, and the line numbers
have been set; the “ls 2” is then inserted (using the editors’ insert command
‘7”?) in this fashion:

3As a convention, the <er>

will stand for the RETURN

key on the keyboard, terminating a line that you have typed.

Introduction

4 Producing the Source File

:l<er>
1 I do not remember that you and I have ever met personally.
i<er>
1 Js 2<ecr>

2 .<er> lend of insert]
To generate triple-spacing or quadruple-spacing, you may merely change the
two to a three or four. The following example uses the ‘substitute’ command
available in ed, edit, and ex:
ib dhe %
:8/2/3/<er>
Ib Wigs

To see the results of this change, you must save it with the editors’ ‘write’ comrand, and you may quit.

Then nroff can be invoked:

:w<cr>

"article" 301 lines, 14573 characters
:q<cr>
$ nroff article<er>

This will produce your text file on the screen, double-spaced; it will also recompose your lines to a six-and-a-half-inch length‘ and adjust along the right-hand
margin. Though some of your text may not look right, you can now see what
you're driving toward.

Commands like “.ls 2” can be scattered throughout the article; a “ls 1”
will generate single-spaced text on the following line, and this will continue
until the file ends or nroff/troff meet another “ls” command. To further illustrate the approach, the command to stop printing on the present page and

begin a new one is “.bp” [‘begin page’]; in the following example, a new page is
needed after line number 233, containing the phrase, “I commend the case to

the special attention of the Secretary.” In this case, the editors’ ‘append’ command adds the request, like this:
233

to the special attention of the Secretary.

‘:a<cr>

234
235

.bp<er>
.<er> lend of append]

Now, when you ask to have this formatted, nroff will space down to the sixtysixth line of that page and begin a new one. troff will do this as well, but it’s
not easy to see on the terminal.

Nearly all the commands of the “ls N” [the Nstanding for the number of
spaces] and “.bp” class have two distinguishable features: they begin with a
period and they are typed on a line all by themselves. The roffs will in fact
process your text with no commands whatsoever, but unfortunately what they
do automatically is seldom acceptable. If you will be using the -ms macro
‘This recomposition is called ‘fill’ in the documentation and is described at length later. Basically, filling is the process of breaking long lines and adding words to short lines to make them a fairly uniform length.

Introduction

Producing the Source File 5
package, the one command that you must have is an indication that text is to

start, and the most usual way to show this is a “.GO” or an “.LP” [for “leftjustified paragraph”) or “.PP” lordinary paragraph] placed on a line by itself
just before the text. As the Basic Functions section will show, a large number

of commands are available; with these, you can set options like page length
and line length, and reset them at various points in the text. Most of these go
into effect immediately, though some wait until the next page.

Conventions

In order to distinguish a command from a literal word, the UNIX system
in general utilizes a number of conventions that need detailed explanation;
some have a basis in logic and consistency, but in general they must simply be
learned, just like conventions in any other language. nroff and troff share
many of these conventions but in addition have a few that are peculiar to each.
I have been told that once a user becomes familiar with them, they become
second nature, but I suppose the truth of the matter is that they’re a pain that
users have to put up with. The five conventions that are most important are
the special meanings of the period |.], the apostrophe |’], the backslash |\], the
plus and minus |+/-—], and the vertical bar ||]; the special meanings are in
effect when these characters begin a line or precede other characters.
The period
By far the largest number of commands in nroff/troff have the two distinguishing features just mentioned for “.ls” and “.bp”: each begins with a
period, and each is typed on a line all by itself. Placing a period as the first
character on a line indicates that what follows is a formatting command rather
than a literal word. I have already introduced you to some of these commands;
the essential logic is that, since no English sentence begins with a period, a
line beginning with one must be something else. This works, after a fashion.
For example, if in your UNIX editor’s file you had a line that looked like this:
in 1863 Lincoln wrote, “I have endured a great deal
the formatter would treat it straightforwardly, as ten words of a certain length
and height. If, however, you had written this:

in 1863 Lincoln wrote, “I have endured a great deal
nroff or troff would believe that you wished to indent almost two thousand
characters, and it would ignore the rest of the line. Well, you argue, English
does not begin lines or words with periods, so there is almost never a chance of
this happening.
‘Almost never’ means that there are exceptions, however;

in the normal

course of transcribing text a decimal number might begin a line:

.45 proved to be
or there may be ellipses:
...1 cannot refrain from tendering to you

In both of these cases the formatter should interpret the initial period as a

Introduction

6 The period

literal punctuation mark, not a command signal. To insure that it does, you
are required to precede the line with a backslash followed by the ampersand,
like this:

\&.45 proved to be
\&...I cannot refrain from tendering to you
The backslash-with-ampersand represents a character that will neither appear
in the text nor assume any space; it will, however, deprive the period of its
position on the line, and thus avoid interpreting it as a command signal.
The apostrophe
The apostrophe serves as an alternate to the period for a specialized use:
it offers the opportunity to delay those commands that otherwise automatically
jump to a new line or new page. The delay lasts until a full line of text has
been composed. This class, known as ‘break’ commands, immediately cease filling in the present line, so that it is always partial. Though usually you do
wish to break the text (a new paragraph, for example), occasionally you would
like the line to fill out to the right margin before continuing. An apostrophe
before the command delays that break in text until the line has been formatted
completely. This can represent a benefit, in that a great deal of the guesswork
about where the line will end has been removed; but it also involves a slightly
larger risk of error. To demonstrate this convention in action, the following

example uses the command “.bp’, which immediately breaks the text on the
current page. Here the formatter will stop composing the line after the word
‘good, leaving it short of the right margin, and subsequent text will start at
the top of the next page:
Human nature cannot change.

In any future great national

trial, compared with the men of this, we shall have as
weak and as strong, as silly and as wise, as bad and as
good.

.bp
I desire so to conduct the affairs of this administration
that if at the end, when I lay down the reins of power,
I have lost
If, however, you wished the last line on the page to extend to the right margin,
you could use the apostrophe to suppress the break in text until that line has
been filled out:

Human nature cannot change. In any future great national
trial, compared with the men of this, we shall have as
weak and as strong, as silly and as wise, as bad and as
good.

I desire so to conduct the affairs of this administration
ie

that if at the end, when I lay down the reins of power,
I have lost

Notice that the apostrophe command is placed a line lower than the period
command, in order to allow part of the next line to fill in after “good,” before
beginning a new page.

There are eight commands that automatically break the text, and each
can be used with an apostrophe. The following is a list, with a short explanation; details on all of them can be found in the ‘Basic Functions’ section:

\"temporary indent

Problems with the apostrophe
The problem with this convention is that while it’s unlikely that you will
start a line with a period, there are a number of instances in which you might
start with an apostrophe. The general choice for a single quote-mark in
typewriting is the apostrophe, so one might logically and frequently enter a
text of this sort:

"IT shall try to correct errors when shown to be errors
Like the period, such apostrophes will have to be ‘protected’ with a backslash
and ampersand:
\&’I shall try to correct errors when shown to be errors

Unfortunately, these characters may have special meanings for the line editors,
so that they may be difficult to insert with a substitution. The section ‘Ultimate Issues: Safeguarding the Text’ provides a global command that can be
used in ex, but only with care.

There is one other use for the apostrophe that should be mentioned; by
convention, it often functions to ‘quote’ a measurement or sequence of characters (string) in a command, in order to perform some special operation. For
example, the two characters “\o” request an overstrike, and the letters to be
overstruck are bracketed by apostrophes immediately before and after, so that

A\o’n’’o nuevo
will produce
Ano nuevo

Actually, like a number of similar commands, “\o” will accept whatever character immediately follows the ‘o.’ So, as a matter of preference, some use double
quotes instead of the apostrophe:

A\o"n""o nuevo
The question is ultimately one of readability.
5Ignore the backslash-with-quotes for the time being.

It’s another convention, explained below on p. 9.

Introduction

8 The backslash
The backslash

The backslash is one of the most annoyingly pervasive punctuation marks
in UNIX. It has six distinct functions among the nroff and troff: 1) to introduce certain commands, 2) to control vertical and horizontal motion within the
line, 3) to create lines and special characters, 4) to access storage locations for
numerical values or literal words, 5) to allow comments in a text, and 6) to
define certain kinds of spacing.
Commands

There are six or so sequences beginning with the backslash that function
exactly like commands beginning with a period. The following list is meant
generally to give you an idea of their range; details can be found in the
appropriate sections of this guide.
\d_

\"move down 4 a space

\u\"move up 2 a space
\r
\"move up I[reverse direction] 1 space
\sN \"vary type size in typesetting
\fx \"vary font |roman, italics, bold] in typesetting
The advantage of these requests is that they can be located throughout the
text easily, often with a global command; each or all can then be modified if
necessary.
The disadvantage of these commands is that they can do as much harm to
a formatting project as any of the more ambitious commands: I once reduced
the point size on all brackets without returning to the normal size each time,
and the text gradually faded into fine print.
Movement

A second function for the backslash is relative motion within the line,
whereby the formatter can be instructed to move horizontally or vertically a
stipulated distance that has been set off by apostrophes. Examples of this are
‘\h” [horizontal movement], “\v” [vertical movement], and “‘\x” [extra line
spacing]. These come in handy when allowing for special diacritics or larger
characters.

Drawing

A third generalized use for the backslash might be called ‘drawing’: horizontal lines |\l’distance’| and vertical lines [\L’distance’], brackets |\b’string’|,
and the large number of special characters available in the sequence “\(xx”’, all
of which are listed in Appendix VI. The most common of these are

\(14 \"one-quarter=%
\(12 \"one-half=%
\(34 \"three-quarters
=%

\(em \"three-quarter ‘Em’ dash =—
\(bu \"bullet
=e
It depends on the typesetter, but usually in troff all five of these are available
in roman, italic, and bold fonts.

Introduction

The backslash 9

Storage locations
Fourth, the backslash may introduce special storage locations that are
described fully below: number registers and specially defined strings.
Comments

A fifth use for the backslash is to render material within the file invisible
to the formatter, specifically by means of a backslash with double quotes |\"].
This most often acts like the ‘remarks’ line or lines in programming; any comments to the right of the sequence ‘\"’ are ignored by nroff and troff. Unlike
such provisions in many programming languages, however, it can share the
same line as a command or text, as some examples of this function have
already demonstrated.

Spacing
Sixth, the backslash can insert spaces of various sizes between letters or
words, spaces that will neither be decreased nor broken as the formatter composes each line. This guarantees that characters—for example, in italic and
roman type—do not touch, or that integral strings (like “B. C.”) are not broken
across two lines. The most common of the various commands of this class is

the ‘unpaddable’ space |\ |, so called because it is not lengthened when the line
has been justified. But it is also ‘unsplittable,’ keeping characters and spaces
whole when necessary (“‘B.\ C.’’).
There are, beyond these, some five or six other functions for the backslash
that will appear in the pages following. You may correctly presume that if
you
ve tried everything else to get a particular sequence to work, you might
precede it with a backslash.
The plus, minus signs and movement

The plus (‘+’) and minus (‘—’) signs have three distinct meanings in
nroff/troff: 1) positive or negative motion, 2) addition or subtraction in calculation, and 3) increment and decrement. The first is discussed here; the others
appear in the section on Mathematics (pp. 12-18). Any command specifying
movement (such as those for vertical [‘‘\v’] and horizontal [“\h”] motion) will
default to a positive movement—a

right if horizontal.
sign, so that

movement, that is, down if vertical or to the

Motion up or to the left can be requested with the negative

\v’—distance’
means go back up the page by distance (two inches, for example), and

\h’ —distance’
means return to the left by distance.

The vertical bar
This symbol has the rarely needed but nonetheless important function of
indicating absolute vertical or horizontal distance—distance from the left margin or page top, no matter where the previous text has ended. It is used to
draw boxes and to return to a certain location quite independently of the
amount of text processed.

Introduction

10 Units of Measurement
Units of Measurement

A major aspect of formatting is placing words and lines at certain distances on the page. Some of this can be accomplished through straight numerical commands (as in “ls 2”), and some others need distances expressed in common units, like inches or centimeters. To continue the typist analogy, if you
needed a certain blank space on a page, you would tell the typist to ‘skip five
spaces here,’ or ‘skip two inches there.’ With nroff and troff, the command for
a space is “.sp”, and the counterpart to the first instruction would be
sp 5
The second instruction, ‘skip two inches,’ must necessarily be different, and the

difference is signaled by a lower-case ‘i,’ standing for ‘inches’:
sp 21

Throughout this Guide, those commands (like “.ls”) that usually require
numbers will be written with an N following, the Nstanding for any digit;
those commands that accept either numbers or units of measurement (like
sp”) will be written with an M: “.sp M”. Actually, in the latter case what
you might think was simply a number (“.sp 5”) in fact represents a unit of
measurement as well; it’s just that nroff and troff will interpret that number
in the kind of unit set for the command (in the documentation called ‘default
scale indicator’); for example, the kind of unit set for the line length is the ‘m,’
a measurement borrowed from typesetting that roughly describes the width of
the letter ‘m’ in that size of type (see below for its exact width). To define a
line length as six inches, then, you would type the following:
6
To set the line length to 60 m’s (in nroff at ten-pitch spacing, the same as six
inches), no letter is necessary:

1 60
Unfortunately, though the unit of measurement that most comes to mind in
formatting is the inch, not one of the scale indicators is the inch (I presume
the logic is that the inch is too big, and doesn’t allow proportional setting in
troff the way the m does). So, if only to be like everyone else, at least once
you'll err in defining line length:

1 6
will print lines only six m’s wide, which is usually not what you want.
There are eight different units of measurement in nroff/troff; some, like
the inch and centimeter, are quite straightforward, others not. The three complicating factors are the differences between nroff and troff, the differences in
various machines (‘devices’) that format text, and the differences between horizontal and vertical measurement. Naturally, these differences compound each
other. But to start at the beginning, here are the eight ‘scale indicators’:

Introduction

Units of Measurement

11

inch
centimeter
pica
point

m lor ‘em’]
n [or ‘en’]

aie
Ss
=
Sg
ie

internal ‘machine’ unit
vertical space

All of these units are understood by nroff and troff, but the four middle ones
pertain directly to typesetting; they are drawn from the typesetter’s trade and
represent a considerable advantage in communication with that industry. A
pica is one-sixth of an inch, and a point one-twelfth of a pica, so that there are
seventy-two points in an inch. This in turn has a direct bearing on the calculation of the m and n lone-half an m]; type sizes are given in points, so that
twelve-point type is type whose largest vertical expanse is twelve points
(twelve seventy-seconds, or one-sixth of an inch).° An m in a specific type size
can therefore be defined in troff as the same number of points as there are to
that size, so that an m in twelve-point type equals twelve points, one-sixth of
an inch. An n is one half that, or one-twelfth of an inch. In nroff, the m and
n are equivalent, measuring the width of a typewritten character; this is gen-

erally either a tenth or twelfth of an inch |‘ten- or twelve-pitch’].
The scale indicator ‘u’ deserves a paragraph all to itself. nroff and troff
automatically convert whatever unit of measurement has been used to a common denominator, in this case internal or machine units. Under the best circumstances you should not even know of the existence of these units, but
unfortunately they do figure—much more often than they should. The simpler
of the two processors, nroff, assumes that there are 240 units to the inch.
Typewritten copy generally occupies six lines to the inch vertically and ten or
twelve letters to the inch horizontally; a vertical space would therefore be

defined as forty machine units

and a single letter either twenty or twenty-four

units in width. A half-vertical space would measure twenty units vertically;
since this is the usual limit of typewriters, we can say that (depending on the
printer), its resolution is twenty units. On old-fashioned printers with no halfline spacing, the resolution is forty units. Again, normally you wouldn’t need
to know this, but it does explain a few characteristics of the system. For example, all of the units of measurement will accept fractions, but the limitations of
the units may not produce the exact measurement you requested. For example,

a request of a six-and-a-half-inch space in nroff will be translated into 1560
units in nroff, or thirty-nine lines.

A command to generate a six-and-three-

eighths-inch space (“sp 6.3751”) translates as 1530 units, which should be
thirty-eight and a quarter spaces; but since there are no quarter spaces in
nroff, the formatter interprets this as 1520 units, or thirty-eight spaces (six
and a third inches). This becomes even more apparent in the use of centimeters.

In nroff, a centimeter is translated as 94 units, so that resolution in that

formatter is necessarily poor, especially without half-line spacing.

Measure-

ments, for example, of three centimeters, three and a half centimeters, and

three and three-quarters centimeters resolve to 2.96 centimeters, 3.38 centimeters, and 3.81 centimeters in nroff (seven, eight, and nine vertical spaces).

6The vertical expanse is measured from the top of the highest letter to the bottom of those lower-case letters which
descend below the line. But this is not exact because of various type styles and the need for space around the letter.

Introduction

12 Units of Measurement

In troff, the number of units to the inch depends on the specific typesetting device. There may be 300 units to the inch, or 1728 or more. Throughout
troff’s stages of development, the Graphics Systems phototypesetter at Bell
Laboratories defined an inch as 432 units, and this is still a common situation.
But, while the GSI phototypesetter could resolve horizontally at 432 to the
inch, vertically it could divide an inch into only hundred and forty-fourths
(making an available vertical resolution of three machine units), so that
requesting a vertical space of 280 units would be rounded down to 279, and 281
would be rounded up to 282.’ This does not in fact create terrible problems, but
it can be annoying with diacritical marks in smaller type sizes.
The final scale indicator, the ‘v’, should now be a little more comprehensible. It measures the vertical space from the bottom of the present line to the
bottom of the line above (in the documentation, ‘base line to base line’). Thus
it is entirely dependent on whether the processor is nroff or troff, and, if troff,
the type of machine that is typesetting the text. In nroff, a ‘v’ is a normal

typewriter space, one-sixth of an inch, or forty units; the command “sp 2”
actually means take two ‘v’ spaces |“.sp 2v’’], or eighty units, one-third of an
inch. In troff, there is far more flexibility in vertical spacing; for ten-point
type you might want a slightly larger vertical space for it to sit in, usually
about twenty percent bigger to permit comfortable reading, which would be
twelve-point. The command “.vs M” sets this vertical spacing (in this case,
“vs 12p”), and from then on a ‘v’ is equal to twelve points, one-sixth of an inch,
or seventy-two units on the Graphics Systems machine. The vertical spacing
command defaults to points (“.vs 12” is the same as “.vs 12p’’), but often you
will find the ‘p’ written out anyway, so as to insure that the document can be
processed by both nroff and troff. And later, as you will see, it will become
important to test whether the vertical spacing exists internally in points or
machine units, so as to avoid requesting too much or too little space (twelve
units as opposed to twelve points, or seventy-two points as opposed to seventytwo units). ‘v’s’ will accept decimals, though they aren’t much good beyond the
second place with a resolution of 144 to the inch:
sp .625v

and
sp .62v
are exactly the same under those conditions.

Mathematics

In the view of many users, nroff/troff documentation is extremely uninformative on the formatters’ ability to do mathematical computation. Actually,
it may not have occurred to anyone at Bell Laboratories that further expansion
was needed, since nroff and troff are capable of almost every operation necessary to print text. But there are difficulties, and these must be addressed.
Specifically, a great deal of confusion lies in five aspects: default units of

measurement; complex operations involving those units; decimals; relational

Single and multiple operations

Both nroff and troff delight in computations of all sorts. They will add,
subtract, multiply, and divide; they will also furnish you with a remainder if

one value does not divide evenly into another [‘modulus”].

The only rule that

you must follow is to avoid inserting any spaces in the equation.
If there is a
space between one element and another, the roffs will consider the characters
literal. The plus sign (‘+’), as you might expect, signifies addition, the minus
(—’) subtraction, the asterisk (‘*’) multiplication, the slash (‘/’) division, and
the percent-sign (°%’) returns the modulus or remainder. All of the following
calculations, then, are legitimate:

8+4
sie)
4x*3
36/3
87%15
12%32
All equal twelve, including the last one, because thirty-two goes into twelve
zero times, with a remainder afterwards of twelve.

Multiple arithmetic operations are also fairly straightforward. Each of
the above can be used in combination with the others, the calculation proceeding from left to right (unlike programming, where quite often one kind of calculation precedes the others). The sequence, therefore,

8+4-17+5+4*3-36/2
has an answer of minus twelve. Should you wish to group statements so as to
affect the order of operation, parentheses are available:

(8+4) —(17—5) +(3* 4) —(36/3) +(87%15) —(12%32)
The parenthesized calculations are performed first, then the entire line is
totalled. Parentheses may be placed inside one another:

((8+4)
—(—17+5)/((3*
4)
+(36/3)))*(87%15)
The most internal (36/3) is calculated, then added to the next (the product of
3*4), and so on until the entire left-hand sequence is multiplied by the product
of the last.
Complications in mathematics processing
The following are not so much obstacles outright in nroff and troff as
characteristics of the system that have caused difficulties in the past. For the
most part they are consistent, if that’s a comfort.

Introduction

14 Complications in mathematics processing
Default units of measurement

In attempting to use math in your document, a problem arises because, as
it turns out, many commands will interpret an unmarked number solely in
terms of their default scale. In order to use addition for the spacing request
“sp”, for example, you might think that this would work:
sp 2+31

But this does not produce a space of five inches; since the default measurement
for the “sp” command is ‘v,’ the vertical space, this command will produce a
space of three inches and two vertical spaces. To obtain a five-inch space, both
numbers must be marked. Subtraction behaves in exactly the same way, so
that
sp 31-1

will space down three inches less a vertical space.
This becomes even more crucial in multiplication and division, where it is
natural to enter a whole number and believe that it will behave like a whole
number. The following, for instance, might seem an easy way to get a ten-inch
space:
sp 2i*5

Unfortunately, since the five will be understood as vertical spaces (5v), and
then translated into units (in nroff, 200 units), and since two inches is also

changed into units (nroff 480), the space will be considerably larger than ten
inches (in nroff, 96,000 units, or 400 inches).

Division has the same exaggerated effect, if only in the other direction;
unpredictably,

sp 21/3
will not produce a two-thirds of an inch space. Rather the two inches and
three vertical spaces will be translated into units (in nroff, the 480 divided by
120, making four units, which will figure as zero).
Similarly, the modulus calculation

(which will have its uses later on), also

expects scale indicators. If, for example, you wished to space down by the
remainder when five inches have been divided by four (a remainder of one,
hence, you believe, one inch), you might try this:
sp 51%4

But you would be wrong.

The four is again understood as four vertical spaces

(160 units in nroff), which when divided into the five inches (1200 units) has a
much different remainder (80 units).

The solution to this is to mark every single distance with a scale indicator
(i,’ ‘m,’ ‘v,’ and so forth) and any number that is meant to be whole with a ‘u’
for machine units, so that the figure will not be increased because of default
scale indicators. The five examples above should have been written

Introduction

Complications in mathematics processing 15

sp 2i+3i

\"addition

sp 3i-1i

\"subtraction

sp 5i*2u
sp 2i/3u
sp 51%4i

\"multiplication
\"division
\"modulus

It is significant that the remainder will not work with a ‘u,’ even though the
four looks very much like a whole number. If you had given it a ‘u’, the four

units would have divided evenly into nroff’s five inches (1200 units) and troff’s
units as well, giving you a remainder of zero. With this exception, the best
practice is, when in doubt, to throw a ‘u’ in any calculation; if for some reason
it’s not needed, the formatter will ignore it.

Complex operations
Complicated arithmetic operations become a little more complicated
because of the default units of measurement, but otherwise they follow the
left-to-right rule given above. The command, therefore, that stipulates
sp 2i—2v* 16u/3u%5m
will be understood as two inches minus two vertical spaces times sixteen (totaling 6400 units in nroff) divided by 3 (2133, rounded to 2140 units) and divided
again (by 120 units®), and the remainder is the amount of space skipped
(ninety-nine units, which is rounded to one hundred, two and a half vertical
spaces in nroff).
Grouping also requires measurements, so that

sp 2i—(2v* 16u)
will produce a space of minus eight hundred units in nroff (80 times sixteen
taken from 480), and

sp 2i—(2v* 16u)/(3u% 5m)
would be a space of minus
twenty.

266, which resolves to 260, the nearest multiple of

Decimals

One by-product of this default to measurement is that the use of decimals
is somewhat restricted. Specifically, basic units cannot admit of any further
division, so multiplying or dividing by what appears to be an ordinary decimal
will actually be performing that operation with a rounded down whole number.
Thus

(121/2.5)
is in fact

(12i/2u)
8Though the m in nroff is usually a horizontal measurement, the formatter would translate it into the appropriate
number of units and calculate accordingly.

Introduction

16 Complications in mathematics processing
with the decimal rounded down for an answer of six inches.

The product of

(3.2u* 4u)
(3u* 4.8u)
(3.2u* 4.8u)

will always be twelve.

(3.2u* 4.8u)/25.5u
will return zero, while

(3.2u* 4.8u)/(25.5u/2.99u)
equals one.
This is not to say that decimals are forbidden in all operations (you have
already seen them in various scales of measurement), nor that the processor
cannot generate decimals; one example of how it could be done can be found in
Appendix VIII, which determines vertical space in decimals of an inch. But it
does mean that extra care should be taken in calculating various distances and
other quantities. While it is perfectly acceptable to enter
‘spi 1.921

you cannot enter the following and get same value:

sp ((7u* —4u+3u/13u)* 432u)
Certainly seven times minus four is minus twenty-eight, plus three is minus
twenty-five, and divided by thirteen should be —1.92; but since the computation
rounds down to the nearest integer, which is minus one, in turn multiplied by
432, this command produces a reverse spacing of only one inch as opposed to
nearly two.

Placing relations in parentheses is not necessary for basic comparisons, but it

The first of these complications is the use of the not (‘!’), which can
appear in combination with any of the relational operators. The technique is,
however, not obvious, in that the following will not work:
(6u!=5u)
What nroff and troff demand is this instead:
'(6u=5u)

Alternatively, this could be written without the parentheses, “!6u=5u”.

The logical operators ‘and’ (‘&’) and ‘or’ (‘:’) do not require any certain
syntax in order to work, but it is useful to place parentheses around the entire
test:

((6u> 5u)&(4u> 3u))
will produce a true condition.

And

will also be true.

Unlike programming, failing to match parentheses is not necessarily a source
of machine error, but there is the same human error if you’re not sure what is
grouped with what.
Increment

and decrement

In addition to their functions as arithmetic operators, the plus and minus
appear in a number of commands as an increase or decrease in a stipulated
amount. This allows relative change (as opposed to absolute change, in which
a specific figure is given) of the sort you have already seen with the “.sp” command; a plus or minus will increment or decrement the present value, and then
continue. The ‘begin page’ command can serve as an illustration. Taken by
itself, the command merely begins a new page. If an unsigned figure were

placed on the same line, immediately after the “.bp” (an intervening space is
not necessary but useful), the command would number the next page with that
digit. For example, the command
.bp 12
will renumber the next page as twelve. This is absolute, since the next page
will be numbered twelve, no matter what. More often, though, given the batch
orientation of nroff and troff, you have no idea of the number of the current

Introduction

18 Complications in mathematics processing

page, but you still want to skip one page that is reserved for a table or illustration. In that case you would request
bp +2

(A plus one would simply put you on the next page.) What you've actually
done is increment the storage area for page numbers by two, so that the next
number (whatever it is going to be) is skipped. Similarly, a “.bp —1” would
renumber the next page with the number of the previous page, whatever it
was. Therefore, even though the twelve in “.bp 12” is a positive number, it is
not the same as “.bp +12”. There are many commands that utilize this notation; some (like the indent command) use it constantly, others (like the pagelength command) never.

Definitions:

Number

Registers, Strings, Macros

Essential to any text-formatting program is the ability to perform three
tasks: (1) to reserve values (such as the measurements just discussed), (2) to
establish easy abbreviations for long or complicated words, and (3) to combine
commands so as to perform repetitive operations uniformly and automatically.
The roffs reserve a specific command for each of these three functions. The
first, the number register command (“.nr’”’), stores numbers or values under a

user-supplied name; the second, the defined string (“.ds”), designates words
and characters;

and the third, the macro

(“.de’’), clusters commands

(and, if

necessary, text, number registers, and strings) all under a single heading.
These three options represent both the great power and the challenge of UNIX
formatting. They can be ridiculously simple, though for some reason they
never are. All three depend on one or two character names of your own making; the trouble with any name is that it has to be different, it has to be called
at the right time, and (worst of all) you should remember it.
Number

registers

Number registers are simply locations that store certain values; the
values can be whole numbers or quantities in any of the scale indicators. They
can be set, reset, automatically incremented, and used to form other number

registers.

Setting occurs in the command

“.nr”:

nr X value
nr Xx value

Here ‘X’ and ‘Xx’ stand for any alphabetic or numeric one- or two-character
name of your choosing. Typically, a single space separates the name from the
command

on one side and the value on the other.

If, then, you wished to save

the value of twenty-one in a place arbitrarily called ‘Z’ and the distance six
inches in another location, called ‘Di,’ you would place in your source file, on
lines by themselves, these commands:
sore 4 Pa
nr Di 61

Should you wish to redefine ‘Z’ or ‘Di’ at any point, you would repeat this command, with the new value inserted after the space.

Introduction

Invoking a number register 19

Invoking a number register
A number register may be invoked to place its value directly in the text
(numbered paragraphs, section heads) or as part of a command. In either case
it is called by entering a backslash (‘\’) and an ‘n’ at any point on the line
(unlike commands, it need not be at the beginning of the line); if it is a onecharacter name, this is all that is needed:
nr Z 21

January \nZ, 1865
will produce a ‘January 21, 1865’ in the text, and

nr Z 15

.bp \nZ
will begin a new page, numbered fifteen. For two-character names, an open
parenthesis is necessary after the ‘n,’ but otherwise the same rules apply:
nr Un 12

.bp \n(Un
Commands with various scales of measurement present a difficulty, in that the
formatter converts the distance given to machine units. When invoking the
number register, then, it is important to add the ‘u’ to prevent an interpretation of the value as m’s or v’s:
nr Z 101
ll \nZu
nr Di 61

sp \n(Diu
Since the formatter has stored Z’s ten inches and Di’s six inches in units,
which in nroff would be 2400 and 1440, the ‘u’ is necessary to keep “ll” and
“sp” from thinking that those values are default m’s and vertical spaces.

Automatic increment and decrement
In order to automatically increase or decrease a number register by a set
value each time it is called, the value is placed after the initial definition, like
so:

nr Z51_ \"change by one each time called
mrUnwi
22
\"change by 2
nr Di 61 li
\"change by one inch
To effect the change, the number register is invoked a bit differently than normal, using the plus and minus signs. To increment, a one-character number
register would be invoked ‘\n+x”; for a two-character register, it would be
“\n+(Xx”. For example, ‘Z,’ ‘Un,’ and ‘Di’ above would be incremented by their
set values in this way:

\n+Z
\n+(Un
\n+(Di

Introduction

20 Invoking a number register
Decrement works in the same way:
\n-Z
\n-(Un
\n-(Di

The change occurs just as the register is invoked, so that the sequence
nrZ01

is necessary in order to start out with one when “\n+Z” appears; but such
registers otherwise behave like any other, so that a “\nZ” will keep returning
its last value.
Mathematics with number registers
All that was said about arithmetic functions applies to number registers,
and indeed it applies best to them. The calculation itself nearly always takes
place in a command, such as a request for a certain space or line length. Each
of the following, then, is legitimate and fairly common in deep troff and nroff:
nrZ 5
nr Zz 4

ssp (\nZ+\n(Zz)
sp
sp
sp
sp

(\nZ—\(nZz)
(\nZ*\n(Zz)
(\nZ/n(Zz)
(\nZ%\n(Zz)

These commands would produce spaces of nine lines, one line, twenty lines, one
and a quarter lines, and one line, respectively. It is important to remember
that the ‘v’ (scale for vertical space) need not be given in the definitions of ‘Z’
and ‘Zz,’ since the “.sp” command interprets whatever numbers it receives as

‘v’ |“.sp 1” is the same as “sp lv’’|. But if you do store a ‘scaled’ value in the
number register, you must specify the ‘u’ linternal units] in the command
itself; thus again, the ‘u’ in the third example below is necessary
nr Z 5v
nr Zz 4v

sp (\nZu—\n(Zzu)
since the processor has already translated those vertical spaces into units.
Should you wish to perform calculations apart from any command, this is
usually accomplished in another number register, for which see number registers within others (p. 21).

‘Read-only’ number registers
As it happens, nroff and troff themselves use number registers to store a
good deal of very useful information about the current line length, vertical
spacing, point size, and so on. These obviously cannot be redefined directly, so
they are called ‘read-only’ and are used extensively in tests or in formatting
calculations. All of them are two-character, so every one is invoked through

the sequence “\n(name”.
