Number registers within others
The roffs allow you to define a number register within another, so that a
product can be derived from two or more variables, or one value can be made
dependent on the particular circumstances of the text; this is done by placing
the ‘“\n” sequence where a value would normally be in a definition. If, for
example, you would like to print the difference between number register ‘Z’
and ‘Zz,’ you could enter this:

nr x (\nZ—\n(Zz)
\nx
This is also the technique that can be used in commands. Let us say that you
would like to reserve a value equal to three-fifths the current line length,
which is in read-only register “\n(.1”. You can store that value by establishing
a new number register, called “Tf’:

nr Tf (3u*\n(.lu/5u)
Then the next command will produce a space three-fifths of that line length
that was in effect at the time the number register was established:

sp \n(Tfu

Introduction

22 Invoking a number register

The only danger in utilizing number registers within others is forgetting that
the “u” is often indispensable (as here).
Naturally, you can also do the opposite with number registers: you can
establish a default value for various commands, so that no matter whether or

not you’ve changed format in one section, the next section will return to the
standardized house style. You do this by defining your defaults at the beginning of the file and then by invoking that number register at the start of each
new section. If, for example, you wanted a default line length of six inches,
you could define this at the beginning (here number register “Ll”) and then
invoke it throughout, like so:
nr Ll 61

\n(Llu
(Once again, the ‘wu’ is essential, since the default scale of “ll” is that of ‘m’.)
Why not just enter “ll 6i” throughout and save the bother? The reason is that
you can then change the line length at only one place (rather than several
score) if you decide on a new default, which may be necessary if you wish an
nroff copy of a troff text.
Defined strings

Both nroff and troff provide the capacity to define in a one- or twocharacter name a complex set of characters and in-line instructions (the entire
set is called a ‘string’). This is one of several ways the roffs can save entering
a long or complicated string over and over; but there are other uses as well. In
a fashion analogous to the number register, a string is defined with the command “ds”, followed by a space, the one- or two-character name,’ another
space, and the string itself:

.ds X string
.ds xX string
Like the names in number registers, the one- and two-character names are
invoked with a backslash; but this time it is with an asterisk instead of an ‘n’:

\*X
\e (aX
Finally, like number registers, they can be invoked anywhere in a line.
could, for example, define the following:

.ds cH

One

Caleb H. Carlton

The string could then be invoked at any point in the text with a “\*(cH”:

the distinguished \*(cH of the
Strings of considerable length can be defined, and there can even be multiplelined strings if the carriage return is ‘hidden’ or ‘concealed’ with a backslash
GNA):
*Though there are apparently few restrictions to the names of number registers, defined strings cannot have the
same
names as commands or as macros (fully defined later in this section). One can avoid any conflict with the first and
reduce the chance of interference with the second by using single capitals |‘Y’| or lower-case and capitals |‘aD’|, which

are rare in commands

and macro packages.

Introduction

Defined Strings 23
.ds X Jesse L. Williams of Indiana\
George Ashmun of Massachusetts \
Charles T. Sherman of Ohio \

Springer Harbaugh of Pennsylvania, and \
Timothy J. Carter of Illinois

\*X
The space was necessary before each backslash because, unlike ordinary processing, no space is automatically inserted. There seems to be a limit of some
two thousand characters in a defined string; anything longer should use the

command that inserts an outside file |“‘.so filename’’| instead.
Defined strings understand the various backslash requests, including
those governing horizontal |\h’distance’| and vertical movement |\v’distance’|.
This allows all sorts of complex definition, as, for example, in diacritical marks
or special abbreviations. Like everything else in nroff and troff, it does take
some experimentation, but with satisfactory results the definition can be saved

and used again. A superscript ‘th,’ for instance, that is to follow a date in
typesetting might be initially defined like this:
.ds T \v’—.5m’th\v’+.5m’
February 9\*T, 1865
As you can see, the vertical movement up must be offset by an equal movement
down, or the rest of the lines will be too high.

Also, the measurement

is given

in m’s, which has the advantage of allowing proportional motion in the various
sizes of type in printing. Speaking of type sizes, you can make the ‘th’ smaller
by bracketing it with internal size requests:

.ds T \v’—.5m’\s—2th\s+2\v’+.5m’

Instead of a ‘\s+2’, a ‘\sO’ [return to previous point-size] would also work on the
right side, but a symmetrical number is safer. If you would like to draw a little line underneath the ‘th,’ the command sequence is more complicated but not
mysterious:
ds T \v’—.5m’\s—2th\h’—\w’th’u’\(ul\(ul\s +2\v’ +.5m’
The addition here is in a backwards

horizontal movement

the width of a ‘th’ in

units, which is then followed by two underline characters, in the same point
size as the ‘th.’

If your string is to begin with a space, you must ‘quote’ that space with a

double quote mark |"| so that it will be taken literally:
.ds X " George W. Getty
Only one quote mark is needed; if for the sake of symmetry you ended the
string with another one, it would be printed.

Comments are possible in order to identify strings, but be careful not to
separate the comment from the definition by means of spaces or tabs:

.ds T \v’—.5m’th\v’+.5m’\"date definition

Introduction

24 Defined Strings

If spaces or tabs are entered, these will be taken as part of the definition, with
all sorts of different results.

Including number registers and other strings
Defined strings can include number registers and even other defined
strings, but with an important and unfortunate caveat. The string is stored in
memory when first read, and at that time the register will be given its value.
Generally, though, this is not what you want; if you define a string to report
the page number, for example, you do not want page one forever, which is what
the following would give us:

.ds N Page \n%

Similarly, you would like to be able to vary the internal string within this sort
of definition:

.ds S Dear \*(nA
so that ‘nA’ could be redefined. Consequently, there has to be a mechanism to
delay ‘reading in’ variables, in this case preventing the processor from filling
in the number register or text until the string is actually invoked.

This is accomplished through another backslash, which as I mentioned
has the ability to treat the next character as a literal (‘escaping’), so that the
first time a string definition is read the first backslash disappears, but the
second is allowed to pass. Our two definitions above should therefore read:
.ds N Page \\n%
.ds S Dear \\*(nA
Now, whenever ‘\*N’ or ‘\*S’ are called in the text, they will include the last
reported definition of the page number or of string ‘nA.’ This convention is a
pain for the same reason it is a pain in the UNIX editors: the backslash is,
indeed, rare in standard language, but it is so ubiquitous in nroff and troff
commands that it starts taking over. But it does defer interpreting the
number register or string until invoked within the text, and I suppose we
should be grateful for that.
You need not double the backslashes before the vertical and horizontal
motion commands, even though a number register or a string is involved; all of
the following work well:
nr Zz .6m

.ds tH \s—2th\s +2
.ds nA Winfield Scott
.ds 2Z \v’—\\n(Zzu’\\*(tH\v’ +\\n(Zzw’
.ds xX \\*(n A\h’—\w’\\*(nA’u’\\*(nA

After troff has processed these definitions, the requests ‘12\*(zZ’ and ‘\*(xX’
will produce the following:
1 oth
Winfield Scott

The string ‘tH’ is six-tenths of an ‘m’ higher than the 12, and General Scott
has been slightly emboldened in the overstriking. This latter routine will also
work in nroff.

Introduction

Macros

25

Macros

Macros represent the means by which commands, number registers, and
string definitions (and literal strings as well) can be clustered together to perform multiple operations under a single request. Unhappily, the only experience most UNIX users have with macros is in the macro packages, like -me or
-ms, where the power of macros is more apparent than the ease with which
they can be constructed. Clearly they can be extremely elaborate, but this is
not always necessary or desirable; a too ambitious system is not only more
prone to error, but also harder to adjust in areas where adjustment is inevitable. Every nroff/troff user has the ability to define macros, personal or otherwise; with a little care, such macros will not interfere with any package, and
in general the more direct, simple, and specific they are, the better they will
function.
Macros should be created for any point in the text where consistency is an
overriding factor (chapter and section headings, forms); they should be created
for commands that have become repetitive and tedious (paragraphs, bibliographies); and they should be created for those circumstances where the advantage is clearly with the formatter (running heads, footnotes, columns, automatically numbered paragraphs). Less likely candidates for extensive macro definitions would be infrequent situations like the title page (which admits toa
large variation) and minor tasks like changing the type size (which can usually be performed directly by a basic command). The insulation and convenience that a macro provides have a negative as well as a positive aspect.

The following section outlines the essential components of a macro,
describes the two means by which they are invoked, discusses ‘arguments,’ and
indicates something of the nature of diversions and environments. I would
hope, however, that before the end you will have experimented with a few macros of your own making, and perhaps reviewed the sections on paragraph macros and header/footer macros later in the book.
The macro disrob’d

Every macro has two aspects: definition and invocation. Usually a macro
is defined at the beginning of the source file, so that the entire document will
be affected, but definitions can occur anywhere. Invoking a macro can be done
automatically, when the formatter reaches a certain position on the page, or it
may be specific, because you wish that function at a particular point in the
text.

Every macro definition has four components: the definition command
(usually ‘“.de”), a name (these on the first line), a body of commands, and an
end (by default, a “..” on a line all by itself).

Abstractly, this might look like

the following:
.de Xx
commandl
command2

The macro name can be any character, or any combination of two characters;
unless you have some reason for doing so, the name should not be the same as
that of a command (like “.sp”), or a defined string, because the macro will
usurp it.

Introduction

26 Macros

A sample macro: chapter headings
Using the few commands and other devices described so far, you can construct a simple but useful macro for chapter headings. Let us say that, after
considering the problem for a while, you have decided that the chapter heading
should begin on a new page, start two inches down from the top of the page,
contain the word ‘CHAPTER,’ and finally space down an inch more. Therefore
you can expect four elements: three commands (begin page and two different
spaces), and a literal string. You decide to call this macro ‘Ch,’ for ‘Chapter
heading,’ and proceed to define it. As is natural, macro definitions expect the
commands and strings to appear in the same order that you would use if you
were doing each chapter heading manually, so you start with the Dp os
.de Ch

\"begin macro definition; name is ‘Ch’

.bp

\"begin new page

.sp 2i\"space down from top two inches
CHAPTER
.sp 1i\"space an inch, and end

This does not seem to be taking advantage of the formatter’s potential, however. You feel that what you'd really like is the ‘CHAPTER’ to be followed by
an incrementing number on the same line, and followed in turn by a string of
your choosing. The chapter number involves a number register, so you set one
(here called ‘X’) before defining the macro. The chapter title can be a defined
string (here, ‘nC’). The following is the expanded chapter macro, still named
‘Ch
nr X01
\"set X at zero, add one when incremented
.ds nC Introduction\"define first chapter name
.de Ch
.bp
sp 2i

CHAPTER \\n+X: \\*(nC \"literal string, register, defined string
sp li

It is of the utmost importance that there be two backslashes when the number
register and the defined string are invoked. As you already saw with the “.ds”
command, this puts off (‘delays’) reading the register or string until the macro
is actually called. Otherwise they would always be ‘1’ and ‘Introduction.’
Invoking a macro

Of the three special-features options in nroff/troff, the macro is the most
easily invoked: either it can be specifically called at the place in the text where
the function is needed (the case with ‘Ch’ above), or it can be automatically
‘sprung’ at a specific vertical position on the page.

The first is quite straightforward: you merely place, on a line by itself,
the macro name preceded by a dot |‘.’| in order to invoke it. For our example
above, the following would invoke the chapter heading macro, redefine the

chapter name, and invoke it again:

Introduction

Macros 27

.Ch
.ds nC Basic Principles
.Ch
As soon as the macro is invoked, it will go into effect, and generally this is
what you want.

Traps
The other way to invoke a macro is to request that it be called automatically when the processor reaches a certain vertical position as it composes a
page. This is the ‘trap’ described in the documentation, since when the processor arrives at that place, the trap is ‘sprung.’ Because it is a ‘when’ situation,
the trap command is
.wh distance macroname

The distance, if unsigned or positive, measures from the top of the page and, if
negative, from the bottom. If the page length is eleven inches, for example, a
‘“‘wh —1i” springs a trap ten inches from the top. To illustrate the mechanism
of the trap, the following example contains a date macro (‘Da’) that prints the
date one inch from the top of the page on every page:
.de Da

\n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr \"set date
wh

li Da

As in the example, the most common traps are set at the top or bottom of the
page, generally activating header and footer macros. These macros in turn
contain running headings, pagination placed left and right, and the top margin, or footnotes, running footers, and the bottom margin.

Universally, the

trap for the header is set at zero, so that the header will be printed before any
text; the footer is normally minus one inch.
Like most macro definitions, traps are generally set at the beginning of
the file, so that they can change the entire text; this is not a requirement,
however, and traps may be placed on any line in the source document in order
to affect the current page or, if the processor has already passed that position,
the next page. It is a good idea in any case to define the macro to be invoked
before setting the trap. To remove a trap, you simply enter another “.wh” at
that distance, but without any macro name:
wh

li

removes the trap that was originally placed at that one-inch position.
Within the text, the position of a trap can be changed with the “.ch” command, so that
.ch Da 1.51

means that ‘Da’ will, from now on, be called an inch and a half from the top.
Two points to note are that, unlike “.wh,” the command “.ch” demands that the
macro name come first and the distance second; moreover, the command will

function only if the macro has been given a “.wh” position previously:

Introduction

28 Macros

wh —-1li fO
ch fO —21

This has its advantages, because using the “.ch” makes it possible to ‘cover’ one
macro with another lusing two “.wh’s” in a row would cause the second to

replace the first], so that if the macro ‘on top’ is changed once again, the macro
below is uncovered and begins to function:

wh

—2i oT

.wh —1li fO
.ch fO —2i
(ht Oe teor

\"“oT’ is set
\"macro ‘fO’ must be set before change
\"‘oT’ is masked
\"now ‘oT’ will begin operation

This looks more complicated than it is; it comes in quite handy in footnotes,
where, if the room provided for the footnote text is not enough, a last-resort
macro at the bottom of the page will save that text as overflow.
There are two other varieties of trap that are rarely used: one, “it”, will
spring after a stipulated number of input text lines (those lines in your source
file that are not commands); the other, “.em”’,, has some practical application
because it will automatically spring at the end of the source file. This allows
you to print, for instance, overflow footnotes from the last page.

There is a

fifth sort of trap, relating to diversions, which will be discussed in that section.
Bracketing
Many macros are of a variety that might be best described as ‘bracketing,
in which one sequence of commands begins an operation and a counterpart
sequence ends it, usually returning to normal processing. The best example of
this is the table macro tbl, which is included in every version of UNIX formatting packages; to utilize tbl, it is necessary to bracket the tabular material

with a “.TS” |‘table start’] and end with a “.TE” |‘table end’].

This is a bit dif-

ferent from commands that act in tandem to change a parameter and then
change it back, as, for example, a temporary variation in line length that
would have one “ll” to change it and, when that passage is finished, another
“11” to return to the previous length. In the case of bracketing, the end macro
is a necessity, rather than an option; furthermore, it may not represent the
obvious difference that a change in line length would. For this reason the end
macro is often forgotten, usually with annoying results. To cite a common
example, any footnote macro (whether of your own making, or supplied by a
macro package) will channel text into a storage area for printing later, at the
foot of the page or at the end of the document; it will always need some indication that the footnote has ceased and the formatter can treat subsequent
matter as straight text. To neglect to design or insert a footnote-end macro
will instruct nroff/troff to treat all the rest of the document as a footnote,
which is not what you had in mind. The bracketing function is to be taken
seriously, then, and checked carefully before final production.
Macros within macros

The macro packages make extensive use of the formatters’ capacity to

define number registers, strings, and even new macros within a macro. In
some cases, a single command produces a document entirely different from any
other. If, for example, two different date formats were needed, one definition

could be set at the beginning of the file and the other tucked away in a macro.
In the following, string ‘dA’ returns the current date in full; if macro ‘Da’ is
called, however, ‘dA’ is changed to give only the year:

Introduction

Macros

.ds dA \n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr

29

\"define date

\"begin definition of ‘Da’
.de Da
.ds dA 19\n(yr \"different date format
The extensive cover-page macros found in the -ms package rely heavily on this
procedure.
To include macro definitions within other macros, you must enter a double backslash before the “..” that ends the internal definition; this prevents the
roffs from misunderstanding that end-of-definition line for the end of the
entire macro. The example below combines the chapter-head macro with the
alternate date format above to allow two kinds of documents, an informal one

and a formal one. If the chapter-head macro is not invoked, a rough-draft format (double-spaced, full date as page header) will be produced; with chapter
headings, the text will appear single-spaced and with only the year as a running header, triggered when the chapter heading is invoked, in single-space:
nr x 01
.ds nC Introduction
.ds dA \n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr
.de Da
\"running header macro ‘Da’
\\#(dA
\"print date (two backslashes delay)

ls 2 \"double space
.de Ch
.bp
sp 2i

CHAPTER \\n+X: \\*(nC
sp li

.de Da
\"begin new definition of ‘Da’
.ds dA 19\n(yr

\\\\*(dA
1s 1 \"single space

AS

awh li Da
The four backslashes before the different date format represent some of the
extremes to which computer logic will take you: the first time the ‘Ch’ macro is
read, the four are reduced to two la backslash, remember, ‘escapes’ the next
special character]; when ‘Da’ is read within that ‘Ch,’ the two are reduced to
one; and finally when ‘Da’ is invoked the string ‘dA’ is interpreted as it was
last defined. If the chapter heading macro were never invoked, the new definition of ‘Da’ would never replace the old one, so that the document can assume
two totally different appearances, depending on a single command.

Arguments
An extremely useful characteristic of the macro is its ability to accept
strings of characters at the time it has been invoked. Like similar usersupplied strings in the UNIX shell, these are called ‘arguments, and they usually function either to insert a literal word or to change a measurement. Up to
nine strings (separated by a space) can be placed on the same line as the

invoked macro name; the macro then assigns each a sequential number.

The

string is retrieved within the macro with two backslashes, a dollar-sign, and

Introduction

30 Macros

that number. Two are necessary because you want to defer reading the string
until the macro is actually invoked. To see how this might work, you can
revise your chapter-heading macro to accept title and spacing arguments:
nrX01

.de Ch
.bp
sp 21
CHAPTER \\n+X: \\$1
___\"first argument is chapter name
sp \\$2i "space second argument in inches

Now, whenever you invoke ‘Ch,’ you can dispense with the defined string of the
earlier version and simply place the title and lower spacing arguments thus:
.Ch Introduction

2

There is a potential difficulty in this, however, because of the computer’s
extraordinary inability to understand what constitutes a single unit. If, for
example, you had entered the following
.Ch At Springfield 2
the macro would understand three strings, not two, so that ‘At’ would have

been printed nicely enough, but ‘Springfield’ would end up in front of the ‘i’ in
the spacing command (which would ignore the alphabetic string, producing its
default one space), and ‘2’ would be forgotten. One solution is to place doublequotes around the multiple-word string, so that it will be treated as a unit:

.Ch "At Springfield" 2
Another solution is to place an ‘unpaddable’ space |“‘\ ”| between the words:
.Ch At\ Springfield 2
This latter method must be used if there are literal double-quotes in the string:

.Ch At\ "Springfield" 2
Luckily, punctuation marks are not interpreted as string separators, or you

would have trouble with ‘2.5’ and ‘Lincoln’s.’
Another potential difficulty occurs when you wish to skip a string that
the macro would otherwise expect. For example, you might have designed a
subsection macro (‘Ss’) that will have a heading and a shorter line length. It
might look like this:
.de Ss

\\$1
Sp
ll \\$2i
The first string contains the heading, and the second the line length in inches:

Introduction

Macros 31

‘Ss "Emancipation Proclamation" 4
But, let us say, you have a subsection with no heading; if you entered

Ss 4
the ‘4’ would be your heading, and the line length would be unchanged. To circumvent this, you must provide the macro an empty string for the purposes of
counting. This is done with two double-quotes, placed next to each other, in
the position normally occupied by that string:
Ss

we

4

This most often comes up in superscripts and subscripts, both in their macro
form and in equations, but it might also become necessary in a labeled paragraph.

A special read-only number register (“\n(.$”’) has been set aside to count
the number of arguments that appear when a particular macro has been
invoked. The value of ‘.$’ when ‘Ss’ was called in the last example, for
instance, was two. This is different from the number of strings that the macro
has the ability to incorporate; a macro may recognize six arguments, but if
only three are given, ‘.$ will be three. Though the utility of such a number
register seems remote right now, it is in fact extremely helpful in conditionals
within macro definitions.
Odds and ends

Macros can be removed with a “.rm macro” command, and they can be
renamed, with a
rn oldname newname

This is largely employed in macros within macros. You may append commands,
defined strings, or number registers to a macro with the command “.am”,
which is constructed like “.de”’; to add a few more instructions to ‘Ch,’ for
example, you might enter

.am Ch

Page \\n%
.sp

in order to have the page number printed below the chapter heading. The command doesn’t come up very often, as you might imagine. (I found one example
in the -me macro package and none in Bell’s original -ms.)

Diversions

Diversions in nroff/troff offer the means to store text in a particular
location (actually a macro), from which it can be retrieved when the occasion
arises. This is especially convenient when you want to print an unbroken block
of text on a single page (‘keep’) or reserve information for a later time (footnotes, automatic indexes). The diversion command is “.di”, followed on the
same line by the macro name (again of your choosing); the text will be stored
under this name. The diversion ends with a “.di” on a line all by itself.
nroff/troff process the diverted text as it is stored (so as to facilitate

Introduction

32 Diversions

measurement), formatting it according to the line length, spacing, and so on
that you’ve requested; since it is necessarily separate from the main text, the
diverted text is almost always bracketed with break commands (here a SP’;
though “.br” |‘break line’ will work as well). A typical sequence, then, might
be

di Li

.S
ae who like this sort of thing will find
this the sort of thing they like.
.sp
di
When the text is to be retrieved, two components are needed: the instruction to

bring back the diverted text without reprocessing the lines'® (‘no fill’ mode, for
which see), and the macro name, preceded by a period, on its own line. Usually, you would like to return to filling in text, so you would turn off ‘no fill’
with a ‘fill’ command (‘fi’). A typical sequence would look like this:

We may say, in the words of Abraham
nf

Lincoln:

Li
fi
There is no limit to the number of times this macro may be invoked, and
scarcely any limit to the size of a diversion, so this is another way of adding
repetitive texts. If ‘“.di Li” were used again, however, the previous contents of
‘Li’ would be replaced. And the macro may be removed like any other, with a
““

rm

Should you wish to add text to an already established diversion, there is a
cumulative version, “.da” |‘diversion append’], which will store sections of text
one after the other (or create a macro if one does not yet exist). This is used in
multiple footnotes and indexes.

It is possible and occasionally profitable to store a diversion within
another, so that texts can be measured and positioned correctly; this arises
with some of the more complicated displays (in which a passage is to be
presented as a centered block, for example). The diversion inside is not ‘locked
up’ within its surrounding diversion and is accessible from the level of normal
processing.
There are four read-only number registers associated with diversions, all
of which figure prominently in display and footnote macros. Three relate to
the various dimensions of the diversion, and one allows a test of the diversion
name (so, for example, if a footnote diversion is ongoing at the bottom of a
page, an overflow mechanism is triggered). The four are
\n(dl \"widest line in last diversion
\n(dn
\"vertical height of last diversion
\n(.d \"present vertical position within diversion
\n(z \"name of present diversion
lf the diverted text were processed once again, any breaks in the text that you intended would be ignored;
the formatter would just see short lines and think that they were lines that needed to be ‘filled out’ to their
proper length.

Introduction

Environments

33

The first two only relate to diversions that are finished, so that using them
within a diversion would furnish information about the previous diversion.
A very rarely used form of macro ‘trap’ appears in conjunction with diversions, the “.dt” [diversion trap], taking the form

.dt M Xx
This is set within a diversion to invoke a previously defined macro (here ‘Xx’)
when the diversion reaches M vertical distance. It is not found in any of the
packages, and I assume its function is extremely limited.
Environments

The name ‘environment’ is nicely descriptive of this function in
nroff/troff, in that it represents the ability to switch into a completely
independent set of parameters (line length, point size, and so forth) for a block
of text, and then move back to the parameters that were in effect before the
block. Environments can be found in macro definitions for running headings,
in which the point size, for example, is to remain consistent no matter what
changes there have been in the text. There are three environments available:
.ev 0 \"normal text
.ev 1
.ev 2

To switch from normal processing, then, you would merely enter “.ev 1” or
“ev 2” on its own line and establish the new parameters. You would return to
the previous environment with a simple “.ev” by itself; though this is frequently “.ev 0”, the documentation is quite specific that you use “.ev” with no
appended number.
To illustrate a typical use of environments, the following example defines
a number of parameters in environment one governing line length and line
spacing; there is a break after the first line, to separate the new text from the
old, and a break after the environment has been completed, so that the
remainder of the document can be printed in the previous environment:

In 1862, Lincoln wrote to Horace Greeley,
.br
.ev 1

js 1
ll 4i
I intend no modification of my oft-expressed personal
wish that all men everywhere could be free.
.br
ev
Greeley’s response

From now on, environment one will retain the characteristics of single-spacing
and a line length of four inches, no matter what has happened to the regular
text.
Environments are frequently used in conjunction with diversions (footnotes, for instance, are traditionally set in a smaller point size and vertical
spacing), and usually this is quite straightforward, or fairly so. For example,
you may wish to store and retrieve a diverted text in single-space when the

Introduction

34 Environments

document is double-spaced; you could bracket the diversion and retrieved
one:

A third environment, environment

2, is needed in addition to zero and one for

those rare cases of diversions within diversions.

In such situations, you may

need to know the size of the diverted material

(and so, for example, must store

it double-spaced and indented if need be), but you cannot have it printed with
further double-spacing and more indentation than it has. If you use two in
this way (there’s no necessity), you must remember that “.ev” is required to
leave an environment and that it always returns to the previous one, so that a
bare-bones sequence for switching environments might look like this:
.ev 1 \"move from environment zero to one

.ev 2 \"move to two
.ev.
\"move back to one
.ev. \"move back to zero
As I say, this is a rare case, but helpful when you’re aware of it.
Conditionals

Because macros are supposed to make decisions as well as perform menial
tasks, conditionals (also called ‘if’ tests) loom large in macro packages. Conditionals can, however, be used as straight requests, and in fact you would want

to do so if you wished the same document formatted differently in nroff and
troff. Moreover, after the swamps of diversions and environments, conditionals
are easy and enjoyable; the flexibility is so great that you can use them almost
anywhere. Conditionals in their abstract form look like this:
if test command
In practice, it’s even more simple. “if” understands ‘n’ to mean ‘nroff, and ‘t’
to mean ‘troff, so if you wished to double-space in nroff but not in troff, you
would enter the following at the head of your file:

Introduction

Conditionals

35

‘ifonielse2

It’s hard to imagine anything easier. In a similar vein, the conditional understands ‘e’ to be even and ‘o’ to be odd, so that placing a title to the left on an
odd page would take this form:
if o Title

(There is a title command, but why spoil the moment ?)
The conditional is the prime candidate for all the comparative relations
you saw earlier, so that

if (\n(Xx/3)>1

.bp

will begin a new page if the current value of number register ‘Xx’ divided by
three is greater than one. Negatives, constructed with an exclamation-mark
(“!’) in front, appear as

if !\nX>5.sp 5
This will space down five lines if number register ‘X’ is not greater than five.
Though parentheses would certainly be more safe in such circumstances, they
seem rarely to be used in the macro packages.
At times you might like to increment or decrement a number register
automatically [“\n+X”, “\n+(Xx’, “\n-X’, or “\n—(Xx”’] and simultaneously
test for a condition; this is not only extremely handy, but it saves you from the
following error:

\n+X

if \nX>5 .bp
This is a mistake because it will literally print the incremented X, thinking
that you wanted this. The problem, however, with

if \n+X>5 .bp
is that number register ‘X’ will increment even if the test is false. Some systems have now been modified to ignore the addition or subtraction if the condition is not true, but in the meantime you may have to enter the following if
the register must be kept inviolate:

if (\nX+1)>5

.bp

This does rather defeat the whole point of the automatic increment.)
In the various macro packages the logical operators ‘and’ |&] and ‘or’ |:]
rarely appear. The following is legitimate:

if ((\nX%4)=3)&(\nY> 5) .bp
but the macros packages seem to prefer the construction

if \nX%4=3 if \nY=5 .bp
And, instead of

Introduction

36 Conditionals

if (\nX>4):(\nY=5) .bp

the packages will generally use the ‘if/else, described below.
There are two additional features to the conditional that weren’t discussed in the Mathematics section: the test for existence and string comparisons. The “.if” understands existence (in a simplistic fashion, of course), with
the result that, if number register ‘Xx’ is neither negative nor zero, the following test

if \n(Xx .bp
will be true, and the formatter will begin a new page. This is the basis for
many of the flags the various macro packages use to signal, for example, the
presence of footnotes or columns; but it can have its delightful side as well.
The -ms macro package defines a string, ‘MO, which is the current month in

English (as opposed to “\n(mo”, the current month in Arabic).
the way it’s done:

The following is

if \n(mo—0 .ds MO January

if \n(mo-1 .ds MO February
if \n(mo—2 .ds MO March
if \n(mo-3 .ds MO April

if \n(mo—4 .ds MO May
if \n(mo—5 .ds MO June
if \n(mo-6 .ds MO July

if \n(mo—7 .ds MO August
if \n(mo-—8 .ds MO September
if \n(mo—9 .ds MO October
if \n(mo—10 .ds MO November
if \n(mo-—11 .ds MO December
If the current month is 1, then the first test is true and January pops out as
‘MO’; since all the remaining calculations would be zero or negative, the tests
for the remaining months would fail. If, however, the month were 2, January
would have popped out, but would have been immediately replaced by February, and so on. It does work.

A third characteristic of the ‘if’ test is that it can compare strings, which
is particularly useful in manipulating arguments within a macro. It is logical
that a conditional can be used to prove the existence of arguments through
such mechanisms as
if \\n(.$>0 .bp
or, from what you just saw,

if \\$1 .bp
But the conditional surpasses this, allowing you to test if, for example, the first
string is ‘yes’:

if "\$1"yes" .bp
or a situation where the first string might be blank:

Introduction

Conditionals 37
af AY $1""

.bp

This string comparison works more broadly than just testing arguments, of
course, but it can accomplish a great deal in this capacity alone.

Multiple commands in a conditional
For more than one line of commands, definitions of number registers, and
strings, and even more ‘if’ tests, conditionals provide a bracketing character, in
this case curly braces, preceded by a backslash |\{ and \}]. This vaguely resembles the convention for multiple lines in C, awk, and sed. It’s customary to
dedicate the first line to the conditional by itself, so the opening curly brace is
followed by another backslash; the following, then, is perfectly legitimate to
enter:

if \nX<3\{.sp4
.ds yY Report
.bp

\}
But it is more legibly done as

if \nX<3

\{\

sp 4

The ending brace can be on its own line (as above) or joined to the last command:

-bp\}
No additional punctuation is needed.
natural and uncomplicated:

Conditionals within conditionals are

if \nX=5
\{\
if \nY>3
\{\
.bp
nr Y 0\}
nr X 0\}
If this series were inside a macro, the backslash before the ‘n’ for number
registers ‘X’ and ‘Y’ would be doubled (“‘\\n”) to delay reading until the macro
is called.

‘flelse’ conditionals
The UNIX formatters also offer the capacity to carry out one set of commands when a particular test is true, and another when the test fails, through
‘if/else’ |“‘.ie/.el”|. The structure is nearly identical to the ‘if’ test, except, of
course, that it’s in two parts:

ie \nX>3 .bp
.el sp 5
A multiline series of requests within each component is handled with braces,
just as in the ‘if’:

Introduction

38 Conditionals

ie \n+X<7

\{\

nr X 0

.bp\}
el \{\
nr Y 0

sp 5\}
A whole string of “‘ie”s is possible, if you can think up a use for them:

ie \$9 To: \$9 \$8 \$6 \$4 \$2
ie \$8 To: \$8 \$5 \$3 \$1
ie \$7 To: \$7 \$5
ie \$6 To: \$6 \$5
.el To: All Staff

A macro using this series could be furnished to everyone in lieu of a routing
slip, and the names simply taken off to change the direction of the memo. The
‘if/else’ allows a memo to be sent to names five, six, and eight in different combinations.

Invoking nroff/troff
Calling for nroff
When all your formatting commands are in place, you must write them
into the article with a ‘w.’ After this, you can quit (‘q’) to get a computer
prompt again, and then you may invoke nroff:
$ nroff article<er>
or, if you're using a macro package,
$ nroff -ms article<er>

The computer will then show you, on the screen, what your article looks like in
format. There should be pages of consistent length, even margins, pagination,
centered titles, footnotes, and so forth.

If you see a grievous error as your arti-

cle comes spilling out, an error that will ruin the rest of the text, you can stop
the whole operation by pushing the equivalent of a DELETE key. Then you
can correct the error and use the individual page option (below) to preview just
that page and the remainder of the text. This, unfortunately, happens more
often than anyone deserves.
If you see errors as you preview the article on the screen, you’ll have to
use one of the UNIX editors to correct them, or make a printed copy to take to
a consultant. Some will find it inconvenient to move back and forth from the
editor to nroff in order to correct errors, so there is an option of previewing the
formatted text while in ed, edit, ex or vi. Again, you must write |“:w<er> ”|
your changes; but instead of quitting, you type:
:!Inroff article<er>

This is called ‘an escape to shell’; the editor prompt in combination with the
exclamation point can be considered the equivalent of the system’s prompt (‘‘$”
or

“soy”?

“%"). You will be shown the nroffed text, then returned to the editor.

A

frequent problem in these previews is that a long text whips by before you can

Introduction

Invoking nroff/troff 39
check it; if your UNIX system has more, you can pipe it through that program
so as to see only a screenful at a time:

:!Inroff article |more<er>
The text will pause until you hit the space bar.
Multiple files
nroff and troff permit the formatting of many files in a row, simply by
having them listed in the right order after the command:
$ nroff chapterl chapter2 chapter3<er>
The pagination will be continuous and format consistent under these circumstances. However, it is worthwhile to check each file carefully; if, for

example, files ‘chapterl’ and ‘chapter2’ do not end with a “.bp”, then chapter2
will take up right where chapter1 left off, which will most likely be in the middle of a page. Also, it’s not generally a good idea to do a large batch of formatting at one time, since an error early on (where they tend to congregate) will
make subsequent pages worthless. It is preferable to do processing in manageable bits, taking advantage of the various page renumbering options given
below.

Hardcopy: lineprinters
If you’re pleased with what you see, you can get a hard copy of the formatted article from the lineprinter in this way:
$ nroff article |lpr<er>

As with more above, the vertical bar serves as a pipe, and the Ipr is the system request that the material be sent to the lineprinter.

Hardcopy: automatic typewriters
Though all distributions of nroff should include written instructions on
how to print on the various automatic typewriters (here used synonymously
with ‘printers’), this is not always the case. For complicated situations, the

systems consultant should be aware of ‘/usr/etc/termcap’ (see ‘automatic typewriters’ in the Glossary). The following section can only sketch the typewriting process in the most general terms.

The typewriter will be either attached to a terminal or directly wired into
the computer. If the former, there should be a switch between the typewriter
and the terminal, or a series of commands written on the terminal to open contact with the typewriter. Be advised, however, that sometimes the terminal
‘filters’ commands to the typewriter, and certain macros (notably columns) may
not work as well in this system. If the printer is directly connected to the computer, you may have to log in just as you do the terminal, or the typewriter
may simply be wired in to the computer. In both cases, you have the choice of
ten-pitch [ten characters per inch (pica type)], twelve-pitch (elite), or proportional spacing (this last depends on the specific device, so cannot be discussed
here). There will always be a switch on the machine for character spacing.

The command to nroff is the old, familiar one. If you have a terminal
and typewriter, you would turn on the typewriter, insert the paper, then type
the command, but before giving the carriage-return flip the switch to the

Introduction

40 Invoking nroff/troff

typewriter lunless you want “nroff -ms ...” to appear in your article—generally
not a good idea]. If you have just the typewriter, you'll type the nroff command on a piece of scrap paper; and before giving the carriage return, scroll
out the scrap paper, put in your bond, and then push RETURN.
The command line will have one or two additional elements, reflecting the
demands of the typewriter. The first one will be “-Tn”, which means the terminal is n. Most printers, including the NEC Spinwriter and the Xerox Diablo,
will understand a “-T450” |four-five-zero] identity (though again your systems
consultant should be able to help you); if this is so, you must add that to your

command line:
$ nroff -T450 article<ecr>

or
$ nroff -ms -T450 article<ecr>

Furthermore, if you’ve decided to type in twelve pitch rather than ten, the
machine must be told this by adding a “-12” after the “-T450”:
$ nroff -ms -T450-12 article<er>

The other addition to the command line depends on whether or not you’re feeding in individual pages; if your paper is fed in automatically from a hopper, or
is provided on a roll with perforations along each side, you merely have to
scroll it in; but if you’re doing it sheet by sheet (a tedious process), then the
machine will have to stop after every page, so you can roll it out and put in
another. The command to stop after each one page is “-s1”, and it generally
appears after the “-T450” stipulation:
$ nroff -T450 -sl1 article<er>

The typewriter will halt at the bottom of the page. To start it up again (after
you
ve put in a fresh sheet), hit CTRL and the D key simultaneously. Be careful not to hit the D key twice in starting a new sheet. The formatter will
remember and just keep going at the bottom of the page.
Postprocessors and hardcopy
The large variety of printers has caused two major problems in production, problems which are becoming increasingly rare but occasionally still show
up: the propensity of certain devices to automatically add spaces around the
perforations in fan-fold paper and the inability of some to reverse movement in
order to generate columns. Solutions to both are found in two ‘postprocessors,’
programs which specifically overcome these difficulties: pg for pagination, and
col for columns. In both, the text is first formatted and then piped through
the program, like so:

$ nroff article |pg<er>
$ nroff article |col<er>
These also may be used with any of the macro packages.
pg was developed to circumvent those line printers that automatically

supply a half-inch margin on either side of the perforations.

This adds an inch

to each page, so that a text which is supposed to be formatted in eleven-inch

Introduction

Invoking nroff/troff 41
pages actually prints as twelve. To counteract this, pg by default prints the
sixtieth line, then skips the next six, then begins again. Any text in those six
lines is lost. These defaults can be changed, the first with a “-IN” (in which N
is any number of lines), the second with a “-dN”, so that

$ nroff article| pg -157 -d9<er>
will print the first fifty-seven lines, ignore the next nine, and then begin over.
pg also has “-s”, a stop-page option analogous to nroff’s “-s1”; it however takes
no number and is started again with a simple carriage return.
At many sites, pg for lineprinters has been superseded by a special lineprinter function: the “-N” (no space) option, which simply turns off the
automatic spacing, thus avoiding the possibility of lost text. For such sites the
appropriate command would be

$ nroff filename

| lpr -N<er>

Still a third option is to be found at the page-length command in Basic Commands.
col is discussed in the section on columns in the Macro part of this book.
Luckily, there are fewer and fewer printers around that cannot reverse motion,
so it needs less and less attention.

If you need to use col, however, and must

stop after each page, it will be necessary to use “pg -s” as well, since col is not
able to stop on its own:

$ nroff article! col | pg -s<er>
In this case, the “nroff -s1” will not work.

Background
If you do send your format to the lineprinter, you may wish to do it in
background (rather like ‘off-line’) so that your terminal won’t be tied up during
that time. This is done with an ampersand (‘&’) placed immediately after the
command:

$ nroff article |lpr&<cr>
You will then be given a process number (17456, for example) and a shell
prompt. The process number allows you to kill the operation if it has been
going on too long. You may sense this, but to find out (the system may be very
busy), you would request a process summary through the shell command ps,
like so:

$ ps<er>
The system will respond with something like this:
PID TTY
17150 29
17454 29
17456 29
17465 29

$

TIME CMD
0:00 /bin/sh -i
0:00 sh -i
0:15 nroff article
0:02 ps

Introduction

42 Invoking nroff/troff

If the process has been going on for an extremely long time, it may be necessary to kill it and examine your output file to see where it stopped. The kill
command uses the process number, like so:
$ kill 17456<er>
If all goes well, however, you’ll soon get a message that the the lineprinter has
finished with your text.
Error conditions

Both nroff and troff will attempt to process text in spite of error conditions, so that you will have a better idea of what the problem is; this includes
unbracketed macros, lines longer than the width of the paper when you've
requested that they be displayed as they are, and unintentional blank lines or
leading spaces. The processor will quit, however, when a nonexistent outside
source file is invoked or a word or line is so long that it cannot accept it (an
unusual condition).
Invoking troff
Because of the wide diversity of devices and organizational configurations, it is difficult to generalize about invoking troff; many word-processing
centers have added options either for the purposes of bookkeeping or to suit
their equipment. The following represents some of the more common circumstances.

It may be that you are able to obtain typeset copy, either through a laser
printer, photo-compositor, or whatever, simply by entering the following on
your terminal:
$ troff article<er>

$ troff -ms article<er>

Though this is still unusual, the declining price and improved quality of typestyle printers will make this more and more the case.

Otherwise you may have to request formatting when the printer is available with a ‘-w’ or allow yourself to be placed in a queue until your text can be
processed (perhaps with a ‘-Q’). At some locations it is possible to preview the
typeset text on a graphics terminal like the Tektronics 4014 or some newer

output device like the Pixel 80G'! high-resolution graphics terminal.

If the

terminal has some graphics capabilities, you might type a terminal identification first, then the troff command with a ‘-t’ [for “terminal”; for the Tektronics, the terminal setting is “tek”, and the troffed text is then piped to the Tektronics:
$ stty tek<er>
$ troff -ms -t article |te<er>

1180G is a trademark of Pixel Computer, Inc.

Introduction

Invoking nroff/troff 43
In complicated formatting such previewing is an absolute necessity, even
though the quality of resolution may not be very good on older terminals.

Alternatively, for a very quick (and somewhat dirty) view of a troffed
file, you may generate an ASCII version on your terminal screen with a ‘-a’:
$ troff -a article<er>

This produces largely unsatisfactory copy because the measurements are
disproportionate, but it will indicate where every line ends (thus aiding in
hyphenation checks) and where every page ends (a manual check for ‘widows’
and ‘orphans’). It will also tell you how many pages long your document will
be when finally formatted.

Production: individual and renumbered pages
As you have seen, like good soldiers nroff and troff keep track of the
current page number in register “\n%”; -ms will even automatically number
your pages for you. Because of this, you have the ability to call for a specific
page (only page fifteen, for instance) or to renumber the pages (say, for a book
chapter that is supposed to start on page thirty-four).

If you would like only certain of your pages to be printed, you can exercise the option ‘-o’ [for ‘only’] when you request nroff:
$ nroff -ms -oN article<er>

The Nstands for page number (see immediately below); notice that there is a

space before and after the “-oN’’, but none between the ‘-o’ and the number.
For obtaining particular pages, the convention runs as follows: after the ‘-o’7,a
number by itself will print only that number [“-o11” will print only page
eleven]; two numbers joined by a hyphen will print those numbers inclusively
[“‘-05-8” will print pages five through eight]; a hyphen before a number indicates the beginning up through the number [“-o-3” requests pages from the
beginning up through page three]; and finally, a number followed by a hyphen
means that page all the way to the end [“-022-” means pages twenty-two to the
last page]. It is possible to combine any and all of these with the use of a
comma between each page request; thus, for example,
$ nroff -ms -o-6,11,15- article<er>

stands for ‘only pages one through six, page eleven, and fifteen to the end.’
The second option relates to the numbering process itself. The computer
will begin numbering the pages as you wish with the ‘-n’ command; whatever
number is placed behind the ‘n’ is printed on the first page of your material;
therefore “-n16” numbers the first page with a sixteen. This can be useful in
writing chapters. The ‘-n’ option governs the ‘-o’ option, so that the request
$ nroff -ms -n22 -o-28 article<er>

will number the first page with a twenty-two and then print the next six
pages, up through page twenty-eight.

Introduction

44 Invoking nroff/troff
Redirection into a new file

As mentioned much earlier in this Introduction, an nroffed text may be
saved as its own file through the use of the shell’s redirection operator ‘>’,
which passes output into a named file rather than to the terminal. This comes
in handy if a few changes need be made to a formatted text (typographical
errors, for example, or a simple global correction), not enough to completely
reprocess the document. Or it may aid in proofreading, since formatting done
on the screen cannot be called back once it has passed. The redirection is quite
uncomplicated:
$ nroff article> output.art <cr >
When the computer is finished, it will return a shell prompt. If you wish to do
other things in the meantime, the process can be put in background:
$ nroff article>

format.art&<er>

You will then be given a process number and a new shell prompt. You may use
any of the UNIX editors on the new file, though you shouldn’t be surprised to
see a few unusual characters like the control-h (‘“H’) for backspacing.
Testing commands and macros
To test the effect of an individual convention, command, or macro, you
may want to generate a short test file that, when written, can be formatted
quickly through the shell escape (‘!’) mentioned earlier:
:15,20w testfile<er>
:'nroff testfile<er>

To save time, it is also possible to use nroff (or troff -a) directly, simply by
calling the processor from the shell with no input file. The roffs will then
accept whatever you type in and disgorge it formatted when you insert a flush
command (“fl”). For example, if you wished to know quickly how many vertical units were in a vertical spacing of seventeen points, you could follow the
following procedure:
$ troff -a<er>
.vs 17<er>

\n(v<er>
fl<er>
102

troff responds with the number, and awaits further input. If you have none, a
control-d or delete key will remove you from the program, and furnish a system
prompt.

Preprocessors
Every release of the UNIX formatting system will contain two macros:
tbl for formatting tables and eqn for constructing equations. These are very
large and fairly complex, so that a document utilizing them needs to be
passed
through them first before troff or nroff get their greedy paws on it.
For this
reason tbl and eqn are called ‘preprocessors,’ and the way they work is
by

Introduction

Preprocessors 45
taking the file, then piping it to the processor itself.

A document containing

one or several tables, for instance, would be formatted in this way:
$ tbl document | nroff<er>

All of the page options discussed above fit in after the processor, so that the
following command is legal (though prone to mistyping):
$ tbl article | nroff -ms -T450-12 -n25 -0-30 -s1<er>

or
$ tbl article | troff -ms -a -n0O -030- | lpr<er>
As I mention in the section explaining tbl, it is far faster and more efficient to
do tables separately and insert them manually. The hand is surer than the
processor.
eqn is treated like tbl, so that a text with interspersed equations might
be invoked in this way:

$ eqn article | troff -w<er>
All of the options are available with eqn as well.
needed, tbl takes precedence:

If both tbl and eqn are

$ tbl article | eqn | troff -ms -n48 -096-100<er>
There is also a variation of eqn for nroff called neqn, which produces approximations of mathematical symbols for the purposes of proofreading and (on
occasion) acceptable equations:

$ neqn article! nroff<er>
or, if conditions require it:

$ neqn article! nroff -T450 -sl1<er>

eqn and neqn have recently (1982) received more characters, and these are
available in ‘/usr/pub/eqnchar’ (see Appendix VI), which is to be read as if it
were another file to be processed:
$ eqn /usr/pub/eqnchar article | troff<er>
This more than doubles the existing character set.

nroff/troff Commands as Files
As requests for nroff and troff become more complicated, there is a
greater chance of omission or typing error. Fortunately, the UNIX shell provides an easy way to repeat a complex command like those given above, and
even allows variables when necessary. Basically, the command is written into

a separate file (‘com.nr’ below), and the mode of the file is changed to make it
executable; from then on, typing that file name will execute the command.

If,

46 nroff/troff Commands

Introduction

as Files

for instance, you were testing an equation/table file, you might need to type
the following command many times over:

$ neqn eqn.file | tbl | nroff -ms -T450-12
The shell allows you to create a file containing this command, so that the
whole line can be invoked by entering the file name. The only obligation is
changing the mode with the ‘chmod +x’ routine. Below is the entire sequence,
using the editor ex:
$ ex com.nr<cr>
"com.nr" no such file or directory
:a<cr>

neqn egn.file| tbl | nroff -ms -T450-12<ecr>
Cie
:w<cer>

"com.nr" 1 line, 41 characters
:!chmod +x %<er>
chmod +x com.nr

!
:q<er>

$
The chmod operation can occur on the level of the shell, with the difference
that the file name must be entered as well:
$ chmod

+x com.nr<er>

After this, the neqn command will be triggered any time ‘com.nr’ is entered
after the shell prompt and followed by a carriage return.
The argument

convention, moreover, permits the substitution of other file

names and variables. The convention demands that a “$N” be entered for each
variable; the shell in turn will expect that number of strings to be appended to
the file name and will insert them when necessary. In reformatting specific
pages, for example, you may want to vary not only the source file name, but
also the starting number for the pages and the exact page numbers to be processed. The ‘com.nr’ file would then look like this:
neqn

$11

tbl | nroff -ms -T450-12 -n$2 -0$3

Therefore, to format pages 35 to 40 in a file called ‘chapter.2’ (which starts on
page 23), the shell command would look like this:

$ com.nr chapter.2 23 35-40
As long as there are no internal spaces, each string may be as long and peculiar as possible: the last argument, for example, could easily be “-33,35-40,46-”
if that were needed.

2. BASIC FUNCTIONS

The following section describes those essential word-processing functions
that are found in basic nroff and troff, and as such it covers such fundamental

aspects of formatting as centering titles, indenting, double-spacing, and underlining. Macros, treated in the next section, perform their more complicated
tasks largely by clustering these simple commands in logical sequence. Since
there are well over a hundred basic commands, varying widely in utility, I have
chosen the most serviceable, grouping them under topical headings. Very
rarely used requests—those that rename special characters, for instance—have
been left to the Appendixes.
Some of these commands have analogues in -me and -ms; if so, they are
given after semicolons, -me first. The simplest form of the command has been
given in boldface, and the discussion afterwards presents the command

both in

its abstract form and the way it would appear in an example. Since the
abstract form is difficult, the definitions follow consistent notation, in order to
convey what the command expects: N signifies any number, M any measurement (inches, centimeters), c any character, and x or Xx any number register,
defined string, or macro name. Finally, where it is pertinent, I have furnished
the default setting at the beginning of each discussion and the relevant readonly number register at the end.

47

48 Adjusting

Adjusting

Basic Functions

| .ad |

|.adl
Default: left and right
Both nroff and troff automatically right- and left-justify margins,! giving those absolutely tidy blocks you're beginning to see in typewritten reports
and even memoranda. Many people prefer this, though I find such texts
extremely difficult to read; the typewritten line almost always contains irregular vacant spaces, and the typeset line is seldom better.
There are four different types of adjustment available on the system:
left-and-right [default], left only, right only (giving a ‘ragged’ look to the left),
and centered. Following that order, the appropriate commands are

cad b
adn
.ad |
adr
ad c

\"adjust
\"adjust
\"adjust
\"adjust
\"center

right and left
right and left
left margin only
right margin only
lines on the middle of the page

cad

\"return to last type of adjustment

(The “.ad b” and “.ad n” are synonymous.) These are simple enough, but
‘ad c” does need a word of explanation; it differs from the centering command
“ce” in that it fills text—that is, it composes lines to the specified length (six
and a half inches, for example), then centers the newly created lines at the
midway point; no spaces are inserted to straighten the right margin.

To turn off automatic adjustment, as I have in this book, you would enter
“na” [no adjust] among your set of preliminary instructions. If you wish to
return to right-justified margins at some point in your report, you would
insert, right before the text, the command “.ad’, which will reapply the last

kind of adjustment in effect usually “.ad n”].
Read-only number register: \n(.j |b andn =1; c =3;r
l and .na = 0]

Beginning a (New) Page

=5;

_[.bp!

The “.bp” command immediately halts printing on the current page and
begins a new one, though it does spring all the ‘traps’ on the way down. It will
take a number after it, either by itself or with a plus or minus. By itself, the
number tells the processor to make the next page that number: “.bp 6” means
call the next page number six. A plus or minus also indicates what the next

page should be numbered, but in this case it would be Nmore or less: “.bp +5”

means, if you’re on page twenty, that the next page should be called twentyfive. This is useful if there are to be intervening tables or graphs.

‘A very large group of nroff and troff users confuse ‘adjustment’ and ‘fill’; to avoid this problem, imagine ‘fill’ as
composing roughly equal lines out of the unequal lines in the original file. Then adjustment adds spaces to make
the
margins perfectly straight. It is possible, therefore, to want to fill text without adjusting it. This book is a kind
of

proof. If you wished to keep the lines the length they are in the file (poetry, perhaps), then you would turn off the fill
command. Adjustment will no longer operate.

Basic Functions

Breaking the Text

Breaking the Text

| .br | 49

[.br]

The command “br” is a local interruption of the automatic filling of text,
in that it places the text following on the next line. It might be used to set off
an example from the text:
that of Bruce Catton:
-br

\fIMr. Lincoln’s Army\fR
.br
Another standard source...

The second “.br” was necessary, or else the text following would have been
filled in after the book title. For an extensive list of items, it is possible but
cumbersome to place a break command between each element; for this purpose

the no fill command (“nf”) is more convenient.
Six other commands break text. One of these is the temporary indent
command (“.ti”), which indents only the next line. In the example above, if you
wanted to temporarily indent Mr. Lincoln’s Army, you might enter the following:
that of Bruce Catton:
tile Zoi
\fIMr. Lincoln’s Army\fR
.br
Another standard source...

Notice, though, that you still needed the second break to prevent ‘Another
standard source’ from appearing on the same line as the book title. The six
commands that break to a new line are “.bp” (begin page), “.ce” (center following line or lines), “fi” (fill text), “.nf” (do not fill), “.sp” (space), “.in” (indent),
and “ti” (temporary indent). An empty line, or a line beginning with a space
or tab, will also break the text; but it’s not a good idea to rely on them, since
they are often indistinguishable from each other.

Centering

[.ce]
The “‘.ce” will center the text on the line following; it often appears for
that reason on a title page:

.ce
Glory Road
If you wish to center a great many lines, the “.ce” understands numbers placed
after it (with or without a space) and will center that many physical lines that
appear below it:
.ce 4

Great Britain
and the American Civil War

by
E.D. Adams

50 Centering

Basic Functions

| .ce |

The command “.ce 0” |zero| immediately stops the centering; for this reason, it
often is included in various macros which end front page and title formatting.
One macro begins the centering with a “.ce 1000” command, the large number
necessary because of the indeterminate number of affected lines; the ending
macro then uses the zero to keep that state of affairs from continuing.
Normally, the command uses the line length, independent of changes in
the right margin,” to determine the center of the page. If the text is being
indented, however, “.ce” subtracts the indent from the line length and centers
the text on the space remaining. To override this, you may use the title command (“tl”), which ignores indents.
For those who rely on -ms, the center command represents a simple soluthe problem of the title page, if for some reason they wish to avoid the
to
tion
-ms cover-page macros. Given the complex initialization in -ms, it is very difficult to center a title on any line length except the default six inches. The “.ce”
is not affected, however, and so can be used throughout the first page:

nr LL 6.5
\"setting line length
.GO \"initialization
rs
\"restore spacing
spl
ce
Charles Francis Adams
After the first page, the title command can be safely employed.

Constant Spacing

[.cs]

Books customarily attempt to duplicate examples of computer programs in
the form that they would appear on a terminal screen or on a lower-quality
lineprinter. Quite apart from a different font, this requires constant spacing,
which overrides the proportional spacing usual to printing. The command to
accomplish this contains two different arguments, in its simplest form
“cs F N”. The Fis the specific font, addressed as 1, 2, 3, 4, or their
equivalents “R” (roman), “I” (italic), “B” (bold), or “S” special mathematical.
The N represents the constant width of the character in thirty-sixths of an m
(an m in twelve-point type is one-sixth of an inch). The command, then,
.cs B 24

would direct the formatter to set any text that has been requested in boldface
in a constant width of two-thirds of an m in the current size (this would
translate to each character occupying one-ninth of an inch in twelve-point,
two-ninths in twenty-four, and so on); the individual characters would be

exactly centered in that space. The widest characters will certainly touch in
the current size, so a further argument may be added: another N, indicating
what point size will operate in your calculations. This has no effect on the
point size in the text. For example, you might use the following command to

produce adequate constant spacing for a ten-on-twelve text:

The line length is controlled by an “ll” [default six and a half inches in nroff and troff|, and the margin
by page
offset, ‘“.po” [default one inch in troff, zero in nroff|.

Basic Functions

Constant Spacing

| .cs | 51

.cs B 24 14

The formatter will now print any subsequent boldface text in a constant width
of two-thirds of an m at fourteen-point, which is sufficiently generous to spread
out all the characters.

To return to normal proportional spacing for that font, the command and
font name are reinvoked, but without the various numbers:
.cs B

This entire process may need some test runs, since there are serious questions
of readability as well as of esthetics.

Date _ [\n(dw, \n(dy, \n(mo; .DA, .ND]
The present day of the week, day of the month, month, and year are available as numbers in nroff and troff, so that the string “\n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr”
will produce today’s date. -ms translates the month into the appropriate word
(see the Introduction above); nroff -ms automatically prints the date in the
footer. That can be turned off with a “.ND”, which is what most of us do; some
of us lie about the date, and this is accomplished through “.DA”,, so that
.DA April 1, 1984
will make them think you finished the project months or years ago.

Diacritical Marks

[\(aa, \(ga; \*c|
Only two accents are available in straight nroff and troff: the acute

accent |\’—made with an apostrophe—or \(aa], and the grave accent |\° or \(gal.
To print an accent over a letter, you are forced to use the overstrike command
(\o"xy", which will overstrike y on x): “sc\o"e\(ga"ne” produces ‘scéne”; it is
also possible to use the command \z, which stops the forward motion of the
next character, so that the effect is an overstrike: “gagne\ze\(aa” produces

“gagnee”
Accents in -me and -ms are stipulated by typing a backslash (\), an asterisk (*), the accent, and finally the letter to be accented. The lineprinter or
automatic typewriter will show only the letter with the accent above or below.
There are seven accents available in -me and -ms:

\*’?
\«‘
\e5
\**

(\*’e = é lacute accent])
(\*‘a = a lgrave accent])
Q*iu
=i lumlaut])
(\*°e = é€ lcircumflex!)

\x”

(\*°n =n

\xC
\*,

(\*Cc = ¢ lhacek])
(\*,c = ¢ lcedilla!])

Itilde])

The backslash and the asterisk have the effect of a foreign-language typewriter, stopping the automatic advance so that the letter can overstrike the
accent. A line including accents would be entered in this way:

52 Diacritical Marks

| \(aa, \(ga; \*c |

Basic Functions

Eston\*,ces fabl\*’o Alvar F\*’a\* nez
When the text has been processed by nroff, this line looks like this:
Estonces fablo Alvar Fanez
Though I suppose we should be grateful that nroff and troff can duplicate
foreign accents at all, I find the accent format cumbersome and prone to errors.
Some of the accents, such as the umlaut, are not very satisfactory; the acute
accent is acceptable on the lineprinter and in typesetting but terrible on the

typewriter, since a vertical single-quote is often substituted. Furthermore, the
accent commands will not operate well on capital letters, so that \* N produces
this: N. And, as if these problems were not enough, many of the accents use
punctuation that may unnaturally divide a word when you're using the computer to form indexes.
Many of the difficulties associated with accents have been alleviated,
though not solved, by more extensive definitions designed primarily by Richard
Accurso and Michael O’Hagan of the University of California at San Diego.
The set includes the Japanese and Latin long vowel and many Scandinavian
marks like the thorn and bolle. If you have only a few accents, it’s probably
best to use the ordinary issue; if you have many, you might consider the alternate, San Diego definitions.
With the UNIX editors you can, of course, use the power of global
searches and substitutions to help you in typing accents. For example, you
could put in a unique symbol for the accent before the appropriate letter, like
So:

Eston %ces fabl+o Alvar F+alnez
When you're ready to have the article formatted, you'll need to globally substitute the correct symbols:
:g/%/s//\\*’/g <er>
Notice that there are two backslashes on the replacement side, because of the
special character conventions of ed, edit, ex, and vi. And, if you use the dollar

sign |$], the circumflex |"|, or the asterisk |*] on the target side, you must precede them with a backslash:
:g/\"/s/\\*"/g<er>
If you use the tilde |"], it must be escaped on both sides:
:g/\"/s//A\*\"/g<er>
Again, this type of maneuver looks more difficult than it is, and it should save
you time and money.
