Fill

Fill?

|| fieantalse

[.fi, .nf]
Default: fill

In the regular course of things, the processor composes lines roughly
equal to the established line length (“.ll” or “nr LL”), no matter how they are
found in the original file; this is called ‘filling.’ And then, usually (though not
in this book), the filled lines are given spaces so that the margins are adjusted
on the right, for which see ‘Adjusting’ above. Fill is the normal mode, and normally you wouldn’t even have to read this section, except that occasionally you
would like to turn off fill and print the lines exactly as they appear in the file;
a common application is a vertical list, poetry, or a specific display. To cease
filling text, you would place the no fill command (“.nf’”’) before the text, like so:
nf
For Collector

Edwin H. Webster

Bel-Air, Harford Coy, Md
“ Post Master

Genl Andrew W Denison
Baltimore, Md

The no fill command is itself turned off with a fill (‘.fi’’):

as

Ephram F. Anderson
Hagerstown, Washington County, Md

fi
Both no fill and fill cause an immediate break in the text; with an apostrophe
nf], the no fill can be delayed until a full text line has been printed if you
wish, though I’ve no idea why. Perhaps it would work for shaped poetry.

There is one other use for no fill that bears mentioning: when you need to
print text that has been stored in a diversion, it must be retrieved in no fill, so
as to preserve its current format. Then you may return to fill, or use a flag to

return to fill if it was in effect before the diversion started.

Read-only number register: \n(.u [0 in no fill, 1 in fill]

Fonts

[.ft, \fl
Default: roman

A font, strictly speaking, comprises all the individual letters and characters available in a particular size and style of type: ten-point, Century Schoolbook; in UNIX, ‘font’ is used more loosely to describe the classes of a typeface:
roman (regular type), italics, and boldface, the last two used for emphasis or
other special purposes. These can be requested in numerous ways in troff, and

-me and -ms even add a few more.

The multiple commands arise in part

because occasionally a whole passage will be italicized, and occasionally a sin-

gle word.

If only the normal nroff/troff dot command (“.ft I”) were available,

then an italicized word followed by a non-italicized comma or semicolon would
have to look like this:

54 Fonts

Basic Functions

| .ft, \fe |
I was
brew

anxious\c
ft R
, if not fearful;
The “\c” [continue—more technically, suspend automatic functions] would be
necessary to prevent a space between the word and the punctuation. For single
words or phrases, then, the “\f” can be used, like so:

I was \flanxious\fR, if not fearful

These two conventions are not mutually exclusive, and a request may begin in
one form and end in another.
Another complicating factor in font requests has been the use of numbers
rather than names, so that different fonts could be mounted in one of the four
quadrants of the Graphics System typesetter. Though technology has overcome
this limitation (see ‘Other fonts’ page 58), some companies and institutions still

maintain the equipment; and when there is a choice of type styles (for example, Times Roman, Century Schoolbook, Newspaper Gothic) requesting a face
that is not default may require a different command: rather than “I” or “B’,
you might need to stipulate whatever is in font position 2 or 3 (1 is generally
the normal font and 4 the special character set). It is possible, for example,
that at a specific site a “.ft I” will produce italics only in Century Schoolbook;
if another face (Times Roman, for example) has been chosen, a “.ft 2” might be
necessary instead.
Boldface

|.ft 3, ft B, \f3, \fB; .b; .B]

There are four different methods to embolden text in troff: (1) the boldface font itself; (2) bold for the other fonts; (3) ‘overstrike’; and (4) the userdefined overstrike. The last two also function in nroff. I will treat each of
these in order.
The boldface font

troff offers an independent boldface font for many type styles. The exact
method of access may vary from system to system, but generally boldface is
invoked either through commands on lines by themselves (as was indicated
above), or by specific font requests situated within the line. For the first
option, either a “.ft 3” or “.ft B” on a line all by itself will print the text below
it in boldface, so that
Afites
To Gideon Wells

will generate the next line and all subsequent lines in a true bold, not just a
heavy roman font.
To embolden words within a line, it is usual to use the font requests ‘‘\f3”
or “\fB” before the element or elements:

let them have the credit \f3now.

Basic Functions

Fonts

| .ft, \fe | 55

(There is in fact a bold period in the font, as there are a bold underscore,
hyphen, comma, and so forth.)

Normally you would like the boldface turned off at some point, usually to

Roman type, and this is accomplished, with either a “.ft 1” or “.ft R” on its own
line, or a “\f1” or “\fR” within the text:
ft B
To John F. Miller
et ake

or
let them have the credit \f3now.\f1
A simple “.ft” without any accompanying letter or number will return to the
previous font, but it’s a good idea to specify it. And, though it is possible to
mix the dot and in-line styles of commands,

I wouldn’t recommend it because

this will make it difficult later to check if all requests for boldface (or italics)
were turned off.

The boldface command and others like it do not negate other ‘period’ commands, even if they all are affecting the same text. The following title is perfectly legal, which is useful to know, since boldface is often used in such circumstances:

ft 3
.ce
To Ulysses S. Grant
The two may be reversed if you like, with no difference in the text produced.

Bold for italics, roman, and the special font |.bd!
Basically, the “.bd” command causes the typesetter to double-print any
text in any font, with the amount of offset determined by the user; this
renders a kind of ‘shadow’ bold. The command for italics and roman has two
parts: the first to name the affected font, the second to define the number of

units that it will be offset (which figure is actually defined as the number of
units plus one). To embolden the italic font, then, with a second printing at
two units to the right, you would enter the following command:

bd 13
After this command, an italicized text from now on will be printed in that light
bold. To turn off this command, the font is named but the number is left
blank, like so:

.bd I
For the special font (that is, the Greek characters and mathematical symbols
normally mounted in the fourth position), the command is a little more complicated, in that it expects three rather than two arguments: the second argument is ‘S’; but the third is now ‘R,’ ‘I,’ or ‘B, meaning that the special font is

to be emboldened when the text being processed is in the roman, or italic, or

56 Fonts

Basic Functions

| ft, \fc |

boldface font; the fourth becomes the number (plus one) of units in the offset.
For instance, the following will offset print the special font by three units
whenever the text is in boldface:

bd SB4
Canceling the request is a matter of leaving off the number:

bd SB
If you wish to print the special font in this sort of bold, no matter what the
font, three separate commands

will be necessary.

Overstriking | \o|
The “\o” command is most often used to amalgamate different characters
into one (as with diacritics above), but it can be used to embolden a single
letter by asking nroff or troff to print it continually at the same position. To
generate a particularly thick capital ‘R, for example, you could enter the following:

\o,RRRRRRRRR’appahannock
Fortunately or unfortunately, nine is the maximum
overstruck in this way.

of characters that can be

User-defined overstriking
There are two ways to overstrike on the most basic level of horizontal
movement. They have the disadvantage of a more complicated entry and the

advantage of encompassing whole strings and, like the “.bd” command, allowing you to specify the number of units (if any) to move forward for the second
pass. The first is described in Brian W. Kernighan’s A Troff Tutorial (p. 6) and
uses both the horizontal movement command “\h” and the width function “\w”.
The string is entered, then the typesetter is instructed to move backwards horizontally the width of the string in units and then to move forwards one or two
units; finally, the word is entered once again. The entire operation looks like
this:
The Doll Jack\h’—\w’Doll Jack’u’\h’2u’Doll Jack is pardoned
by order of the President.

As Kernighan indicates, it is possible to create a macro that will do this
through the arguments function (p. 11). This technique, however, is to be used
with some care, as it sometimes will not behave very well when up against the
right margin of a page of justified text. A somewhat safer user-defined overstrike (discussed in the Nroff/Troff User’s Manual, §11.3, p. 21) involves the inline mark command “‘\kx”’, details for which can be found below in the section
on marking. The following example uses an underscore to mark position:

\k_Doll Jack\h’!\n_u+2u’Doll Jack
The two-unit offset is, again, the user’s option. The advice is that it produces a
satisfactory bold in the default ten-point setting. As for the complications in
typing this, I apologize in advance.

Basic Functions

Fonts

| .ft, \fe | 57

Boldface in -ms and -me

The macro packages -me and -ms each add one further command to the

above: a “.b” |-me] or a “.B” |-ms|, which always appears at the beginning of a

line. Both “.b” and “.B” may take two different forms, however: (1) all by
itself, before any quantity of text that is to be printed in bold (and thus is like
the dot commands above), or (2) it may be followed by a single string, like so:
Every
-b Unionist
ought to wish the new government to succeed.
or, in -ms,
.B Unionist

If there is punctuation following that is not meant to be emboldened, both -me

and -ms allow that as a second ‘argument’:

give the bearer, Mr. Hammond, an
.b interview ?
or
.B interview

?

The punctuation will appear in the previous font, directly after the word. If it
is a two- or three-word string that is to be printed in bold, you may use
backslashes or double quotes to make the formatter think that there is only a
single string:

.B informally\ expressed ;
.B "informally expressed ;
.B "informally expressed" ;
These will all work. The advantage to this is that you won’t have to remember
to turn off the boldface command, and this in turn is one fewer thing that
could go wrong. As a final note, you should know that if you are using nroff
to test a document that will eventually be typeset, in nroff -me and -ms the
text set off in boldface will be underlined.

Italics |.ft i, .ft 2, \f1, \f2, ul, .cu; .i; .1]
The commands for italics are of three varieties, two of which duplicate
the dot command and font request for boldface; the third represents a link
with nroff. In the first case, either a “ft I” or a “.ft 2” goes on its own line
before the text to be italicized; with the exception noted above (concerning the
various type styles available), both of these commands can be used interchangeably. An example of the “.ft 2” would be the following:
Washington
ftez
Daily National Intelligencer
fied:
(April 11, 1865)

58 Fonts

Basic Functions

| .ft, \fe |

Note and remember that a return to roman font must be signaled by the
appropriate command; naturally, you will forget, and produce at least one
whole paragraph or whole page or whole document in italics. Think of it asa
kind of initiation.
To italicize a single word or a group of words without changing punctuation, the command is “\fI” or “\f2”, immediately preceding the word (no
spaces):

It also shows how \fIsound\fR, and how \fIstrong\fR we still are.
It is possible to mix the two styles of commands, but it does make proofreading
difficult.
There is a third way to italicize in troff, though the commands

are more

appropriately considered as underlining in nroff. These are “.ul N”, in which
N represents the number of lines to be italicized; and “.cu N” |continuously
underline], which in these circumstances behaves like “.ul’”. These appear
rarely in a text prepared for typesetting, and in fact only function to maintain
compatibility between nroff and troff.
Italics in -me and -ms

The macro packages also allow slightly different requests for italics, more
or less suited to their style of commands, and exactly parallel to their boldface
commands. In -me, you may use an “.i” beginning a line, either standing all
by itself on a line before the text that you wish to italicize or followed by a sin-

gle string (temporary italic). In -ms, there is an “.I’, exactly like -me’’s “i”:
the temporary command “.I” before a single string; or “.I” on the line before a
passage. As in “.b” and “.B’, the string may be a word or group of words preceded by or bracketed by double quotes or containing ‘unpaddable’ spaces:
.I"Miss Clara Barton
.I"Miss Clara Barton"

.I Miss\ Clara\ Barton
Any ending punctuation that is not to be italicized may be added as a second
‘argument’ on the same line, separated from the string by a space.
Roman

|.ft r, ft 1, \fR, \f1; .r; .R|

Since roman is the default font and normally only invoked when italics or
boldface are no longer needed, it does not require discussion. The ‘period’ commands for roman are “.ft R” or “.ft 1”, the in-line commands are “\fR” or “\f1”,
and -me and -ms have defined an “.r” and “.R”, respectively, to create a symmetry with their boldface and italic commands.
Other fonts

While it was always possible to utilize other fonts with the Graphics System typesetter, only four could be used at one time. Now, new typesetters have
been developed that completely eliminate the old restrictions on the number
and type of fonts, and ‘ditroff’ now makes it easier to access as many as are
needed.” Among those families most often mentioned in connection with
3See Brian W. Kernighan, “A Typesetter-independent
No. 97 (revised, March, 1982), p. 10.

TROFF,”

Bell Laboratories Computing

Science Technical

Report

Basic Functions

Fonts

| .ft, \fc | 59

‘ditroff’ are Helvetica, Helvetica Italic, Helvetica Bold, Outline Helvetica Black,
and Old English. These may be accessed with a “\fH”, or (going into the twoletter name), “\f(HI”, “\f(HB”, “\f(OK”, and “\f(OE”. As these multiply, the
number equivalents may become more and more difficult to remember, even if
they still work.

Read-only number register: \n(.f |1-4, indicating placement |

Font (previous)

|.ft p, \fP]

At times in macros it is necessary to switch into italics or boldface or one
of the other fonts, and then return to the current font in the text.

If the font

can be represented by a number, it is possible to store that number in a register and retrieve it, though this is clumsy and obviously limited. troff provides
a better solution in either “.ft P” (on a line by itself) or “\fP” (within the line).
To return to the font that was in effect before the change, no matter what it
was, you simply enter either of these commands.

Horizontal Movement _ [\hl
The most common method for moving horizontally is to use the command
\h’M’, in which M will be the required distance |default m’s], and almost any
character can act as a separator (the apostrophe is conventional). If Mis
unsigned or positive, the movement will be to the right; if negative, to the left.
You are permitted to use absolute spacing (see ‘User-defined Overstriking,’
above, and ‘Spacing, Absolute,’ below), so that \h’15i’ will place any text following five inches from the margin, no matter where on the line you were originally. The horizontal movement command often appears in conjunction with
the width function |\w’string’u], so that the motion will be correct in nroff or
troff, and proportionally the same in different point sizes. The trick is to make
sure there are four separators:

\h’\w’ Walt Whitman’u’
If there aren’t four, the function will fail completely.

Hyphenation

[.hy, .nh, .hw]
Default: (.hy 1)
Options for hyphenating words in nroff and troff exist on two levels: (1)
as a global command, normally placed at the beginning of the file, and (2) as a
specific request for individual words. On the global level, there are three
choices: hyphenation can be allowed with no restrictions, allowed under restrictions, or halted altogether. Hyphenation for individual words can be defined at
the beginning of the file or at certain junctures in the text; in both cases,

Basic Functions

| .hy, .nh, .hw |

60 Hyphenation

specific commands act either to prevent hyphenation altogether or to force a
—at a parhyphen—if the composition of the line makes it absolutely necessary
ticular position in the word.

Global level: allowing hyphenation

|[.hy]

In nroff and troff, it is possible to designate at the head of a file the kind
of hyphenation desired. Specifically, you may stop any hyphenation on the
first two or the last two letters of a word, or on the last line of a page. The
relevant command is “.hy”, followed by an appropriate number code. ‘0’ means
‘off’ (and hence is the same as no hyphenation |“.nh’]); ‘1’ allows all hyphenation; ‘2’ allows it everywhere except on the last line of a page; ‘4 prevents
splitting on the first two letters of a word; and ‘8’ prevents hyphenation on the
final two letters of a word. If you wished all of the three last conventions, you
would simply total them up and place the sum after the “.hy”:
chy 14

(2+4+8). This would be entered on a line all by itself before your text begins.
14 is the default value for -ms, which means that any macros which initialize
(‘““PP” and “.LP”, for instance) will reset to this automatically. A change,
then, must be stipulated after such macro commands in the text.

Individual hyphenation
There are two methods of controlling hyphenation of individual words.
The first marks words within the text, the second allows a list of correctly
hyphenated words (or words to be left alone) before the text begins. To insure
that a hyphen is correctly placed in a specific word, you would employ the two
characters ‘\%’. If positioned within the word, the ‘\%’ indicates the place
where it should be split; if before a word, the word will not be divided. Ina
typical passage, you might write
I claim not to have con\%trolled events,

or
but \%confess plainly that events have controlled me.

The other aid in individual hyphenation is available in the list of words placed
after the command “.hw” (for ‘hyphenated words’), which appears before the
beginning of the text. This will regulate the hyphenation on the words as they
appear throughout the body of the document. The list can contain either whole
words (to prevent hyphenation) or words with a hyphen signaling in the
appropriate break:
-hw malice char-ity
If more than one line is needed for this list, all but the last line must have a
UNIX continuation-symbol ‘\’, like so:

-hw intend modifi-cation personal everywhere mental\
reserva-tions construe, purpose, consti-tution\

hypercritical

Basic Functions

Hyphenation

| .hy, .nh, .hw | 61

Unfortunately, the space for this list is small, at 128 characters (the words in
the example above total 114).

Suppressing hyphenation

_[.nh|

Another option is to cancel hyphenation altogether; the appropriate command, given at the beginning of the text, is
nh

for ‘no hyphenation’ (the same as “.hy 0”). This option is most successful in
text that has not been adjusted.* Even so, words containing hyphens (for example, ‘commander-in-chief’) will be split if necessary at a hyphen.
Previewing hyphenation
You have only to pick up a newspaper to witness the dangers of
computer-generated hyphenation; unfortunately, the solution—short of full
hyphenation tables that the machine would need to consult frequently—is
elusive, since the rules of syllabic division in English are irregular, depending
for the most part on pronunciation. The principles that UNIX utilizes generally work, but occasionally the division can be embarrassing and even confusing. All of the options described above (except suppressing hyphenation altogether) require a preview of the formatted text, and then another preview to
check whatever new hyphenation has been forced in the paragraphs that were
changed.
Fortunately, previewing for hyphens is not as tedious as it sounds, given
UNIX’s pattern-finding utility grep. grep can be invoked from the shell or—
more efficiently in this case—from inside the UNIX editors ed, edit, ex, or vi.
For using grep to list hyphenated words from within the editor, you first format the text, then pipe it through grep, like so (here shown with the editor’s

colon prompt):

:Inroff %| grep "\-$<er>
The exclamation mark represents what the documentation terms a ‘call to
shell,’ so that the prompt and exclamation together can be thought of as the
equivalent of the shell prompt. The percent sign in such calls is interpreted as
the name of the file you’re editing; naturally, if you were in the shell at the

time, you would use the filename instead.

The ‘pipe symbol’ ||| will send the

formatted text through grep, which will print on the screen only those lines
ending in hyphens. Since grep uses the hyphen to indicate options, a single
backslash is placed before the hyphen; this convention with the backslash

(‘escaping’) insures that the following character will be taken literally.
To preview hyphenation for troff, the command is similar:

:Itroff -a %|

grep "\-$<er>

The ‘minus a’ allows troff to send a formatted copy to the terminal, a copy that
will show the composition of lines and pages, and a little more. Finally, to
repeat this command in order to check hyphens once again, you write in the
44s was mentioned earlier, “.hy 14” is default in -ms, and this value applies in all of the section and paragraph macros.

An “.nh” must therefore be reinstated immediately after any such macro commands.

Basic Functions

| .hy, .nh, .hw |

62 Hyphenation

changes you have made (with a “:w”) and then type another exclamation mark
next to the first, like so:
!!<er>

The second exclamation instructs the system to duplicate the last shell command.

Indents

|.in, .til
In nroff and troff, the most common command for indenting is “in M” on
a line before whatever text is to be moved over. M by itself [not preceded by a
plus or minus] will determine the distance from the margin that the text block
will be indented (the margin is itself defined by the ‘page offset’ command). To
illustrate, in the following example the text is first indented one inch, then two

inches:
ata hn

When you can’t find it any where else look into this.
in 2i
Herndon’s recollection was “look in this.”
To return to the left margin after an indent, the best policy is to insert a zero
indent:
Herndon’s recollection was “look in this.”
in 0

Otherwise, it is possible through a minus or plus before Mto move that distance to the left or right:

in 1i\"since it’s from the margin, “.in +1i” is synonymous
When you can’t find it any where else look into this.
an +h

\"the indent is now at two inches

Herndon’s recollection was “look in this.”
in —2i
\"the indent now is at zero
In other words, a positive
decrementally to the left.

M moves incrementally to the right, a negative

Playing ping-pong with single lines like this can be dangerous (it’s easy
to lose track), so nroff/troff have a special command to indent only the next
line: the temporary indent, “.ti M”. As above, if the distance is entered as an
unsigned integer, it effects a temporary indent from the margin; and again, a
‘+’ or ‘—’ adds or subtracts from an indent already in place. So the above
example could be rewritten:
ti 1i \"with zero indent, synonymous with “ti +1i”
When you can’t find it any where else look into this.
ti 2i \"still zero indent
Herndon’s recollection was “look in this.”

Basic Functions

Indents_

| .in, .ti | 63

The temporary indent operates on one line only, so subsequent text will return
to the indent in force; if there has been no request for an indent, then the text

will revert to the margin.

Both “in” and “.ti” of themselves break the text, so no other command is
necessary to start a new line. But on those rare occasions when you need to
forestall a break until the line has been completed (so as to maintain left justification while making room for a figure to the left, for instance), you may

substitute an an apostrophe |’in] instead of a period, like so:
Lincoln replied to Miss Bedell (see daguerreotype, left)
chara
that ‘as to the whiskers, having never worn any, do you not
think people would call it a piece of silly affectation if
I were to begin it now?’
You must recall, however, that the apostrophe allows text coming after to fill
the line, so that the indent may not occur when you might expect it to.

A text can be indented both left and right, using “.in” and “ll” in combination. For this it is important to remember that the indent is still figured
from the left-hand margin; so, to produce a four-inch-wide block from a sixand-a-half-inch line, you would insert the following instructions:
jl 51
in li
Text goes in here.
in 0
ll 6.51
The exact order of commands does not matter, with the usual slight advantage
going to a mirror-like symmetry. What is crucial is that indenting one inch
and then asking for a four-inch line simply will not work.

An extended sequence of commands for blocks of quoted material makes
this a perfect candidate for a pair of bracketing macro definitions. For a quotation in the text, you might enter the following commands:
sp l
ll 5i
in 10n
.ps 9
Sigcoe tal
.ne 2
Quoted text this time
._ps 10
.vs 12
in 0
ll 6.51
sp 1

But to save all this typing over and over, you could place the two series in com-

plementary macros at the beginning of the text.

There is the added advantage

Ligature

|.lgl
Default: on (in troff)

The ‘f and i’ and ‘f and |’ ligatures are produced automatically in troff, a
generally desirable feature, though on a number of occasions hyphenation fares
poorly. There are five ligatures, all simultaneously set with a single command,
“lg”; each may also be requested as a special character, quite apart from
whether or not the ligature setting has been turned on. The following are the
three double-letter and two triple-letter ligatures, given with their individual
requests:

\(fi
\(fl

\(fF

EB
ER
ERD

\(Fi
\(FIl

To cancel the automatic printing of ligatures, you need to enter the command
“lg 0”; to recommence it, “.lg” by itself or with any other number except 2;
“lg 2” will allow only the two-character ligatures (fi, fl, ff).

Line Length

[.11; .nr LL]
Default:

6.5 inches (6 inches in -ms)

Outside the macro packages, line length in troff and nroff can be altered
with the request “.1] M”; Mhere represents the new distance [default n’s|. The
command allows the current line to finish, and begins printing all subsequent
lines in the requested length. Though this enhances its flexibility, the norm is
that new length governs new text, so typically a break precedes the change:
Governor Andrew Johnson, Nashville, Tennessee
.br
WLG.25%
Let the matter of the McKendee Church remain as it is
without further action until you see me.

The ‘i’ linches] was extremely important here; otherwise, the roffs would have
tried to compose lines six characters wide. Try it sometime. Changing line
lengths back and forth is perfectly legitimate, and sometimes necessary if you
have a text that you want to indent from either side, as was explained in the
section on Indenting. It is important to note there that the indent does not
change the line length, which is always calculated from the left margin.

In -ms, the default line length is six inches; but you may request a different size with “nr LL M”. This should appear before initialization (the
“.GO”). Within the text, the line length command has two forms, depending on
how long you want the new length to function. If it’s just for a short term

Line Length

| ll; nr LL | 65

(that is, within a paragraph), any number of “ll M” will change the line. At
the beginning of the next paragraph or section, the paragraph macro will
automatically restore the length to its ‘LL’ setting. If, then, you wish to vary
the line length within a paragraph, and then change it for good for the next
paragraph, you might enter something like this:

.PP

\"line length default in -ms is six inches

As to peace, I have said . . . that three things are indispensable.

1. The restoration of the national authority throughout all the States.

IO.on

In other words, if in -ms the change to the line length is both within a paragraph and permanent, governing text beyond the present paragraph, the

“11 M” and the “.nr LL M” must appear in tandem—the first to change the
length immediately, the second to function after the next paragraph or section
heading:
ll 6.251
Soe JEL, 24s

This makes sense, sort of. If you’re not certain, but wish to effect
change, nothing is lost by including both commands.

Line Numbering

a permanent

[.nm; .nn|

The roffs provide in “.nm N” the ability to automatically number lines as
they are printed in format.’ The obligatory N indicates the number with which
to start (usually one, but any number is acceptable). By default, the command
places a count for every line to the left of the text; the allocated area is three

spaces wide (so that a number with three places is left-justified), and separated
from the text by a single space. To vary some of these defaults, the command
understands three ‘arguments, all numbers, separated by a space and entered
after the N: M (second position) stipulates that only numbers in multiples of M
are to be printed; S (third position) governs the amount of separation between
the numbers and the text®; and J (fourth position) assigns an indent to the
numbers (default zero). The command, therefore

nm

240421

5Blank lines are not numbered, nor is any title created with the “.tl” command.
®This can also be manipulated through the indent command, “.in”; I thought youd want to know.

66 Line Numbering

Basic Functions

| .nm; .nn |

will begin numbering the next line at 240, printing only in multiples of four
(so on the first line after the command, the ‘240’ will be printed); it will be two
digit-spaces to the left of the margin.
These options would seem to cause a problem, however, because the formatter expects each instruction to be in the right position. In order to request
a new separation, for example, you might think you would have to repeat the
default for the multiple in the second position, like so:
Aavaay
Ih 1h

Luckily, the roffs have a way out: a non-numeric character (‘x,’ for example) in
the first, second, third, or fourth positions signals that the default (or whatever
was specified last) is to continue:
FILIV

eK

So it isn’t necessary to remember what previously was in effect; nor is it necessary to revise all subsequent commands if the first is to be changed. There is a
slight risk to this benefit, however: in the following example,
MmMcoore a)
Andrew Johnson
nm 480

all three parameters would remain, since no characters have been provided.
“nm N” is disabled with an “.nm” by itself. To temporarily stop both
incrementing the line count and printing the numbers, “.nn N” is available,
with N standing for the number of lines to be skipped. In the following example, the numbering is begun at eight, then four lines down the next two lines
are skipped, and finally numbering is turned off:
nf
President Lincoln
Vice President Hamlin
.nm 8
Secretary of State Seward
Secretary of Treasury Chase
Secretary of War Cameron
Secretary of Navy Welles

Postmaster General King
Attorney General Bates

The importance of these appointments

11 Secretary of Navy Welles

As you can see, the “.nn” retains the indent necessary one digit-space from the
text margin.

Nin “.nm” may be plus or minus, so that the numbering can be reinitialized to a figure more or less than the last line number. In the following
example, the line numbering begins at ten more than the last count:
Vice President Hamlin
nm +10
Secretary of State Seward
Occasionally you need to halt the line numbering process, then at some point
take it up again; naturally, you have no idea what the current number is. For
those cases, there exists the special request “.nm +0” (zero). It picks up the
last count and begins with that; it also accepts the arguments described above,
so that even this is legal:
nm

+05

The “.nm +0” is similar to “.nn”, except in no continuing indent.
The “User’s Manual” mentions that, under this command, the current
line length is retained, not shortened as you might expect. The line therefore
extends four digit-spaces into the margin (or more, if you’ve set the indent).
To keep the margins tidy the line length must be shortened, and this is as good
a command as any:

ll —\w’0000’u
Another potential difficulty is a line number of more than three digits; if every
line is numbered there will be no problem (apart from shortening the line
length one more digit), but in multiple numbering those lines without numbers
will remain at their allocated four-space indent. There seems to be no solution
to this aside from laboriously inserting digit-spaces (“\0”) in front of unnumbered lines, which seems to defeat the purpose.

68 Line Spacing

ls 2

or
Is 3

just before the place where the spacing is to begin. This command does not
break text, but it has an immediate effect, because this instruction simply
places that many carriage-returns at the end of all lines from then on.

Margins

[number N|

[.po; .nr PO]
Default: zero in nroff, 26/27 inch in troff

The left-hand margin is controlled through the page offset command
‘po M” on its own line before the text to be printed. Distance M without a
sign represents a movement to the right; a plus Mincrements (moves further
to the right) and a minus M decrements (moves leftwards) any offset currently
in place. This is not a command that one uses as frequently, say, as the indent,
because a change in offset also moves over the line length (“11”) and length of
title (“lt”), which the indent does not do. It occasionally has its uses, however,
in correctly positioning special displays and equations, since it is a kind of

absolute. Because typewriters offer complete flexibility in feeding paper, the
margin in nroff is set at zero; the formatted text will be aligned on the left
from its original position. For troff the margin is a default 26/27ths of an
inch, so as to allow some left-hand space in the typeset text. The number
register in -ms that determines page offset is “nr PO M”; it is checked at the
top of each page, so a “.po” command in -ms will last only until then.

Read-only number register: \n(.o

Marking Place (Returning)

[distance in machine units]

[.mk (.rt), \k]

The roffs make it possible for you to designate precise vertical and horizontal positions to which you may return; the option is often invoked for such
purposes as establishing columns, drawing, and overstriking.

The relevant

commands are associated with absolute spacing, and a review of the material
given below (under ‘Spacing, Absolute’) might be helpful. The vertical command “.mk” may be requested in either of two almost synonymous guises: by
itself or with an accompanying single character (as in “.mk X”’). The unadorned “.mk” appears in those instances where there is to be a straightforward
upward return, effected by the equally unadorned command “.rt”. A paragraph
with multiple labels, for example, can contain instructions to mark the beginning of the paragraph, print the labels down the left-hand side, indent, and
return to that marked position. If there are two mark commands, the return
will move up to the most recently defined (this will come in useful in columns).
If the marking is complicated, downward, or extensive, the mark command
with a single character is necessary: when placed in the text, the command
stores the current distance from the top of the page in a number register
named after its accompanying character. If, for example, the formatter had
already printed two inches of text on a specific page when it encountered the

Basic Functions

Marking Place (Returning)

| .mk (rt), \k | 69

command “.mk z”, it would store in number register ‘z’ those two inches (in
machine units) plus whatever header margin there might be (one inch in -me
and -ms). To return to that marked position, you would request spacing |“.sp”|
followed by the number register; it would be necessary to precede the “\nz”

with a request for absolute spacing [“l”| so as to insure that the distance will
be calculated from the top of the page (in sum, “sp |\nzu”).
The command

for horizontal marking, ‘“\kX”, behaves much like the

second form of “.mk”: it will store in the named number register the distance
from the left margin to the current position. To return to that mark, you may
utilize the horizontal motion command, “\h”, with the distance available in the
number register as an absolute horizontal distance (indicated, once again, with

a vertical bar |“|”’]), a fixed space, that is, from the margin. For an example of
this, the following is a defined string that prints an ‘o’ overstruck with a tilde:
.ds ~ \kxo\h’!\\nxu’~
The mark command “\k” has placed in number register ‘x’ the present position
on the line. Next the literal ‘o’ appears, and then the horizontal motion command instructs the formatter to return to the newly defined marked position as
a fixed distance in units from the margin. The tilde is printed, and processing
continues.

Needing Space

_[.nel
The “.ne M” command can define the amount of space that must remain
on the page in order to print the text that follows. If that amount does not
remain, the formatter immediately begins a new page. The primary purpose of
the command is to prevent widows, which are section titles, first lines of paragraphs, or parts of lists that should not be cut off from the rest of the text.
For that reason the need command is universally employed in section and paragraph macros. Though it accepts any distance M, it usually is requested in
terms of the number of vertical spaces, so that
ne

3

will check to see if three lines remain on the page from that point. It is not
quite as effective with the orphan, the single last line of a paragraph at the top
of the page, though a “.ne 2” coming a little more than two lines before the
end of a paragraph generally works.

Next File

[.nx]
The ‘“.nx” command switches permanently to a new file, the name of
which must appear immediately after:
nx newfile

It differs from “.so” in not returning to the original file when the next file has
been processed; its primary use arises in repetitive letters, in which the ‘next’
command names the letter file itself, so the processor can cycle though it once
again, this time with a fresh address and personal message.

| .af |

Numbering Format

_|.afl

Basic Functions

Default: arabic

The “.af” command (for ‘assign format’) allows you to establish an alternative way of printing sequential integers for any number register: arabic
numbers, roman capitals and lower case, capital and lower-case alphabetics,
and ‘0001’ series are all possible. It is invoked as “.af Xx c” on its own line,
where ‘Xx’ is any number register, and c is any of six character sets:
at XxX4
Hy te <i
af Xx a
af Xx A

\"assign
\"assign
\"assign
"assign

number
number
number
number

register Xx lower-case roman
register Xx capital roman
register Xx lower-case alphabetics
register Xx capital alphabetics

af Xx 1

\"assign number register Xx arabic numbers l|default |

af Xx 0001

\"assign number register Xx four-digit arabic

The alphabetics will begin at zero, then go through the alphabet, then after z
return with double letters, then triple letters, and so on. The last of the above
(‘0001’) will in fact format in as many digits as have been requested: ‘001’
three digits, ‘00001’ five, with no practical limit. The ‘assign format’ command
may be used before a number register has been defined, but it cannot be used
to initialize the number.

You cannot, for example, enter

af Xx 0004
and expect number register ‘Xx’ to begin at ‘0004.’ It will simply assign that
format to ‘Xx,’ not begin it at four; “.nr Xx 4 1” will begin it at four, and incre-

ment it by one at every “\n+(Xx”.
“af” is used most often for pagination, especially to generate upper- and
lower-case roman numbers or letters or numbers in various styles. Such page
numbers are requested with a “.af % c” at the head of a file or within the
header/footer macros.

Page Length

[.pll
Default:

11 inches

The request for page length is refreshingly simple and unassuming:

‘“\pl M”.

Mcan be as little as a line of text (not in -ms) or as much as twelve

feet in nroff and a little more than six in troff; such straining to the limits
would, however, be unnecessary, since straight roffs (without the macro packages) have no automatic headers and the text could just run continuously.
The command utilizes vertical spacing as its unit of measurement, so that
a command of *“.p! 11” will produce a very short next page; it accepts pluses
and minuses as a means of incrementing or decrementing the current length,

and “.pl” by itself returns to the default eleven inches.

Page length in -ms and -me

The presence of automatic header and footer margins in the macro packages sometimes causes confusion over what is actually affected by the pagelength command. As it happens, a change in page length will not alter the two

margins, since these remain at a default one inch; the “pl” command will
therefore only change the length of the text block itself. For example, a page

Basic Functions

Page Length

| .pl | 71

length of ten inches in -ms with no accompanying change in the header or
footer margins will produce an eight-inch text block, and a page length of nine
inches a seven-inch block.

Page length and lineprinters
The ‘Postprocessors and hardcopy’ section of the Introduction discusses
the problem of those lineprinters that automatically supply six spaces to the
sheet; sometimes the arrangement is one additional space at the top and five
at the bottom. The option given there was to pipe output through “pg,” which
by default deletes the 61st through 67th lines of output. This effectively
removes any extraneous space on each page:

$ nroff filename

| pg | lpr<er>

Otherwise, at some UNIX locations, the request ‘‘-N” added to the lineprinter
command will cause the printer to avoid the additional spacing:

$ nroff filename

| Ipr -N<er>

Either of these is acceptable, though the latter is to be preferred since it will
not accidentally remove any text lines if the page length is not eleven inches.

Read-only number register: \n(.p

Page Number

{distance in machine units]

|[.pn, \n%]
The begin page command is one method for renumbering the next page; if
you wish simply to have subsequent pages numbered in a new sequence whenever the current page is finished (to provide for a table or figure on a facing
page, for instance), you can request “.pn N”. The Nmay be either a literal
number or, when preceded by a plus or minus, an addition to or subtraction
from the current page number.

If the macro packages are not used, the roffs will automatically keep
track of the pages (as defined by the page length command) but will not print
these numbers unless you request them. The current page number is always
stored in the number register ‘“\n%”, which will produce it on demand.

By con-

venience, since the title command |.t] ’”’| utilizes this register so frequently in
header and footer macros, the percent sign by itself in a title will automatically produce the page number. This may be changed through the page character command, “.pc c”, in which c would be the new character; if the c were
absent, there would be no simple synonym for “\n%”.

Point Size _ |.ps, \s; .sz; .nr PS]
Default:

10-point

In troff, at least fifteen point sizes are currently available; on newer
equipment, there are many more, and this causes a slight problem that will be
described below. But every troff installation is capable of printing in these
sizes: 36-point (almost one-half inch from the top of the tallest character to the
bottom of a descender),® 28 (seven-eighteenths inch), 24 (one-third inch), 22
8] say ‘almost’ because even when ‘set solid’ |36 point on 36 vertical spacing, for example|, there is a small amount of

72 Point Size

Basic Functions

| .ps, \s; .sz; .nr PS |

(eleven-thirty-sixths inch), 20 (five-eighteenths inch), 18 (one-quarter inch), 16
(two-ninths inch), 14 (one-fifth inch), 12 (one-sixth inch), 11 (two-thirteenths
inch), 10 (one-seventh inch), 9 (one-eighth inch), 8 (one-ninth inch), 7 (onetenth inch), 6 (one-twelfth inch). If you do not specify a type size for your text,

troff will automatically produce 10 point.
Point sizes are requested in two ways: as a ‘period’ command or in-line.
The first is a straightforward “.ps N” on a line by itself, in which Nis one of
the above numbers. Like a number of commands in nroff/troff, it is possible to
place a plus or minus sign before Nin order to increment or decrement the
current size, so that “.ps +2” will print subsequent text two points larger; if
that size is not available, the text will be printed in the next largest size. The
in-line command is “\sN”, in which Nis the point size desired:

Damned if we haven’t \sllgot\s10 them!
For added flexibility, ‘“\s0” (zero) is understood as returning to the point size in
effect before the command. Or the size number may be signed with a plus or
minus:
How did you \s+lget\s—1 them?
with the limitation that the change cannot be more than plus or minus nine.
The problem arises in that newer typesetters having the capacity for 72-point
type, for example, will not receive the correct information when a “\s72” is
entered; rather troff will interpret this as a request for the number two in 7point. ‘ditroff’ has not to date solved this, so caution must be exercised in
using this command in certain circumstances.

-me and -ms offer a few point-size options that are generally useful. In
-me, the command “.sz N” not only changes the point size to N, but also
changes the vertical spacing by a fixed ratio. In -ms, as with line length, a
change in point size within a paragraph requires the lower-case command; a
change that is to extend beyond the boundaries of paragraphs or sections needs
aan bom:
._ps 11
nweo
.GO

ll

This is generally the safest course.

For what it’s worth, -ms contains two tem-

porary point-size changes that increase (‘.LG’’) or decrease (““.SM’’) by two
points the size of a word following on the same line. “.LG” or “.SM” also will
increase or decrease, respectively, by two points the size of words on the next
line, until turned off with a normal (“.NL”). And, finally, in -me and -ms footnotes are automatically set in a size one point smaller than the one you’ve
requested for the text.

Read-only number register: \n(.s

[number represents point-size|

space to prevent ascenders from touching descenders on the line above (in 36 point this space is one sixteenth
of an
inch). All of the following measurements are to be taken as ‘almost.’

Basic Functions

Read From Outside File

Read From Outside File

| .so | 73

_[.sol

This is an extremely useful function that allows other files to be processed (‘read in’) at a specific point. Though it often serves to introduce text
into a document (quotations, evidence), it most frequently acts to call in a file
that contains a common

set of macro definitions, as, for example,

so macrofile

tl Chapter 6”
The various chapters or sections of the text can then be consistent, and a
change in the macro file will produce the desired result in every text. If no
outside file of that name exists, processing will halt immediately, with
appropriate error messages.

Read From Standard Input

_[.rdl

This command represents a communication option different from the
“so”; it will halt formatting while it receives information from ‘standard
input’—usually but not necessarily the terminal. The formatting will resume
when the information contains a signal, two carriage returns, one directly after
the other. A possible application for this is the completely prestructured
memorandum macro described in the next section. There the operator is
prompted with a message written to the terminal [the “.tm” command], so that
“rd” may read in the variable parts of the memo.
Another application may be found in the macro on repetitive letters, also
to be found in the next section. Since ‘standard input’ can be manipulated in
UNIX, it is possible to imitate “.so” and read from an outside file through the
use of the redirection operator ‘<,’ as in:
$ nroff document

< outsidefile<ecr>

The contrasting difference between this and “.so” is that, while the latter will
interpolate the entire outside file, “.rd” with redirection operator will permit
piecemeal reading of the file, such as would be needed for addresses and personal touches in repetitive letters. Since the command expects two carriage
returns in a row, the outside file defines individualized units as whatever is

followed by a blank line (in effect a carriage return all by itself after the
return at the end of the last line).

Return Upwards

[.rtl

When this command has been invoked with a distance accompanying it
(“rt M’’|, it will reverse motion upwards to that distance as measured from the
top of the page. A “rt 2i” will, then, return to a point two inches from the top.
When invoked by itself (without any M), it will move the text processing back
to the last position marked by the command “.mk”.

74 Spacing
Spacing

Basic Functions

| .sp |
|.sp!

The command for vertical space in both nroff and troff is “sp M”, in
which M may be positive or have no sign (synonymous) to space down distance
M, or negative to space up that far. A “.sp 6i” on its own line, then, will move
down six inches before resuming printing, and a “.sp —3i” will move up. Normally we wish to space so many lines up or down, so the default unit of measurement is the vertical space, one line in nroff or one ‘v’ in troff (default
twelve points, or one-sixth of an inch). A “.sp” by itself is the same as “.sp 1”.
The command has the useful property of spacing only as far as the end of a
page, or the next ‘trap,’ so that there’s no danger of crossing a page-boundary
with excessive space.

A blank line in the text is also the equivalent of a “.sp 1”, but I wouldn’t
recommend relying on them, for the simple reason that they may contain tabs
or horizontal spaces that can’t be seen but will be filled in with the text that
follows.

Spacing, Absolute _ [| M!|
On occasion, you need to draw a line or simply return to some fixed point
on a page, no matter where you’ve been on that page. The vertical bar ‘|’
before a measurement permits printing at any specified horizontal or vertical
location on the page. Horizontally, the ‘|M’ can be used where any measurement would go; to draw a line back from a position within the line to the
beginning, the command would be \I’10’. Vertically, in addition to spacing
down or up the page (with a “.sp M” or a “.sp —M”’), you would use the same
vertical bar before the Mto space down or up to a line at a specific, fixed
point —for example, six inches from the top of the page or three inches from
the bottom: “.sp |6i” or “.sp —|3i”.

Spacing Canceled

_|[.ns]

This command will temporarily nullify all spacing commands and is most
often observed as part of the header macro. The straight spacing command
will not proceed beyond a trap like the footer, but it might happen that the
new page and a spacing request coincided, thus causing an embarrassing and
generally unnecessary series of blank lines at the top of a new page. ‘“.ns” also
negates the straight “.bp’” command (when it doesn’t have an accompanying
page number), because beginning a new page at the top of the old one wastes
paper. The no spacing command is deactivated with a single line of processed
text, or with the “.rs” below.

Spacing Restored

_|.rs]

“.rs” only exists as a means to undo the evil that a well-meaning “ns”
hidden in a macro does. For example, in the -ms macro package, “.rs” is
needed if you want any space between the top of the page and the title immediately below it:

.GO
rs
sp li
tl John G. Nicolay’

Basic Functions

Spacing Saved | .sv, .os | 75

There are more details on this problem in the ‘Cover Page’ section that follows
in Part 3.

Spacing Saved

[.sv, .os]
For those cases in which a vertical block of space needs to be produced (so
that a figure or illustration might be included later), the roffs offer the
“sv M”. The command is inserted at the point in the text where you would
like that space; it instructs the formatter to measure whatever distance is left
to the next trap (in most instances, the end-of-page trap) and, if there is
enough room, to print the space and resume processing. If there is not sufficient room, subsequent text is filled in and the space held in storage until an

“.os” [output space] command appears.

If at that time there is room, the space

is finally created on the page; if not, the space continues to be held in reserve
until an “.os” turns up at a point on the page that does have sufficient room; if
the processor encounters another “.sv’, the previous request is lost.
This is pretty primitive, so -me and -ms both have special ‘keep’ macros
that allow a block of space to be saved until the first possible instance (usually
the top of the next page) and also allow a choice on whether or not subsequent
text is to be filled in.

Tabs, Leaders, Fields

[.ta, \t, .te; \a; .fel

The tab system in nroff/troff is one of the most unexpectedly straightforward things about UNIX word processing. Just as on an ordinary typewriter,
the formatter defines various tab stops; a tab entered in the text will space
over a certain distance and place the following character in the next position.
You may reset tab positions with the “.ta” command, described below.
In nroff, the distance from one stop to another is by default 0.8 of an
inch. For a ten-pitch typewriter, then, a letter following a single tab would
appear at position 9, two tabs position 17, then 25 (3), 33 (4), 41 (5), 49 (6), 57
(7), 65 (8), and so on. If a text ended at position 23, a tab on 24 would take the
following material to position 25. On a twelve-pitch typewriter, nroff fixes the
tab positions at 10, 19, 28, 37, 46, 55, 64, 73, and so on. In troff, the tabs are
set at every half inch. Internally, the nroff and troff settings look like this:

.8i 1.61 2.41 3.21 4.01 4.81 5.61 6.41 7.21 8.01 etc.
.5i 1i 1.51 2i 2.51 31 3.51 41 4.51 5i 5.51 Gi etc.
As you can see, the tabs, like line length, are calculated as an accumulated distance from a left-hand point. And like line length and title length, the pageoffset command “.po N” shifts the tabs; a page-offset of an inch, for example,
in nroff will mean that tabs will start text at character-position 19 (measured
from the left edge of the page), 27, 35, and so forth in ten-pitch nroff. But
unlike line length, indents affect tabbing, so that an indent of one-and-a-half
inches will produce tab positions of 23, 31, 39, and so on.

When writing macros with tabs in them, you should realize that just
pressing the tab key to embed a tab will not always produce the desired results.
Luckily, nroff/troff tender a literal way to fix a tab within the definition: \te
Unluckily, it only works within macros, defined strings, and fields. But under
those conditions it does allow us to state unequivocally that a certain distance

76 Tabs, Leaders, Fields

| .ta, \t, .tc; \a; fc |

Basic Functions

in the macro is a tab, and therefore the macro can accept strings of variable
length. For example, the indented paragraph macro with provision for a ‘hanging label’ usually contains the following line:

\\$1\t
This allows the user to define the label as an ‘argument,’ then tabs over so that
any text following will align with the rest of the indent. The label can be of
any length, the tab guaranteeing that subsequent text will appear in the
correct position.

:

Redefining tab stops
The tab positions can be redefined with a single command: “ta pl p2...”,
where pl , p2 refer to places along the line. Therefore the command

ta li 21 2.2251
resets the tabs to stop after those points, measuring from the left margin.
Like many other basic commands in nroff/troff, the “.ta” accepts increments,
so that

ta li +21 +2.2251
will set the tab stops at one inch, three inches, and five-and-a-quarter inches.

If you are using -ms, however, you must define tab stops after initialization
(usually a new paragraph command like “.PP” or “.LP’’), since macros that initialize in -ms automatically return to the default values.
A very handy feature of this command is the ability to right-adjust or

center on the tab stops by simply appending ‘R’ or ‘C’ |‘L’ is default]:
ta li 2iC 2.225i1R
The last stop is defined as a point two and a quarter inches from the right
margin. This right option is especially useful in generating tables of contents
or labels hanging to the right of texts.
Tab characters

By default, tabs generate empty space, but you do have the ability to substitute any character for the space through “.tc x”, in which x represents the
character. To produce a line of periods for a typical table of contents format,
for example, in a book with a four-and-a-half-inch line length, would involve
something like this:
nf
ta 4.51R
UCe

Second Inaugural Address(tab)234
Sp

\h’.5i'I. Letters(tab)

\h’li’a. Family(tab)243
\h’1.5i1. Tad(tab)243
\h’1.57ii. William(tab)245
\h’1.5i’iii.

Robert (tab) 246

Basic Functions

Tabs, Leaders, Fields

| .ta, \t, tc; \a; .fc | 77

In this example, the horizontal motion command must be used because indents
(temporary or otherwise) would shift the tab stops and insert dots there as
well.
An alternative exists, utilizing the leader, which automatically produces
repeated characters—usually periods—across a tab stop. Usually (and by
default) this character is a period, but the command to change this, “lc c”,
allows you to assign to c any character you desire. Leaders are invoked with a
simultaneous use of the control key and the ‘a’ (though in macros, defined
strings, and fields, you may use a ‘\a’, analogous to the ‘\t’ of tabs).? Their presence allows us to rewrite the contents page given above in at least a less complicated fashion:

Working with control-a is frustrating, because it immediately renders previous

need to add more than one control-a, like so:

the necessary number of control-a’s can be estimated (in nroff at 10 characters

name, then, will require three more tabs to clear the indented section).

Fields
Fields are a secondary part of tabbing among the roffs, but they can have
their uses. Basically, a field is the area between a character of your choice—
called a delimiter—and the next tab stop. Various strings can be inserted
within the two delimiters; and another character of your choice—called a ‘padding indicator’—can stipulate where the various strings will appear within the
field. Both characters are defined with the command “.fc cl c2”’, where the
delimiter is cl and the padding indicator is c2. For an example of their use,

let’s say the delimiter is a vertical bar [‘|’] and the padding indicator is a percent sign |‘%’|. If you wanted a left-hand message ‘Nota bene:’ followed by a
string centered on the remaining space, up to a tab stop at five inches, you
would write:
ta 51

fel %
|\fINota bene\fR:%\s +2Macbeth tonight !\s—2%
This should look something like this:
Nota bene:

Macbeth tonight!
With a ruler, you should be able to see that the distance between the end of
‘Nota bene’ and the five-inch mark has been exactly centered by the message.
Leaders, mentioned a bit earlier, can be used in fields, so that we can generate yet another version of the table of contents given above:
nf
ta .5i .75i 1i 6iR (6i+\w’\0\0\0’u)R

1c.

\"period is default

fe | %

|Second Inaugural Address\a\t|234
Sp

|Appendices\a\t|240
I\tI. Letter\a\t 1242
\\t\ta. Family\a\t|1243

\t\t\ti. Tad\a\t1243
\t\t\tii. William\a\t1245
\\t\t\tiii. Robert\a\t1246
Interestingly, even though there are no padding indicators present in the text
lwe want to fill the entire line with periods], an unused one |‘%’] nevertheless
has to be placed in the “.fc”” command.

Basic Functions

Titles

Titles

| tl | 79

[.tl]
This is one of the most generally useful basic commands because it will
place up to three given strings left justified, right justified, or centered on a

line. The length of that line is governed independently of the “11” command,
by the “lt”, for which see immediately below. The title request usually takes
this form:
tl ’string left’string center’string right’
The apostrophes act as dividers, indicating where the string or strings should
be situated, so that

tl Mrs. O.H. Browning’
will place that string against the right margin, and

tl "Judge John McLean’
will center the Judge. Though the apostrophe by tradition acts as a separator,
in fact any character may be substituted for it; the command will accept the
first character it meets as the delineating one. This becomes necessary if there
are any literal apostrophes in the title; in such cases, to avoid strangely scattered or deleted strings, you must use something else, like so:
tl $$Lincoln’s Secretary of the Navy$$

The dollar-signs are arbitrary, gratuitous, and shouldn’t appear with eqn, but
they do afford a little pleasure and perhaps one of the few opportunities to
employ them.
There are some other elements of flexibility in the format of this command. The space after the “.tl” is not necessary, nor are all four characters, so
that the following would accomplish the same centering as above:

tl##Lincoln’s Letter to Joseph Holt
It is a good idea, however, for the sake of symmetry, to include all four of the
marks, whatever they are.

Title Length

[.lt; .nr LT]
Default:

6.5 inches (6 inches in -ms)

It is possible to stipulate a different length for the title line with the command “Jt M’, in which Mis the distance from the left margin, in turn defined
by the page offset (“.po”) command. Unfortunately, this is not often to the
good because you nearly always want the line and title lengths to be the same,
and because the command, though standing for ‘title length,’ should actually be

called ‘length of title’ because the ‘l’ precedes the ‘t,’ and entering

tl] 5i \"this is wrong

80 Underline

bn

eu

Basic Functions

Ui

will give “.tl” the idea that ‘5’ is the dividing character, so that youll get a
mysterious ‘i’ on the left margin, and title lengths that remain what they were
before. So you should think ‘length of title’, or perhaps become more familiar
with fields.

Underline

[.ul, .cu; .UL]
nroff translates all the italics commands described above under ‘fonts’
into underlining. This compatibility is extremely helpful for proofreading a
text to be typeset without going through the time and expense of troff formatting. Otherwise, there are two commands specifically appropriate to nroff that
underline: “.ul” |word-by-word] and “.cu” [continuous]. The “.ul” command will
draw a line only under each word; the “.cu” command carries the line unbroken under the entire text. Like the “.ce” command, both of these are placed on
lines by themselves before the passage.

There is no single command for underlining in troff, though -ms thoughtfully provides “.UL”, but this should only be requested with a single word, like
this:
that we
.UL win
our Galatin and Salem cases

For more than one word, wisdom dictates that you simply line up commands
with words:
.UL
.UL
.UL
.UL

Resolved
that
the
legislature

This is cumbersome, but safe; -ms has merely reproduced the underline macro
given in the nroff/troff User’s Manual (p. 21), which contains the following line:

\\ $1\V'1 0\(ul’\$2
a concept already introduced in the absolute spacing discussion above (the
second argument is added for punctuation following, like the “.I” command in
-ms). The difficulty, as you can see, is that the macro—in relying on absolute
spacing —cannot handle more than one line at a time; so, while it is possible to

quote ‘that’ and ‘the’ above, ‘fooling’ the processor into believing they constitute a single string, it is not a great idea.

Vertical Movement

[\v]

Within a line, vertical motion is effected through the command \v’M’, in
which Mif negative is the distance up, and if unsigned or positive is the distance down. The default unit of measurement is ‘v,’ the vertical space, but the
m is used universally because the movement is then linked to changes in point
size. The superscript macro in -ms, for example, contains this dual set of commands:

Basic Functions

Vertical Movement

\s—3\v’-.6m’

| \v | 81

\v’+.6m’\s0

(the superscripted string fits between them). Maintaining a ‘mirror image’
quality in the size and vertical motion requests needs to be emphasized, as does
insuring that the distance down exactly equals the distance up.

Vertical Space _ [.vs; .nr VS]
Default:

12 points

In abstract form, vertical spacing is controlled by the request
.vs M

or for -ms, analogously to “.nr PS”,
nr VS M
in which Mis a distance from one line to the next (‘base line to base line’).
This command pertains almost entirely to troff and typesetting, and therefore
the usual unit of measurement is the point (1/72 of an inch), so that the
default vertical spacing for the system, 12p, provides a space one-sixth of an
inch high. This measurement is translated into machine units, which vary
from device to device; if the device has defined 432 units to the inch, the
default internal vertical spacing is converted to 72 units. In nroff, the scale of
measurement employed is also points, but machine units are calculated at 240
to the inch, so that the default vertical spacing becomes forty units. Macros
that store the vertical spacing for functions in troff should test to determine
whether points or units have been used; furthermore, if the macro is to serve
both nroff and troff, a test for forty units of vertical spacing should be avoided
as much as possible. Given, for example, a number register ‘Vs’ that is storing
a desired vertical space in points |“.nr Vs 12”, for example] or units
“nr Vs 72”], you can test to see which measurement is in effect, and at the
same time circumvent the whole question of nroff’s forty:

if \\n(Vs>
40 .vs \\n(Vsu
if \\n(Vs< =39 .vs \\n(Vsp
This set of tests is found throughout the macros in the -ms package (where the

number register is called ‘““VS’”), and seems to work with some efficiency.
Unfortunately, it poses a potential problem with vertical spacing larger than
thirty-nine points.

Read-only number register: \n(.v

Write to Terminal

|[spacing in machine units]

[.tm]
This command, given in the form

tm string

will send that string to the terminal (or elsewhere, as I'll explain).

The string

may be 255 characters long, but, depending on the system, most of that might

82 Write to Terminal

| tm

Basic Functions

|

be overprinted on the right side of the terminal screen. When previewing the
formatted text at the terminal, you will see the message appearing among the
lines of text; when you are redirecting the text, as in the command

$ nroff document

> document.nr

or when you are requesting straight typesetting, as in

$ troff -w document
the string alone will appear on the terminal screen. Since the roffs happen to
treat the string as an error message, however, you may also redirect it into a
file, using the ‘2>’ convention:
$ nroff document > document.tr 2> file.
$ troff -w document 2> file.
$ troff -a document 2> file.
$ nroff document 2> file.
The last two will show the formatted text on the terminal, but still send all

strings preceded by “tm” into file.
There is very little limit to what can be placed in the string. Number
registers, defined strings, and various control characters are all legal. In the
following example, a control-G (‘bell’) is used to draw the operator’s attention
to the terminal, and a section head (string “Sh”’) and the current page number
are then printed on the screen:
.ds Sh Gideon’s Navy
tm °G Section: \*(Sh
Traditionally, in fact, this command

Page: \n%

has been only utilized to send error mes-

sages to the terminal. One example would be to test if the page offset and line
length were greater than eight and a half inches:

if (\nCo+\n(.1)>8.5i .tm Page offset & line length too long.
You might place such a test within an initialization macro at the start of the
document, or in a heading macro at the beginning of each page.
But the command is also effective in a number of other applications,
including an interactive system and a very simple indexing program. For an
example of the first, Part 3 of this book describes a memorandum macro: “.tm”

prompts the user for various kinds of information, and the read command “.rd”
captures each reply as it is entered. The second application is also treated in
the Part 3: Macros, under ‘Indexes.’ This takes advantage of the redirection
mentioned above, so that, quite simply, the “.tm” prints the key term and
accompanying line or page number in the redirected file rather than with the
text or on the terminal.

3. MACROS

Macros

This third part describes specialized formatting functions that are most
conveniently performed through macros—defined commands that combine basic
functions, number registers, strings, or other macros under a single heading.
The seventeen sections that follow cover a wide range of tasks common in processing text: footnotes, headers and footers, columns, and so on. Some sections
are more complicated than others, and all discuss general principles and basic
commands which may not be apparent from the title; for this reason I have
prepared the following table, indicating the relative complexity of the section
and some of the associated concepts. Those marked ‘basic’ are recommended
for those just starting out with the roffs; there is not a great deal of difference

between ‘intermediary’ and ‘advanced.’ The two preprocessors treated in this
Guide, eqn and tbl, are self-contained, and range from basic to advanced.

Each section follows a pattern of a general introduction to the function, a
discussion of simple, do-it-yourself versions, and finally a review of the operation as found in the macro packages -me and -ms, with some analysis of their
internal logic.

Even for beginners, it’s a good idea to know something about the basic
commands that constitute a particular function, if only to avoid some of the
flaws buried in the macro packages. In troff -ms, for example, invoking a
quoted paragraph within a footnote produces a particularly unacceptable form
of garbage. And it is worth insisting that the packages themselves can be
modified for your own purposes; even when you cannot change them on the
system level, they can be copied down into your own directory and given an
appropriate name. At most sites, the relevant directory is ‘/usr/lib/tmac, and a
typical copying command would be
$ cp /usr/lib/tmac/tmac.e me.macros < er>

or
$ cp /usr/lib/tmac/tmac.s ms.macros <er >

Your files would then access this version through the “.so” command, which is
usually placed on the first line:
‘SO mS.macros

And, naturally, you would no longer need to enter -me or -ms after the nroff
or troff command. The packages are extremely complicated in their inner

Macros

Macros |Description] 85
workings, and often a change in one macro
produces unwanted changes elsewhere. Nevertheless it is worth experiment
ing to gain the kind of control over
the system that we all need to perform our
work; it might be fairly said that
UNIX itself encourages that experimentation
.

BIBLIOGRAPHIC FORMAT

Lists of References

Bibliographies, no matter what their individual style, generally have the
same four physical characteristics: (1) a full first line and the remaining lines
indented; (2) each citation then separated from the others by a space; (3) at
least two lines of the citation printed at the bottom of the page; and (4) a
blank or underline for a repeated author. This section demonstrates how the
first three of these requirements can be defined in a macro, called ‘Br’ (for
‘Bibliographic Reference’). This is similar in almost every respect to the biblio-

graphic macro found in many versions of -ms, “.BR’, which I also describe.
The fourth characteristic, repeated authors, can be duplicated with tab commands, either inside or outside the macro.
The basic bibliographic macro carries out its three tasks straightforwardly enough, the only trick arising from the indenting. Since it’s impossible
to estimate in the source file when the first line will end in format, or how
many lines of the reference will be indented, the macro stipulates that indenting is the rule and that the first line represents a temporary exception:

.de Br
in .5i
ti 0
sp
ne 2

\"simple bibliographic reference macro
\"half-inch indent always in effect
\"first line temporary indent of zero
\"space between references
\"two lines needed on page

Not accidentally, the bibliographic reference macro can function like a paragraph macro, and -ms even goes so far as to use it to initialize text. That is, it
can perform all the handy tasks described in the section on Paragraphs, like

redefining running headers, varying fonts, and so on.

86

But as usual each one of

Macros

Bibliographic Format |Lists of References| 87
these features carries a warning: the user must remember what is done
automatically and must stop it if it’s not needed. Even in the simple definition, the indent stipulated will have to be canceled with a “.in 0” if straight
text is to follow the reference section.
The definition given above would be inserted at the head of a source file,
and then all references could be introduced with a “.Br’”, like this:
.Br

McNeer, May Y. \fI[America’s Abraham Lincoln\fR.
(Houghton, 1957).
.Br
Phelan, Mary Kay.

If such citations are relatively short (fewer than 256 characters), they can be
entered, one to a line, in a list file, and alphabetized using the UNIX ‘sort’ program, as in

$ sort listfile > alphalist
The bibliographic request can then be inserted at the beginning of each line
with this global command in edit or ex:

:g/"/s//.Br\ <er>
/<er>

In effect, this command globally retrieves the beginning of each line and
substitutes the macro request for it, followed by a carriage return.

Repeated Authors
If you have a recurring author in your bibliography, some styles dictate
that underlining or hyphens be substituted for the author’s name in subsequent citations. The two considerations are the type of character to be used
and the length of the string. The most basic solution is simply to define a set
of characters of that length, like this:
.ds X
for a ten-element

underscore.

This then could be entered like so:

.Br
Foster, Genevieve. \fl[Abraham Lincoln’s World\fR.
1944).
.Br
\xX \fI[Abraham Lincoln: An Initial Biography\fR.

(Scribner,

Hyphens or rows of dots would be handled the same way, with the exception
that the dots will need to be ‘protected’ as a defined string with a backslash
and ampersand: “.ds \&...”.
Another approach utilizes the tab and tab character. Before the list of
references begins (in -ms, usually before the .GO), you would set the tab to the
appropriate length, like so:

Macros

88 Bibliographic Format |Repeated authors]
.ta 10n

Next, the tab character can be defined through the “.tcx” command;

to under-

line the length of the tab, for example, the request would be:
C4

for hyphens, it would be “.tc-”; and so on. With each repeated author, you
would press the tab key, followed immediately by whatever end punctuation is
needed. Taken together, the commands and citations might be typed in this
way:
ta 10n
tC~
“Be

Foster, Genevieve.
(Scribner, 1944).
.Br

\flAbraham Lincoln’s World\fR.

(tab). \f1Abraham Lincoln: An Initial Biography\fR.
When the roffs have processed the text, it will look like this:
Foster, Genevieve.

Abraham Lincoln’s World.

(Scribner,

1944).

. Abraham Lincoln: An Initial Biography.
If within the bibliography or subsequently in the source file you wish to use
tabs for something else, you must clear them of the assigned character by typing
:UC

This redefinition of the tab character is not necessary if your text contains
tables and you're using tbl; but you should be aware that the tbl program will
reset the tab length as well. In any case, the wisdom is to avoid the danger of
automatic operators by keeping in mind the various settings even if they can’t
be seen.

Bibliographic Format in -ms
The bibliographic macro found in many versions of -ms follows the general principles outlined above; but it complicates matters by having the macro
either initialize or reset the default format (for which see the section on the
cover page and that on paragraphs). Another exception is that most of the
work is accomplished by an ‘exdented’ paragraph macro, “.XP”!:

'This set of macros represents a slightly less complicated approach than can be found in the original -ms, which can
handle several ‘arguments’ as hanging labels.

As you can see, certain decisions have been made for you (the bibliography is
