which determines the amount of indent. To change them, you need only redefine the appropriate number registers:
nr PD 0
nr BI li

Since the macro delays reading these registers until actually called, they may
be defined and redefined at any point; you should keep in mind, though, that
the ‘PD’ will also affect any paragraph in the document.

CHAPTER HEADINGS

Chapter Macros

As an indication of how easy chapter heading macros can be, an example
of one has already been given in the Introduction (pp. 25-30). When we last
joined it, the macro was accepting arguments for the chapter title and the
space below it and automatically incrementing the chapter numbers. With an
addition because of -ms, it looked like this:
nr X01
.de Ch
.bp
.rs__\"necessary in -ms, ignored otherwise
sp 21

CHAPTER \\n+X: \\$1__\"first argument is chapter name
sp \\$2i \"second argument is space down in inches

When this macro is invoked, the formatter will immediately begin a new page,
restore spacing (the new page macro in -ms cancels spacing), space down two
inches, print the word ‘CHAPTER’ followed by an incremented number and the
chapter name (first argument), all on the same line; next, the macro will space
down the amount of the second argument in inches. This last provision was
necessary in the case of a multilined title, so that you could insure that the
chapter itself always began at the same point. With this macro definition, a
call like

90

Macros

Chapter Headings |Chapter Macros] 91
.Ch Sangamon

3

will print the chapter number (one more than the last time it was invoked) and
Sangamon’ two inches down on the next page, followed by a three-inch space.

You might construct a slightly more pleasing macro on this essential
base: the chapter numbers made roman rather than arabic, the point size
changed for the chapter title, and vertical spacing correspondingly increased.
The demands on you can be reduced as well: the second argument, allowing
variable space below the title, is unnecessary, thanks to absolute spacing,
which assures that the text will begin at an identical position, no matter how
many lines are in the title:

caf X I

\"roman numerals for the chapter numbers

.ps 14
if t .vs 17p

CHAPTER \\n+x: \\$1
sp |3i
._ps 10
\"or just “ps”
tatr.Veul 2p
"or .vs"

The ‘if’ test on the larger vertical spacing for the title may seem redundant if
you work only in troff, but it does keep open the option for a preliminary nroff
version (nroff ignores point size but tries to approximate vertical spacing).
Since default vertical spacing in nroff is one-sixth of an inch (the same as
twelve points), the corresponding ‘if’ to return to twelve-point is not a requirement but still a good idea.
A few enhancements may be added to connect this definition with other,
similar heading macros; if there are incrementally numbered sections and
numbered paragraphs, these can be reset to zero within the chapter macro:

nr Se 0 1 \"section number

af Se A

\"section alphabetic format

nrx01_

\"paragraphs here labeled at three levels

nry0Ol
ni 2.001

Strictly speaking, I suppose, the paragraph number registers do not need reset-

ting at the chapter level (the section macro should be responsible), but this
does guarantee that no embarrassing numbers will be left over. And, as the
pages below on paragraphs and sections indicate, each register will increment
automatically as soon as it is invoked (hence the need to start at zero).

The chapter macro provides for a multilined title, but with the problem
that the second and any other lines would be left-justified below the word
‘CHAPTER.’ One solution to this can be found in the bibliographic record
(“Br”) and memo (“.Mm’’) macros, in which an indent is established, and then
a temporary indent cancels it for the first line. To measure the amount of
indent, we need the width of the string ‘CHAPTER’ (fixed) as well as the
chapter number (variable, especially with roman numbers). An easy approach

in many circumstances).

Chapters in -me

The macro package -me contains a number of commands which are
related to chapter headings. These are almost exactly parallel to the range of
commands which -me offers for the section macro “.sh”, and I leave to that section a full explanation of the various uses of these options. In -me, chapters
are only one of many components in a document, all determined by the “.++ C”’
command (see the section entitled ‘Cover Page’). If a chapter is needed, you
would first enter “.++ C”; then, whenever you request “.+c 7”, the processor
will increment the chapter number (accessible in number register “\n(ch”),
print ‘CHAPTER’ followed by the number centered on the page, print Tasa

COLUMNS

Columns

The following section describes methods for producing text in columns; as
usual, it first indicates the basic components, then the relevant macros in -me
(“2c”) and -ms (“.2C” and “.MC’”’). As in the other sections, you can create
your own macro for columns, but you should realize that the situation is a little more complicated than usual, because columns necessarily involve header
and footer macros and traps. On the other hand, both -me and -ms are
extremely dependable and much more flexible than the sequences given below.

If you are generating columns through nroff, moreover, you might have
to pay some attention to the terminal type, and hopefully your version of UNIX
shows some sophistication in stipulating the various terminals and printers
available.

If all else fails, the roffs provide a reliable option, which was first

created for the old Model 37 Teletype and has the advantage of a kind of safety
net. The option is col, used after the pipe symbol, like so:
$ nroff article | col<er>

This should produce your columns, though it will almost certainly have difficulties with subscripts and superscripts within them.!

1Often the problem resides in the terminal, which may be filtering out reverse spacing signals before they reach the
printer. In that case it may be necessary to connect the printer directly with the computer.

94

Macros

Columns |Two Columns (One Page Only)| 95

Two Columns [One Page Only]
The approach I’ve taken is to begin with the most basic column function
and elaborate on it: in this case, the simplest situation would be text in two
columns on a single page. Even so, the operation involves two separate macros.
The first (‘Co’ below) must both shorten the line length and store in memory
the current horizontal position, so that the formatter can return to that position when the right-hand column has been printed. To accomplish this, the
macro (like all column macros) makes use of the mark/return (“.mk/.rt”) commands, which as the name indicates identify and revert to specific positions.
‘Co’ then sets a ‘trap’ at the bottom of the page which will call a second macro
(‘Cs’) to complete the process. ‘Cs,’ automatically invoked when the text in the
first column reaches the trap, takes responsibility for the return itself, and at
that point requests a page offset (“.po M’’) to start the second column. The
example given below presumes that the text in the columns will fit on one
page, that there is no footer, that the line length is six and a half inches, and
that the columns are at the end of the document:
.de Co
.br
.mk
ll 3.1251
.wh 60 Cs
.de Cs
It

.po 3.3751

This places a half-inch gap between the columns, which can of course be modified. In order to save a step, the page offset was used here rather than the
indent: an indent would require redefining the line length to its original six
and a half inches.

To make this macro a little less specific and therefore a little more portable, you might insert a couple of improvements. Instead of indicating a
column width in inches, you can write a routine that will divide the current
line length by two and then subtract a little bit so that the columns are
separated. This involves the read-only number register for line length

[“‘\n(.l”—the backslashes have been doubled so that the current line length will
be read]:

1 (\\n(lu/2u)-2n
.wh 60 Cs

.de Cs
rt

.po \\n(.lu+5n

As you see, when the ‘Cs’ second-column macro is called, the current

Macros

96 Columns |Two Columns (One Page Only)|

length has already been reduced; to move the page offset over, you need only
request the same register, adding to the command enough of a gap to clear the
right-hand margin of column one.
Few documents end with columns, so at this next stage you might want
to define a mechanism for returning to single-column format. This macro
needs to know the original line length and page offset, so the main column

macro |‘Co’] must: reserve these values in number registers (here ‘Ll’ and ‘Po’),
utilizing not only the “\n(.1” but also the “\n(.o”, the current offset. The endof-column macro (called ‘Ec’ below) works best if it simply breaks to a new
page, and hence the “.bp”; it is also necessary to clear the trap, and you do this
by including the “.wh”, line number, and nothing else.
and page offset values are restored:

Finally, the line length

.de Co

nr LI \\n(lu _ \"store current line length
nr Po \\n(.ou_\"store current page offset
.br
.mk

I (\\n(lu/2u)-2n
.wh 60 Cs

.de Cs
Jae

.po \\n(.lu+5n
.de Ec
-bp
.wh 60

1 \\n(Llu \"back to line length
.po \\n(Pou
\"back to page offset

The “.Co/.Ec” begin and end the material to be set in columns, providing satisfactory results for a single page.

Multiple Columns [Still One Page]
Again, limiting yourself temporarily to a brief text which will fit on a single page, you have the power to define a macro which would allow three, four,

or as many columns of text as you wish. Basically, this macro |“.Mc”] depends
on one number register |‘Cc’ below] which keeps track of the number of
columns; in the following example, the macro also accepts a number request
(“.Mc 3”, for example, for a three-column text), and uses the number for its cal-

culations, storing that value in register ‘Cn’. The page offset is not only stored,
but incremented as the trap for the next column is sprung.
.de Mc
nr L) \\n(lu

nr Po \\n(.ou
nr Cc 0 1 \"establish counter
nr Cn \\$1
.br
mk

\"store given number as value

Macros

Columns |Multiple Columns (Still One Page)| 97
1 (\\nGlu/\n(Cnu)-2n

—\"divide text into equal short lines

.wh 60 Cs

.de Cs
if \\n+(Ccu<\\n(Cnu \{\ \"compare counter with number desired
rt
\"if less, return to mark
.po \\n(.ou+\\n(.lu+4n\}
\"increment offset by length plus offset

Notice that the counter was incremented within the ‘if’ test. In most
nroff/troff installations, the increment occurs even if the test fails; you can be
fairly confident of this, but may want to double-check. Inserting a “\\n+(Cc”
on its own line would have also increased ‘Cc,’ but it would also have had the
undesirable side-effect of printing the counter number within the text.

first page has been filled; the column-counter and page-offset are then reset:

with Header and Footer Macros

As I will mention in the ‘Headers/Footers’ section below, both -ms and
-me use those two macros to control columns, the header marking the top of
the page and the footer signaling a return. The obvious advantage is that this
allows you to have running headers and footers as well as columns and that
you can determine where the column-head will start on multiple-page formats.
The commands needed will be broadly the same as those given above, but distributed in different places. One notable change is the ‘if/else’ test at the end
of the footer macro, so that the page offset will keep to current settings when
there are no columns; specifically, the test checks for the existence of the

column counter ‘Cc.’ If it’s a zero then the current page offset is read in, which
is precisely where it should be if no columns have been invoked as yet. Also,
the macro that ends the column display spaces down to the trap (a distance

stored in read-only register “\n(.t”’), thus triggering the footer and subsequent
page break. Notice, however, that no special command is needed for the mark
at the start of the multiple columns; the return in the footer macro will move
up to the first one it meets and ignore the previous mark in the header macro.

After the “.Ec”, the ‘if/else’ comparison between ‘Cc’ and ‘Cn’ will always fail,

begins. I realize, however, that this is hardly what anyone might call convenient.

Columns

in -me

Both -me and -ms considerably simplify this process, and for that reason
ought to be investigated. -me provides for multi-columned text, and it does
that very well. The relevant command is “.2c”; for the default two columns
and a half-inch gap between them, the command is straightforward enough:
.2C

To return to single-column format, you need only enter the command
ohKe

As with our macros above, this command necessarily begins a new page.

To vary the width of the columns, the command may then be followed by
a redefinition of the inter-column gap; it may be given in any of the units of
measurement, with ‘n’s’ default:
Ae Al

This will produce a two-columned text with a 4-n gap. You may also specify
three or more columns, but you must place a gap measurement first, even if
you are sticking with the default half-inch:

.2c .d1 3
This efficiently produces three columns with the necessary gap.
For the purposes of defining your own macros, the various number registers for columns in -me are saved under the following names:

\n($s\"current gap lgiven in units]
\n($l \"width of the column
\n($c\"current column [‘1’ for first, ‘2’ for second,
\"and so on]
\n($m
\"number of columns requested
-me has one other feature which can be quite handy. Occasionally it is necessary to end a left-hand column before it reaches the bottom of the page, in
order to display parallel texts. To accomplish this, -me offers the command
.be

which will begin a new column if there’s room for it on the page.

Otherwise, it

will take you into a new page. In order to start regular text once again, you
must enter “.1c” (this is that ‘bracketing’ convention that has been mentioned

100 Columns

|Columns in -ms|

previously |[tables, footnotes, equations, and so forth]); because of the complexity of the macro, “.1c” always begins a new page.

Columns in -ms
-ms offers a large number of options in columns under two basic commands: “.2C” for two columns and “.MC M” for any number greater than two.
Either of these commands is placed just before the material. To end, you
merely request “.1C’, which always breaks to a new page.
The “.2C” requires very little explanation; when invoked, it will produce
text in a left-hand column to the bottom of the page, then return to the beginning of that column to provide the right-hand side. Subsequent pages will be
filled from the top of the text block (one inch from the top of the page) to the
bottom. If your entire text is to be in columns, you must still begin with an
initialization lusually “.LP’’]; it is important, too, to remember to use “.ce” for

centered titles within the column, since the length of the title request |.t] ’””’] is
not affected by this command.
lowing:

A typical opening in this case might be the fol-

.LP
.2C
ce 3
Remarks at Pittsburg,
Pennsylvania
February 15, 1861

Sp
The condition of the country is an extraordinary
one, and fills the mind of every patriot with anxiety.
The width of these columns will automatically be set at 7/15 of the line length
(“nr LL”), and the gap between columns at 1/15. Ail of the paragraph macros
can be used within the columns, including the indented paragraph and its
appropriate labels.
To generate a break in one of the left-hand columns, so as, for example, to
produce parallel texts, you will use an “.RC”; this begins a new column, or, if

you're already in the farthest right hand column, a new page:

will yet swell the chorus of the Union when
again touched, as surely they will be, by the
better angels of our nature.
.RC
.ce
Conclusion as originally set
Sp
\(rh My countrymen, one and all,

This command is the equivalent of “.bc” in -me.
-ms produces more than two columns through the command “MC” this
very cleverly accepts the column width desired as a number following the command and then fits in as many columns as the line length will allow. Thus the
request

Macros

Columns |Columns in -ms] 101

MC 21
will display three columns on a six-and-a-half-inch line, with a quarter-inch
gap between them. The inter-column gap can be as small as a single character.
Some of the older nroff/troff documentation describes two number registers—

that for column width (“nr CW M”) and that for the gap between the columns
(“nr GW M”). In recent versions of -ms, these two number registers no longer
appear to function, since through a fairly complicated mathematical process
newer systems allocate text and space where it would make the most sense.
Multiple-column requests can follow one upon the other, but any change
in the number of columns requested (from two to three, for example) will begin
a new page; and, as with “.2C’, a “.1C” returns the text to its default width.

For more complicated columnar format, especially one containing individual items (as opposed to running text), you should peruse the table formatter
tbl.

COVER PAGE

First-Page Macros

Writing macros for the first page of a document would seem like the perfect opportunity to establish a consistent house or personal format, and it is.
Through such macros, you can create commands that will automatically space
down a uniform distance from the top of the page; place the title correctly centered and in the same point size; automatically insert names, dates, and routing sequences; and so forth. But you should proceed with the understanding
that overly elaborate first-page macros are often more of a hindrance than a
help. They may be so completely intermeshed with initialization, define so
many macros within macros in order to anticipate every contingency, and make
the process so complicated that it might be difficult to create any other kind of
first page. The extensive cover-page macros in -ms could serve as an example
of this danger, making initialization both necessary and oppressive (-me wisely
limits itself here). I do not intend to criticize them, because they do what they
were meant to do very well; but the lesson remains about trading off the roffs’
flexibility for pure simplicity, consistency, and friendliness. This section treats
some of the choices you have in creating your own cover-page format and
describes the advantages and disadvantages of -ms in this area. Since, even
without -ms, the title page is intimately bound up with the concept of initialization, I must begin there.

Initialization

Initialization is properly defined as establishing commands which generate a set format for the entire text: if, for example, several documents

are

being formatted simultaneously, it clears any leftover indenting, centering,
footnotes between documents; in conjunction with paragraph and header

102

Macros

Cover Page [Initialization] 103
macros, it defines default line length, point size, vertical spacing, and so on.
These commands generally precede the definitions of various macros which
appear at the head of the file and usually store in number registers those
values which the macros will need in order to create standardized text. If the
line length for the document is to be six and a half inches and each paragraph
is to contain an instruction to return to that line length (should it have
strayed), you might initialize by setting a number register [‘Ll’ below] to that
value, and with each new paragraph [macro ‘Lp’] insure that the line length
returns to it. The beginning of the file might, then, look like this:

nr LI 6.5i \"initialize line length
.de Lp
\"define left-paragraph
\\n(Llu
\"reset to initialized length
.br
\"what paragraphs do
\"end definition
(The double backslashes before the line length register represent, as I have
indicated in the Introduction, the way to delay reading the register just in case
you’ve redefined it.) Now, whenever the “.Lp” is invoked, that initialization
will function.
Initialization therefore concerns more than just the first page, but it is
all the more important to discuss it here because any initializing is done at the
beginning of the file, and—ironically—the first page sometimes represents an
immediate departure from the defined standards. You may, for example, want
the title in a larger point size, or with a different line length and vertical setting. And even the macros might be different for the first page: for your running headers and footers, you may prefer page numbers in the upper-right
corner of each page except the first; or you may want the first page to be numbered with roman numerals, or unnumbered, and the text following to begin as
page one. In the following paragraphs I will describe the macros which are
useful for the first page and give some indication of the way text macros can
be adjusted to behave differently if that is your desire.

Page One: General Considerations
Before getting involved in any macros whatsoever, we should recognize
the advantage of avoiding them. Macros afford their greatest benefit when
consolidating repetitive commands or establishing a dependable page-related
format, like headers or footers. Neither of these is relevant to the first page,
and it would be perfectly acceptable in nroff or troff [though not in -ms] to
compose a title page with no macros whatsoever, but simply the necessary commands. It could look like the following:
sp 3i
cul

tl ’Second Inaugural Address”
Sp 2

tl by”
sp 2

tl ” Abraham Lincoln”
sp 4i
tl ’March 4, 1865”
.bp

104 Cover Page [Page One: General Considerations]

Macros

If the object were to insure consistency, either personally or for a ‘house’ style,
this sequence could be made a separate file with a name like ‘title.pattern,’
which could be read in (using “.so filename”) and modified accordingly.
Without too much effort, you could make provision for multiple titles and multiple authors:
sp 3i
ul 20

\"titles of more than twenty lines are boring anyway

.ce 1000

\"whole cover page is centered

Speech on the Dred Scott Case
ul 0 \"no more underlining/italics needed

sp 2
A. Lincoln
sp 4i
June 16, 1857
.ce 0 \"no more centering needed
.bp

Point size and vertical spacing could be added, though with each elaboration
you are brought closer to the need for macros; you would require a macro to
test, for example, whether it will all fit on the page. But I hope you perceive
the virtue of this simpler approach.
The example of centering above does raise a consideration of some
moment. The more complicated and complete the first-page macros are to be,
the more likely they should be the ‘bracketing’ type—which is to say in two
parts, with a beginning and end. If, for instance, the entire cover sheet is to be
set in boldface, then there must be a command to return to the roman font
before crossing into the text. A macro can be defined to accomplish this return
to regular processing, or the footer macro can add it to its share of duties. The
advantage to the first is that, once there, its function is obvious; the disadvan-

tage is that you might forget to include it in the first place. The advantage to
the second is that it’s automatic; the disadvantage is that it’s easier to overlook in special circumstances (a cover two pages long, for example). In the
macros given below, I will choose the bracketing option, defining an ending
macro (‘Ce’ for ‘cover end’); I do this both because I have an innate suspicion of
the ‘automatic’ and because the symmetry itself affords an important lesson.

The Title

In a fairly straightforward way, you can create a simple title macro which
will do all that you need:
.de Ti
Sp 3i
ft B
.ps 14

.vs 17p
.ce 1000
.de Ce
ft R

Macros

Cover Page [The Title] 105
._ps 10
.vs 12p
.ce 0

.bp

In its most basic format (without the complication of headers, footers, traps,
and so on), the macros and title itself then can be requested in this way:

Ti
Address to the People
of Sangamon County
.Ce
In troff, this will produce a centered title of that number of lines in the desired
font, point size, and vertical spacing. In nroff, the title would be underlined,
and the vertical spacing would be one and a half lines.

Author and Organization
In very much the same way, the author’s name and status, organizational
affiliation, and so on can be preformatted with the right macro. It is helpful to
use a macro name which will be mnemonically close to the contents, like ‘Au’
or ‘Ao,’ but otherwise they would look similar to the title macro.

Abstracts

Abstracts represent a slight challenge in that the abstract text is usually
not centered but filled and has other requirements: you would most likely want
the abstract single-spaced and indented on either side, and, if typeset, in a
smaller type size and vertical spacing than normal. The following may be considered a typical example:

.de Ab
ifn js 1 \"if nroff, single-space
if t .vs 1lp
\"if troff, eleven-point vertical spacing

._ps 9 \"point size ignored in nroff
ft I \"italic font
in .5i
\"indent
ll -.5i
\"shorter line length

The abstract will of course go on (perhaps to the point where a summary of it
will be needed), but eventually you'll need to return to normal conditions. As
you will have an opportunity to discover, if you haven’t done so already, the
trick of macros is not only invoking them but disinvoking them. It is possible
to guarantee a restoration with an ‘abstract end’ macro (as -ms does), but if
the abstract commands have not been too complicated, the cover end [“.Ce’’] can
take care of most of the potential problems. You must keep in mind, however,
that a document may not have an abstract, and you should not so construct
safeguards as to expect one. “.Ce” has already reset the font and point size,
and line spacing and indent commands may simply be repeated:

Macros

106 Cover Page [Abstracts]
in 0 \"if indent already zero, no change

ifn Js 2 \"ditto on line spacing
Line length, on the other hand, requires special consideration, in that a

“11 +.5i” will add a half inch no matter what, even if there hasn’t been an
abstract. Three options are available: (1) restate the line length (“ll 6.51”); (2)
request a return to the previous line length (“ll”), no matter what it was; or ;
(3) set a flag [basically an on/off number register] which will only be ‘raised’ if
the abstract macro has been invoked. Of the three, setting a flag is probably
the safest because there is local control, and it will work no matter what the

line length is. The abstract macro first sets the number register [‘Af’ below],
and when invoked the end macro tests for it:
.de Ab
nr Af 1 \"flag set at one
ifn is 1
if t .vs llp
._ps 9
ft I
in .5i

ll -.5i
.de Ce
in 0

if Af> 0\{\
ll +.5i
nr Af 0\}
ifn ds 2
ft
._ps 10

if t vs 12p
.ce 0
.bp
-ms uses this flag technique extensively in order to keep track of processing;
flags are set, for example, to determine formatting routines for columns,
displays, and footnotes.

Forms

In certain cases, you may want to have the processor automatically place
certain kinds of information at certain points in a form. If all the information
is to be situated at certain predetermined positions, you can use the absolute
spacing command (“sp |M”, in which M represents the distance from the top of
the page). For example, a title three inches down and an author four inches
down could be preset thus:
.de Tl

sp |3i
.de Au

sp |4i

Macros

Cover Page [Forms] 107
In this way, if a particular category were left blank, the other categories would
still appear in their proper locations. Another advantage is that no strict order
has to be followed in entering the author, title, and so forth, though it’s probably easier to keep to pattern.
Indenting certain categories and not others does not represent a particular difficulty. You have already seen the indenting in the abstract; by extension, any category in the form which was not indented would simply have a
“in 0” at its head, a command which is ignored if not needed:
.de Tl
in 0

sp |3i

If only one line is to be indented, the temporary indent [.ti M] will move the
material over and then return to the indent in effect.
Naturally, no form is this simple.

There are sections which are parallel to

each other, which are out of alignment with each other, and which contain text
or titles or single numbers. In these situations you can use both the absolute

spacing command and the indent, in addition to line length and an occasional
no-fill if necessary. In the example below, the title and date (automatically
stored in number registers) are to be printed on the same line, three inches
from the top, with the date four inches over.

The date is processed first, since

it is sure to occupy only one line, and then the format is ready for the title:
.de Tl
in 41

sp |3i
\n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr
in 0

sp |3i
leat
Message to Congress

Using a temporary indent command for the date (“.ti 4i”) would shorten the
macro by a line and would also make the macro responsive (or vulnerable) to
any indenting which might be in effect. If the title is to be printed exactly as
it was entered, a no fill [‘‘.nf”] command may be added, and the abstract or ‘Ce’

macros would then contain a fill [“.fi”] to turn this off.
You can experiment with various combinations of these commands

(there

is no particular reason, for example, to include the date within the title

macro); since typewriters sometimes slip when beginning a sheet on the exact
top, you may want to print alignment marks on the form itself, and recalculate
the macros accordingly. For extremely complicated forms, I might suggest
perusing the sections ‘Marking Place (Returning)’ and ‘Tabs, Leaders, Fields’
in Part 2 (‘Basic Functions’), or the table preprocessor tbl described below.

Macros

108 Cover Page |Conditionals for the First Page!
Conditionals for the First Page

Infrequently, a macro is to behave in one way on the first page and in
completely different ways elsewhere; so the macro must contain a conditional
that will test for this. The most convenient approach is to include one of the
two types of ‘if’ tests [“.if” or “ie/.el”] within the macro. The matter of such
page-testing conditionals most often arises with header and footer macros, and
so a fuller treatment can be found there, but you should not feel that it must
be limited to headers and footers. A document cover, for example, may need to
accommodate a large logo on the first page, which in turn requires that the
text be indented or shortened, or placed in a different point size or font.
Rather than defining a special macro, you might include a conditional in a regular text macro like the left-justified paragraph:

.de Lp
.br
ene 2

if \\n%=1 \{\
in 1.5i
\"create a text-block three inches wide
jl 4.51

ft Ini

\"end ‘if’ test

To return to normal processing, in this example the footer macro would have
its own test for page one and would restore the line length, indent, and font at
the bottom of the page.

The Title Page in -me
Mercifully, -me does not do a great deal to establish a first page. The
non-macro title page given above would work fairly well, with two annoying
but minor differences: -me would ignore the “.sp 3i” command (the header by
default turns off spacing), providing an inch header margin and an inch footer
margin instead; and if you were to start the file with a title command (.tl ””),
the title would appear at the top of the page, followed by an unexpected inch
gap. But the first can be easily avoided with a “.rs” (‘restore spacing’) command, and the second with a “.ce’’.
”

Title pages in -me are instructively simple. The sole title-page command
is “tp”, and it is noteworthy for what it does not do: it does not generate the
header or footer margin, nor print the header or footer (hence no page
numbers); it does not increment the register for page numbers. It does allow
spacing from the top. Another -me command which might be conveniently discussed here is “.++ C”, a general-purpose identification for front matter,
chapters, and so forth. The ‘C’ identifies the specific kind of section:

.++P
\"preliminary material
.++ AB
\"abstract [roman pagination]
.+4+C
\"chapter [number in arabic numerals]
.++ RC
\"chapter, start renumbering pages
.+4+B
\"bibliography
+A
\"appendix [number in alphabetics]
.++ RA _ \"appendix, start renumbering pages
After this definition, the chapter or abstract itself may be called with “.+c”.

Macros

Cover Page [The Title Page in -me| 109
All of these commands accept a heading appearing after the ‘C’;; any spaces
within the heading must be rendered ‘unpaddable,’ like so:

.++ A” Appendix\ Section”

This will place that header throughout the section (at the foot on the first
page); as the example shows, the header can be shifted left, right, and center
with four apostrophes, just like -me’s header macro “he”.
Beyond that, all title, authors, abstracts, and so forth can be defined in

the various display macros available for text in -me: the centered list

[“.Q/.)1 C”] for the title and author, and major quote [“.(q/.)q”] for the abstract.
The -me chapter macro (briefly discussed in that section) may also figure on
the first page.

Cover Sheet and First Page in -ms
The good news is that the elaborate cover-sheet and first-page macros in
-ms have been tested extensively; they have always been able to generate titles
of any length, up to nine authors (with or without institutions), and a properly
set abstract. Under certain conditions, if your cover sheet is longer than one
page, you’re reprimanded. Furthermore, with one command |“.RP”’], the cover
sheet automatically generates a title page, and it then reprints this information on the next page, with the title, author, and organization (but without the
abstract) to head the text. Other formats specific to Bell Laboratories are
available, from letters and technical memoranda to internal memoranda, all
there free in the package. The bad news is, as I mentioned above, that very little of this is portable to other sites. So you and I will spend a great deal of
time understanding (with the potential aim of circumventing) all of the firstpage macros which have been established for the user’s ease. Part of this
involves initialization, and it is to this that we turn first.

Initialization in -ms: Titles, Spacing, and Headers
Initialization in -ms occurs in three stages: at the beginning of the text,
each time a paragraph macro is called, and each time a new page is called. At
this point we are most interested in the characteristics of the first stage,
involving two internal macros, “.IZ” and “.BG”. “.IZ” is defined and invoked
before any text processing occurs. This macro establishes all the number registers which will retain default values for line length, point size, vertical spacing, paragraph indent, the footnote environment, and the traps for headers and
footers. For example, “.IZ” stores the value of six inches in the line length
register (“nr LL M”) and does the same for the title length register (“.nr

LT M’”). The line length (“ll”) and length of title (“lt”) are then given those
values, and so they no longer have nroff and troff’s default six and a half
inches. After finishing with “.IZ”, the processor reads in the rest of the macro
package, and then starts your file; at this point it starts getting sticky. If you
have used the -ms cover sheet macros (“.TL’, “.AU”’), the package will not
print them as yet, but rather will store them in a large number of nested diversions. Finally, when it reaches the first reset macro in your document, the
internal macro “.BG” prints the entire cover sheet material under the format

instructions it has received. The reset macros are “.GO” [see the definition
abovel, “.PP”, “.LP”, “IP”, “.BR”, “.NP”, “.QP” [all paragraph macros], “.BR”

110 Cover Page [Initialization in -ms: Titles, Spacing, and Headers]

Macros

[bibliographic reference], ‘“.SH” and “.NH” [section headings], “.RS/.RE” [right
shift], and the “.TS/.TE” [the table preprocessor]. This should explain why the
documentation is so insistent on beginning a file in -ms with one of the reset
macros to initialize the text. But more to our purposes, it should explain why
both redefined number registers |“.nr LL 6.25i”] and redefined macros in -ms
(‘“.PT” for running heads, for example] must be placed before initialization if
they are to affect the first page; if they are defined after initialization, they
will function only after the next paragraph or even the next page.

Title length
This brings us to the title length number register |“.nr LT M”]; because
-ms presumes (wrongly) that you'll always use its cover-sheet or first-page
macros, this register is only read and reset with a “.TL’, or, failing that, at the
beginning of a new page (the internal “.NP” macro); so redefining the ‘LT’ at
the head of the file will not be sufficient to change the length-of-title setting
on your first page. In other words, after the “.GO” initialization, the line
length can and will be reset through its ‘LL’ number register, but not the
length of title. Two options are open; if the title-length change is going to be
permanent, I would suggest a combination of both the number register and the
straight nroff/troff length-of-title command, like this:
nr LL 6.251
nr LT 6.251

At 6.251
.GO \"initialization
tl Appeal to the Border States”
tl ’in Behalf of Compensated Emancipation”
tl ’July 12, 1862”
If you simply wanted to center a title on a temporarily longer or shorter line
and get on with it, the centering command “.ce” can accomplish this because it
centers on the line length:

nr LL 6.251
.GO \"initialization
.ce
Address to the Legislature
.ce
at Columbus, Ohio

.ce
February 13, 1861

If you choose this option, remember that the length of any titles in the rest of
the document (including those in headers and footers) will remain at -ms’s
default six inches.
Spacing

An analogous situation occurs with spacing. If when using -ms you skip
the various cover-sheet macros but do want your own title page to have two or
three inches of white space at the top, the following will not work:

Macros

Cover Page [Initialization in -ms:

Titles, Spacing, and Headers] 111

.GO \"initialization
sp 2i\"this won’t do it
.ce
Address Delivered at Cooper Institute
Neither will “sp |2i” help (I’ve tried it), for the simple reason that the firstpage macros (like the header macros) turn off spacing with the “.ns” command.
The natural remedy is to restore spacing with “rs”:
.GO \"initialization
Ts
Sp 2i
.ce
Reply to Douglas
.ce
First Joint Debate (August 21, 1858)
.ce
Ottawa, Illinois

If you feel adventuresome, you could also try “.sv 2i”, which saves that space
and then prints it if there’s sufficient room before the next ‘trap.’ The result is
the same, but sometimes it’s uplifting to try an unusual option.

Running headers
A third area of frustration for the -ms user is the construction of running
headers and footers which are to appear on the first page. I have seen people
try all sorts of tricks to force these, nearly always with a waste of paper and
sometimes with a very strange page numbering. However, now that you
understand the stages of initialization, you can see that what is needed is an
indication of a new page, performed by the internal macro “.NP”. This does not
begin a new page (that command is in the internal footer macro “.FO”), but it
does considerable service in clearing and resetting footnotes, returning columns
to the left (neither of which pertain to the first page), and finally resetting the
title length (which does).
The section on headers and footers below describes the options in -ms, but
a brief introduction should be useful here. -ms allows you to develop a running
header without disturbing the other functions of “.NP” by defining a fairly

straightforward macro called “.PT” [for page title], and a corresponding footer
macro called “.BT” [bottom title]. To change either of these, you simply redefine them; but in order to print them on the first page, you must do it before
the “.NP”, like this:

.de PT
Jt \\n(LLu
nr PN \\n%
tl”’Reply at Jonesborough’
tl’’September 15, 1858’

Jt \\n(lu
nr LL 6.251
nr LT 6.251
.GO
.NP
Ladies and Gentlemen, There is very much in the principles

112 Cover Page [Initialization in -ms:

Titles, Spacing, and Headers]

Macros

Because of the length of title resetting mechanisms in “.PT”, no “.lt 6.251” was
necessary, but the “.NP” had to appear after the “.GO” so that the new line
length would be in effect when the header was invoked. Some local versions of
-ms have already been modified in response to complaints about this restriction
on running headers and footers, and at such sites the “.PT” or “.BT” can simply be redefined before the “.GO” to place it on the first page.

Cover-Sheet Options in -ms

Options for the first page exist on two levels in -ms: (1) there is a format
for each kind of document (released paper, technical memorandum, internal
memorandum); and (2) there are macros for the categories of information—
author, date—that each kind requires. Some categories are shared (title,

author), some are peculiar to the specific format (“.OK” [other keywords] for
technical memoranda). Categories which are not appropriate to a format are
ignored, thus allowing a single text to serve any purpose. Unfortunately, these
macros are totally dependent on Bell Labs forms, even down to the precise location of the manual number, the charging case number, the filing case number,
and so forth; so their general utility is extremely limited. Nevertheless they
show what can be done, and occasionally they can be converted to your standardized forms without too much difficulty. In the sections to follow, I will
briefly list the kinds of documents, then describe the first-page macros, since it
is likely that you are more interested in the macros than the various formats
for papers and memoranda.
Kinds of documents

As I mentioned, the philosophy behind the various format options in -ms
is to retain the same basic text, together with a large number of cover-sheet
options, and then permit the user to reformat that text by merely changing a
single macro call at the beginning of the file. The eight formats that usually
come with -ms are the following:

.EG
.IM
.LR
.MF
.MR
.RP
.TM

\"engineer’s notes
\"internal memorandum
\"letter
\"memorandum for file
\"memorandum for record
\"released paper
\"technical memorandum

.TR

\"technical report series

The technical documents typeset the entire cover sheet, including logos, lines,
boxes, titles, absolutely everything; but the rest are not so ambitious: SDR:
for example, prints the day’s date (‘\x(DY”) and no special macros. And many
of the others print the bell logo and subject lines, but not much more. In common with the design approach inherent in much of -ms, the options draw internally on one common macro (“.TM” in this case), relying on number registers

to flag various labels and formats.

The released paper macro [“.RP”] provides

both a cover sheet (complete with title, author, author’s institution, and
abstract) and the following title page (which automatically reproduces the
title, author, and institution). Finally, it’s nice to know that, for all the
vagaries in -ms, footnotes are possible on the cover page, as are tables and
equations.

Macros

Cover Page [Individual Cover- and First-Page Macros in -ms} 113

Individual Cover- and First-Page Macros in -ms
It is possible to use the various first-page macros without one of the eight
formats, this having the benefit of creating a consistent title or first page
without too much trouble. There are four relevant macros for the cover page
in -ms: the title (“.TL”), author (“.AU”), the author’s institution (“.AI’”), and
the abstract, which is to be bracketed (“.AB/.AE”). These exist in two slightly
different permutations, depending on whether they are functioning on a true
cover-sheet (therefore before the “.GO” initialization), or simply on the first
page and within the text (after the “.GO”). Each of the four may be used
independently or in any combination; they must, however, follow in the given
order (title first, author, institution, author, institution, and so forth, ending
with the abstract). In order to print them, the cover-sheet macros must be followed by initialization (a “.GO” or a paragraph macro), even if a cover sheet
was all that you wanted.
Used without the eight format commands, the cover-sheet macros will not
immediately begin a new page when they’re finished; text will be printed on
the same page, after the end of the abstract. To force this, a “.bp” should be
placed on the line after the initializing command.
When formatted, the title appears an inch and a half from the top of the
page (not in first page or in text, where only a space separates it from the
rest), centered, with no hyphenation. In nroff, it is single-spaced and underlined; in troff, it is set boldface twelve point (or two sizes larger than current
point size if “PS” has been redefined), with fifteen-point vertical spacing. The
title itself may be entered on as many lines as you like after the “.TL”; but
remember that troff or nroff will make their own decisions about how the title
will be arranged in the center of the page. If you would like to control this,
you must place a no fill [“.nf”] command just before the title itself. Unlike
“TL”, the author and author’s institution macros demand only one line of text
after them, and will ignore a second line; but you can put as much information
as you like in that line. If you wish to center each individual author (with or
without any accompanying institution), the “.AU/.AI” may be repeated, up to
nine times. A cover sheet with a mixture of multiple authors, then, might look
like this:

ATE
A Protest against the Proclamation
of His Excellency Andrew Johnson, Military Governor
.AU
Wm. B. Campbell
.AI
Wilson County
.AU
Thos. A.R. Nelson
Al
Washington County
.AU
James T.P. Carter
Al
Carter County
.AU
John Williams
.Al
Knox County

.GO

114 Cover Page [Individual Cover- and First-Page Macros in -ms|

Macros

.bp
In troff the author’s name will be centered, italicized, ten on twelve (if on first
page, point size and vertical spacing defined by PS/VS), separated from the
title and other authors by a space. In nroff it is also centered, but there is no
underlining, and there are two spaces in between. The institution is centered,
in roman font, same point size, with a half space between it and the author; in
nroff this is a full space. The abstract begin and end (“.AB/.AE”) is kind
enough to center the capitalized word ‘ABSTRACT’ one space above the
abstract itself; if you wish to avoid this, a ‘no’ (or a ‘-’), entered after the
‘ AB’ , will forestall this.

If you would like a different word, like ‘Summary,’

enter that word after the ‘.AB’. The abstract text is printed in the point size
and vertical spacing set in ‘PS/VS,’ indented on either side by one-twelfth the
line length, and the first line is further indented by the value in the paragraph

indent number register ‘PI’ [default half an inch].
I suppose using the -ms cover-page macros is a matter of two very different kinds of convenience: against the convenience of having tested macros
already at hand is the convenience of (eventually) utilizing your own. In my
experience, the latter finally wins out.

DISPLAYS

Fundamentals

Displays are simply blocks of text that the formatter prints exactly as
they have been entered in the source file, set off from the surrounding document. Characteristically, therefore, they are not filled; that is, short lines are
not composed into lines of regular length, and longer lines are printed unbro-

ken [cf. the “.nf” command]. This section will first construct a series of display
macros from the ground up, then review similar macros in the -me and -ms
packages. The two packages make a wide variety of display formats available,
and this might at first deter the novice. -me, for example, offers one set of
displays that keeps the block integral on a page and another set that allows it

to be split. Similarly, -ms has a definition for a display [the ‘DS’ series] which
‘keeps’ text and other macros for left display, center display, and block display
which do not.

In spite of the range of choice, there are only a few ways to

display material:

1) as a ‘keep’ or not; 2) left-justified;

3) with all lines cen-

tered on the page, or only on the longest line; and 4) indented. The -me and
-ms display macros contain all of these features; in almost every case, the macros are straightforward enough. Probably the most important thing to
remember at the outset is that all of the following macros are the bracketing
type, with a separate indication of a beginning and end (like tables, equations,

and footnotes).

‘Keep’ Options
With the ‘keep’ option, the remainder of the page is measured against the
size of the display. If the display block won’t fit in its entirety on the page, the
block is not printed until there is room for it, at the top of the next page. You
have the choice of leaving the remainder of the page blank or filling it in with

115

Macros

116 Displays |‘Keep’ Options!

the text that comes after the display (a ‘floating’ keep); while this latter is
useful for tables, it does not generally apply to displays, which cannot usually
be shifted from their position in the text.
Since the ‘keep’ option has wider applications than just displays, it is
treated in its own section in this Guide. The ‘keep’ macro is a bracketing type,
its start and finish identified with “.Ks/.Ke” lor “.Kf/.Ke” for floating keeps];
these names roughly parallel -ms’s “.KS/.KE” lor “.KF/.KE”]. In the following
examples, the ‘keep’ macros are usually included without comment, to show
where they might be inserted if you wish to retain the text block on one page;
taking them out of a particular display macro will usually mean only that the
display now can be divided over two pages. Also, since keeps utilize the
environments (‘“.ev N”’), you will have to be a little more careful about formatting commands (‘parameters’) within the display.

Left Adjustment

The term ‘left-justified’ is something of a misnomer, since all it means is
that the display is placed against the left margin. If one or all the lines begin
with spaces, the no-fill mode retains them. So all that has to be done is to precede the display text with a “.nf”, follow it with a “fi”, and perhaps throw in a
space before and after to set it off. Here the left-adjust macro will be called
‘La’ and the display-finish will be, as always, ‘Df’:

de La

\"left-adjust display

.de Df

\"end display

The following constitutes a typical use:
.La
By God he swore a lie.
He swore a lie, and I can prove it.
I can prove that he swore falsely and perjured himself.
I can prove by witnesses in Ohio that he swore a lie.
I am not afraid to tell him that he swore a lie.
He swore false, and I will prove it.
The court instruct the jury:

Center Display
Two sorts of centering are possible in displays: every line (1) centered on
the page, or (2) centered on the longest line in the block. The first of these is
by far the easiest, relying on a technique which the cover page macros made
famous: using the command “.ce N” to center everything (or, more precisely,
the next thousand lines) until the formatter is told to center nothing. In all
other respects the centering display macro [“.Cl”] should look miraculously
familiar:

nr Ps 10

nr Vs 12p
.de Cl

.de Df

.Ke

We all presume a thousand lines will be enough, though -me has bumped it to
ten thousand in a spirit of liberality.
A display macro which centers the block on its longest line presents a difficulty, though not a major one; at the end of the first macro [‘Bc’ below], the

entire block is diverted to and stored in a named location (“.Bs”’):

Indented Displays

Following the lead of -ms, you can define indented displays which either
remain a fixed distance from the left margin or vary according to values you
give it. In fact, with the help of the read-only number register “\n(.$’, which
counts the ‘arguments,’ the macro will check to see if you wish another value.
If so, the new value will replace the fixed one. In the following example, an
absence of value means that the indented display [“.Id”] will be one inch from
the margin. However, if the “.$” register has sensed a trailing argument, that
string [‘$1’] is used for the indent:
nr Ps 10

nr Vs 12p
.de Id
.Ks
ftel
.ps \\n(Ps
.vs \\n(Vsu
nf
Sp
in li

if \n(.$ in \$1

The end-of-display macro will have to have a command that either cancels the
indent or returns the text to the current indent.

Displays in -me
The -me macro package contains both kinds of display macros: those that

do not attempt to keep the whole display on a page [“.(1/.)l” and “.(c/.)c”], and
those that do [‘‘.(b/.)b” and “.(z/.)z”]. Except for the center display “.(c”, each
of these offers extensive options through a nicely parallel series of ‘arguments’
(specific letters or numbers which immediately follow the macro when it is
invoked); in fact, since one of the options is to fill the text, the “.(b” and the
“(z” may also function as straight ‘keep’ and ‘floating keep’ macros [“.KS/.KE”

and “.KF/.KE” in -ms].
The default font for displays in -me is roman (“ft 1’); this can be reset
for all subsequent displays through the ‘df’ number register. For example,
nr df 2

will set the material in italic.

Macros

120 Displays |Spacing in -me’s Displays]
Spacing in -me’s Displays

All displays are single-spaced in nroff, 10/11 in troff; the vertical spacing
can be altered by redefining number register “\n($R N”, in which ‘N’ is a
whole-number multiple of the current point size.

Vertical distance in nroff, as

you may remember, is measured in units of 240 to the inch; a single-space line
in typewriting is one-sixth of an inch (40 units); the default “\n($R”, then, in
nroff -me is 40 divided by 10 (the default point size), or 4. To double-space all
of your displays, therefore, you would enter:

nr $R 8

\"80 units [two lines]

If you wish just one display double-spaced, put a “.ls 2” after the ‘display begin’
command (it need not be reset at the ‘display end’).
In typesetting, machine units vary according to the device; for those
which define 432 units to the inch, default “\n($R” is rounded up to seven (11p
vertical spacing [66 units] divided by 10 points). Using the “.sz N” command
to change the point size in a display will automatically increase the vertical
spacing in units, just as it will in ordinary text la “.sz 12” will, for example,
produce a vertical spacing of 14p]. This is not a permanent change for subsequent displays, however. If you do wish to alter the vertical spacing
throughout by using “\n($R”, the process is complicated but not impossible.
Suppose that you would like a display vertical spacing of eighteen points for
ten-point type. Eighteen points translates into a quarter-inch movement from
base line to base line, which comes to 108 units on a 432-unit device; rounding
it up to the next whole number, you would then set the number register in this
way, at any point before the ‘display begin’ command:
nr $R 11

This is probably more trouble than it’s worth. Except for the center display, all
displays in -me are set apart from the text by a space equal to the point size in
troff, or one line in nroff.

Non-Keep-Display in -me

The most usual display macro for -me is “.(1”, ‘list,’ which must always be
accompanied by its end-of-display macro “.)]”. If these bracket a text, it will be
printed in no-fill, with the spaces mentioned at the end of the previous paragraph, and slightly indented.’ This list macro exists in four variations, which
differ according to the horizontal position of the display; they are invoked bya
capital letter appearing after the “.(1”: ‘I’ indented, the same as “.1)” by itself);
‘M’ [against the left margin]; ‘L’ [adjusted with the text —identical with ‘M’
unless the text has been indented]; and ‘C’ [each line centered]. Therefore, to
indent a display a half-inch, you would precede the block with the following:
nr bi .5i
AEA

There is a further choice, should you desire it: following the ‘I,’ ‘M,’ or ‘L’ flags,
you may enter an ‘F,’ which will put the display in fill mode:
Four ‘n’s’ [four-tenths of an inch] in nroff, a quarter inch in troff, a value stored
in number register ‘bi.’

Macros

Displays |Non-Keep-Display in -me] 121
l(MF
This sequence would take the display to the left margin, composing all of its
lines to the line length currently in effect.

The other display command which does not save text is the “.(c/.)c” command, which centers the block on the longest line (as opposed to “.(1 C”, which
centers each individual line). If you wish to keep this block integral, it is possible to place the entire sequence inside “.(b L/.)b” or “.(z L/.)z”, the ‘keep’ macros.

Keep-Display in -me
The “.(b/.)b” command to bracket a display will save that block until the
formatter finds the first possible place where the text will fit in its entirety,
even if this means going onto the next page. With typical solicitude, however,
-me allows you to stipulate in number register ‘bt’ the maximum amount of
blank page that you will tolerate. If, therefore, you think anything up to two
inches is acceptable, you may set ‘bt’ in this way:
nr bt 2i

This means that if saving the display for the next page will mean more than
two inches of white space on the current page, -me will divide it, just as if you
had used a “.(1” command. To turn off this command [default], you may rede-

fine it as zero [“.nr bt 0”).
“(b” has precisely the same options as does “.(1”: ‘IT’ [indent —also “.(b”
without any trailing letter]; ‘M’ [left margin]; ‘L’ [left-justified with text]; and
‘C’ levery lined centered]. There is also a ‘F’ if you wish filled text:

b

LF

This will produce a keep-display aligned with the text (even if it is currently
indented), and in a fill mode. As in “.(1”, the indent can be redefined, this time

in the number register ‘bi,’ so that a “.nr bi 1i” will indent both “.(b” by itself
and “.(b I” by one inch. To change the font of the display, “.(b” and “.(1” share
number register ‘bf’ mentioned above.
The ‘floating’ keep command for -me is “.(z/.)z”; it looks and behaves very
much like “.(b/.)b” except that, if there’s not enough room on the page for the
whole block, the text beyond the block will be processed to fill the page. This
is useful for figures and tables whose exact position in the text is a matter of
no particular consequence. “.(z” can also appear with ‘I,’ ‘M,’ ‘L’ and ‘C,’ like
“(b”, with a trailing ‘F, if you wish. There are two slight differences, however: “.(z” defaults to the left margin (‘M’) if no letter follows, and the spacing
between the block and the text can be changed in number register ‘zs,’ which is
originally set at one vertical space.

The keep-display commands in -me have no counterpart to “.(c” [center
the entire display as a block], but rather allow the use of “.(c/.)c” within
“(b L/.)b” or “.(z L/.)z”, like so:

Macros

(z L

EVERY DAY

ONWARD TO RICHMOND
BY A SELECT

The ‘L’ option seems most appropriate in order to center it in the text without
the default indent of “.(z” (and “.(b”), though it isn’t required.

Displays in -ms
There are eight display commands in -ms, four of which ‘keep’ the defined
block (saving it for the next page if it doesn’t fit on the present one) and four
of which do not. Members of the first group uniformly begin with the com-

mand “.DS”: the left-adjust display [‘‘.DS L”]; the centered display [‘‘.DS C’’];
the block display [‘.DS B”], and the indented display in two forms [“.DS” (a
half-inch indent in troff, eight ‘n’s’ in nroff) and “.DS I M” (variable, determined by the measurement M)]. The four in the second group are the left
[“.LD’’], center [‘‘.CD’’], block [““.BD”], and indented displays [“.ID”]. All eight
must be coupled with a “.DE” (display end), which is typed on its own line
after the block has been entered.
Each type of display functions horizontally, that is, where it puts the
block in relation to the text, as defined by the page offset and line length regis-

ters and by the current indent.

The left-adjust displays |“.DS L” and ‘“.LD”|

align the block against the left margin of the text.

The center displays

“DS C” and “.CD”] center each line of the block, the center point being the
text line length (“.nr LL’, not “1l”) minus any indent, divided by two. The
block-center displays |“.DS B” and “.BD’’] are quite different, in that the longest line of the block is centered, and the other lines printed exactly as they
stand in relation to that line. The indented displays |“.DS I M”, “.DS”, and
“ID M”’| add their indent value M to the current indent, if any. All the
displays add a line space above and below in nroff and a half vertical space in
troff.

Keep-Display in -ms: ‘‘.DS”’
The “.DS/.DE” commands in -ms are all of the straight ‘keep’ variety,

utilizing for this purpose the -ms macro “.KS/.KE”; they do not allow ‘floating,’
in which, if the block has to be moved to the page following, text coming after
the display is processed in the blank space remaining on the current page.
(This can be changed by redefining the “.DS” macro, for which see the section
‘The Display Macro Itself,’ below.) For a typical display sequence in -ms, the
following serves as an example:

Macros

Displays [Keep-Display in -ms: “DS’’] 123
-DS I -75i
.ce

When lilacs last in the dooryard bloomed
And the great star early droop’d in the western sky
in the night.
I mourn’d, and yet shall mourn with ever-returning
spring.
Ever-returning spring, trinity sure to me you bring.
Lilac blooming perennial and drooping star in the west,
And thought of him I love.
.DE

As you can see, ordinary troff/nroff commands can appear in displays; blank
lines and lines beginning with spaces may appear as well.
The internal parameters for these displays are the usual defaults for -ms
(a point size/vertical spacing of 10/12, roman font in troff, single-space in
nroff), with the exception that the display is in no-fill mode (adjustment therefore is of no consequence, as is line length). If you wish to change one of these
for a specific display, you must enter the command right after the display
macro, and turn it off right before the “.DE”. For example, to double-space a
display in nroff -ms, you would enter the following:
.DS B

The Display Macro Itself
The various displays are actually all connected through an ingenious
sequence of macro definitions found in a file called something like

‘/asr/lib/tmac/tmac.sdisp’ [troff macros, the display for -ms].

Since the

how these things work (also, if you have a good memory, you’ll see a certain
indebtedness to this macro set on my part earlier in this section):

As you can see, the non-keep macros join the system below the “.KS”; otherwise they’re nearly identical. The only two components which are not immediately clear here are the indent “if \\n(.$” tests and the ‘BD/EI’ in centering.
For the first, the macro command “.DS I N” has been changed in the first
macro to “.DI NI’, and “.DS” by itself has become simply “.D’. In the next
step, “.D” is made “.DI’. Finally, “.XD” brings in the current indent, to which
defaults are added. Then “.if \\n(.$” checks for the presence of arguments;
simple “.DS” and “.ID” by themselves have none, so they skip on. If an argument exists, the test further checks to make sure the first argument is neither
‘T [an error] nor blank; if this is true, it replaces the current-plus-default with
the current, and again, if this is true, it adds the value originally stipulated in

the “.DS I” or “.ID”. In the centered block display, there is a check first to
make sure that the block which has been diverted is more than one machine
unit; if so, then the register ‘BD’ becomes the width of the diversion. If this
width is less than the line length, the indent for the block is calculated at the
line length, minus the diversion width, all divided by two. Finally, the right
margin is figured in number register ‘EI,’ and any tabs are set to that margin,
with instructions to right-adjust.
If, therefore, you wanted the ‘floating keep’ option for your displays, you
would merely copy this sequence at the head (but after initialization) of your
file (using the “:r /usr/lib/tmac/tmac.sdisp” command in the UNIX editors) and
change the “.KS” to “.KF”.

EQUATIONS

The Basics of eqn

eqn, forming with tbl (and a few others) an exclusive group of preprocessors to troff, produces a wide variety of mathematical symbols and equations.!
The preprocessor name is apt because in order to use it, eqn is placed before
the file name and the results are then piped through troff. Typically, the command would look like this:

$ eqn filename | troff
The troff part can contain all the command-line options
-ON, -8N, -nN

and -me or -ms as well. Without the macro packages, the equation will be
placed against the left margin; with them, it will be automatically centered. If
you're using both eqn and tbl together (work for the computer but sometimes
necessary), it is advisable to have the tbl command go first, followed by the
filename, pipe, eqn, pipe, and troff command:

$ tbl filename| eqn | troff

lhe standard work on eqn is Brian W. Kernighan and Lorinda Cherry’s “Typesetting Mathematics—User’s Guide,”
2nd ed., Bell Laboratories, March 26, 1980. Further symbols have been developed by Carmela Scrocca and are
described in “New Graphic Symbols for EQN and NEQN,” Bell Laboratories, © 1982.

127

Macros

128 Equations |The Basics of eqn]

As Appendixes VI and VII indicate, however, the conjunction of tbl and eqn is

not always the simplest of matters.”
In fact, even when used individually both tbl and eqn tend to need special
care (that is, frequent tinkering). It is a very good idea, then, to reserve text
with tables or equations in files separate from the source document.’ With a
“bp +2” in the source file at the place where the table or equation is to appear,
you can instruct the processor to skip the page. After the entire text has been
printed, the specially formatted page can then be assigned the skipped page
number with the ‘-n’ option, as, for example:

$ tbl filename| eqn| troff -n25
If the table does not go on its own page, then of course the text will need to be
processed as well, but in terms of load on the system and your time, it’s best
not to send very much straight text through either or both of these preprocessors.
I have here spoken only of troff on purpose; nroff does have a version of
eqn called neqn, which attempts to produce the various symbols, given the limitations of line printers and most automatic typewriters. ‘Attempts’ is
extremely accurate in this case, since the combination of poor vertical resolu_
tion, missing symbols, overstrikes, and offstrikes makes the equation extremely
difficult to read correctly; the common wisdom is to use neqn for inexpensive
and quick production at a proofreading stage. The best use of neqn is in fact
on the terminal (where the output looks very strange indeed) for the very first
test of an equation, since it can plaster the screen with error messages just as
well as eqn does on paper. If you’re using neqn with an automatic typewriter,
and especially if you’re doing any movement within the equation, you will need
to specify the terminal type:

$ neqn filename| nroff -T450
If there is no half-line capability, you may want to pipe it through col, like so:

$ neqn filename| nroff | col
With patience and the use of “ndefine”, an option which allows you to define
symbols for neqn, anything is possible, for which, see below.

Bracketing: ‘‘.EQ/.EN’’; delim cc

Macros

Equations |Bracketing: “EQ/.EN’’; delim cc] 129
Equations apart from the text

For the first, functioning to separate the equation from the rest of the
document, the bracketing commands are “.EQ/.EN” on lines by themselves; the
symbols and instructions for the equation are placed between the “.EQ” and
“EN”, like so:

.EQ
symbols instructions more symbols
.EN

If there is more than one equation in a series, you must create multiple
“EQ/.EN’s:

.EQ
symbols instructions symbols
.EN
.EQ
instructions symbols instructions
.EN
You must also use separating commands if the single equation contains vertically distinct parts, as in the case of a mathematical problem that needs to be
worked out in stages.‘ If the specific instructions needed for the equation
occupy more space than the width of the screen (happens all the time), or if
you need to break up the equation into its components for the purpose of readability (an excellent idea), you may use as many lines as you wish:

.EQ
instructions symbols <cr >
instructions symbols symbols<cr >
more symbols instructions
.EN
The carriage return will be understood as the equivalent of a space, but, as you
will see, a space isn’t exactly a space.

Equations within the line
If you need the equations folded within the text, special delimiters may be
defined with the following command, usually placed at the beginning of the
file:
.EQ
delim cc
.EN

in which cc is almost any set of two characters, usually ‘$.’ Curly braces Lt}
and tildes [~] are not allowed for reasons that will become clear; the circumflex

[*] is similarly forbidden, and when invoking eqn with tbl it’s unwise to use
4The macro packages -me and -ms automatically put a space (one space in nroff, .5v in troff) between the equation

and the text; they also will ‘keep’ the expression if it doesn’t fit on the page, as might be the case if it is a complicated fraction, pile, matrix, all of which necessarily involve multiple lines. -me also has the capability to keep several

equations together through the “.EN C” option lsee the end of this section].

130 Equations |Bracketing: “EQ/.EN’’; delim cc]

Macros

pluses [+], minuses |—], sharp-signs |#], and vertical bars ||]—this doesn’t leave
you with much, I admit. The ‘delim’ statement means, however, that the
source file cannot utilize the two characters for any other purpose, or it will
drive you and eqn wild. If you have a few such non-eqn purposes early in the
document and the in-line equations come toward the end, you may want to
place the ‘delim’ definition immediately before the equation rather than at the
beginning of the file. Or if the equations exist only in the early part of the
text, whatever delimiters you’ve defined can be freed from service with this
command:
EQ
delim off
.EN

I have found no other way around this, but there may be a simple one.
The equation itself
In the first, the set-off-from-the-text configuration, the various symbols
are requested within the two commands “.EQ/.EN’, so that a simple equation
involving a lower-case delta would look like the following:
.EQ
delta
.EN

In straight troff, this will produce a delta, divided from the text, on the lefthand margin. With a nice if wasteful kind of consistency, a capital delta would
be requested with a “DELTA”.
Within a line, a capital delta would be requested in this way, given ‘$’s as
delimiters:
rate of change is signaled by a ‘$DELTA$.’
In this latter capacity, eqn is not bothered by space or punctuation (like the
period and quotation marks above) immediately outside of the delimiters,
which almost always is an advantage. Naturally, a file may mix equations on
their own and equations within lines throughout a text with carefree abandon.

eqn’s Components

The examples demonstrate the basics of eqn, but unfortunately few equations are single Greek letters, and so it does get complicated. For the purposes
of clarification, this section will treat five potential components of any equation: symbols, movement, special constructions and figures, typesetting

enhancements, and user definitions. The five components will be treated in
order, starting with the various other symbols available.

Grouping elements
The discussion of special symbols raises a perennial problem in word processing, if not computing: how does one distinguish between literal elements
and special commands of the same form? This might not initially seem like a
problem. After all, eqn would accept the following and correctly italicize the
letters:
a+b+c+d=e

But you might want to write ‘xi=é,’ in which ‘xi’ is not a mathematical symbol,
but simply the name of that character. eqn solves this by expecting either

spaces, carriage-returns, circumflexes |"], tildes |"], or braces ({}] around any
special symbol. To print the potentially ambiguous combination of ‘x’ followed

by an ‘i’ as a literal ‘xi,’ then, you must make sure that something else is next
to it, as in ‘xi=,’ ‘xi+,’ and so on; or, if surrounding space is absolutely

132 Equations |Symbols and Grouping in eqn|

Macros

necessary, the string must be ‘quoted’ with those ubiquitous UNIX double
quotes: " xi ".° If a letter is not potentially ambiguous (‘p,’ ‘x’), the defining
spaces are not necessary, but for readability and clarity it is customary to put
them in, and from now on I will do so.

Separation through spaces (or their equivalent, carriage returns), tildes
(or circumflexes), and braces all have their place; as usual, the last of these is
at the same time the least natural and the safest, and wisdom would prescribe
braces whether or not they’re strictly necessary, as a kindness to yourself and
others if the equation ever needs to be used again. Spaces merely define the
element, and are eliminated after the mathematical equation is processed, so
that ‘p = log N’ produces ‘p=logN.’ Beginning users of eqn err all the time in
forgetting that a space is primarily there to separate unique elements and not
act as a space, when they’re not forgetting to insert the spaces in the first
place. The tilde not only separates elements, but inserts a space for each tilde:
‘p = log N’, a more pleasing visual experience; the circumflex inserts half a

space. The braces define an element absolutely, and in complicated equations
such groupings are an absolute necessity.
At first the liberal use of braces would seem to have little to recommend
it. They do not, for example, add spaces, so using braces for our p formula
seems like a waste of byte:

tp} = {log}{N}
Not only that, but at this level they would only get in the way. If we were
interested in placing ‘log’ and ‘N’ physically together, entering ‘{logN}’ would
be exactly the wrong thing to do, since it would be asking eqn to find a meaningful string called ‘logN,’ and when it couldn’t, the four characters would
appear italicized. To juxtapose the two elements, we could write ‘{log}{N},’
which hardly seems worth the trouble, or, and this will be important, ‘{log N}.’

The crucial point is that eqn will now think of the ‘logN’ as a unit which will
always be kept together. We could also retain the space between the two and
maintain the unit status by using a tilde instead of a space:‘{log~N}.’
This property of ‘unit status’ becomes essential in complex fractions,
exponentials, and so forth with multiple levels of relationship. In those
instances it will be necessary, for example, to distinguish between
a plus b-over-c and a-plus-b over c. The first, in which b forms a unit with c,
should be written (to give you the fraction command early) “{b over c}”. The
second, in which a forms a unit with 6, must be written “{a + b}”. The first
could dispense with the braces, so that
a+boverc

would work; but you can already see the inherent danger with anything more
complicated, like this:

°With welcome consistency, eqn accepts any command or function as a literal if it is surrounded with such double
quotes. Literal tildes, circumflexes and braces, for example, need to be quoted: "",""", and "|". To print a double
quote itself, you must precede it with a backslash, like so: \". Local horizontal and vertical movement |‘\h” and

“\v”| must be quoted. The various troff number registers must be both quoted and preceded with a double
backslash
(as in ‘‘\\n(.f” to determine the current font).

Macros

Equations [Symbols and Grouping in eqn] 133

( alpha ) + {beta over gamma}
(Just to reiterate a point, you may notice that the special symbols need to be
separated even from the parentheses, but not from the opening and closing,
separating braces.) And finally, braces can be used within braces, as certainly
would be true if the last equation were part of a greater one. In any case, keep
the opening and closing braces matched®; eqn is just cruel enough to berate
you if you’ve entered more opening than closing braces, and then as a lesson it
will proceed to print the garbage you’ve created.

Movement

The movement of symbols and groups occurs in a large number of different ways: diacritics, superscripts and subscripts, fractions, alignment, piles,
and matrices. All of these use a specific one-word command which usually precedes the material to be moved, though occasionally the command stands
between (as in ‘over’) the affected symbols. Diacritical marks represent the
only time that the command would follow. Each of these is understandable
enough when taken individually, but in the aggregate, they do cause problems.
Diacritical marks

Eight diacritical marks are available through eqn; three are similar to
the accents which exist as defined strings (the umlaut, circumflex, and tilde),
and five are more or less specific to eqn. In every case the character to be
marked is written first, and then followed by a space and the one-word com-

mand:
x bar
x dot
x dotdot

x dyad
x hat
x tilde
x under
x vec

RR.
RPIR
RI
Rr

These would naturally use a space to separate them, not a tilde. The ‘bar’ and
‘under’ will draw a line the entire length of the complete preceding unit, so

that “LM bar” will produce a line over the italicized LM. Again, though,
braces may be necessary; for example, to denote the mean of a minus £ times
y, you must use braces: “{( alpha - beta ) * gamma} bar”.
Superscripts and subscripts
These, the most local of motions, are also the most straightforward: the

formulas are “x sup y” and “x sub y”, in which y will rise a proportionate distance above or below the line and appear in a smaller point size. If there are to
be subscripts of superscripts or superscripts of subscripts (and so on), grouping
becomes a must. By convention, if a symbol is to be both subscripted and
superscripted, the subscription command is to come first:
place
"If you use the UNIX editor vi, the percent-sign key \““%,”| has been dedicated to finding matching braces. Just
the key
the cursor over a beginning brace, press the %, and the cursor will find the closing brace. Another press of
will return the cursor to the opening brace. There'll be a squeak if no complementary brace is found.

X sub j sup 2

This will produce

xX?
Should you reverse these and put the superscript first, the square will be
indexed, and there you'll be. But again you could ignore the convention by
properly bracing the individual units:
