Even if you really did mean to formulate something like

f subj

d subj sup 2

entering it as this, instead
{f subj} {d subj} sup 2

will allow you to figure out what it was the next morning.
It does happen that the superscript or subscript is to appear before the
symbol, rather than after it. Here we are reduced to the same ploy used in
skipping macro arguments, which is to insert a pair of double-quotes, signifying nothing, before the lead subscript or superscript. A combination, for example, of sixteen objects taken two at a time would be entered in this way:

{"" sub 16} {C sub 2}
In the text, this would appear as

16C’2
The same approach is used in text superscripts and subscripts (“.SU” and
““SB”) to print a raised footnote number in a note.

Alignment
The difficulty of having several parts to one equation, or several equations for the same variable, is solved through a system of alignment, which

allows you to mark a position in one formula which will be remembered in
every subsequent equation until you change it. It functions horizontally,
returning to places within lines of text. The command “mark” is placed
appropriately, and the complementary command “lineup” will move the
material to the left over to that position.’ The following is a simple example:

EQI

.EN

.EN

Piles

The command “pile”, coming immediately before a group of symbols,
stacks them up neatly. Naturally, the symbols must be braced; eqn is told that
one should be placed over another through the command “above”:

pile { s'= above v = above a = }

“pile {theta r above omega r above alpha r }

This works well with big brackets, literal braces, parentheses, and bars.

The

pile is by default centered (a synonym is “cpile’); should you wish them leftjustified, you would use the command “pile”; for right-justified, the command
“rpile”. Though these last two are a little larger in format than “pile” itself,
none of the pile commands permits subscripts or superscripts; matrices will.
Matrices

Matrices in eqn are quite similar in construction to a succession of piles;
like piles, they rely on the command “above” and braces to sort out cells and
columns. But matrices also represent a further development, in that differentsized characters can be used. Because this is a more complicated operation, a
more complicated set of instructions is needed. In essence, there are two distinct commands: “matrix”, which introduces the entire matrix (separated by
braces); and within, “ccol”, “Icol”, or “rcol” [paralleling the “pile” command]
which governs its own column (each separated by braces). For example, if you
wished to create a three-by-three matrix, you would first establish it in its

Macros

Equations [Movement] 137
entirety and then define the three columns (in the following example, centered); the “matrix” command involves the kind of complexity which often
demands a multiline arrangement, so that it is conventionally entered in this
specific fashion:

matrix {
ccol { x sub 1 above x sub 2 above x sub 3}

ccol { y sub 1 above y sub 2 above y sub 3}
ccol { z sub 1 above z sub 2 above z sub 3}

The result would be

X2 Y2 22
x3 Y3 23

Special Constructions
Beyond the many symbols which eqn can create, there are a number of
special figures which automatically expand in size to subsume other elements
in the equation. They can conveniently be divided into two major classes: the
first contains symbols for intersection (not found in neqn), and union, integral,
product, summation, and square root. This group can in turn be divided in
thirds. The second major class comprises the bracketing functions
(parentheses, brackets, braces, and bars).

Intersections and unions
Commands for these two characters are straightforward enough:

inter

(1)

union

LU

In fact, the only feature that differentiates them from other symbols is their
size, which is proportionally larger than the current point size. If they are
placed within a line (as in the example), extra space is given above and below
(this is true for all the characters in this section).

Integrals, products, and summations
In their most simple form, these three are also requested in a direct
fashion:

il

sum

Il
>D

and “to” commands, and thus have to be checked with some care (even the
manuals and other published eqn equations seem to have a problem printing
the “to” section). It may be necessary to attempt fine-tuning with the “up” or
“down” commands to center the summation sign, for example, between the
“from” and “to”. For an example of the problem, this may be a common
enough instance:
sum from {j=1} to {j= inf}

Brackets, parentheses, braces, floors and ceilings, and bars
In a manner almost identical to the straight bracket-drawing function in
troff, eqn permits all sorts of parenthesizing devices, with the important
difference that it’s a lot simpler in eqn. Instead of the eleven-odd pieces which
require complete assembly, eqn recognizes the basic construction and does the
building itself. But you do have to recognize left and right, since ‘left’ and
‘right’ not only determine opening or closing brackets, but in the case of braces
tell the preprocessor that you want a literal brace rather than a logical separator.

The four sets are, then,

left |

right |

left (

right )

left {

right }

left |

right |

Since there are no ‘floor’ or ‘ceiling’ keys on the typewriter, eqn accepts these
names:
left floor
left ceiling

right floor
right ceiling

Like the square root, these will be made large enough to include the material
that follows (defined by braces), so that

Global size and font definitions

Equations may be defined globally in either point size or font with global
commands, “gsize N” and “gfont c”. The first will accept a point size or a +/number (like “size”) for increment or decrement. The second accepts an ‘R,’ ‘I,’
‘B, or a number for that font.

will place all subsequent equations in nine-point and bold (with the exception
of the special words, which are still printed in roman). As the eqn User’s Guide
advises, this is an excellent way to control the point size of equations in footnotes, as long as you remember to return to default setting (with another
“gsize’) before the footnote ends.

User Definitions

With the command “define” you can make any string stand for any other,

whether the latter is a symbol, command, or even complex construction; this
can save on retyping (and concomitant errors), as long as you define it
correctly in the first place. In essence the command is

define c’X’
where c is any number of characters and X is any component, preceded and followed by single quotes or any other punctuation lor character, for that matter,
as long as there are only two of them|.’ For example, you might establish ‘&’ as
a complex formula, like so:

define & ’sqrt { { SIGMA { x sup 2 } } over N }’
and then pull it out whenever it’s needed:

to get

N
As usual with such definitions, you must use care not to preempt useful symbols or commands or define something in terms of itself (for which see the eqn
User’s Guide).
The “define” command can be further subdivided into a “ndefine”, which
functions only for neqn/nroff, and a “tdefine”, which affects only eqn/troff.
The differences should be clear from examining the “eqnchar” definitions in
Appendix VII of this book, which shows how many of the special characters are
built.

eqn in -me and -ms
Both -me and -ms offer a few improvements to eqn, though in many
instances (particularly when you create a separate file just for equations),
these improvements may not justify the cost in extra processor time. All occur
on the level of the bracketing command “.EQ/.EN” and concern either the
placement of the equation on the page or the title which is to appear on the
right. For placement, -me and -ms will accept the following:

.EQ C
.EQ I
.EQ L
In -me, the amount of indent is saved in register “\n(bi”; in -ms, the indent
can be controlled by an external “.po” command.

9An excellent discussion of the complexities of the “define” can be found in Carmela Scrocca’s “New Graphic Symbols
for EQN and NEQN,” cited in note 1.

144 Equations leqn in -me and -ms|

Macros

The other feature which -ms and -me offer is a provision for labeling
equations, by means of a second ‘argument’ to the “.EQ”. The label will be
right-justified in the margin, displayed exactly as you have entered it (if it
contains spaces, you must precede them with backslashes, or ‘quote’ the entire
label):

.EQ I (6.4.1)
.EQ L (example)
EQ C (3.\ 4)
EQ C "(3. 4)"
Since -ms and -me expect either ‘C,’ ‘I,’ or ‘L’ for the first argument, the following ploy won’t work:

EQ” (3.4)
Not only is this a practical way to label equations, but we can have them
automatically incremented with number registers:
nrX0O1l
.EQ I \n+X

(Only one backslash is necessary.) This should save renumbering if you decide
to move the equations around. And of course defined strings may be included:
.ds Z Example
Dr AO

.EQ I \*Z.\n+X
If the string contains a space, the space will need to have a backslash preceding it:

.ds Z Chapter\ 1
soe >< O) 1
.EQ I \*Z.\n+X

If the string were to have a double quote instead |“"Chapter 1’’], two
backslashes would be needed before the “*Z”.
Finally, -me allows an argument to be appended to the “.EN”, indicating
that more equations are to come:

.EN C
This has the effect of ‘keeping’ the next equation with the previous one, so that
if the two don’t fit together on a page, they will both be printed at the top of
the next page.

FOOTNOTES

General Considerations

Footnotes represent one of the more ambitious and important challenges
to the capabilities of a text formatter. In nroff/troff, both the -me and -ms
macro packages generate true footnotes, and their various functions are
described in the following pages; these preset macros work well in almost all
circumstances, and both exhibit considerable flexibility in details such as footnote line length, spacing, and automatic numbering. Should you wish to know
how those macros work or to create your own for a specialized purpose, this
section begins with a step-by-step analysis. You should recognize, however,
that the footnote procedure is one of the more complicated in the system, and
that the analysis does not cover such situations as a text with multiple
columns. All of the footnote macros discussed below —packaged or otherwise—
will expect the notes to be entered immediately below the cited texts in the
source file; in order to distinguish the note from the text, all also expect you to
‘bracket’ the note with beginning and ending commands. The roffs will then
print those notes at the bottom of the page, spilling them over to the next page
if necessary. To do this, all three macros utilize the diversion command
(“di Xx”’], defined fully in the section on ‘keep’ macros; a diversion moves text
into a named storage area (here ‘Xx’) until called.

A footnote macro will almost certainly use the environment-switching
command “.ev N” as well; this supplies a completely independent set of formatting commands for specialized processing. It is not as complicated as it sounds,
and it should be understood before stepping into footnotes. You may define a
new environment by inserting the command with a number [either 1 or 2] as
its ‘argument’ before the other commands; an “.ev” by itself returns to the
environment in effect before. Until you redefine that environment, it will
retain those parameters no matter what has been specified for the text. This
makes it useful in macros like those controlling the header and footer, in

145

Macros

146 Footnotes [General Considerations]
which a local change in text line length or indentation must be ignored.
is an illustrative example in a running header macro:

Here

.de He
.ev 1
._ps 12
if t .vs 14

91;
tl ’Executive Mansion”
eV

.wh 0 He
troff will print the header ‘Executive Mansion’ in twelve-point type, fourteenpoint vertical spacing, boldface; the environment command guarantees that
these conditions will not affect the current parameters of the text itself. Once
again, the “.ev” without a number then returns to current formatting (an
action called ‘popping the environment’). The advantage is that switching
back to the regular text does not require special commands such as were
needed by some of the headers in that section because of the risk of returning
to an unanticipated format. A further advantage is that any subsequent macros can access this same environment simply by requesting that number. The
“ev 2” allows you to stipulate an environment within an environment, and if
you're curious, I would recommend reading the appropriate sections in the
Introduction.

Since the footnote macros tend to be complex, the following discussion
will be divided into its constituent parts, based on the analysis of footnotes in
the Nroff/Troff User’s Manual, which contains an extensive footnote sequence in
the appended tutorial (pp. 29-30). Though both -me and -ms improve slightly
on this macro sequence, the essential logic is precisely the same, and that logic
is most obvious in the Manual version.
Footnotes in nroff or troff take advantage of diversions to store text and
environments to put them in an appropriate format—usually a point size and
vertical spacing smaller than the text and is usually in fill and adjust mode. A
basic footnote sequence would, then, naturally contain four parts: (1) a macro
to begin, (2) the note itself, (3) a macro to end it, and (4) a means to call the
note out of storage at the bottom of the page. When it is called, it must be
returned in a no-fill mode so as to guarantee that the note will be unchanged
from its original processing in its special environment. Below is a highly simplified depiction (good only for a short note on a single page) which demonstrates the sequence as you would create it for your text. Defined first are the

two footnote macros: 1) the footnote begin [“.Fb”], which will divert the text
into a holding area [“.Fh”] in a specific environment [“.ev 1”], characterized by
a number of formatting commands;

and 2) the footnote end [“.Fe”], which will

use the break [“.br’’] to ensure that all of the note text has been included, then
return to the original environment and end the diversion.
are placed at the beginning of the text:

Again, these macros

.de Fb
.di Fh
\"divert note into storage location “Fh”
.ev 1 \"the footnote will be in an independent environment

Before we start congratulating ourselves on this, we should recognize that the
sequence given above is extremely limited in three categories: 1) it doesn’t
allow for multiple notes on a page; 2) a note of more than one line would produce bizarre results; and 3) there is no provision for splitting, saving and
retrieving on the next page that note which is too large for the present page (a
condition called ‘overflow’).

Multiple Notes
With a little help from the Manual, you can easily solve the first problem,

that of multiple notes.

You simply use the divert/append command [“.da Xx”’],

which, like the simple divert, creates a storage place if none exists; the two
commands are different, however, in that while “.di” overwrites the previous
contents of the storage area, “.da” merely adds to it:
.de Fb
.da Fh
\"divert and add note into storage location ‘Fh’
ev 1
._ps 8
if t.vs 9
Jl 4.1251

Macros

148 Footnotes [Multiple Notes]

This means, however, that the storage macro ‘Fh’ [‘FN’ in the Manual ] will
have to be cleared after it is finally called at the bottom of the page, or the
notes from page to page will simply keep incrementing and dumping their nox-

ious contents over and over on subsequent text. You can clear the storage area
by having the footer remove it after the macro has done its job.
.de Fo
ev 1
nf
Fh

rm Fh

__\"see how easy?

fi

.eV
bp
.wh -li Fo

The Manual places the “.rm Fh” immediately after the call for “.Fh”, as does
-ms; -me, however, separates invoking and removing the storage macros.
Again, this construction is extremely limited, but it is a start in the right
direction.

Fitting the Footnotes on the Page
A far greater challenge with multiple notes is to have them all appear on
the page. Clearly here you need to keep moving the trap up each time a new
note is encountered, so that when the footer is invoked there'll be plenty of
room for the footnotes (if the formatter is in the middle of a note when it
springs the trap, it’s in overflow and will have to deal with that separately).
This in part involves measuring the vertical size of the notes stored so far and
then using the change-trap command “.ch Xx M” [in form, the reverse of
“wh” in order to keep redefining the trap that springs the footer. You accomplish this in four steps. First, in the macro defining the header, you would
establish a number register, the value of which is equal to the ordinary footer
trap (typically, in an eleven-inch page, either ten inches from the top or minus
one from the bottom); [the number register will be called ‘Yy,’ corresponding to
the Manual’s ‘y,’ set as a minus number]. The example below uses a shorter
header macro, since it’s only necessary to see where ‘Yy’ fits in. I will also fol-

low the Manual in defining ‘y’ with another number register, ‘b’ [in the examples below, “Bb’]; right now it’s only convenient, so that you can control two
different variables with a single value (this is always good procedure). Later,
the ‘Bb’ will be a necessity.
.de He
’sp 2
tl ’”’ James Conkling’
tl ”’Union, %’
’sp 3

nr Yy 0-\n(Bb
nr Bb li
.wh 0 He

Macros

Footnotes |Fitting the footnotes on the page] 149
The number register ‘Yy’ is set at “0-\n(Bb” [minus one inch] rather than simply “-\n(Bb” because the latter is understood by the roffs as a command to
decrement ‘Yy’ by ‘Bb’s inch [minus one on page one, minus two on page two,
and so on]; the “0-\n(Bb” starts it all over again at a minus one. The second
step involves, as part of the footnote end macro [“.Fe”—‘“ef” in the Manual],
the vertical size of diversion, which is read from the number register ‘dn.’ This
read-only register has been silently keeping track of the vertical size of the
last diversion for just such purposes as these. ‘Yy’s’ minus value is increased
by this quantity, so now the trap is that much further up the page:
nr Yy -\\n(dnu \"‘units’ always a good idea
In the third step, the footer trap is changed to the new negative value of ‘Yy.’
So altogether the footnote end macro looks like this:
.de Fe
-br

eV
.di

nr Yy -\\n(dnu
ch Fo \\n(Yyu

Fourth and finally, the footer trap is set back to its original value in the
header, so that the next page doesn’t inherit the previous page’s trap. This is
easy enough to do:

.de He
"sp 2
tl ”’ James Conkling’
tl ”’Union, %’
’sp 3
nr Yy 0-\n(Bb
.ch Fo -\n(Bbu
-nr Bb li
.wh 0 He

The command “.ch” will come in extremely handy in subsequent discussion,
especially because it can ‘mask’ another trap by covering it.

Saving the Reference Line
Using a footnote macro involves a potential difficulty for the text itself,

arising from a characteristic which is generally found in any typesetting process: a line is not printed (‘output’) until it has been composed. Letters and
words are collected in a short-term storage area and placed on the page only
after an entire line has been assembled. Therefore if a note were called in the
middle of a line still being processed, that line (the ‘reference line’) would be
withheld while the macros diverted the footnote. The line would be printed
only after the last command of the footnote-end macro. Usually this causes no
problems, but if “.Fe” coincidentally moved the footer trap to the same position
as the reference line, then the trap would be sprung, interrupting the

150 Footnotes [Saving the Reference Line]

Macros

processing, and that line wouldn’t be printed until the next page. What is
clearly needed is first a test, then a change-of-trap for the footer macro, 1n
order to allow the line to be processed.
The test part involves two calculations: one to indicate how much of the
page is now text, and the other how much room the footnotes have already
claimed. The default scale will be vertical units (at least 1/144th of an inch).
To make the first calculation, you can rely on one of those other handy readonly number registers, ‘nl,’ which makes a cameo appearance as a measure of
the length of page processed so far. Since this has not yet counted the reference line, you should add one vertical space to ‘nl’; this will give you the vertical dimension of the entire text block thus far produced on the page:

(\\n(nl+1v)
For the second calculation, you need another read-only number register, ‘.p,’
the length of the entire page, from which the footnote area will be subtracted.
This amount is found in ‘Yy,’ but since this is already negative (since it indicates how much space up the page the footnotes now require), it will be added
tor-p

(\\n(.p+\\n(Yy)
For example, if ‘.p’ were 1,584 vertical units and ‘Yy’ were —600, the formula
would basically take this conversion: 1584+(-—600). A minus rather than a
plus would have the effect of rendering ‘Yy’ as a positive.

The two calculations are made and compared on a single line, as part of
an ‘if’ test:

if (\\n(nl+1v)>(\\nCp+\\n(Yy)
Usually the second half is larger than or at least equal to the first, so the

reference line will appear. But if the text block is larger than the allotted
page length, minus the footnote space, then the footer trap will have to be
lowered so as to make room for the reference line, and the closest position is
the first part of the calculation:

if (\\n(nl+1v)>(\\nCpt\\n(Yy)\{\
.ch Fo (\\n(nl+1v)\}
The footer can then call for all of the footnotes immediately after the reference
line has been printed.

The entire end-of-footnote macro as we have it thus far, therefore, looks
like this:
.de Fe
.br

ev
di

nr Yy -\\n(dnu
.ch Fo \\n(Yyu
if (\\n(nl+1v)>(\\n(p+\\n(Yy)\{\

.ch Fo \\n(nlu+lvu\}

Macros

Footnotes [Overflow] 151
Unfortunately, this still hasn’t solved the problem of overflow; in fact, it’s

made it worse, since adding a vertical space to accommodate the reference line
makes it extremely difficult to fit the combined notes on the page. But
courage.

Overflow

Overflow occurs when the last footnote in the diversion has proven to be
too large for the space remaining in the page. The macro given above would
simply take the excess material and print it at the top of a new page, then
obediently begin a next page of text. The Manual, -me, and -ms all solve this
by storing the part of the footnote that doesn’t fit in a temporary diversion

(called “fy”, “lo”, or “.FY’”], which is then included at the beginning of the
regular footnote diversion on the next page. (If there is no next page, the endof-text macro can force a new page in order to print overflowed footnotes.) The
process of detecting overflow, storing it, and printing it involves three basic
steps: 1) setting a ‘trap’ so that footnotes in overflow will be diverted automatically; 2) establishing the temporary diversion; and 3) inserting a checking
mechanism within the header which will channel the temporary diversion into
ordinary footnote storage. Each of these will be discussed in turn.

The overflow trap
A functioning overflow trap depends on two characteristics of the trap
command “.wh”: (1) that it can be ‘covered’ and ‘uncovered,’ and (2) that if
uncovered it will spring no matter what else is going on. ‘Covering’ is the process of setting a trap for a particular macro at a vertical position on a page,
and then at the same position installing a trap for another macro, the second
on top of the first, as it were. You accomplish this not by setting two “.wh’s”
at this position (since the second would erase the first), but rather by placing
the second at some fictitious point and then using “.ch” to move it, masking
the first, like so:
.wh 10i Of
.wh 16i Fo
.ch Fo 10i

The first macro |‘‘.Of” will function only if the trap for the second macro is
changed again, through the “.ch” command; otherwise it will remain covered.
The second characteristic of a trap, that of springing no matter what else
goes on, works in this way: your footnote-end macro moves the footer trap up
to a position, thereby uncovering the overflow macro, should the text ever reach
it. This is an important point, and to explain it I should review our footer
macro thus far:
.de Fo
ev 1
nf

.Fh
rm Fh
fi
.eV
bp

ah -\n(Bbu Fo

Macros

152 Footnotes [Overflow]

We now prepare for overflow by putting the footer at some wildly improbable

location, setting a trap for an overflow macro at the bottom of the page, then

changing the footer trap to cover it:
.wh 16i Fo

wh -\n(Bbu Of
.ch Fo -\n(Bbu

If your footnote-end macro “.Fe” did not allocate sufficient storage area for the
footnotes, the footer macro “.Fo” will be invoked as soon as possible; as the
notes are being printed, that is, while still at the “.Fh” step above, the excess
line or lines will spring the now uncovered trap at the old footer position; the
macro “.Of” can then redivert the excess footnote into a new storage location.
If, on the other hand, there has been enough space, the footer macro will begin
a new page before reaching the “.Of” trap. There is one potential problem: in
an exceptional case, the footnote processing might end on the line immediately
before the overflow is to begin, and a false signal would then be sent to the
overflow macro, but this can be provided for (provision below).

The overflow macro

The overflow macro (here “.Of”, “.fx” in the Manual) is simple enough,

since all it does is channel the excess note into another macro [“.En”—.“fy” in
the Manual|:
.de Of
.di En

No environment commands are necessary, since the footer macro has already
stipulated the footnote environment when it retrieved the notes from “.Fh”
[“.FN” in the Manual |. When all the overflow has been diverted, processing
returns to the footer macro, which utilizes an ‘if’ test to check for overflow and,
if there has been one, end the diversion. The test relies on one of the read-only
registers you thought you’d never use, the ‘.z,’ which stores the name of the
last diversion (not the macro, mind you, but the storage area itself). Using the
quotation-mark |"] option of ‘if, which compares strings to see if they are
identical, you can test for an overflow, and if so end it:

.de Fo
.ev 1

nf
Fh
rm Fh
if "\\n(z"En" .di
fi
eV
’bp

This test is quite necessary, since otherwise, the processor would be completel
y

confused about why you wanted to end a diversion that hasn’t started yet.

will itself invoke the ‘footnote begin’ macro, print the overflow text macro

[“.En”], and cease operation with the footnote end [‘.Fe”].

The Manual per-

forms the test by checking for the existence of the register which keeps track

.de He

Controlling the Overflow Trap
Both problems are of the same order as the one which did not allow for
the reference line to be printed: traps can be sprung too early and lines processed too late for material to be placed where it ought to be. In the case of
the overflow trap, it is possible to spring it even though all of the footnote text
has been printed, if it ends on the line just before. The Manual, -me, and -ms
use a footnote counter macro to prevent this, as well as to perform a number of
other housekeeping tasks. In the Manual, this counter is number register ‘x’

[here ‘Xx’], and it is set to zero at the beginning of each page, within the
header (or new page) macro:
.de He
nr Xx 0 1 \" ‘Xx’ reset to zero at each new page

Here ‘Xx’ has been set at zero, with the additional specification that each time

it’s incremented [with a “\n+(Xx”], a value of 1 will be added to it. It doesn’t
matter where in the footnote sequence “.Fb/.Fe” the “\n+(Xx” appears, though
it should be early, like so!:
Fb
.da Fh
ev 1

nr Xx \\n+(Xx
fi

The Manual makes the increment part of an ‘if’ test for printing a line before
the footnotes:
.de Fb
.da Fh
.ev 1

if \\n+(Xx=1 .Fl

Once ‘Xx’ has been established as greater than zero, the processor knows
that footnotes are present, and ‘if’ tests can blossom; such flags occur in the
interests of both speed and efficiency, since unneeded operations can still cause
trouble if they’re not insulated from other functions in some way. And this is
precisely how the overflow macro is controlled. As soon as the footer macro
has finished its printing of the notes and has ended any diversions, the ‘Xx’ is
no longer necessary as a counter and can be set to zero:

rm Fh

This should keep the overflow diversion from butting in where it’s not wanted.

Uncertainty Correction
There remains a problem which the Manual is kind enough to call an
‘uncertainty, concerning the last line of the notes. It works in a fashion analogous to the solution to not printing the reference line, discussed above: ‘Yy’
[measuring the size of footnote storage] is made slightly larger, so that the
overflow trap is not prematurely sprung on the last line of the footnote. In the
Manual, this ‘uncertainty correction’ is calculated as the difference between the
vertical spacing in the text itself (found in number register ‘.v’), and the vertical spacing in the footnotes (also found in ‘.v, but this time in the footnote
environment), stored in number register ‘Zz’ |‘z’ in the Manual]. The footnote-

counter register ‘Xx’ is tested to see if this is the first note (since the fudge is

not needed repeatedly on a page, just once), and then enough vertical space is
added to the footer trap to process the line. In the example below (here given
with the UNIX editor’s line numbers for reference),

Line seven is sufficiently complicated to require division into its logical parts:
1) The footnote counter ‘Xx’ is tested for its first appearance. 2) The value of
number register ‘Yy’ is both preserved and expanded by the minus-sign which
appears before the parenthesis; without that sign, the number register would
have been completely reset to the value in the parenthesis. It is important to
remember that ‘Yy’ is a negative number, and as such is being increased (the
footer trap moved up a little on the page) so that there will be room for the
last of the footnotes. 3) The distance is the text vertical spacing minus the
footnote vertical spacing (in number register ‘Zz’); this yields the difference,
which as a negative increases ‘Yy.’ Interestingly, both -me and -ms call fora
slightly larger fudge factor: -me uses “.V” (the ‘available vertical spacing’ on a
line), and -ms “.v” itself.

Footnotes {Footnotes in -me] 159

Footnotes in -me

True to form, -me offers a wide number of formatting options within its
basic footnote sequence. That sequence is straightforward enough: “.(f” begins
a note, and “.)f” ends it:
put twenty thousand men in motion at once
for the Shenandoah *

Ch
*McDowell had been meant to join McClelland
on the Peninsula.
i
As I have noted above, -me allows for reference lines, late and early triggering,
as well as multiple notes, sufficient storage, and overflow.
The options are indeed extensive:
font: \n(ff (default roman =1)
size: \n(fp (default eight point)
space between: \n(fs (default .2v)
first line indent: \n(fi (default 3n)

indent from margin: \n(fu

(default zero)

It is possible, then, to control the footnote format from the first five lines of
the text:
nr
nr
nr
nr
nr

tf Z
fp 9
fs lv
fi 6n
fu 3n

The separator is a macro defined as “.$s’, defaulting to a line an inch-and-ahalf (or two inches, depending), so it can also be reset easily:
.de $s
Nia
.sp

It also by default adds a third of a vertical space in nroff, to keep the superscripted numbers clear.
-me originated the feature of automatic note numbering which -ms has
since adopted, in the same form but with a slightly different approach. Instead

of inserting numbers in the text, one merely places a “\**” after the cited
material, like so:

the Radical Republicans.\**
Abe
\«* Those not supporting the Wade- Davis bill.
Jf
The documentation suggests that the numbers can be begun at any figure by
redefining number register “\n($f” like so:

Macros

160 Footnotes |Footnotes in -me]
nr $f 26

(after initialization, of course). But I found I had to change two defined
strings in the macros to make it work, from this:

.ds * \*[{1\*]\k*
to this:

.ds * \*(\\n($f\*)\k*

These numbers can be superscripted, with a “\u\**\d”, or with the -me super-

script ‘“\*[\**\*]”.
In columned output, the footnote length is set to the width of the column
and placed below the column referenced.
Finally, there is some provision for checking if a footnote beginning has
been called without an end, and vice versa, a common enough problem. But
this should be supplemented with safeguards which do not rely solely on the
processor, which is a dangerous habit.

Footnotes

in -ms

The footnote process in -ms is fairly reliable, resembling that in -me in
most respects. The footnote is typed immediately after the word that is being
noted, and the computer automatically places the note (with any others) at the

bottom of the page. The beginning and end are signaled by a “.FS” (for ‘footnote start’) on a line all by itself, and a “.FE” (for ‘footnote end’) after, again
on a line all by itself.

So a typical note would look like this:

Congress seemed entirely preoccupied with re-election.*
FS
*This seems to have been a problem peculiar to
Lincoln’s time.
.FE
By the spring of 1863,

It is not absolutely necessary to have the footnote follow the referenced word
immediately, but it is a good idea, just in case the reference just happens to be
the last word on the page.
One disadvantage -ms has in comparison to -me is that it does not
automatically supply a space between the separating line and the note, which
causes a slight problem in superscripting; one solution is to provide a space
[“.sp”] or one of the paragraph macros (provided you haven’t set the
paragraph-gap register ‘PD’ to zero):
Rumors of assassination were rife.*
FS
.PP

*Pinkerton received anonymous notes daily.
.FE

The regular paragraph macro is not only necessary for the space, but it also
makes a pleasant indent.

Macros

Footnotes [Line Length and Other Parameters in the -ms Footnote] 161

Line Length and Other Parameters in the -ms Footnote
In -ms, the footnote line will be slightly shorter than the text line

(default 11/12ths of ‘LL’); if you would like it shorter or longer, you can redefine it with the command
nr FL M

the M standing for the length you desire in inches, or whatever.
be inserted before the text has been initialized.

This should

The other parameters for footnotes in -ms have been set at initialization:
point size is the text size minus two; vertical spacing is also minus two [so that
a 10/12 text will default to 8/10]; footnotes are automatically justified; line

spacing is one; and the font is roman. These can be changed in at least two
ways: the first is a little easier, though with the disadvantage that one might
forget the changes in moving from file to file; the second is more clumsy, but it
can be put in a commonly accessible file. If there’s just one change, the former
is best; you simply include the change within the first “.FS”. This has the
effect of permanently resetting the footnote environment. To switch to noadjust, for example, you would insert the “.na” command like so:
FS

na
\ul1\d Major Robert Anderson.
FE

A second approach is to create an empty footnote after the initialization but
before the text; this footnote can predefine all the parameters you would like:

.GO
FS
na

\"initialization

_ps 9
jf t .vs 11

nr PS 9
ift mr VS 11
£2
FE
ce
III. Chicago and the Convention
Those first nine lines can then be tucked into a file |‘starter,’ for example], and
accessed by other texts:

.so starter \"go to source file ‘starter’
.ce
IV. The Campaign, 1860

Though most of the time this isn’t necessary, it’s nice to have the control.

162 Footnotes [Automatic Footnote Numbering in -ms |

Macros

Automatic Footnote Numbering in -ms

-ms automatically places footnote numbers in sequence within your text
and at the beginning of the note if you put a backslash and two asterisks [\**|
at the end of the reference, just before the “.FS”:
but McClellan’s popularity with the troops has been
exaggerated.\**
FS
In the 1864 election, for example, Lincoln received

116,877 votes from the Army, as opposed to McClellan’s
33,748.
.FE
Notice that a corresponding ‘\**” was not needed in the note itself; in most
versions of -ms, the same number is produced at the beginning of the footnote.
If yours does not, simply insert “\n*” after each “.FS”:

George G. Meade,\**
FS
\n* 1815-1872.
.FE
commanding general at Gettysburg
You should insert a space between the invoked number register and the footnote itself, adding a period or other punctuation if you wish.

Numbering Footnotes
If you do not have access to -ms, or if by chance you have no automatic
numbering, you can define the number register and dependent string easily;
you would insert the following at the beginning of the text:
mr*

01

.ds * \\n+*

The first is a simple register, the second an incrementing call. In the text
itself, the reference line and note number would be tagged just as above:
Salmon P. Chase,\**
FS
\n* 1808-1873.
.FE
probably never deserved
Any character can function for the definition, though it’s always a good idea to
check the macro package to see if it is already being used.

Macros

Footnotes |Resetting the Number] 163

Resetting the Number
If you need to start at a number other than one, you may define the
number register ‘*’ at any time before you call for the footnote. Remember,
though, to set ‘*’ at a value one lower than the number you need. If, for example, you were to start on note number 27, you would redefine the register like
so:
nr * 26

You can of course manually insert a number if you wish, and these numbers

can be superscripted (see below).

Superscript Numbers
-ms may not have guessed exactly what it was you wanted in terms of
superscripting the footnote numbers, so you may have to experiment with various options. The reference number can be scripted easily, in either of these
ways:

William H. Seward,\u\**\d
or

William E.

.SU Seward, \**
But the number within the note represents a problem.

One way to handle this

is to avoid ‘*’ altogether, and define your own counter [here ‘X’] and numberregister strings |‘+’ increments, ‘#’ does not]:
nr X:0i1
.ds + \\u\\n+X\\d

.ds # \\u\\nX\\d
William H. Seward,\*+
FS

\*x# 1801-1872
.FE

For fancier typesetting, you may borrow the definition for the “.SU” superscript macro:
nrX01

ds + \s—3\v’—.6m’\\n+X\v’ +.6m’\s0
ds # \s—3\v’—.6m’\\nX\v’ +.6m’\s0
Just as in ‘*,’ to start with a number other than one, you would define it as
one lower with a “.nr X N11”.

164 Footnotes |Safeguarding Footnotes]

Macros

Safeguarding Footnotes

The most common mistake in entering footnotes is forgetting to close a
note, and the second most common is remembering to close it but forgetting to
open it. Both -me and -ms have means of detecting footnotes within footnotes,
and various other obvious errors, but it’s good policy to check them yourself, if
only because you might not be in a position to rectify an error if you have to
wait for the processor. A quick way to see if you have at least the same

number of “.FS’s” as “.FE’s” is to use the counting option [‘-c’] in the UNIX

searching utility grep (here shown with the UNIX ‘$ prompt and the returnkey signaled as a <ecr> ):

$ grep -c "\.F" chapter3<cr>
Within the editor itself, a global search of all lines beginning with ‘.F’ (using
the same notation as the grep script above) allow you to scan them, though
more than twenty notes will probably make you dizzy.

What If They Don’t Want Footnotes
Some publishers, even those who claim to have had ‘experience’ with troff
files, will demand that the footnotes be collected in one location, so that the
staff can either position them at the end of a chapter, or paste them in by
hand. If you’ve written your own footnote macro, you need only disarm the
various mechanisms for measuring the size of the diversion and for printing
and removing the footnote storage macro, and simply invoke that macro at the

end of the text.

In -me, you would globally change all “.(f/.)f’ commands to

““(d/.)d” (the ‘delayed text’); then, after the text has finished, the contents of
this diversion can be printed with a “.pd” on its own line.

In -ms, unless you’re willing to tamper with the package itself, you must
write the footnotes into a new file, and then remove them from your text. This
need not be a burden, thanks to the special capacities of the UNIX editors. In
ed, edit, ex, or vi, you may establish a new, empty outside file and then write
all the footnotes out to it, like so:
:!touch outsidefile<er>
!

:g/\.FS/,/"\.FE/w> > outsidefile<er>

The ‘touch’ creates the file, and the write command serially appends to it. Or,
you may use the stream editor sed; like grep this works outside the file, and
again like grep it shares much of its syntax with the UNIX editors. To copy
out the footnotes from a text file into another file, you type, after the shell
prompt:

$ sed "/"\.FS/,/"\.FE/p<er>
> //d" <Chapter3> footnotefile<er>
(The shell provides the ‘greater-than’ sign at the beginning of line two.) This
will not delete the footnotes in the chapter; to do that, you can in one of the
editors use this global command:

Macros

Footnotes | What If They Don’t Want Footnotes) 165
:g/\.FS/,/\.FE/d<er>
In the footnote file itself, the “.FS’s” and “.FE’s” will have to be removed, but
before doing that you should use edit or ex to substitute in a space and the
automatic counter:

:g/°\.FS/s//&\<er>
.sp\<er>
\\**/<er>
In ed the command would be

:1,$8/"\.FS/&\<er>
.sp\<er>
\\**/<er>
In vi you would need to type a capital ‘Q’ to change to ex, and then type the
global command above.

HEADERS AND FOOTERS

Running Heads and Page Numbers: Preliminaries
Headers and footers are the indicative titles appearing at the top and bottom of the page; they typically contain page numbers, chapter names, and possibly the date. Both -me and -ms offer efficient header and footer options, and
these will be discussed towards the end of this section. But—especially if
you're the adventurous type—I do recommend that you peruse the first few
paragraphs; the macros are as simple as they are gratifying, and they may
allow you much greater freedom and power in processing text the way you
would like it.

Experimenting with headers and footers is in fact an excellent way to
learn how macros work; the main reason is that headers and footers do what

macros do best —in this case, appear automatically and keep track of the pagination at the same time. The automatic part utilizes the most common of the
three ‘traps’ discussed in the Introduction, the “.wh”. All it really does is electronically mark some vertical position on the typewritten or typeset page in
order to invoke a macro; when nroff or troff processes that line number or
absolute measurement, then the macro is called. The page-numbering part is
even easier, since the roffs have set aside a number register that keeps track of
the pages, designated by the string “\n%”. It is possible, as you'll see, to substitute Roman for Arabic numbers, or even use an alphabetic numbering system.

166

Macros

Headers and Footers |Nroff (and Some Troff)| 167

Nroff (and Some Troff)

To create a header, first you would define your macro [‘He’ below], and
insert, after the definition, a “.wh M Xx” (in which Mis the position of the
header ‘trap’ and Xx is the macro name). The most common setting fora
header trap is 0, since you want the header to come before any text has been
processed. The macro itself can decide the precise location of the header or
footer on the page (for example, two spaces down from the top or three spaces
up). The following sequence, which works best in nroff, establishes a centered
page header on line three, followed by three blank spaces before the text
begins:
.de He
"sp 2
tl ’Page %”
’sp 3

.wh 0 He
The title command “tl” understands the percent-sign in itself as the current
page number; the ‘\n’ is not necessary.! Again, the two periods on the last line
must be entered exactly as you see them here; they signify the end of the
definition, and without them the roffs assume that the rest of the file is an
extension of the macro. Finally, the macro must utilize the apostrophe rather
than the period for both ‘sp’ commands, in order to avoid leftover text; if you
tried it with the “.sp”, you’d find spare words hovering before and after the
header.

“tl” is not necessary for the macro to accept the text; a string placed in
that position would appear as a left-justified header. Quite apart from the con-

vention with page numbers, however, the advantage to using “.tl” for headers
and footers is that it allows you to spread the text over the top or bottom of the
page. If, for example, you wanted your name in the top left corner of the page,
and the pagination top right, it would be a simple matter of placing the
material so:
tl ’James H. Hackett’%’

If you would like the header placed more to the left or right, you may add
spaces like so:
tl ’\ \ Page %’”
or a horizontal movement

[\h’M’, in which Mis a given distance]:

tl $\h’1i’Page %$$$
For this example, the dollar-sign delimiters were necessary because of the apostrophes needed for the horizontal command.

IThe roffs have already defined the percent sign as the current page through the page character command, pec,
which cis the character. Other characters may be used, but ‘167’ always remains the page number register.

10

Macros

168 Headers and Footers |Footers]

Footers

Footers behave in exactly the same way as headers, so there’s no need to
discuss them extensively. In general, it’s a good idea to define the header and
footer together, since occasionally they depend on each other. The footer trap,
of course, will be set at some line or absolute space towards the bottom of the
page:
.de Fo
’sp 3
tl "Page %”

’bp

.wh 57 Fo

After the ‘Page %,’ the ‘’bp” insures that no further text will appear on the
page. The ‘“.wh” can also be defined negatively, measuring from the bottom of
the page, so that the above command could have been written “.wh —9”.

Multiple Lines

In designing your own header and footer macros, you are not limited to
any specific number of lines of text. (If you’re using -ms, the header and footer
macros without adjustments limit you to two, as will be described.) A header of
the following sort would work just as well as the one given earlier:
.de He
‘sp 2
tl ’’ James C. Conkling’
tl ’’The Union’
tl’ Page %’
’sp 2
.wh 0 He
In nroff this definition will do splendidly, creating a header of seven lines,
with three text lines flush right.

Alternate Page Numbers: Roman, Alphabetical
Roman numerals or continuously sequenced alphabetics la,b,c ... aa,bb,cc ...
aaa,bbb,ccc] are available should you need them. You merely reset the “af”
[for alternate format |command to your desires. The following are all of the
options now available (here given with the ‘%’ register for pagination):

af % 1 \"arabic numerals [default |
af % 001

\"arabic numerals in at least three digits

af %i \"lower-case roman
af %I \"upper-case roman
.af % a \"lower-case alphabetics

.af % A \"upper-case alphabetics
Any one of these commands can be placed in the header or footer macro:

Macros

Headers and Footers [Alternate Page Numbers: Roman, Alphabetical] 169
.de He

sp 2
af %i

tl’ J. W. Fell’
tl’ Preface, %’
’sp 3

This will place roman numerals after the comma throughout the preface.

The First Page
If you plan to run a header on the first page and follow it immediately
with a title in the text, you should precede the “.tl” of the text title with a
“br”, or, if that title is to be centered, use the “.ce” rather than “tl”. As you
may remember, both “.br” and “.ce” cause a break but “.tl” does not; the roffs,
however, need either a break or text itself to begin processing, so that the
absence of one produces strange page numbers and text allocation. This seldom
becomes an issue, especially since headers are usually not wanted on the first
page, but in this case it is important to make the necessary alterations. Here
is an example of a centered title for page one:

.de He
’sp 2
tl ’Page %”
’sp 3
.wh 0 He
.ce
First Message
sp 4

tl To the Congress, July 4, 1861”
tl ’in Special Session”
tl ’by”
tl ’Abraham Lincoln”
As you can see, other titles using “.t]” can appear immediately after the first
centered title.
In most text formatting, however, the first page is given either a different header or no header at all. There are two ways to accomplish this. The
one you were probably thinking of is to place the definition and its accompanying trap at some point deep in the text of the first page, so that the roffs will
only pick up on the header after the processor has crossed the trap position on
page one. This would work, but three problems arise: 1) if you put the definition too far down in the text, there is the chance that it would affect page
three, not page two; 2) it’s generally not a good idea to place macro definitions
within the text, where they can be easily overlooked in proofreading; and 3)
the titles on page one will now start on line one, so that a “.sp 1i” will have to

be inserted before the titles.

A better solution—better because it not only

avoids those problems but offers flexibility in formatting page one—would be to
make use of the ‘if’ test in creating the macro, like so:

Macros

170 Headers and Footers |The First Pagel
.de He

’sp 2
if \\n%>1 .tl "Page %”
’sp 3
is 0 He
This flexibility comes in handy when you wish to substitute something like

tl ROUGH

DRAFT’

as a right header on page one. You need only extend the “if” as an “.ie/.el”:
ie \\n%>1 .t] "Page %”
.el tl ” ROUGH

DRAFT’

Naturally, both of these lines can be multipled through the use of the ‘\{\’ and
ee

.de He
sp 2
ie \\n%>1\{\
tl Cuthbert Bullitt”
tl’ Interim Report’
tl ”’\n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr’

tl’’Page %\}

el \{\
tl ROUGH

DRAFT’

tl ”’\n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr’

sp 2\}
’sp l
.wh 0 He

This will put Bullitt’s name, the identification, and that day’s date flush right
on all pages after one, and ‘ROUGH DRAFT’ and the date on page one; in both
situations it will provide a seven-line header.

Even and Odd Pages
Both nroff and troff allow you to vary headers and footers on even and

odd pages, again using the ‘if’ test. To save you time ‘if’ understands ‘e’ and ‘o,”
so the test is an extremely straightforward matter:
.de He

sp 2
if e tl Chapter 1”
if o tl Constitution”
’sp 3
.wh 0 He

This is most handy in book production, in which running headers regularly

Macros

Headers and Footers [Even and Odd Pages] 171
differ left and right and the page numbers are customarily placed at the outer
edge. The following definition will give you the idea:

.de He
’sp 2
if e tl ’%’Chapter 1’Section 6
if o tl Section 6’Constitution’%’
’sp 3
.wh 0 He

On odd pages (usually the right page), the chapter identification is centered,
with the page number on the outer margin, and the section number in; on even
pages the two numbers are reversed and the different header appears.

Varying Headers
Often you need to have the header change to reflect a different subject,
for example, or different section numbers. You effect this by defining a string

[“.ds”] and redefining it when appropriate:
.ds Vh Horace Greeley
.de He
"sp 2
tle \\etVh%
’sp 3
.wh 0 He

Then, later in the text, the string “Vh” can be redefined to correspond to a
change of subject; if this is to begin on a new page, the sequence is quite simple:
.ds Vh John Soule

-bp
If the header is run on through the text, then you must estimate how far back
the redefinition will have to be placed in order to appear at the head of that
page, a problem avoided on the first page; you might estimate, or you might do
a preliminary run with nroff or troff (using the ‘-a’ option) on the lineprinter,
and insert the string definition safely before the page break. This should be
done shortly before final processing, since such definitions are often moved
when text blocks are shifted in editing.

Varying chapter and section numbers can appear in the running headers,
simply by requesting those number registers. In the example below, chapter
and section macros have been defined, both having numbers set at zero that
are increased each time the macro is invoked.” All the header macro needs to
do is utilize those registers as well:

2For details on chapters and sections, see the appropriate discussions in other parts of this Guide.

Macros

172 Headers and Footers [ Varying Headers]

nr X 01

\"chapter number

.de Ch
.bp
sp 21
CHAPTER \\n+X
nr Se 0 1 \"section number
sp li
.de Sh
sp 2
GIL
.ne 4

\fBSection \\n+(Se: \\*(Vh
.Sp
.de He

sp 2
tl Chapter \\nX. Section \\n(Se’\\*(Vh’%’
’sp 3
.wh 0 He

There is practically no end to the levels of numbering and format (arabic,
roman, alphabetic) available for chapters, sections, subsections, paragraphs,
subparagraphs. I would use discretion, however.

Troff (and Some Nroff)

Headers and footers for troff are as usual going to be a little more complicated, since there are so many more options. The above macros would usually work; but if, for example, a vertical spacing temporarily larger or smaller
is in effect when those macros are called, that condition will produce a header
or footer larger or smaller than the previous ones. To avoid this, you have two
choices: to set up an environment [using “.ev N’’] which would be insulated
from the surrounding text or to deal with the options one after the other.
Though for the sake of simplicity setting up an environment is preferable, I
will also describe the other so that you can have some sense of the controlling
factors.

Switching Environments

The “.ev N” command represents an escape to an entirely different set of
controls and can be terminated [returned to the previous environment] with a
“ev” by itself. You can, then, put the above header definition into environment one, for example, with no fear of contamination:
.de He
ev 1

’sp 2
af % i

tl’ James Conkling’
tl ’’Preface, %’

Macros

Headers and Footers [Switching Environments] 173
>

sp 3
eV

It is important to keep in mind that the values in a new environment, if not
otherwise specified, are those of default troff: 6.5 inches for line and title
length, 10/12 Roman font. If your page layout demands otherwise, you must
redefine these in the header or footer environment as well.

If, for instance, the

text line length is to be six and a quarter inches, you would probably want to
readjust the length of title command in the header:
.de He
ev 1

’sp 2
It 6.251
tl ’’ James Conkling’
tl ’’ Preface, %’
’sp 3
.eVv

If you use environment 1 in another macro definition, you should remember
that it now has the characteristics of its last invocation; normally this presents
no problem, as each macro redefines the environment for its own purposes, but
occasionally a font or indent command will slip through.
One more command should be added in order to secure the troff header
macro: the “.mk”, or mark. “.mk”, when followed by a character, will cause
nroff or troff to remember that vertical place in a specific register. A “.rt c”
(c representing the character) will send troff up to that marked place. In this
case, we need no character, because there will be only one situation in which a
return to the base of the header is necessary: multiple columns. When the
first column ends, a “.rt” will be necessary to find the top of the text block, and
so a mark after the header is needed. Setting a mark after we have exited
from the environment, then, gives us a reasonably complete macro:
.de He
.ev 1

"sp 2
It 6.251
._ps 12
.vs 14

|
tl ’’ James Conkling’
tl’ Union, %’
’sp 3

ev
mk

This, as I say, is the best way to handle headers and footers. But a great deal
can be learned from experimenting with the other options; we now turn to
them: line length, absolute space, and constant point size, vertical spacing, and
font.

Macros

174 Headers and Footers [Line Length]
Line Length

The first macros described in this section would not produce suitable

headers if different lengths of title [“.lt M”] had been defined in the text
proper; each header’s title would simply be the length of the last stipulation.
For example, if you were running a narrower text block which needed internal
titles, you might reset “lt” to shorten the width of those titles. A header
invoked while this different value is in effect would also be shorter and look
poor. To prevent this, you can at the head of your source file establish a
number register with the correct measurement for a header, then call for it
within the macro. The roffs will read the number register and perform to standard. The question then becomes how to revert to the current length-of-title
setting. Here you can assume that this value is the same as the current line
length, which nroff and troff have been kind enough to store automatically in
the read-only register ‘1,’ accessed as “‘\\n(.1”. The following, then, would protect a header in a sea of shifting title lengths:

nr Tn 6i

\" register ‘Tn’ given standard length

.de He

’sp 2
Jt \n(Tnu
\" set to standard title length
tl ’’ William Wallace Lincoln’
Jt \\nGlu \" return to current title length
’sp 3

Notice that it isn’t necessary to redefine ‘Tn’ every time, since it has been set
since the beginning, and therefore there is only one backslash for that number
register. But ‘]’ is always changing, so that must be read at the time when
the header macro has been invoked; hence you need to insert the two
backslashes.

Absolute Spacing

In troff, a different setting for vertical spacing [‘‘.vs”] will produce
different-sized spaces within the footer or header.

To overcome this, you can

specify absolute spacing [‘l’], so that the text will always appear in the same
place:

.de He

sp 1.333
tl ’Page %”
sp Ili
.wh 0 He
The footer would resemble this, with the second line reading, for example, ten

inches (“’sp 110i” or “’sp |—1i”), with the trap set at nine-and-a-half inches
(“.wh 19.51 Fo” or “.wh | -1.5 Fo”).

Macros

Headers and Footers [More Troff: Point Size, Spacing, and Fonts] 175

More Troff: Point Size, Spacing, and Fonts
As with varying line length, if in troff the header or footer contains a different point size, vertical spacing, or font, you will have to make some provision for a direct return to the parameters previously in place. Remember, it is
not a good idea to be too literal in your commands; if, for example, you’ve been
running 10/12, but you want a two-line italic header 12/14, the following may
seem to work, though in fact it is asking for trouble:
.de He

sp |.33i
._ps 12
.vs 14

al
tl ’’Congressional Report’
tl ”’\n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr’
._ps 10
.vs 12

iy
’sp 11.251
.wh 0 He
There’s just no guarantee that the text you just left at the bottom of the previous page was actually operating under normal conditions. It might have been
in the midst of italics, for example, or in a much smaller point size. Luckily,
just as with the current line length register ‘1,’ the roffs provide other registers for these values, registers which can always be accessed and stored before
the header, and retrieved after it. Registers for the three commands that we
have been discussing are
8 point size in effect
.v vertical spacing in effect
f font in effect

Before the header changes things, these values can be stored in temporary
registers and retrieved afterwards. The above example should be written, then,
as this:
nr Tn 6i
.de He

sp 1.33:
Jt \n(Tnu
nr Si \\n(s
nr Ve \\n(.v
cnr Ft \\n(f
._ps 12
.vs 14
JI

tl ’’Congressional Report’
tl ”’\n(mo/n(dy/19\n(yr’
.ps \\n(Si
.vs \\n(Veu
ft \\n(Ft
Jt \\n(lu

176 Headers and Footers |More Troff: Point Size, Spacing, and Fonts]

Macros

’sp 11.251
.wh 0 He
Again, the two slashes in the first and last parts of the macro are necessary to
obtain the current value of these registers.
The header macros I’ve described up to now have gotten progressively
longer, and they still could use some tuning on this issue of current parameters. In nroff and troff there is a little-used convention which will return to a
previous setting after any single change. When given by itself, without any
value, a number of commands automatically revert to their last setting. The
indent command can serve as an example:
Jn .5i
in li

in

\"return to half an inch indent

For the magic three we have been discussing, the commands are

.ps
.vs
ft
These will bring you back to the point size, vertical spacing, and font previously in place. Why bother, then, with all the number register business of the
previous example? Well, in fact, the reason the convention is little used is that
it’s difficult to keep track of what indeed has been previous. It would be dandy
to place the commands in the header, but you should seldom use them in the
text itself, because, quite simply, such a command subsequent to the header
would call out those settings, rather than the ones intended. For the example
given above it would be 12/14, italic. To insure the header desired as well as a
return to previous values, a three-step operation is necessary: store the current
values, invoke the default values, then recall the current ones. This is how it
looks, with lower-case ‘c’ meaning ‘current’ and lower-case ‘p’ standing for
“previous”:
nmr Sc \\n(s \"store size in effect
._ps \"call for previous size

nr Sp \\(.s \"store previous size
nr Vc \\n(.v \"store spacing in effect
.vs \"call for previous spacing

nr
nr
ft
nr

Vp \\(v_ \"store previous spacing
Fc \\n(f \"store font in effect
\"call for previous font
Fp \\(f \"store previous font

._ps 12
.vs 14
J

tl ’’Congressional Report’
tl’ Artemus Ward’
tl] ”’\n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr’
.ps \\n(Sp \"call previous size
.ps \\n(Sc \"call size in effect before header

.vs \\n(Vpu

\"call previous spacing

Macros

Headers and Footers [More Troff: Point Size, Spacing, ond Fonts| 177

.vs \\n(Vcu \"call spacing in effect before header
ft \\n(Fp \"call previous font
ft \\n(Fe \"call font in effect before header
This might not happen too often, but you should know how to adjust the macro
when it does.
The -me Solution

The -me package offers a number of macros which both simplify this a bit
and at the same time take care of housekeeping chores like resetting header or
footer length. In nroff -me, the header appears at about line four, the text, as
we have mentioned, beginning at line eight; similarly, the footer appears four
lines from the bottom, which has already been defined by the page length com-

mand “.pl”. The basic commands [“.he” and “.fo”] can be described briefly;
each utilizes the apostrophe system of “.tl” to place material left, center, or
right. To have a page number in the upper right corner, for example, one
should type:
che

IY

The “.fo” command is identical:

fo ”’%’\fI1Confidential\fR’
This will center the page number, and place ‘Confidential’ italicized in the
right corner. In troff, the point size of the header or footer can be manipulated
with the “\sN” convention, so that “fo ”\s14Draft Proposal\s0” will produce a
fourteen-point footer; as the example implies, the headers and footers will
return to the point size, vertical spacing in effect when the macro was called.

Neither “he” nor “.fo” easily understands “if” or “.ie/.el”, but -me has
taken care of the demand for alternating titles by offering header and footer
macros for odd and even pages:
.eh ”” \" even header
.oh ”” \" odd header
.ef ”” \" even footer
of ””’ \" odd footer

This seems a fairly manageable number.

Within the header, you are not limited to the vertical position -me has
decided for you. In its usual fashion, it has set aside four macros to control the
placement of headers or footers on the page.

It is useful to note that numbers by themselves following the macros are interpreted as vertical spaces (‘v’); any other unit of measurement will also function, but only if that unit is identified [“.m1 1i” for a space of one inch].

Many users, once they have learned these conventions, have been understandably reluctant to switch to the more complicated -ms or in-house macros.
But there are problems; for example, you will need to accept the fact that the
macros simply will not allow multiple lines without redefining a macro entitled
‘$h.’3 Since this voids the straightforward header and footer capabilities of -me,
it in effect begins to undercut its advantage.

The -ms Solution

As usual, -ms offers a wide and dazzling display of options, and as usual
there are sometimes hidden traps in the code. Like -me, a simple command is
offered you for placing texts in the left, center, or right top or bottom of a
page. These are the six choices:
Page Top

Bottom

.ds LH text
.ds CH text
.ds RH text

.ds LF text
.ds CF text
.ds RF text

The command to place a running head against the right top margin, beginning
with the next page, would look like this:
.ds RH Hannibal Hamlin, %
.LP

Again, what this means is you're defining the string (and again, a string is one
or more letters and spaces) in the right header as this text, with the appropriate page number. Like -me, these commands do not easily respond to ‘if’ or
‘ie/el’ tests, so you'll have to see below for the steps necessary to alternate
texts. -ms also keeps the first page clear of headers and footers, and this is
usually what you want, but there are ways to request these if necessary, for
which also see below.

In constructing headers or footers with -ms, early on you’ll want to decide
where to place page numbers; by default, -ms automatically paginates topcenter, with a hyphen/space on either side: “- 2 -”. To turn this off, you should
type this command:

3See the -me Reference Manual, p. 4.

Macros

Headers and Footers |The -ms Solution] 179

.ds CH
In effect, this clears the defined string by substituting nothing for the page
number. Then the page number can be placed, say, center footer with a
“ds CF %”.
For more complicated options, you'll need to redefine the top and bottom
of the page. This is not especially difficult, and it’s interesting to see the basic
definition that -ms uses. In their simple form, the macros ‘PT’ [page title]

.de PT

pe %

if \\n%>1

\"reverting to defined offset

A clear benefit to -ms is that both ‘PT’ and ‘BT’ are called at the end of a long
series of internal macros which are quietly turning off spacing, writing in the
cut lines for typesetting, and so forth. These are almost always elements
which are desirable, or at least not offensive, and they normally will not be
affected by a redefined header or footer macro. Before extensive redefinition,
however, I would reread the section on the cover page for a notion of initialization in -ms.
This should make it safer and easier for you to redefine your headers and
footers with a great deal of flexibility, though that doesn’t mean there can’t be

problems; I would recommend, for instance, that definitions for both the bot4Since -ms defines the page number in register ‘PN,’ this is the register which should be used for alternate formatting
