INDEXES

Indexes:

The Basics

One real benefit of text formatting is the ability to generate an index: to
tag words in a text with their page numbers for inclusion in either a Table of
Contents at the beginning or an index at the end of the completed document.
Both -me and many versions of -ms already offer macros that begin the process, establishing a list of the marked terms and their page numbers in sequential order.

The list may then be captured as a separate file, sorted, and modi-

fied with one of the UNIX editors. The following pages first describe simple
approaches—both non-macro and macro—for creating such lists; a section then
explains the macros found in -me and -ms; finally, a third section outlines the
routines for creating a true index.

General Considerations

Both the macro and non-macro techniques described below share a common feature: The term to be indexed is not printed in the text itself. Thus
there appears to be a certain unavoidable redundancy in both the non-macro:
If McClellan

tm McClellan, \n%
is not using the army, I should like to borrow it for a while.
and the basic macro:

182

Macros

Indexes |General Considerations! 183
If McClellan
iS
McClellan
iE
is not using the army, I should like to borrow it for a while.

This characteristic redundancy requires a little explanation. Though it is possible to mark particular terms within the text itself, there are sound reasons
for differentiating the item in context from the item as it will appear in the
index. In a surprising number of instances, the text term and the index term
tend to differ considerably in form. For example, your index might require the
following to fulfill its purpose:
Not so, however, General Stuart
tm Stuart, James Ewell Brown, General, CSA, \n%
and his staff

Similarly, an index often groups information under broader headings, headings
which are not found in the text itself:
with a shambling, loose, irregular, almost unsteady gait, a
tall, lank, lean man, considerably over six feet in height,
iS
Lincoln, appearance of
iE
with stooping shoulders, long pendulous arms

This is not to argue that the index cannot be edited after it has been produced;
in fact, it must be. But recognizing that a term in context and its equivalent
in the index are different will substantially aid the work.

Indexing with ‘‘.tm”
As was indicated above, the non-macro

indexing function uses the ‘termi-

nal message’ command (described in the ‘Basic Functions’ chapter and the section on ‘Memoranda’) to identify the index term:
tm Seward’s Folly,

\n%

This is not only easier to enter than the macro is, but it avoids a difficulty in
capturing the index as a separate, sortable file. To funnel the terms into such a

file (here ‘indexfile’) you request, at the end of your formatting command, that
the roffs direct any ‘error’ messages into the named file:
$ troff -w documentfile

2>indexfile<er>

As simple as this is, it isn’t always the best solution. There is, first of all, the
general inadvisability of using basic commands for any purpose other than that
originally intended; and, in my experience, two specific difficulties might arise:

1) In the rare but inevitable cases in which the roffs encounter problems, any
relevant error messages will also be sent into the index file; this will leave you
to wonder why processing has stopped or—and this is worse—why it hasn't

stopped.

2) In certain circumstances, it is possible for “.tm” to bring down the

Macros

184 Indexes |Indexing Macros|

system. This happened to me (twice) when my index term contained two or
three UC San Diego-defined umlauts.! The problem has since been solved, but
the lesson remains.

Indexing Macros
All indexing macros ‘bracket’; that is, they utilize beginning and ending
commands on separate lines, with the term situated between the two commands. In the examples given below, the commands will be “iS” lindex start]
and “.iE” |index end]; in -me the two are “.(x” and “.)x”, and “.XS” and “.XE”
in -ms. The accumulated list, moreover, is printed only when the formatter
reaches a specific command, called “.pI” in the simple macro, “.px” in -me, and
“PX” in -ms.
While the -me and -ms macros are extremely dependable in generating
lists, there are advantages to creating your own version. For one thing, the
packaged format may not be your own, and for another, the indexing macros
may be far more complicated than you need. The packages, for instance,
automatically create a row of periods the breadth of the page, from the index
item on the left to the page number on the right. To accomplish this, tabs are
reset, the line length changed, and various number registers and strings
defined and invoked. Though it is unlikely this will interfere with your own
tab and length settings, there is that possibility. But the overriding advantage
is still the greater measure of control that you will be able to exercise.

With either of two basic formatting commands, “.da” or “.am”’, it is a
fairly easy task to create lists of items followed by their page, chapter, section,
or paragraph reference. Each of these commands is discussed separately, so as
to afford you a choice when you construct your own macro. For the sake of
consistency, only the page number (number register “\n%”’) appears with the
item in these examples.
Index Macros

with ‘‘.da’”’

The command “.da” —divert/append—is described in the section on footnotes and, more generally, in the section on keeps. In essence, it diverts any
text which follows into a macro, where the text is stored until the macro is
invoked. The essential command is “.da Xx”, in which Xx is the macro name;

storing ceases when the formatter encounters a “.di” by itself, with no macro
name. Should “.da Xx” be used again, subsequent material will be added to
the storage macro; in this divert/append differs from a straight diversion—
“.di Xx”, which replaces the old contents with new. As the section on keeps
indicates, diversions most commonly appear with changes in the environment
command—“.ev

N”’; in the discussion below, the main function of the new

environment is to prevent unsightly and unwelcome breaks in the text proper.
The index-start macro is straightforward enough; there is a diversion to
macro ‘sI,’ a new environment, and an end:
.de iS
.da sI
.ev 1

‘Apparently the resultant string was longer than the 255-character limitation.

Macros

Indexes |Indexing Macros] 185
The index end is almost as simple. The current page number is automatically
added to the entry. Then a line-break command is needed to ‘flush’ the index
item, and both the environment

and the diversion end:

.de iE
\\n% \"two backslashes to ‘delay’ reading the number register
-br
.eVv

di

Finally, for the macro that will print the index, the storage macro is invoked in
no-fill mode:
.de pl
Tif
sl
fi

Frills can of course be added: by backing up a space, you might insert a comma
in the end macro, after the item and before the page number:

\h’-\w’ ’u’, \\n%
And various formatting commands may be added after the change to environment one, if necessary. Otherwise it’s a piece of cake.
Index macros

with ‘‘.am ”

The command “.am”—append to macro—also adds text to a storage macro,
named immediately after the command. It differs, however, in a third part,
which indicates the character or characters which end the addition (for this
last, a beginning of the line and a period are assumed). Therefore the command
NIN TOK xe

will direct subsequent text into macro ‘xX’ until it meets a line that contains
three periods. But for all its promise as a potentially useful command, “.am”
appears just once in the macro packages, and that is in -me’s index macro,
copied into many -ms packages (the entire sequence is reproduced in the -ms
indexing section some pages hence). The index macro given here is in fact only
a simplified version of that found in -me.

As with ‘.da, the index-start macro immediately begins appending
material to the storage macro ‘sI’; the difference is that “.am” will stop

appending when it meets a “iE” on a line by itself. A ‘fill’ command is needed
to guarantee that the page number is on the same line as the index item. In
the index end macro, ‘“.am” is again invoked to insert the page number; in this
case, an arbitrary three periods will terminate the append:
.de iS
.am slik
fi

Macros

186 Indexes |Jndexing Macros]
.de iE
am s1 ..
\h’-\w’

i:

\\n%

Because the entries have been stored one to a line in macro ‘sI,’ they will be
printed that way when the macro is called; so no special ‘pI’ macro needs to be
defined for emptying the contents of ‘sl.’ At the end of the document (or wherever), you may simply invoke it, like so:

fool all of the people all of the time.
sl

Because the two index macros created so far are simple, they are also vulnerable. It would be useful, then, to review the following paragraphs on the solution in -me and -ms if you wish to do anything extensive in the way of indexing.

Indexes in -me
As Eric Allman admits in Writing Papers with Nroff Using -ME, the actual
list produced by -me’s indexing macro more closely matches a Table of Contents than an index: the entries are arranged in sequence of appearance rather
than alphabetically. (With the aid of the UNIX utility sort this can be
changed, and the alphabetizing section below describes the procedure.) Since
this function is sometimes handy, the discussion to follow explains the index
macro so as to allow the automatic generation of a table of contents as well.
In -me, you must bracket the potential index entry with a period, opening
or closing parentheses, and an ‘x,’ like this:
“If I knew what brand he drinks, I would send a barrel or

so to my other generals,”
Ax
Ulysses S. Grant
OX
Lincoln is supposed to have said when questioned about

The entry will not appear in the text, but rather will be tagged with the
current page number and stored in a macro until the command to disgorge its
contents, the “.xp’”. The index format is defined at this point. Usually the
“.xp” is placed at the end of the file, especially if you want a Table of Contents;
usually, too, the command appears only after the headers and footers have been
cleared and a new page begun:
che”
Ores
.bp
._ps 8 \"smaller point size for index
.vs 9

Xp
With a few appropriate titles and headings such as

Macros

Indexes |Indexes in -me| 187

heyy
fone
.bp
tl ’Collected Speeches”
sp
tl] Contents”
sp
xp
the resultant last page can be slipped in front of the formatted document. And
all of this makes sense, because the Table of Contents should always be among
the last things done in a document.
The format generated resembles that for a conventional index. The items
appear along the left margin, separated by two-tenths of a vertical space—the
value is stored in number register “\n(xs”. A line of periods extends from the
entry across the width of the page, stopping a default half-inch before the right
margin. Should you like that half-inch changed, you must redefine number
register “\n(xu”. This can occur anywhere in the document, but cannot be

varied; the last definition of ‘xu’ will determine the right-hand space for all
entries. Last, the page number appears against the right margin. This may be
changed as well, through an ‘argument’ option described in the next paragraph.
If the index item is too long for the line allocated, it is automatically broken
into two lines, and the periods and page number are printed on the second.
This is generally what is needed, but may cause problems if you wish to sort
further on. To avoid this, you may want to extend the line length immediately
before the index page.
Index numbers

and labels in -me

The index-end command—“.)x” —recognizes two different format variations, given as two ‘arguments’ which appear after the command. The first
permits a substitution for the page number: a number register, word, string, or
even defined string. The second places a label—another number or word—in
front of the first. Both must obey the rules of arguments in UNIX: internal
spaces must be ‘padded,’ or the entire string quoted; and if only the second
option is desired, a first argument must nevertheless appear, even if it is only a
dummy. The examples illustrate these rules.

Alternatives to page numbers
Occasionally the index or table of contents is better served with another
numbering scheme or with no numbers at all. Such changes can be stipulated
as first argument

after the index end command;

if, for example, you wanted a

section number (here “\n(Sc”) rather than the page number printed for an
item, you would enter the following:
If to be the head of Hell is as hard as what I have to
undergo here, I could find it in my heart to pity Satan.
Ax
Old Nick

x \n(1S

The option can easily accommodate section, subsection, and paragraph
numbers:

Macros

188 Indexes |Indexes in -me]

x \n(1S.\n(2S.\nx.\ny.\nz
If you require a space between such registers, you may either quote the entire
setting, or place backslashes before each space:
x "\n(s. \n(2s. \n(88S. \n(48"

or:
x \n(1S.\ \n(28.\ \n(3S.\ \n(4S
This option does not begin an automatic process; if no string appears after the
ending command, the index will print the page number instead.
On occasion, you would like neither periods nor number registers of any

sort.

In this case, -me interprets an underscore |‘ ’| in the first argument posi-

tion as a request for a simple list of terms:
xe:
Again, this only functions specifically for a particular entry; to suppress
periods and numbers altogether, then, every ending command would have to
have an underscore as its argument.
Index labels in -me

A second argument given after the index-end command will be printed as
a label to precede the page number. Since it is the second argument, there
must be a first, possibly redundant, argument for the macro to count. And,
since no space is automatically inserted, you may wish to include a few spaces,
if only for esthetics; this naturally involves quoting or some other form of protection. In the following example, the first argument is the page number for
the index item; the second argument is a label, quoted with appropriate spaces:
He will write letters to Horace Greeley and any editor or
reporter or saucy party committee that writes to him, and
cheapen himself.
Ax
Ralph Waldo Emerson
x \n% "Lincoln "
The point is that this label option only works if a page or section register is
given first, even if it is the register which has appeared automatically before.

Multiple Indexes

A second extremely useful feature in -me’s indexing macro is the ability
to name different storage locations for different indexes within a document.
The name is a single character, placed after the‘index begin’ command—“.(x”’;
if none is given (as with the examples above), the macro stores the material in
location ‘x.’ Thus

Macros

Indexes |Multiple Indexes| 189
bx
Carl Shurtz
x

and

Ax x
John G. Nicolay
x

will place both names in the same index.

Practically any character may be

used for an index name, though convention dictates a lower-case letter from ‘a’

to ‘z.’ Each index is retrieved with the “.px” command, plus the character
name:
.bp

pxa
.bp
.px

\"if blank, the same as “x”

This option offers the opportunity for various formats, labels, and numbering
systems within the series of indexes; even the two number registers ‘‘\n(xs”
|vertical space between items] and “‘\n(xv” [right-hand space]! can be redefined
before each index is printed.

Indexes

in -ms

The original Bell Laboratories -ms macro package did not contain an
indexing macro, and for that reason alone a number of UNIX users continue to

prefer -me. However, versions of -ms have been distributed that contain an
adaptation of “.(x/.)x”, typically called “.XS/.XE’’, with “.PX” the printing
macro.

Often, because the resulting list looks like a Table of Contents, some

copies of the index macro in -ms automatically produce that heading, with a
centering mechanism the title macro “.TL”. Given this chaotic state, I have
decided to reproduce one working version of this macro below, with sufficient
explanation to allow you to enter it if you lack it and make any changes that
you feel are necessary. The discussion is divided into four easy pieces: how the
macro works (should you already have it), and then the inner workings of the
index start, the index end, and the retrieval.

Indexes

in -ms: Constituent Parts

The index macro described in detail below is meant to ‘bracket’ the
specific item, with an index start (“.XS’”) on its own line before the entry, and
an index end (“.XE”’) on the line following the entry. When the command to
print the index (“.PX”) is given, the items will be displayed against the lefthand margin, followed by a string of periods and the page number, rightjustified. In nroff, there is a full space between lines, in troff two-tenths of a
vertical space; this can be modified in many cases by redefining number register “\n(xs”. The periods will end a half-inch before the right margin; this, too,
can be changed by redefining another number register, “\n(xu”. The entry to
be stored can contain any number of words or even lines, though too long an

190 Indexes |Indexes in -ms: Constituent Parts]

Macros

item can defeat the purpose. In its simplest form, storing a string in an index
might look something like this:
If elected, I shall be thankful;
AS
thankful
XE
if not it will be all the same.

As you can see, the macro can occur anywhere; it is, however, an excellent idea
to enter it as closely as possible to the indexed term, so that if the term is the
last word on a page, the correct page is certain to appear in the list.
If the list is to serve as a Table of Contents, the “.PX’’ command would be
the last in the text file; and it should probably be preceded by a new page command, as well as instructions to clear automatic pagination and so forth:

but there is little harm I can do, thank God.
.EM \"ending macro in -ms

in 0 \"clear any indentation
.ds CH
\"clear center heading (pagination)
.bp

4 34
Should you wish to specify format instructions, these would be inserted just
before the “.PX”. If your version prints the title “Table of Contents” by
default, it will be governed by the “.TL” macro in -ms: that is, boldface, twelve
point (or two points larger than what has been given in “.nr PS’), and centered
on the line.
Multiple indexes in -ms

A great many different indexes can be generated for the same document
by taking advantage of a feature borrowed from -me: the ability to specify different storage locations for different terms. This is effected through a single
character (usually lower-case ‘a’ through ‘z’), placed after the “.XS’. If no
character is given, the term is automatically stored under the letter “x”. Each
storage location is retrieved with a “.PX” followed by the identifying character.
Since the format for a list is defined when the list is printed, you may then
respecify the format instructions before each “.PX”’, like so:
Lincoln, in point of fact, was a practical politician .
XS a
H. L. Mencken
XE
Lincoln was not a type.
XS b
Robert Ingersoll
XE
ll 5i
Poa
ll 61
.PX b

Macros

Indexes |Indexes in -ms: Constituent Parts| 191

How this actually works is described below. The theoretical number of such
indexes is the number of individual characters on a keyboard, though it is
sometimes dangerous to use non-alphanumerics for such purposes.

Three options are available after the “.XE” that allow you to vary an individual item in the index. If you wish to avoid the row of periods and page
number, you can merely place an underscore right after the command. If you
wish sorne other number than the pagination (a section reference, for example),

this would go after the’.XE”?

Finally, if you wanted a label to appear before

the page or section number, this would have to be entered as a second argument after the command. The first argument would represent your numbering
system, even if it was the default one; in other words, if you wanted both to
continue pagination and insert a label, you would have to enter a “\n%”’ first,
before the label. All of these would be legitimate, then:

XE _

\"no periods or page number

.XE \n(Se \"section number instead of page number

XE \n(Se Davis

\"section number and label

XE \n%"E. Markham"

\"pagination and label

But these would not be:
.XE_"E. Markham"

\"second argument will be ignored

.XE"E. Markham"
\"no page number—only name will appear
XE \n% E. Markham
\"no quotes—only ‘E.’ in label
As the examples indicate, spaces within one of these strings or registers must
be quoted so that the macro can keep track of the number of arguments.

nr $R \n(.v/\n(s

Generating a True index
All that has been said up to now relates to a sequential list of items in a
document. A true index does not behave in this way, so the following explains
a method for electronically capturing the results of the index macros, cleaning,
sorting, and finally combining them. Certain procedures given here are more
extensive than they really need to be, especially if the index macro has been
defined appropriately. But I proceed on the principle that it’s always easy to
remove an unnecessary step.

Capturing the Index
In order to be manipulated, the index must be captured electronically.
This is most conveniently done by sending (‘redirecting’) the nroff or troff output into another file in your directory. The technique was described in the
Introduction as one way to proofread your text. Here the difference is that you
want only the index page sent, so that you are going to make use of the ‘only
this page’ option, “-oN”. In nroff, this is fairly simple; if, for example, the
index page were twenty-six, you would enter the following on your terminal:
$ nroff -o26 documentfile

> indexfile<er>

(with -ms or -me if you're using them). In troff, the operation requires an
ASCII equivalent to the output file; in these cases, you must use the ‘-a’ option
as well:
$ troff -a -o26 documentfile

> indexfile<cr>

Unfortunately, often you are not sure of the index page number, and guessing
might tend to complicate things. Since the index page is almost always the

Macros

194 Indexes |Generating a True Index|

last one in the document, one way to get around this is to choose some wildly
improbable page number for the index and add this to the “bp” like so:
.EM \"ending macro in -ms

bp 500
eX

A “nroff -0500” or “troff -0500” will then choose only that page, and no other.
It would, of course, be more convenient to be able to specify the last page, but I
know of no other way to do it. The new file that results from this operation is
in every respect amenable to the UNIX editors, so (as the next section will
show) it can easily be prepared for sorting and a new format.

Cleaning the index entries
Before the items in a list can be sorted, they almost always require
preparation. In the most obvious situation, unless blank lines or indented lines
are modified, they will appear first in the sorted index.’ It is likely, too, that
you'll want to remove the rows of dots that -me and -ms automatically provide
in their macros. A third concern might relate to content: unwanted leading
words like ‘A,’ ‘An,’ or ‘The,’ for instance. Or fourth, the page numbers might
require some adaptation; like most sort utilities, UNIX’s sort arranges noninitial numbers left to right, rather than by their value; sort, for example,
would reverse the first and last entries in the following list:
Allen T. Rice, 3
Allen T. Rice, 27
Allen T. Rice, 101
In nroff, if the indexing macros

of -me and -ms are used, the numbers

will be

right-justified with the correct number of leading blanks; in troff, some routine will be needed to insert the necessary spaces.

Such preparation can, of course, be accomplished manually, and if the
indexing is to occur only once this may be the most efficient approach. If, however, the procedure will be repeated, a program should be written. The program described below represents one of many alternatives; it uses the UNIX
editor sed, which is invoked in a separate file (called ‘cleanup’ below); the
index is then piped through it into a new file (‘“‘indexfile’’), like so:
$ nroff -0500 documentfile|! cleanup > indexfile<er>
$ troff -a -0500 documentfile! cleanup > indexfile<er>

The resultant index file can then be sorted in the knowledge that the major
problems connected with sorting have been solved, though you can still always
depend on the unanticipated and the annoying in any indexing project.

sed is called a ‘stream editor’ because it ‘streams’ through a file, performing a number of simple and complicated operations. Its syntax is almost identi-

cal to that of ed, edit, and ex, with a few exceptions;

are considered global and no ‘g’ is necessary.
one after another;

one is that all searches

sed accepts commands

on lines

in the form described below, this series of commands

begins

and ends with double quotation marks ("). A ‘cleanup’ file to delete blank
lines
A potential problem, that of leading quotation marks or other forms of punctuation,
can be avoided through the dictionary option in the sort utility itself, described below.
2

.

.

.

Macros

Indexes |Generating a True Index| 195
and remove all leading spaces, for example, would take this form:
sed "/" $/d
ek Shia

Once this file has been created, it must be made executable.
plished on the level of the ‘shell’ with the command

This is accom-

$ chmod +x cleanup<er >
After this, a text file with extraneous blank lines and leading spaces could be
piped through it to get rid of them. All other lines would pass over untouched.
But the cleanup file should be more sophisticated than this, in view of the
various requirements listed above. The following version not only eliminates
blank lines and indented spaces, but removes rows of dots, and places ‘A,’ ‘An,’
and ‘The’ at the rear of the term, afterwards eliminating them in the initial
position:
sed "/"$/d
J */s///
/’The /s/ *\.\.\./, the&/
/’The /s///

/’ An /s/ *\.\.\./, an&/
/° An /s///

LA /s/ *\.\.\./, a&/
/” A /s///

/.\.\.*/s///
Notice that, before the rows of periods could be deleted, they were needed to
find the end of the index item, so that the leading article could be placed safely
after it. Since it is usual to separate the index item from the page number
with a comma, on the last line of the sed file you could insert a comma as a
repacement for the row of dots:

A .\.\.8/8//,/
If you are to use the ‘combine’ program described below, which collapses identical entries, then a unique separator might be needed instead, perhaps a sharp
sign (#).
For inserting zeros or blanks before page numbers, you have at your

disposal a relatively rare function in the UNIX editors, one that ‘encloses’ certain strings with a ‘\( and a ‘\)’ so that they can be retrieved after substitution, no matter what they are:

/ (1-9}$/s/ \(L1-9]\)$/ 000\\1/
/ {1-9)10-9)$/s/ \(L1-9]L0-9]\)$/ O0\\1/
/ |1-9Jl0-9JL0-9]$/s/ \(L1-9]L0-9)L0-9]\)$/ O\\1/
This will work for page numbers up to 9999; if the ‘combine’ program discussed
below is to be used, no post-program will be necessary to remove the leading
zeros, as awk does this automatically.

196 Indexes |Generating a True Index|

Macros

Sorting the entries

The sort itself is the simplest part of the operation, relying on the UNIX
utility of the same name. Of all the options available in sort, the ones that are
most pertinent are ‘f’—‘fold’ (treat upper and lower case as the same) and ‘d’—
‘dictionary’ (basically, ignore beginning punctuation). To sort your prepared
indexfile and view the results on the screen, you merely enter the following:
$ sort -fd indexfile<cr>

If the results are satisfying, the sorted material can be directed into a new file
(here ‘sortindex’) with the usual commands:
$ sort -fd indexfile > sortindex<cr>

When you're feeling really adventuresome, you might want to simply pipe the
whole process through the sort routine:

$ nroff -0500 documentfile | cleanup! sort -fd > sortindex<cr>
$ troff -a -0500 documentfile |cleanup! sort -fd > sortindex<er>

In any case, be prepared for a few elements that are out of place and will need
some massaging.
Combining the index entries
The last step in generating an index is to combine identical entries in an
index, so that a list like

Hampton Roads, 0004
Hampton Roads, 0013
Hampton Roads, 0287
Hunter, R.M.T., 0458
Hunter, R.M.T., 0632
can be automatically collapsed into something like this:
Hampton Roads, 4, 13, 287
Hunter, R.M.T., 458, 632

The following solution, written in the programming language awk, is meant to
illustrate how this might be done. Like sed, an awk routine can be written as
a separate file, and thus become another handy device through which indexes
can be piped. Like BASIC, awk does not need to be compiled, and its syntax is
not terrible, which is something.

The collapsing program, called ‘combine,’ works by dividing the index line
into two fields: the entry and the page number. From the start, then, it needs
a reliable field separator, one that can be dispensed with later, but must be

unique for now.

From the examples given above, it should be clear that a

comma will not suffice. A semicolon or exclamation mark might do, though
anything is potentially dangerous. For straight text, a sharp sign (#) is fairly
dependable; if this is chosen, a line of the cleanup file should insert it in the
right place, between the index item and the page number:

Macros

Indexes |Generating a True Index| 197
A\.\.\.* */s//#/
(The spaces before the page number have also been removed to simplify
things.) The sorted file should then look like this:

Hampton Roads#0004
Hampton Roads#0013
Hampton Roads#0287
The program now has a clear demarcation of two fields per line, and can then
proceed to compare the string in the first field with the previous first string,

and behave appropriately.
Here is the program itself, with UNIX editor line numbers for reference:

awk ’BEGIN { FS="#"}
{
if ($1 != prev)

{
printf("\n%s, %d", $1,$2);

printf(", %d", $2);
a"FE
RWN
CWO
ONDA

11

prev = $1;

13

END { printf("\n")}’

(The indentations are not necessary to the program, but are a convenience for
keeping the various tests straight.) Single quotes (’) bracket the entire program. An initial statement sets the field separator (line 1), then the first
string is tested to see if it is not equal to a variable called (arbitrarily) ‘prev’
(line 3). If it is different from ‘prev,’ a new line is printed (\n), as are the first
string (the index item), a comma, and the second (the page number, a digit—
line 5). Then the ‘else’ is skipped over and that first string becomes the variable ‘prev’ (line 11), and awk goes to the next item in the index. If ‘prev’ and
the first string are the same (as in ‘Hampton Roads#0013’ above), then there
is no new line, and only a comma and the page number are printed (line 9).
Finally, when the last index item is read, an extra new line is inserted to finish
the process.

A number of variations are possible, depending on your needs.

It is alto-

gether probable that your index will need internal format instructions, and
‘combine’ can be tailored to insert these. Entering -ms’s bibliographic record
command (‘‘.BR’”’) before each new item, for example, is straightforward
enough:
3

if ($1 != prev)

A
5

printf("\n.BR\n%s, %d", $1,$2);

6

This will indent all but the first line in an index item.
items are repeated on the same page, as in

If it happens that index

By extending this slightly, indexes of three or more levels can be collapsed as
well, so that entries like

Lincoln,

Lincoln, Abraham. Address at New Salem, 145; Address at
Peoria, 9; Address at Springfield, 32, 60

Similar programs may be written to generate outline-style indexes, with a
liberal sprinkling of line-break commands.

Like the sed programs, ‘combine’ can either be used on its own, as in

$ combine sortindex > finalindex<er>
or, once more, as part of a now massive pipe system:

$ nroff -0500 documentfile |cleanup | sort -fd| combine > finalindex<er>
$ troff -a -0500 documentfile! cleanup! sort -fd! combine > finalindex<er>
If this turns out well enough (the chances diminish with each ‘fitting’ to the
pipe), the commands just given can in turn be made into executable files:
‘indexmake.n, for example, for the nroff routine, and ‘indexmake.t’ for the

troff. These files will accept other file names than ‘documentfile’ by substituting a ‘$1’ for the file name, like so:

troff -a -0500 $11 cleanup| sort -fd| combine > finalindex
Then the entire procedure could be invoked by simply typing the following:
$ indexmake.t

documentfile< er >

Whether or not the process was worth the effort is a question that we might
as
well avoid for right now.

KEEPING TEXT

What ‘Keeping Text’ Is About
‘Keeping text’ is the process of defining a specific chunk of your text (or,
more politely, a ‘block’) so that it will not be broken if by chance it does not fit
in its totality on the present page. This is handy for charts and figures, and
especially texts like mailing lists, where the names should be physically associated with addresses. It would have been useful in this text to keep the examples together, but it would have also produced too much white space on too
many pages. It is in fact as a response to this problem of white space that a
subvariety of ‘keep’ has been created: a ‘floating’ keep, which fills in the white
space with text taken from after the block. This is no help if the blocks are an
integral part of the narrative, but it does work well for material which is not
associated with any particular passage.

Diversions

To accomplish this, the keep macro relies on the diversion command “.di”
(or, more accurately, “.di Xx,” in which Xx is an unused macro name); this
command, when invoked, stores text following it under that name until the

diversion is turned off la “.di” by itself]. The stored material is printed when
the name is invoked; since the storage area is considered a macro, you call for
it on a line by itself, preceded with a period. There is, however, one proviso: as
the text is stored it is processed; so, to avoid changing the vertical spacing of

the material when it is reprocessed, you must use the no-fill mode |“.nf”] when
you retrieve the block:

199

Macros

200 Keeping Text |Diversions]
nf
Ss
fi

\"no-fill
\"print diversion
oer

Otherwise, the formatter would attempt to rejustify the lines, collapsing
indents and joining titles to the text.
This raises a problem, because the text might already be in no-fill, and
automatically changing to fill might be a mistake; this will be solved when we
reach ‘environments,’ but for the time being I’ll set a flag [here a yes/no test],
using read-only number register ‘.u,’ which has the value of one in fill and zero
in no-fill:

nr Tf \n(.u
nf
\"no-fill

Ss

\"flag for fill or not

\"print diversion

if \n(Tf>0 .fi

\"fill if “Tf not zero

Though this must seem typical of the kind of compromises one must make in
nroff/troff, it is not as complicated as it could be.

Diversions function best within macros (the footnote macro is an excellent example), so it would be unlikely that you would use the command directly
in the text; but if you did the whole process might look like this:

Dispatch #6
.di Ss
\"diverting the following to storage space
.br
\"necessary so that previous line will be printed
tl Oct.24, 1362:
sp
Major Genl McClellan
Sp
I have just read your dispatch about sore tongued
and fatigued horses. Will you pardon me for asking
what the horses of your Army have done since the
battle of Antietam that fatigues anything?
Sp
ti 41
A. Lincoln

.br
di
nf
Ss

\"matches ‘break’ above
\"end diversion
\"no-fill
\"print diversion

ai

Tele

As the example indicates, five elements are necessary in a diversion: 1) the
command itself, ‘bracketing’ the material to be stored; 2) the storage location,
which can be any single- or double-letter macro name; 3) two breaks (“.br” or,
if you wish, “.sp”) to print (‘flush out’) the partially composed text line before
the diversion, and to print the partially composed line at the end of the diversion; 4) at the appropriate point, printing the contents of the diversion by
invoking the macro, which is preceded by a period; and 5) a no-fill command
(“nf”) before the storage macro is to be called. And, logically, these five
will
also be necessary in any macro which puts away and retrieves text:
footnotes,

Macros

Keeping Text |Retrieving Diversions within Macros] 201
indexes, displays, and the keeps themselves. Of course, displays and keeps
would differ in that storage only occurs if the block does not fit on the page.

Retrieving Diversions within Macros
Basically, in the macros just listed, a block of text is kept in holding until
an advantageous point: for footnotes, it would be the bottom of the page; for

indices, after the last page; and for displays and keeps, the top of a page. In
all of these cases you would establish a macro with a ‘trap’ to be sprung at that
point (or, if you already have a header or footer, you would make the trap part
of your pre-established macro); the macro would test for the presence of the
diverted storage and, if this existed, print it. For the keep macro, the header is
used, because its trap is at zero, before any text is processed, so that the kept
text will be printed before anything else. The header macro in the example
below uses a flag with the name ‘Bo’ to test for the presence of a diversion:
.de He

.wh 0 He

\"if ‘Bo’ is more than zero, print “.Ss”

\"trap for the header macro

For the sake of completeness, the following is an elementary use of a diversion
which would both store a text and set a flag, so that the header would print
the text:
.di Ss
.br
Edward Everett
.br

di
nr Bol _ \"setting the flag ‘Bo’

To be tidy, you may wish to avoid having the header print the diversion over
and over from now on; if this is so, you can, within the header, cancel the flag
and remove the storage macro:

.de He
nf

if \\n(Bo>0 .Ss
fi
-nr Bo 0
.rm Ss

.wh 0 He
Merely canceling the flag would do, but it’s good practice to clean up while
you're thinking about it.

The Keep Macro: Basic Version

At this point you could define a basic keep command—that is, a bracketing set of macros which would divert any text that was placed between them,
retaining that text until it was appropriate to print it. I hope this looks famillar:

\"keep-start—the beginning of a diversion

.di Ss

\"keep-end—not exactly original

.Ks

he will be good but god knows When”
.Ke
You may retrieve this right away (with a “if \\n(Bo>0 .Ss”) or wait until the
next page and the header macro; in the case of keeps and displays, the deciding
factor will be the amount of room left on the page, and it is to calculating this
that we now turn.

Diversion Measurement

The utilization of diversions in macros is enormously facilitated by a
specific read-only number register, one of several associated with diversions:
‘dn, which contains the vertical dimension of the last diversion finished.
While you thought nroff/troff just process text, they also busily keep constant
track of these diversions, measuring their length and width; and both of the
roffs will return these values if you ask for them: “\n(dn” will give you, in
machine units, the amount of vertical space the diversion will take on a page if
it were printed. Since by now you have a sense of the ways of text formatting,
you'll immediately see the use of ‘dn’ in keeping text whole: all you have to do
is compare the size of the diversion with the amount of room left and either
print the passage if there’s enough room or go to the next page if there isn’t.
To establish the distance remaining on the page, you would instruct the processor to calculate the difference between the total page length (found in readonly register ‘.p’) and the length of the page printed thus far (number register

‘nl’). Then if you test the two within the macro, you can either print it if
there’s enough room or break to the next page and print the block:
.de Ks
.br
.di Ss
.de Ke
.br

Macros

Keeping Text |Diversion Measurement] 203
.di

if \\n(dnu>(\\nCpu-\\n(nlu) .bp

Ss

\"print kept text

This macro would work in many simple applications, but it would be insufficient in a normal situation involving footers, because the macro could not take
any ‘traps’ into consideration. Luckily, the nroff and troff just happen to have
a read-only number register (‘.t’), which gauges the distance to the next trap.
We can then use that distance to compare with our diversion size:
.de Ks

-.br
.di Ss

.de Ke
.br

T5\5
.Ss

fi

This will print “.Ss” right away if the size of the diversion is less than the distance to the next trap, or, if the diversion size is equal to or greater than that
distance, begin another page and then print the stored text.! Knowing what
you do about the role of the header macro, you can immediately improve upon
this by making the ‘if? test an ‘if/else’ [“.ie/.el”] and adding your header macro.
It would also be useful to substitute a large number of spaces, so that the processor can step through remaining traps and other elements on its way to the

Congratula-

Environments

Environments represent the means for establishing a sequence of commands which will be completely independent of the ongoing processing; its use
may be immediately apparent in such applications as headers and footers,
which should be set in a consistent point size, vertical spacing, and so forth, no
matter how much the text itself changes. In nroff and troff, an environment
is called, and the text is processed under those commands; then, at the end,

there is a return (the environment is ‘popped’) to current commands. Three
environments that are available are called “.ev 0’, “.ev 1”, and “.ev 2”. The
first of these is the normal systems environment, and generally when a macro
‘pops’ it is returning to the prevalent text settings. The alternate environment
most used is ‘1’; many macros can share it, since it is invariably redefined for
each specific use. This is not altogether clear from the documentation, so
examples might be in order; let us say that a bracketing quoted paragraph
macro (‘Qp/Qe’ below) needs an environment to insulate itself from the text,
and a keep macro (‘Ks’) does as well. You could define both macros and use
“ev 1”, like so:

.de Qp
\"quoted paragraph
.ev 1 \"begin new environment
._ps 9
.vs llp
.de Qe

.ev.

\"end by ‘popping’

.de Ks
ev 1

._ps 10
.vs 12p
fi
.br
.di Ss

When the quoted paragraph macro is called, the text will be stored in the
smaller point size and vertical spacing; when, on the other hand, the keep
macro is called, the point size and vertical spacing are the ones defined in the
keep macro.

There is a problem here, of course, in that a font command

for

italics in the quoted paragraph would, if not redefined, continue as the current
font for that environment. To protect against this, the wisest course is
to use
the ‘end’ part of the macro pair to return to the values in place for that
environment before the command (the “.ft” by itself). And, if you
were using a
great many macros with environments, it might be safe to return
all settings
to previous values:

Macros

Keeping Text |Environments] 205
.de Qe
ps
\"return to previous point size in this environment
.vs_
\"return to previous vertical spacing in this environment
ft

\"return to previous font in this environment

1
eV

\"return to previous line length in this environment

Basically, then, the environment system acts like an alternative working area,
which can always be depended on to produce consistent formatting, no matter
what is happening in the text.

If this is so, the provision for still another environment, “.ev 2”, seems
redundant; but actually there are instances in which a second alternative is
needed, and the keep macro is one of them. We already had something of the
problem with the fill and no-fill modes, but were able to solve it with a flag
(‘Tf above). One, however, that cannot be solved with flags is the problem of
current commands that you want to continue in the environment (the indent,
for example), but you do not want to be in effect when the stored material is
printed once again. To use indents as an example, you may have a text that
has been indented, and you would almost certainly want the kept block to be
indented; so both the normal environment and the keep environment would
contain indent commands. But retrieving the block in environment 1 would
indent it once again. The solution is to retrieve it in a third environment,
which will take the text as it finds it. To illustrate, the following example
saves the current indent (read-only number register ‘.i’) in number register
‘Ki,’ then indents the environment by that value; later, in the header macro,
the third environment is called, in order to avoid both the current indent and
the one now in effect in “.ev 1”. This has another, subsidiary benefit, in that

the header macro no longer needs to worry about whether or not the current
environment was in fill mode or not. It is important to note that environments
share macros and number registers, and that you can move directly from
environment 0 to environment 2, without any intermediary:
.de Ks

nr Ki \\n(i

\"store current indent as ‘Ki’

.br

.ev 1 \"environment 1
in \\n(Kiu
\"indent the value of ‘Ki’
ain
.di Ss

.de Ke
.br

.di
ie \\n(dnu> =\\n(.tu .sp 200

el \{\
nf

Ss//}
.ev.

\"back to current environment

.de He
.ev 2\"environment
nf

Ss

2: indent is zero here

\"already-indented Ss is printed

Macros

206 Keeping Text |Environments]
.rm Ss
.ev.
\"back to current environment

.wh 0 He
The return to the current environment in the ‘keep-end’ macro (‘‘.Ke’’) was outside the ‘if/else’ test because in both cases you are finished with the keep
environment. And the fact that the keep macro is in environment 1 and the
header is in 2 is not consequential; the important thing is that they are different.

The ‘Floating’ Keep
Up to now, the discussion has focused on what might be called the ‘fixed’
keep: the kept block is fixed in its position in the text. Another sort of keep is
possible, one that allows subsequent text to fill in the unesthetic white space
left if it has to be moved to the next page. This is called a ‘floating’ keep; I
assume the name refers to the block, hovering above the text while it’s being
processed. The idea is there, anyway. Floating keeps are excellent for figures
and displays that do not belong to any one point in the text, or for blocks to
which a whole section refers.
The most interesting aspect of the floating keep is that, though it sounds
more difficult than the fixed keep, it is actually simpler: it does not have to
use the extraordinary measure (in word-processing terms extraordinary anyway) of ‘forcing’ a new page if there is insufficient room for it. The “.bp” or
“sp 200” is completely unnecessary, since if the defined block doesn’t fit, the
header will pick it up when the current page is finished. You might even, if
you wished, dispense with the flag ‘Bo,’ substituting a simple ‘if? for the
‘if/else, and reversing the comparison, so that “.Ss” would only print if there
were enough room, like so:

if \\n(dnu<\\n(.tu \{\
nf

.Ss\}
The flag is, however, a good idea, as is the general principle of keeping macros
with similar functions similar, so Ill define the floating keep to resemble the
fixed keep as closely as possible. The keep start half of the sequence is exactly
the same; the end will be different, so it will have a different name: “.Kf”.
.de Ks

nr Ki \\n(i
.br
.ev 2

in \\n(Kiu
afil

.di Ss
.de Kf
.br
.di

ie \\n(dnu> =\\n(.tu .nr Bo 1

el \{\

Macros

208 Keeping Text |The ‘Floating’ Keep]
.de He

if \\n(Bo>0 \{\
.ev 1

.wh 0 He
.wh —1li Fo

Given this set of commands, if the kept block is too large for the space remaining, the formatter will print enough text after the block to fill up the page, and
continue that text after the block has been processed at the top of the next
page.

Keeps in -me
-me does not have a separate system of commands for keep, but rather
uses the display option |“.(b/.)b” for fixed, “.(z/.)z” for floating] to perform
those tasks. Since both sets have an option to fill (unlike -ms’s display macros)
by adding an ‘F’ after the beginning command, the effect is exactly the same as
a keep:

(b F
tl ’’ Executive Mansion’
tl ’’Washington, Aug 23, 1864
sp
ti .5i
This morning, as for some days past, it seems exceedingly
probable that this Administration will not be re-elected.
Jb
The floating keep, “.(z/.)z”, behaves in exactly the same way, except of course
for filling in text following the final command if the block didn’t fit on the
page.

The center-block display in -me |[“.(c/.)c”] does not automatically keep
text, so the macro package very cleverly allows you to bracket the macro command with a “.(b/.)b” or “.(z/.z)”:

(z
(c

\"begin floating keep
\"begin centered block

PLUMS
ARRIVED HERE WITH NUTS
THIS MORNING ALL RIGHT
Jc
\"end centered first

Jz

\"now end keep

Macros

Keeping Text |Keeps in -ms| 209
This will both center the block on the longest line and make sure that it is not
split across pages.

Keeps in -ms
The ‘straight’ or ‘fixed’ keep in -ms takes a familiar form, bracketing the
block to be saved with a “.KS/.KE”; a ‘floating’ keep uses “.KF/.KE”:

.KS
Of all the Whig rascals in Springfield, Abe Lincoln
is the ablest and the most honest.
.KE
Both macros have been instructed to print the block in default point size as set
in number register PS, vertical spacing in VS, line length in LL, and title
length in LT. If you wish to reset any of these, you may do so within the keep
macros, using the basic troff or nroff command (“.ps”, “.vs”, “ll”, and “.lt’’);
-ms will return to the current line length and so forth afterwards:

.KS
HH 5i
Yours of yesterday, naming places, times, and terms, for
joint discussions between us, was received this morning.
.KE
If there are any section or paragraph macros within the kept block, a
“nr LL M” will be needed as well, and this will have to be reset afterwards;
the best place to put it is probably right after the “.KE”’.

Both “.KS” and “.KF” perform admirably in all sorts of cases which are
seldom anticipated: columns, indents, multiple keeps on a page, and mixed
fixed and floating keeps. They even know what to do with a keep block that is
too large for any page, printing it out as if it were straight text. Finally, it is
important to remember that the table preprocessor ‘tbl’ has its own internal
keep system and doesn’t want or need yours.

MEMORANDA

The Interactive Memorandum

Though nroff and troff are essentially batch processors, in certain applications they are able to take advantage of the interactive capacity of the computer. To illustrate one such application, the following section describes an
interactive memorandum sequence. When the file is roffed, it prompts the typist for ‘To’ and ‘Regarding’ information, and then requests the text; the function automatically fills in the date and the ‘From’ line, and generates the document in house format, complete with footnotes, running headers, boilerplate,
and whatever else is needed. It does have a weakness, in that you cannot edit
previous lines (just as you cannot with the utilities write or mail on the system level), and for that reason the entire memo must be canceled if you decide
after the fact that some part of it is unacceptable.
The function utilizes two advantages of nroff and troff: their capacity to
send messages to the terminal, and a complementary ability to read in text
from the terminal. The first is accomplished through the command
.tm message

on its own line. When the source file is processed, message will appear on the
screen, accompanied by a ‘squeak’ (hence its use in error messages) when nroff
or troff encounter it. The second part is performed by the read command
|“.rd”|,which requests information from the terminal or from an outside file
(hence its use in repetitive letters). The most important characteristic of ‘rd’
is that it will accept text typed from the terminal until it receives two carriage
returns in a row; this was clearly intended to allow as much text (with interspersed carriage returns) as you wish, but it does take some getting used to. A
secondary feature is that it automatically breaks to a new line. The ‘tm’
merely prints its message and continues, but it also surprises you at first.

210

Macros

Memoranda |The Interactive Memorandum]

211

Together these commands are empowered to create a kind of interactive system
which will prompt the user for variable text that can then be set in a specific
format.

The following example of an interactive memorandum file (called
‘com.Mm’) first fills in the day’s date, then prompts the typist for the recipient
and for the subject, while automatically filling in the sender’s name in correct
sequence. Finally, it requests the text:

.GO
sp 3

tl’\n(mo/\n(dy/19\n(yr”
.Sp
tm To:
To: \c
rd
.Sp
From: William L. James, Requisitions
.Sp
.tm Regarding:
Re: \c
rd
Sp 2

bP
tm Text:
rd
(The continuation character ‘\c’ was necessary to forestall the read command’s
automatic break.) When this file has been processed (most likely through
nroff), it will send messages to the screen and await the entry of as many lines
as are necessary; when the typist is finished replying to each prompt, two carriage returns in a row re-engage the processor, which continues to the next
point. The material entered may contain all of the roff commands, including
“so”, “.br’, and whatever else. Naturally, if the organization decided on a new
format, or even if William were to retire or stop writing memos, a few seconds
is all that’s needed for the consultant to effect the change.

For the sanity of all concerned, I would recommend that this routine utilize the redirection operator ‘>’ to place the memo in a new file, like this:

$ nroff -ms com.Mm>

memofile<cr>

Otherwise the formatted memo will appear on the screen almost immediately
after the second carriage return completes the text portion, and the printer
switch may not be convenient. If the final copy needs checking, a cat or more
command may be added:
$ nroff -ms com.Mm>

memofile;

more memofile<cr>

Though the memorandum is not the only application possible for “.rd” and
“tm”, it seems a likely enough candidate for a start.

PARAGRAPHS

The Wonderful World of Paragraphs
Because paragraph beginnings are both common and repetitive—the two
conditions most conducive to macro definitions— paragraph commands in nroff
and troff are usually clustered as macros. You may of course construct your
own, and both -me and -ms offer different sorts. In the packages, the paragraph macros also perform a number of invisible housekeeping tasks: ‘initialization’ (clearing and setting various internal registers at the to start of a text),
and resetting to default values within the text. This should not, however,
prevent you from experimenting with paragraph definitions within the packages; the initialization/reset macro can be easily inserted, and there may be
times when you wish to avoid automatic functions—to save time and computing power, for instance.

Ironically, the paragraph macro is not absolutely essential in nroff and
troff. If you, for example, broke to a new line and used a tab to signal a new
paragraph in one of the UNIX editors, both formatters would also break to a
new line and indent the set amount (default eight-tenths of an inch in nroff,
half an inch in troff). Similarly, spaces at the beginning of the line will also
interrupt automatic filling and produce an indent, so that, if it looks like the
following on the terminal (shown here with line numbers),
346
347

short space of four years.
My countrymen, one and all, think calmly

it will appear that way in format. nroff and troff, because they’ve been
instructed to, will move to a new line and indent whenever a tab or space
begins a line. This brings up a potential problem, however: a change in tab
settings at any point will throw off all subsequent paragraphs, as will a change
in point size for leading spaces in troff. This is just the sort of niggling detail

212

Macros

Paragraphs |This Wonderful World| 213
that is overlooked in all word-processing projects. A good general rule is to
make every function obvious, in this case by inserting commands to break the
line and temporarily indent the correct distance:

short space of four years.
.br
\"break to new line
.ti .5i\"indent (temporarily [first line only! one half inch)
My countrymen, one and all, think calmly

Not only does this prevent errors, but it provides just the sort of literally
unique target which global editing requires for reformatting text.

But quite apart from identifying functions in the text, certain commands
also allow you to exploit a few of the advantages to word processing on the
computer. One of the most important of these is the ability to count the
number of lines remaining on the page and to skip to the next page if there is
only one line left, thus avoiding the dreaded ‘widow’ (a paragraph beginning as
a single line at the bottom of the page). The need command “.ne N”’ exists
specifically for that purpose, and all you have to do is decide how many lines
will be needed at the bottom of the page to start a new paragraph (two is
usual). For a way around the equally dreaded ‘orphan’ (the paragraph ending
as a single line at the top of the page), see the last part of the -ms section on
paragraphs below.
Given these three commands, you could introduce each paragraph in your
text, like so:

Housekeeping Functions
As I mentioned above, both -ms and -me use the paragraph macros to
restore whatever default parameters there are. The basic intent seems to be
that an error of line length, font, or point size, for example, should not extend
beyond the one paragraph where it occurs; even if all subsequent pages prob-

ably have to be redone, they at least resemble their form under the desired
specifications. Such restorations would be absolutely necessary if, for example,
you were mixing indented paragraphs with the other kinds. As usual, there is
a certain risk in this, in that you have to remember that the paragraph macros
are ‘silently’ doing this, and then you have to circumvent it if you would like
a
different effect. The following are a number of the reset commands
that can
be added to the above definitions:

Macros

\"return to fill

.ce 0 \"turn off centering
ft 1 \"roman font |nroff, non-underline|
It would be a dangerous practice, however, to specify an exact line length,
point size, and vertical spacing in such macros, even if the house style is absolutely ironclad; this defeats the very flexibility in format which the roffs offer.
As your list of macros lengthened, or even became an outside file (accessible
through the command “so filename”), it would be more and more difficult to
locate and change specific values when the parameters changed.
In such cases it would be far better to follow the lead of the macro packages, defining number registers for these values, accessible to all macros. The
number registers would be entered at the beginning of the file, where they
could be easily checked and modified for each new document. Furthermore, if
the invoked number registers are given two backslashes, interpolating their
value will be delayed until that point in the text; then, if for some reason you
needed to change a parameter, you could do it right before the affected paragraph. For example, a six-and-a-quarter-inch line, 14-point type on 18-point
vertical spacing, with an inch page offset could be defined in four separate
number registers, each given a mnemonic name. They could then appear in the
file like so (again with the file’s line numbers)!:

The advantage to all of this is that your governing specifications are concentrated in one place, either at the head of your file or in a source file, where

Elaborations:

Quoted Paragraphs
More complicated paragraph macros require more intricate constructions.
A true quoted paragraph macro, for example, sets an extract apart from the
text by both indenting and shortening the line length proportionally, as well as
decreasing the point size and vertical spacing; the format, then, depends on the

text for point size, vertical spacing, line length, and indentation but is independent in line spacing (always single) and font (always roman). At the end of
the extract, a concluding macro must return to the text in its previous settings, which may or may not be the presets defined at the beginning of the file.
You have three or four options to accomplish this, all of which are discussed
more fully in the section on headings and footers; here, as in headers, establishing environments represents the best solution. But since the roffs provide a
series of read-only number registers that keep track of current parameters like
point size, line spacing, and line length, it might be useful to construct a macro
that saves these values in special number registers and manipulates them
there. The following example, while not the fastest or the most efficiently
designed macro, does function, and usefully summarizes much of what has
already been said:
.de Qp

nr Sp \\n(s _ \"registers saving current point size
if t nr Sv \\n(.vu__
\"vertical spacing in units
nr Sl \\nGlu _ \"line length
nr Si \\nGiu
\"indent
nr Ss \\nCL_
\"line spacing
mr Sf \\n(f
\"font

if t .vs \\n(Vsu*9u/10u

in \\n(Unu+(\\n(Llu* 1u/10u)

\"single space
fet
\"roman font
.sp
ne 2

.de Qe
.sp

\"return to previous settings

.ps \\n(Sp
if t .vs \\n(Svu
1 \\n(Slu

Macros

Paragraphs |Elaborations: Quoted Paragraphs| 217
in \\n(Siu
ls \\n(Ss
ft \\n(Sf

The point size is rendered at eight-tenths of the preset size, the vertical spacing at nine-tenths, the line length at nine-tenths of the preset length, and
finally the indent at the preset indent plus one tenth of the line length. It is
important to remember that the line length is always measured from the
right-hand margin in nroff and from a one-inch page offset in troff and is not
affected at all by indents.
The calculations could have been made directly on the read-only registers,
and the returns to previous settings could have been made through a series of
commands without values (“.ps” by itself, for example); but there are certain
advantages to specially established number registers, foremost among them
obviousness and safety. The safety figures in those cases where a change in
point size or font occurs within the extract, something that does happen on
occasion.

Elaborations:

Indented Paragraphs and Hanging Labels
Indented paragraphs are conventionally used for ‘labels,’ identifying tags
or marks (usually a ‘bullet’ |\(bu, making a e]). This confuses the issue,
though the confusion is as always instructive. If one were always going to use
just the bullet, the macro would need add only three more lines to the definition given above:

.de Ip
.br
in .5i
ne 2
tao
Ail

\(bu\t\c

The tab on the third-to-last line insures that the text following the bullet is
aligned with the remainder of the indented paragraph; then, on the next line,

the bullet itself is invoked, along with a tab [‘‘\t”] and a command to interrupt
the formatting, so that the text will continue, without the usual intervening

space |‘‘\c’’].
Labels containing an identifying word or group of words entail two conditions: first, the label should be placed in the vicinity of the macro; and second,
there should be provision for a label which happens to be longer than your
paragraph indent. The first problem is solved easily enough with the argument option. Through a “\\$1” rather than a bullet, you would instruct the
macro to expect a string right after the macro invocation:

218 Paragraphs |Elaborations: Indented Paragraphs and Hanging Labels|

Macros

ti -.5i
\\$1\t\c
If there’s a chance that the phrase might begin with an apostrophe \’]ora
period |.|, you can prevent this from being read as a command with your old
friend “\&”:

\\&\\$1\t\c
As an argument, you would append the label immediately after the request for
the macro, like so:

ip Lear:
Punctuation can be included in an argument, but not spaces; the usual method
for enclosing spaces within an argument is either to quote the entire string
\"King Lear"], or to ‘protect’ the space with a backslash |King\ Lear].

There are doubtless a large number of solutions to the problem of an
overly long label, but I will treat only three here: 1) you may indent the paragraph further to contain the label; 2) you may start the paragraph on the next
line; or 3) you may divide the label into parts which would run down the lefthand margin.
The first solution, in which the width of the label (plus one space) determines the amount of paragraph indent, has a certain paper-wasting abandonment about it, in that the paragraph may be narrowed considerably. But it
does have the virtue of simplicity:

in \\w’\\$1\ ’u
ne 2

ta \\w’\\$1\ ’u
ti -\\w’\\$1\ ’u

\$1\t\c
Here as usual the various double-backslashes defer calculating the sundry
measurements until the macro is actually read during processing. But I have a
feeling that this macro would not draw much enthusiasm because it does produce paragraphs of different lengths, and many would find this unesthetic.
The second solution is to measure the label; if it’s greater than the width

allotted to the paragraph, the label would be printed, but the paragraph itself
would begin on the next line. This macro can be best illustrated with a default

paragraph-indent number register |Pil, which has been established at the head
of the source file:

ta \\n(Piu
ti -\\n(Piu

Macros

Paragraphs |Elaborations: Indented Paragraphs and Hanging Labels| 219

if \\n(Piu<\w’\\$1 ’u .br \"break or space

which is only slight larger can be accommodated, but not labels beyond that:

ne 2

ti -\\n(Piu

The three n’s are negotiable, naturally.

Numbered Paragraphs
Some people like to keep track of their paragraphs in several levels of
numbering, and as you will see both -me and -ms have provided for it. The
simplest way is to design your own, however, because then you have control
over the way the numbers appear and how they are automatically incremented.
I know, I know; I warned you about the dangers of having things done
automatically. But this can be balanced against the possibility of deleting a
single paragraph (which, of course, is always at the beginning) and then needing to laboriously renumber all subsequent paragraphs. If you've established
registers for the chapter and section numbers, moreover, you can easily utilize
them.
Automatically numbered paragraphs in their basic form are only a little
more complex than the simple indented paragraph given earlier, with a number
register replacing the bullet and incrementing itself each time it has been
called:

222 Paragraphs |Elaborations: Indented Paragraphs and Hanging Labels|

Macros

jive 9-< (0) I

.de Ip
DE
in .5i
ne 2
ta .51
ti -.51

\\n+x\t\c

The first line appears at the head of the text and would reappear each time the
numbering sequence was to be renitialized. Naturally, several levels of paragraph numbering are possible (the following incrementing on the lowest level):
\\nx.\\ny.\\n+4z\t\c

to get something like 3.4.2.
entirely yours.

It is fairly simple as well to create a paragraph macro which automatically records the chapter and section numbers. In the example below I am
assuming that there is only a primary level of section numbers (e.g., 7.5’ for
the fifth section in Chapter 7), though as the discussions of chapter and section
macros indicate, there really is no limit. The command sequence will also look
very much like that provided for headers and section headings above, which
proves that the system is consistent. At the head of your text you would define
your chapter number macro, section heading macro, and header macro; and
then in the text you may set numbers: in the case below, the chapter number
register is called ‘Cn,’ and the section number ‘Sn’:
.de Ip
-br
an li
ne 2
cae

ti -li
\\n(Cn.\\n(Sn.\\n +(Pn\t\c

Normally, the section macro takes the responsibility for reinitializing the paragraph numbers, but it is possible to have the paragraph macro itself test for
any change in the section number:
.de Ip
if \\n(Sb!=\\n(Sn \{\
cnr Pn 0 1\}
nr Sb \\n(Sn
br
jay Ib
ne 2
ta li
ti —li

Macros

Paragraphs |Paragraphs in -me| 223
Here the paragraph keeps track of the last given section number in a register
called ‘Sb,’ and if this is for some reason not equal to the current section
number, it zeroes itself and begins over.

Paragraphs in -me
-me offers four different paragraph options, all of which resemble in some
fashion the macros described above:

lp

\"left-justified paragraph

pp

\"first-line indented paragraph

ip
np

\"indented paragraph
\"numbered indented paragraph

All of these macros will check to see if the page has at least two lines remaining (“.ne 2”); otherwise, the paragraph will begin at the top of the page. All
will insert a space between the paragraphs: in nroff, one space on machines
with no half-line capabilities, otherwise a half-space; in troff, .35 of a space.

This can be changed before initialization of before the affected paragraph with
a “nr ps M”, in which Mis any measurement in any unit. The indent in “.pp”
is by default 5 n’s, and this, too, can be modified by redefining “.nr pi M” in n’s
or any other units. Similarly, “ip” and “.np” are indented 5 n’s, and this can
be changed by redefining “.nr ii M” in any unit of measurement.
Each of -me’s paragraph macros will cancel a number of commands which
might have been inadvertently left functioning: italics, centering, spacing
requests (it does this through “.ns’’). In troff -me cancels any point size different from default point size (ten point); to retain a change in point size over
paragraph boundaries, it should be reset by defining the number register
“nr pp N”.
The indented paragraph “.ip” will accept both a label and some distance
which provides sufficient room if the label is larger than the default n’s:

ip Rutledge 10n
And, to repeat an important point, if the label has internal spaces, you must
either precede each space with a backslash (in the jargon, rendered ‘unpaddable’), or ‘quote’ the entire label:
ip Joshua\ Fry\ Speed 18n

or
ip "Joshua Fry Speed" 18n

It is possible to let the program measure the label for you by taking advantage

of the “\w” width function:
ip John\ Stuart \w’John\ Stuart\ \ ’u

This is particularly advantageous in troff, where it is often difficult to estimate the exact dimension of a string.

Macros

224 Paragraphs |Paragraphs in -ms|

The numbered paragraph in -me, “.np”, places a digit flush left of the

indented text, and this number is automatically incremented each time it is
called. It can be reset to 1 with either the standard paragraph (“.pp”), the

left-justified paragraph (“lp”), or the section heading (“sh”). It can be set to
any other number by redefining the register “.nr $p N”, in which Nis the new
number.

Paragraphs in -ms
Five types of paragraph are usually offered in -ms:
PP

\"first-line indented

LLP

\"left-justified

IP
.2L

\"indented
\"two-line label indented lalso .3L and .4L]

.QP

\"quoted paragraph

The left-justified and first-line paragraph macros are self-explanatory and need
little further elaboration. The indented paragraph will set the entire text over
a certain number of spaces and is frequently used with identifying labels. The
quoted paragraph was written for extracts cited within a text; it indents right
and left, and the point size and spacing are automatically reduced if possible
(in nroff with “.ls 2” to single-space, in troff by one point |ten-on-twelve

becomes nine-on-eleven]).
The paragraph commands automatically skip one line between paragraph
blocks (three lines if “.ls 2”), and “.PP”, “.IP”’, and “.QP” indent five n’s. Both
the gap and the indent can be reset through two separate commands, typed
before the initialization at the beginning of the file. For all four macros, the

space between paragraphs is changed with a “.nr PD M” |M defaults to vertical
spaces]; and, for “.PP” and “.IP’, the amount of indent is defined through a
“mr PI M” |Mdefault n’s|. To reduce, then, the gap between paragraphs to
nothing, you can include this instruction among your macros in the first few
lines of the source text:
nr PD 0

Or if you would like the paragraph indent to be four n’s, you should insert,
again before the initialization, the command
jmie LAL e

Like many such number registers in -ms, other measurements can be used for
these two settings: inches, m’s, centimeters, picas, and points. A European
publisher, for example, may require a set of instructions like so:
nD
2e
sare JEL ie

The quoted paragraph “.QP” represents a special case. Most important, since it
indents from either side, it is not affected by the “nr PI’. If you wish to
change the indents left and right, the appropriate number register is
“nr QI N’”, so that

Macros

Paragraphs |Paragraphs in -ms| 225
-nr QI 10n

will indent ten n’s on the left and shorten the line by ten n’s on the right.
Other units of measurement can be used. Again, if these adjustments are to
affect all such paragraphs, they should be placed at the beginning of the text;
or, if it’s only to be a local change, it must appear before the particular paragraph.

The indented paragraph “.IP” will accept a label, and a redefinition of the
amount of indent ldefault 5n’s]. in the form “.IP label N”, in which Nis
understood as the amount of space needed. For example, the following would
produce a paragraph with a label larger than the default setting:

.IP Commander-in-Chief 18
The number is taken as n’s, but other types of measurement are also possible:
.IP Commander-in-Chief 6P

\"six pica indent

or even:
.IP Commander-in-Chief \w’Commander-in-Chief\ ’u
This is demonstrably simpler than doing it oneself, but not as psychically
rewarding.
If a label has internal spaces, these present a special case; the label must
be placed in double quotation marks ("), like so:

.IP "Stephen Douglas" 3P
or the space may begin with a backslash:

.IP Stephen\ Douglas 3P
This will insure that the label will be read as a single string.

The indented paragraph will not automatically turn off indents when it is
finished, so if you are returning to normal left-justified text, you must either
use the “.LP” lor other paragraph] macro or cancel the indent with a “.in 0”.

Multilabeled Paragraphs (‘‘.2L”’, ‘‘.3L”’, ‘‘.4L’’)
Multilabeled paragraphs are really variants of the “.IP”, with an important exception: since the paragraph might be shorter than the labels, it is
necessary to signal the end of the paragraph with a “.LE”. Like “.IP”, the two
lor more] labels are placed after the macro, along with a space request if you
wish. A typical labeled paragraph might look something like this:
.L2 Fort Sumter

7n

April 12, 1861
‘LE

Also like “.IP”, labels that contain internal spaces must have backslashes
before those spaces or be quoted:

Macros

226 Paragraphs | Widows and Orphans|
.3L "Bull Run" |Manassas] "July 21, 1861" 15n

Some of the older versions of -ms do not offer the multilabeled paragraphs, but
they can easily be added to /usr/lib/tmacs.

Widows and Orphans

The paragraph commands in -ms automatically check to see if there are
at least two lines left at the bottom of the page, so that there are no widowed
single lines of a paragraph seeming so forlorn down there. The matter of
orphans is something else again, since the paragraph macro has no way of
knowing how long the paragraph is going to be. One solution is to utilize the
keep macro |‘“.KS/.KE’”’| described earlier, but this has disadvantages since the
macro will break the text whenever it’s invoked. A workable and fairly
dependable alternative is to place a ‘“.ne 2” two or so lines before the end of the
paragraph, which is usually the beginning of a new paragraph. The new paragraph can be retrieved globally in ed, edit, ex, and vi, and then an insert com-

mand can be specified two lines up; here is the command, given with a ‘:’
prompt and <cr> for the RETURN key:

:g/°\.LLPIQ|P$/—2i\<er>
ne

2<er>

To make sure that the last paragraph in the text is also protected, you might
issue a similar set of commands, two lines before the end of the text. And,
naturally, if the two text lines ending a particular paragraph are extremely
short, “.ne 2” may not be sufficient. But generally it works.

REPETITIVE LETTERS

Three Commands
Repetitive letters in nroff/troff do not require macros, but the combination of commands is complex enough to be discussed here anyway. Again flexibility comprises the major virtue of the system, because the technique contains
no limits; again clumsiness is the major disadvantage. In order to compose
repetitive letters, you will need a source file containing the common text and

usual format instructions [here called ‘originalfile’]; you will also need an
external file [‘variablefile’] which stores all the addresses and other changes
specific to the letter, each variable element —and this is very important—
separated by a space.

To read in the material from the second file, you would

insert the “.rd” (‘read’) command at each point where a variable would be
printed. Normally, “.rd” is a signal to read in information from the terminal
(the famous old ‘standard input’), and this is its function in the memo macro.
But UNIX offers the capacity to use a file as input through its handy redirection operator ‘<,’ so that the necessary shell command for repetitive letters is
the following:
$ nroff originalfile <variablefile

A quick check of the “.rd” command will show that it accepts any amount of
information until it encounters two carriage returns in a row, and hence the
space between each variable element.
The following is a brief example of a source file, the part of a repetitive
letter that everyone would receive:

227

228 Repetitive Letters [Three Commands]

Macros

tl ’’ Office U.S. Military Telegraph,’
tl ’’War Department,’
tl ”’Washington, D.C., Nov. 15,1864.’
sp 4
nf
\"no fill for addresses

rd
ft

\"read in first variable (addresses)
ec

sp 3
Dear Governor
rd
\"read in name
8p
ti .5i
Please send, as soon as practicable, exactly, or approximately,
the aggregate of votes cast in
rd
\"read in state
at the late election. It is desired with reference
to the forthcoming Message.

tl] ’”A Lincoln’

The variable file might look like this, the ‘(blank)’ standing for an empty line:
Sam] Cony
Gov of Maine
Augusta Me
(blank)

(blank)
Maine
(blank)
Jos. A. Gilmore
Gov of N.H.
Concord N.H.
(blank)
