(blank)
New Hampshire
Notice that the address section has to be preceded by a no-fill command; input
is processed just like material in the file, and that means the default condition
in which text is filled in. This works to the user’s advantage at other points in
the letter, because any amount of material can be read in and the page filled
and composed accordingly. Notice, too, that each set of variants contains
exactly the same number of texts as there are “.rd” commands in originalfile;
if there were one fewer or more, all subsequent letters would be off in their
information by one segment, a potentially embarrassing position to be in.

Repeating the process [‘‘.so filename” or ‘‘.nx filename”’]
The example given above is incomplete, in that only the first set would be
formatted in the text. When the processor reached the end of the file, it would
sense this and shut down operations, so all the rest of the addresses would be
ignored, and that would be that; so you need a command that will start the
process over again, and you have a choice: the “.so” [‘source’] command, which

reads in an outside file; or a relatively obscure command, the
“.nx” [‘next’],

Macros

Repetitive Letters [Three Commands] 229
which switches to another file. Since the command will be placed at the end of
the file, the difference between the two is inconsequential, and both will work
equally well for your purposes. The important thing to remember is that the
filename given after the command must be the original file itself, so that it
can start over; then when it comes to the “.rd”, the next address will be read

in. Finally, since letters usually start on a new page, a “.bp” [begin pagel] is
placed before the “.so” or “.nx”, like this:
to the forthcoming Message.
sp 3
tl’ A Lincoln’
.bp
nx originalfile

I suppose the trick of this is imagining the file reprocessing itself, sort of like
one of Escher’s hands drawing itself.

Stopping operations [.ex]
The analogy with Escher is apt enough, because with the “.so” or “.nx”
you have produced an infinite loop, destined to keep cranking out letters long
after the address file has been exhausted. To prevent this, we rely on a command that is even more rare than “.rd” or “.nx”: “.ex” [‘exit’], which immediately stops processing.! This appears in the address file, after the last variable,
separated by a space, like so:
Michael Hahn
Govr of Louisiana
New Orleans via Cairo

(blank)
Hahn
(blank)
Louisiana

(blank)
CX

Unfortunately, ‘immediately’ means as soon as the “.rd” is encountered, after

the first part of the letter, so a partial letter will be processed before the “.ex”
is read.

This can be discarded.

Summarizing the Process
The routine for repetitive letters is sufficiently dependable to warrant a
summary checklist:

(1)

lhe command
for one section.

The letter itself [here called ‘originalfile’] is in all respects a normal
nroff/troff text.

“.ab” [‘abort’| will work as well, but with an unkind message, and three offbeat commands are enough

Macros

230 Repetitive Letters [Three Commands]
(2)

For each variable in the letter, there should be a “.rd” command on its
own line.

(3)

The variables themselves are stored in an outside file, each one separated
by a space.

(4)

The original file should have a “.bp” on the second-to-last line, so that the
next letter will begin on a new page.

(5)

On the last line of the original file, a “.so” or “.nx” command should
appear, instructing the roffs to repeat the process; the command must be

followed by the name of the original file.

(6)

The entire sequence concludes with a “.ex’”” command at the end of the
variable file, separated by a space from the last variable.

(7)

The formatting command uses the redirection operator ‘<’ to insert the
variables:
$ nroff originalfile <variablefile

or

$ troff originalfile <variablefile

SECTION HEADINGS

Section Headings: What They Do
Aside from the obvious function of introducing new sections of a text, section headings can perform a great many tasks which, in tandem with chapter
headings on one side and paragraph definitions on the other, produce a more
consistent and error-free document. The following discussion will pursue the
normal pattern, describing essential functions first, then options, and finally
the sort of section macros found in -me and -ms.

There are usually four functions which all section headings are expected
to perform: 1) placing a certain amount of space between it and the last section; 2) centering itself, or, more frequently, placing itself flush left; 3) setting
itself off from the text in bold face (and perhaps a larger type size) if typeset,
or underlined in typewriting; and, 4) either starting at the top of a new page,
or at least insuring that there are enough lines remaining at the foot of the
page to print a little of the new section text. All four functions are readily

available from the store of basic commands, so that a preliminary macro [‘Sh’]
can easily be formulated:

.de Sh
sp 2
in 0
.ps +2
.B
ne 4

The various amounts are, of course, purely arbitrary. For some, a single space
between sections might be sufficient; a “.ce” might be used to center the heading, rather than canceling any indent so that the header is flush left; the point

231

Macros

232 Section Headings | What They Dol

size command may be greater or less, and the font italics instead of bold (both
underline in nroff); the ‘need’ command can certainly be less, since this one

anticipates a space after a one-line section heading, and guarantees at least
two lines of text beyond this. Under the worst conditions, this macro could
leave six blank lines at the bottom of the page, which might be extravagant.
But even as it performs its basic tasks, the macro just described causes

two major problems. The most important of these is the absence of instructions to turn off the point size and font after the heading has been printed.
The second, a more minor matter, concerns restoring the text to its usual
indent (if there is one) after placing the section heading against the left margin.

There are at least four solutions, all of which follow a familiar pattern:

(1) you may place the commands within environments 1 or 2; (2) you may use
the paragraph macro to return to default settings, so that a call for a section
heading must be accompanied by a call for a paragraph macro (the solution in
-ms); (3) you may ‘bracket’ the section heading itself by devising a macro
which returns to the formatting commands in effect; or (4) you may attempt to
make the commands as temporary as possible (as in -me).
Each option has its advantages and disadvantages. The first is most convenient, even though the point size command will have to be changed,! and it
does run some risk of invisibly ‘cluttering’ that environment with unusual
commands. The second, utilizing the paragraph macro to reintroduce default
settings, has the benefit of habit, but then you’re stuck with an inflexible format.

The third option, which requires a concluding macro on the line below the
section heading, allows more range in format, but it will cause difficulties if
that macro is ever forgotten. The basic design of this option is discussed in the
section on paragraphs, but perhaps it should be reviewed here as well. In
bracketing, you can use the beginning macro to store all current settings in
number registers; the concluding macro will retrieve them after the section
heading has been printed. In the example below, the current values for indent,
point size, and font are placed in ‘Sh’ and returned in the ending macro, ‘Se’:
.de Sh
sp 2
nr Ci \\nGi
in 0

nr Cp \\n(.s
_ps +2

Hivgd @ aAWre lS
.B
-ne 4

.de Se
sp

in \\n(Ciu
.ps \\n(Cs
l

The ‘w’ is necessary for the indent command in ‘Se’ because “‘\n(.i” measures

With this option, a section heading would look something like the following:
sink and be no more.
.Sh
Section 4: Humorous

Sketches

Dear Madam, Without apologising for being egotistical,

Though this approach cannot anticipate every situation (there is the perennial
problem of two-column text, for example), it has the advantage of a certain
amount of control over processing, as well as a certain flexibility.
The fourth option, that of temporary settings, is the easiest and at the
same time the least straightforward, in that the section heading must be
‘quoted’ if it is more than one word (which is likely), and there are difficulties
if the heading contains more than one line. In its most basic version, this

macro makes use of the ‘temporary indent’ command (which affects only the
next line) and the ‘argument’ function, encountered in the labeled paragraph.
There the labels were entered on the same line as the call for the macro, having previously been identified within in the macro definition through a convention (“‘\\$1” standing for the first string, and so on). The new settings for size
and font can, then, be turned off from within the macro, like this:

.de Sh
sp 2
ti 0
ne 4

\s+2\fB\\$1\fP\s0
Sp

The “\fP” |previous font] allows a return to the font in operation before the
macro, and therefore has the same effect as “\n(Cf” above. The main difference to remember here is that the section heading must be entered on the same
line as the “.Sh,” rather than on the following line, as with the other options.

Since almost every section heading contains more than one word, the
heading for this macro will have to be read as a single string through the
clumsy expedients of either quotation or adding ‘unpaddable spaces.’ The heading in the example given above, then, would be placed in single or double
quotes, like so
‘Sh "Section 4: Humorous

Sketches"

or written with backslashes before each space:

Sh Section\ 4:\ Humorous\ Sketches
Of these, I suppose the first is a little easier and more dependable.

It is possi-

234 Section Headings | What They Do]

Macros

ble to skip the second set of quotation marks, but in this case not advisable
since other arguments may be added later.
A section title is usually not longer than a line (if it is, see the discussion
on chapter macros); but you may well want two separate lines, printing a sec-

tion and subsection, for instance. Naturally, an entirely separate macro may
be constructed for each level of a complex organizational format, and suggestions for them will be found later on. But a single subsection seems hardly
worth the trouble. If it is to work, however, the macro should be smart enough
to count the number of arguments and print them with appropriate spacing, or

ignore the spacing if no subsection is included for a particular section. To do
this, the macro might take advantage of the read-only number register *.$,’
which counts the number of arguments that appear after the macro has been

invoked.

Since the new macro may involve more than one line, the simple “.ti”

must be abandoned for the more complicated system of a number register to
save the current indent, as before. In the following definition, the macro provides for a second argument of a slightly smaller type size than the first; otherwise it should be familiar:
.de Sh
nr Ci \\n(.i
Sp 2

\s +2\fB\\ $1\fP\sO
if \\nGS=2it\
-br

\s+1\fB\\$2\fP\s0\}
in \\n(Ciu
sp

A third heading may be added to this, as may spacing in between each heading,
indenting, and so forth. I suppose that the only thing that can’t be done would
be to make the ‘if’ test recursive, so that up to nine arguments would be available in a single routine. But that does seem excessive.
If the word ‘Section’ is always going to appear, and the section number is
to be incremented throughout the chapter, the macro can automatically accomplish both tasks, just as the chapter macro produced its own title and incremented chapter number. In fact, an example of this would look a lot like the
examples given in the discussion of chapter macros:

nr Se 0 1 \"original setting at the head of the file
.de Sh

This relationship can be extended in both directions: up to the chapter and

zero, and the section counter can reset number paragraphs in turn, each in its
own numerical format. The following example will produce, in addition to a
section heading, the registers necessary for sequentially numbered indented
paragraphs whose elements represent three levels of classification. The section
macro sets the paragraph number registers (here ‘x,’ ‘y,’ ‘z’) and establishes the
format for each (for the purposes of illustration, upper-case alphabetic, lowercase roman, lower-case alphabetic):
nr Se 0 1
.de Sh

\s+2\fBSection \\nX.\\n+(Se: \\$1\fP\s0
Sp

Each different paragraph or subparagraph could then increment at appropriate
levels, and perhaps utilize greater indents and smaller type sizes. Since the
actual numbering would not occur until formatting, each paragraph could be
separated and transposed.

Running Headers
Section titles can be automatically printed within running headers or
footers by a simple provision in the header or footer macro. First the section
macro adds a string definition (“.ds Sr” below) which then defines and redefines the string in terms of the argument given immediately after the ‘Sh’. It
works in this fashion:
-nr Se 01
.de Sh

.ds Sr \\$1
sp 2
.ti O
ne 4

\s+2\fBSection \\nX.\\n+(Se: \\$1\fP\s0
sp

The string definition is entered first because the need command might trip to a
new page, and in that case the header must use the new definition. And, as ;
before, the two backslashes are necessary throughout so that the processor will

delay reading the argument until “.Sh” is actually invoked within the text.

Macros

236 Section Headings |Running Headers]

For its part, the header macro (or ‘PT’ in -ms) includes the defined string
as a component in its running title. The following header, a simple garden
variety, prints the section title in the center and alternates odd (right-hand)
and even (left-hand) pages by placing the page number [*%’] at the edge:
.de He
‘sp

if o tl ’\\*(Sr’%’
af etlos\\«(Sr"
te

<n 0 He

\"zero is the usual header trap

All of the section’s number registers may also be printed in the running
header, either as part of the defined string in the section macro:
ds Sr \\nX.\\n+(Se: \\$1
or simply as elements in the alternating titles in the header macro:

if o tl ’\\nX.\\n(Se: \\*#(Sr’%’
Both of these work, though the first may be simpler because you would need to
increment “Se” separately each time the section macro has been invoked.

Outline Format (Levels of Headings)
Last of all, you should be able to designate various levels of section headings, so that general and subordinate sections automatically receive some
organizing label. The most typical example of such labeling is the numerical
outline, in which section ‘1.’ contains subsections ‘1.1.’ and ‘1.2.’; subsection
‘1.2.’ may be in turn divided into ‘1.2.1.,’ ‘1.2.2.,’ and ‘1.2.3.’ Both -me and -ms
handle this in different ways, but are similar in using a single section macro
that accepts a specific ‘argument’ as a directive to increment at a given level.
This can get confusing.
An alternative would be to create four or five different section macros,
one for each level. The following example is for the fourth level in a system of
five; its name is ‘S4,’ and it increments number register ‘4S.’* Since invoking
the fourth level should automatically zero the fifth level, that register is reset:
nr4S 01
.de S4

sp 2

ti 0
ne

4

Section Headings in -me
The section heading macro -in -me is uncharacteristically complicated,
with a great many options. This arises in part because the macro can also be
used to generate outlines, with indentation automatically corresponding to the
‘level’ of section heading. There is also a great deal of flexibility in specifying
a discontinuous sequence of numbers should the need arise; but all of these
options do make the macro rather more complex than it should be. Two basic
commands are available, “.sh” for numbered section headings, and “.uh” for
unnumbered. Both accept quoted arguments which are then transformed to
section titles; both check for approximately three lines remaining on the page;
both temporarily cancel any indentation which might be in effect; both insert
one vertical space before the title; and both print the title in ten point Times
bold font (usually). The basic form, the unnumbered section macro, looks like
this:
.uh "First Inaugural"

As the example indicates, a multi-word title must be quoted or each space preceded with a backslash. The numbered macro takes this basic form as well, but
with many more format options.
True to form, -me allows each one of the parameters to be readjusted,

simply by accessing an appropriate number register. In the list below, as is
customary, ‘N’ refers to any number (font 3 or size 10), and M any measurement (.33i or 4c).

Macros

238 Section Headings |Section Headings in -me|

nr so M
rss M

nr sf N

The “\n(so” only affects the section head itself, and not the rest of the text.
When the macro has printed its heading, all predefined values (font, point size)
once again apply.
Numbered

headings in -me

The notion controlling numbered headings in -me is depth, which has

been described earlier and will be described in the -ms section as ‘levels.’ The
term involves grouping sections together under a consistent system of labels; if
a major section is introduced with a ‘1.,’ a subsection of this may be called
‘1.1.” and a subsection of that ‘1.1.1.’ Here ‘depth’ is apt, because -me offers
the easy facility to indent subsections and sub-subsections automatically.
The “.sh” macro understand three different types of arguments: 1) that in
the first position specifies the depth, 2) that in the second is the section title,
and 3) in the remaining positions as many as six trailing numbers which reset
at various depths. The first and second of these are straightforward enough:
sh 3 "John Bell, Constitutional

Union"

The ‘3’ would instruct the roffs to print a ‘1.1.1.’ with the title underlined
(nroff) or in boldface (troff). The depth number may also be a plus or minus,
which considerably eases the amount of editing if you need to add a more general level of classification. If the number is left off, that is, if the “.sh”
appears by itself, the macro increments at the depth which was stipulated last.
To take advantage of this and print a section title as well, you must fall back
on ‘quoting’ an empty argument:
sh "" "Wiley & Wood"
Similarly, if there is no title, "" may be used to direct the formatter to the reset

options. An underscore (‘_’) instead of the title sets that depth back to zero, so
that numbering can begin all over again.
The third type of argument, involving trailing numbers, is more complicated, but it does provide a convenient method for resetting a specific level to
any number. After the title, you may enter up to six numbers or hyphens, the
numbers specifying a reset at that depth, and the hyphens no reset. If, for
example, you wished to print a section title on the fifth level, resetting the
third and fourth levels to six and four, but leaving the other levels the same,
you would enter this:
sh 5 "Thurlow

Weed"

- - 6 4

This will be formatted as ‘1.1.6.4.1’ if there has been no previous increment to
the first, second, and fifth levels. This sort of provision becomes very useful

when only one page needs to be processed, and it’s cheaper and easier to establish that page as a separate file, rather than passing the entire document
through the formatter.

Macros

Section Headings [Format Options in -me| 239

Format Options in -me

The indent component of -me’s section macro is normally turned off, but
can be activated by assigning a measurement to number register ‘si.’ This
value is used as a constant distance, which when multiplied by the depth
number (less one) produces the amount of indentation. For instance,
nrsi li
sh 1 "The Douglas Debates"
sh 2 "Ottawa, Illinois"
sh 3 "The Principal Issues"

will left-justify the first section heading, indent one inch at the second level,
two inches at the third, and so on. The number register ‘so,’ mentioned above,
allows an independent indent on the first level.
The other commands and registers associated with the numbered section
macro in -me are more extensive than most would need. But they do represent
examples of the sometimes extraordinary capacity in -me for all kinds of manipulation. Both “.uh” and “.sh” use the macro ‘§$p’ to print the title and section
number if these exist; to do something really different with the section heading (section numbers always in a different font, for example), you may redefine
this macro, though it would be prudent to copy in the entire ‘$p’ macro at the
beginning of your file and change it only at that specific place (in the font
example just given, where the second argument is printed). Another macro,
‘$0’ [zero] is invoked each time ‘$p’ is called. This allows, among other things,
an index of section titles, because it will automatically save as arguments the
newly defined title, followed by the section number and by the depth number.
Through a diversion command, it becomes easy to store each for printing later,
like so:

.de $0

‘br \"break to force out section heading in text
da Sx
\"divert append to storage area Sx
\\$1 {\\$2] \\n%\"title, section number in brackets, page
br
\"break to complete the storage
di
\"end diversion

When a list of section headings is needed at the end of the document, you need
only add the following to your text file:

bp

\"begin a new page

nf

\"preserve the entries, one to a line

Sx

\"print storage area

The section on indexes gives a few more techniques for generating them.
Naturally, you can vary the form of the string which has been diverted,
depending on the nature of the index.
The section macro will also call a macro of your definition with the name
‘$N,’ in which N represents the section level. If you would like the section
heading to be printed with a different font or more or less spacing, you merely
define the appropriate macro. For instance, making the section heading on the
fourth level italic, nine-on-eleven, preceded by two vertical spaces in eleven

point would be handled thus:

240 Section Headings |Format Options in -me|

Macros

de $4
i
._ps 9
.vs 11
sp 2

An entire formatting system can be constructed in this way to control all of
the six headings. Naturally, you will be expected to keep the levels straight.
Finally, it is possible in -me to start at a level without incrementing and
even without printing a title, through the command “.sx N”. The command
inserts a space, then prints the text. As with “sh,” Ncan be signed with a
plus or minus to indicate relative depth. The command of course has a limited
utility, but it is the sort that’s needed occasionally—as is any command that
turns off automatic features.

Section Macros in -ms

There are two types of section macro in -ms, a simple one (“.SH”) and a
more complicated numerical one (““.NH’”). These skip one space, then set the
string on the next line in boldface (underlined in nroff) and in the point size
defined by “.nr PS”; the macros also initialize if it’s necessary (see the discussion on cover page macros if initialization escapes you), and make sure there’s
enough room at the bottom of the page for a little text, though this is not foolproof. The heading may be more than one line, and separate lines can be
printed with a “.br” in between:
.SH
Response to a Serenade
.br
April 10, 1865
The second line will be boldface as well, as will all subsequent lines until the
function is stopped with a re-initialization, usually a paragraph macro (“.PP”,
“.LP”), though other macros will return the document to its default settings.
The section macros’ built-in ‘need’ command checks for four lines remaining on
the page, so a multiple-line section heading may cause problems in guaranteeing that the entire heading is not abandoned down there. But since few headings are more than three lines, and since the paragraph macros contain their
own ‘need’ (set in -ms to 1.1 vertical spaces), this is usually not a problem.
Numerical headings

The numerical heading macro (‘‘.NH”’) will acknowledge instructions for
incrementing numbers on as many as five levels. To accomplish this, you must
specify as an argument, after the “.NH”, the level you wish for the heading.
The roffs will keep track of these, and increment accordingly; the higher levels
will retain their value. If, for example, you wished to classify a budget, there
might be gloss divisions of receipts and expenditures, and subclasses within:
.NH 1
Receipts: $1,394,796,007.62
.NH 2
Customs: $102,316,152.99

SUPERSCRIPTS AND SUBSCRIPTS

General Considerations

Outside of eqn, there are two different ways to place characters above or
below the line. The first is a single command of the sort discussed under Basic
Functions: “\u” [for ‘up’] and “‘\d” [for ‘down’ one-half vertical space, and “‘\r”
[for ‘reverse’— move up] one full space. These will raise or lower any subsequent letters or numbers until a countervailing command is given |two “\d’s”
in the case of ‘‘\r’].

In simple scripting, then, the two commands

precede and

follow the letter or number to be raised or lowered like so:

Dr. Ross,\u6\d
which produces:
Dr. Ross,”
The down command

is of considerable importance, since otherwise the for-

matter will have no idea of when the scripting has ended and will continue to
print text at a half-line up for the rest of the document.
A second option is to use the vertical motion command |\v’M’], in which M
represents the distance moved; this command will raise any following text if M

is minus and lower the text if Mis either unsigned or a plus. The command is
more complicated to enter, as the following example indicates:
Dr. Ross,\v’-.5v’6\v’
+.5v’
But the gain in troff is a greater flexibility in placing the superscript or subscript. This flexibility is improved by relying on m’s for the unit of measurement, so that the movement will be proportional to the type size.

242

Macros

Superscripts and Subscripts |General Considerations| 243
With either the single-letter commands “\u” and “‘\d” or the ‘\v” the
point size of the scripted text can be changed through the in-line ‘\s” command:

Lee Kimball,\u\s—27\s+2\d

\"or \s—2\u7\d\s +2

Since the single-letter commands depend on vertical spacing (“v” as a unit of
measurement), the order does not matter, though it’s always wise to make such
commands symmetrical in order to keep them straight. If, on the other hand,
you use m’s with the vertical command, the order will be important, because
that unit depends on the point size; the following two similar strings would in
fact produce considerably different results:

Harrison Grimsley,\s—2\v —0.4m’6\v’ +0.4m’\s +2

and
Harrison Grimsley,\v’ —0.4m’\s—26\s +2\v’ +0.4m’
Both are legitimate, but the second would raise the superscript much higher
than the first. You should also note that here the mirror image symmetry in
commands is absolutely essential.

Superscripts and Subscripts in -me
-me offers superscript and subscript commands which are defined strings
very nearly resembling the pattern given above. The superscript begins with a
backslash, asterisk, and open bracket and ends with the first two characters
and a close bracket:

Kansas was settled, for example, in 1854.\*|6\*|

It was

The subscript command is identical in form, and utilizing the angle bracket
(s <a 27) :

calculated the cost\*<n\*>

to the Treasury

In troff, the script is raised or lowered four-tenths of an m, and the size is then

reduced by three points.
down (‘‘\d’) commands.

In nroff, -me substitutes the simple up (“\u”) and

A particular advantage to -me’s system of brackets and angle brackets is
that it affords a means to set off the script if the printer for some reason does
not have the capacity to move by half lines. It accomplishes this through a
test involving the read-only number register “\n(.V”, which indicates the smallest possible vertical movement; if, then, this register is equal to one vertical
space, half-lines are not possible and -me will redefine its commands to print
the scripted text enclosed in simple brackets. In the package, the test looks
like this:

if \n(.V=1v \{\
.ds | |
.ds | |

244 Superscripts and Subscripts |Superscripts and Subscripts in -ms |
and so on.

Macros

This internal checking mechanism affords just the flexibility for

which -me is celebrated and represents one more reason why this package

should be examined even if you’re committed to another.

Superscripts and Subscripts in -ms

In -ms you may superscript or subscript text with the macros “.SU” or
“SB”. The definitions for the two are quite short and worth quoting:
de SU

if n \\$1\u\\$2\d
if t \\$1\s—3\v’
—.6m’\\ $2\v’ +.6m’\s0
.de SB

if n \\$1\d\\$2\u
if t \\$1\s—3\v’ +.6m’\\$2\v’ —.6m’\s0

Both macros expect two arguments: the string to be scripted and the script
itself. And the ‘if’ test insures that these macros will use the half-line motion
‘\u” and “\d” in nroff; in troff they will reduce the point size of the raised or
lowered string.
You would request a superscript by entering the command “.SU” at the
beginning of a line, followed by the character or word which appears before the
superscript, then a space, and finally the superscript itself:
reminding him of his neighbor, Mrs. Sallie
.SU Ward, 7
‘who had a very large progeny
Subscripts would be invoked in the same way:
SB \-x 2
which becomes

-x,
As the examples show, the usual rules for arguments apply, in that the first
string may contain punctuation. Each string may also include internal spaces,
as long as the string has been ‘quoted’ with single or double quotes, or the
spaces have been made ‘unpaddable’:
.SU Chase. "note 8"

.SU Chase. note\ 8

A superscript or subscript which is to be printed before a string (as in footnote
numbers) presents a slight problem, since no word precedes the superscript.
This is solved by inserting a set of double quotes after the command:

Macros

Superscripts and Subscripts |Superscripts and Subscripts in -ms| 245
FS
.LP
OU
1
Many of these stories appear in the appendices

(The “.LP” was necessary to provide enough space between the footnote separation line and the superscript.) As you might have had occasion to observe,
entering this sort of ‘dummy’ string is fairly common procedure. When the
roffs process the text, no double quotes will appear.!
In most instances, the -ms macros will serve you well, but there are two
exceptions which require warning. The first involves an argument containing
certain foreign accents; the second concerns the automatic space after the
script. In some versions of -ms, a few diacritical marks (notably the umlaut)
will cause an error message to the effect that the argument is too long, and
processing will cease. A temporary solution is simply to insert both of the size
and vertical commands, like this (here for a footnote numbered nine):

G\* :unter.\s —3\v’—.6m’9\v’ +.6m’\s0
A longer-range solution is to redefine the accent so that it and any preceding
letters fit within a rough 86-character limit.
The second problem arises because both nroff and troff normally insert
one space at the end of each line in the source file; after the superscript or subscript, therefore, a space will inevitably occur. Though most often this is desirable or at least acceptable (as in the footnote example), occasionally it is not.
In European style, for instance, footnote numbers are placed within punctuation, rather than outside. Even in American usage, a note number will often
be included within a bracket. Apart from literally inserting the basic commands (as above), one solution would be to redefine the macro to accept
another argument if there are three:

TABLES

Tbl,

a Preprocessor

Tables are the arrangement of individualized elements in columns (vertical alignment) and rows (horizontal), the elements occupying specific ‘cells.’ In
UNIX word processing, tables are formatted through a semi-independent program called tbl, which establishes the basic columns and rows before the roffs
begin their work. For that reason it is called a ‘preprocessor’ and is invoked
first, then sent (piped) to nroff or troff in this fashion (here given with the
system prompt ‘$’):

$ tbl filename | nroff

or
$ tbl filename | troff
If used in conjunction with another common preprocessor, eqn, the tbl command should precede:

$ tbl filename | eqn | troff

or
$ tbl filename | neqn | nroff
You may take it as a rule of thumb that, because of typos and so forth, such
combinations hardly ever work the first time, and perhaps find a way to skip

the first time.!

'For instance, because of the way both tbl and troff ‘strip’ backslashes, a literal backslash in a text
may need to be

246

Macros

Tables |Tbl, a Preprocessor| 247
There is no limit to the number of rows in tbl, but it is bound to a maximum of twenty columns, and with numerical entries sixteen columns may be
the most that can be printed. By default, the table is printed against the lefthand margin (‘left-adjusted’), but this can be changed. In my experience, tbl
has functioned reasonably well, but a number of my students have found its
complexity intimidating. Michael Lesk’s Bell Laboratories manual? itself
recommends first perusing the examples, which is a very good idea.

As a basic premise, tbl insists that you know the maximum number of
columns in your table. Once this has been decided, you can build outward in
stages, developing a more and more complex command sequence which more
and more closely fits your needs. The following discussion will begin with the
most essential functions (simple column production), then move to headings,
then to text within tables, then to reformatting various sections within the
table. The large number of options will enter the discussion last.
tbl is a bracketing macro, beginning with a “.TS” |start table] on its own

line and finishing with a “.TE” lend table], also on its own line.

It can cross

pages, retaining the same headings throughout; it allows boxes and columns.
Though the processor was meant to work as well in nroff as in troff, I have
found that troff suits it better because of its flexibility in spacing, greater density of text, and relatively stable output devices; in typewriting tables, by contrast, the variety of terminals and printers can often translate into incomplete
or faulty processing.

Basics: Deep in the Heart of tbl
At its minimum, tbl requires three components: (1) the “.TS/.TE” commands, (2) the actual material which will fill the columns (with some indication of what constitutes an individual unit), and (3) distinct instructions on
how the columns and rows should be organized. The first of these is easy
enough; the entire table (except for footnotes) must be contained between the
“TS” and the “.TE”; since tbl has its own complicated ‘keep’ function, no keep
macros are necessary. The second requirement is a little less straightforward,
because of the need to delimit recognizable units; in normal circumstances, for
example, you might presume that the material to be formatted in the table—
numbers or words, usually—can simply be listed, like so:
postal revenues $12,438,253.78
postal expenditures 12,644,786.20
excess 206,652.42 |206,532.42]
Unfortunately, the formatter would have no clue whatsoever about what would
belong in which column: a space is simply not a very reliable separator, since
entities that we think of as individual frequently contain spaces (‘est. 1984,’ ‘in
press,’ ‘ad nauseam’). So, for better and for worse, tbl uses another separator,
the tab, as a default |this is printed in the manual as a “T’ within a circle].
Tabs do work because they are fairly natural separators, and can line up text;
but they don’t line up very well, and this often makes proofreading difficult.
Tabs, moreover, sometimes present problems for other computer operations. In
tbl, you may define another character as a separator |with the “tab (x )”
entered as four backslashes.

See Appendix VII for examples.

Report 49, 1976).
2Tbl — A Program to Format Tables by M. E. Lesk (Bell Laboratories Computing Science Technical

Macros

248 Tables |Basics: Deep in the Heart of tbl|

command], but hardly ever with an improvement in readability. The third and
final component necessary for a table is a set of precise instructions indicating
the number of columns and the placement of the numbers or words within

those columns (left, right, centered; if numbers, the position of the decimal).

In tbl, these instructions appear on a line or lines by themselves, taking the
form of single letters. One letter |‘key-letter’ in the manual] is entered for
each column desired, the instructions standing below the “.TS” and above the
material itself.

The four most common’ are the following:
1 = left-adjust
c = center in column
r = right-adjust
n = number aligned on decimal point |discussed in ‘Options’ |

To distinguish these instructions from the material to be formatted, tbl
requires you to terminate the last of them with a period. “cc.” means, then,
that there will be two columns, and the contents in both columns are to be centered; any combination of key-letters can, of course, be used.

Putting the three components together, we can create our first working
example:

.TS
en:
California(tab)110,000 (Nearly)
Connecticut(tab)86,616
Delaware(tab) 16,924
Illinois(tab)348,235

.TE
(The space between the c and n is not necessary, but it does make it easier to
read.) In format, the example looks like this:

California
Connecticut
Delaware
Illinois

110,000 (Nearly)
86,616
16,924
348,235

All of the elements I’ve described are absolutely necessary: the absence of a
“.TS” would obviously not help, and forgetting the “.TE” will ultimately produce an electronic gastritis; tbl could handle more than two ‘key letters’ for
this table, but needs at least two, and forgetting the period would mean an
attempt to read ‘California’ as a weird series of key letters; and finally, the
tabs insure that ‘Nearly’ attaches to its appropriate cell. tbl can accomplish a
great deal more, but it won’t accomplish anything at all without these three
components.

There are eight in all. The fifth, ‘a’ |for lists of words—‘alphabetic subcolumns’| will be discussed in the ‘Options’

section below. The sixth and seventh, ‘s’ and ‘\’,’ pertain to headings.
column with a full-width horizontal line.

And the last, the underscore

(‘“ ”), fills the

Missing Data
A table that is complete in every cell has usually been fabricated solely
for the purpose of demonstration. tbl understands this and makes it fairly
easy for you to leave cells blank; you can tab through them if they’re at the
beginning or in the middle of the row, or simply ignore the cell if it is in the
final column. In the following example, the first element in row two was missing, as were the second and third elements in row three:

abs,
nenen:
1,364(tab)25,651(tab)53,143

(tab)7,625(tab) 22,681
e224
62,811(tab)26,693(tab)6,368

‘TR
If you prefer an em dash or three periods in the blanks, they can be inserted in
place of the missing value:

1,364(tab)\(em(tab)53,143
or
1,364(tab)...(tab)53,143
Cells which are not centered (as these were not) may need some ‘unpaddable’
spaces (‘\ ’) before the dots (‘\ \ ...’) for the sake of appearance; the discussion
of options further down lists ways to enter repeating characters, if you wish to
fill the entire cell with dots or dashes. Or, if a number of these recur in a
column, you may want to peruse the reformatting section, also below.

Headings

tbl assumes that most tables are pyramidal in structure: that is, there
will be a general heading, then subheadings, and further division until, finally,
the table is spread out fully, in all its columns. And it also expects another
usual case, that the general heading and subheadings each occupy a single
line, one below another, until the actual contents of the table appear.
On the basis of these assumptions, tbl provides for headings by permit-

ting the instruction section (the “cc.” above) to accept multiple lines. Each
line is understood as corresponding to a descending level of heading, with the
last line governing the words or numbers in the columns (tbl knows this is the
last line because of the period). To produce, then, two centered headings on
two columns of numbers, one would enter the following:

.TS
cc
nn.
1860(tab)1864
272,143(tab)280,645
128,331(tab) 143,331

146,216(tab)91,300 (Nearly)
.TE

Macros

250 Tables |Headings|

cells would be requested in this way:
TS

First Place

Missouri

Breckinridge
Missouri
Oregon
Pennsylvania
Tennessee

tbl is quite literal in what it considers a heading, even if humans aren’t. We
would, for instance, consider the heading ‘population (millions)’ a single heading, but if the ‘(millions)’ is to be centered below the ‘population,’ then it has to
be considered separately. This is true even of elements which, not in your
wildest fantasies, would you consider a separate component in a series of headings: a partial horizontal line, for example. But you can hold off on boxes and
line drawing for a while.

Crossing the Columns: ‘Spanning’
The foregoing raises the problem of the general heading which is supposed to be centered on all the columns. For this you must use the key-letter
‘s’ (for ‘spanned’), which is entered just like ‘I’, ‘r’, ‘c’, and ‘n’. The ‘s’ is placed
in the second, third, or whatever column, instructing the formatter to continue
the contents of the previous column in that column, spreading out that con-

tents between (or among) them.

Texts in Tables

So far, there has been little in tbl to get excited about, and indeed it is
only when we start trying to include extensive text in the cells that it starts

Macros

Tables |Texts in Tables| 253

sparking interest. Unfortunately, the technique needed to accomplish this is a
little strange and needs to be stared at for a while.

As always, the basic logic in tbl is that a specific series of words or
numbers, on a line and separated by tabs, is going to translate into a row,
divided into columns. Since a text is almost certainly going to occupy more
than a single line in the source file, tbl allows you to bracket that text with a
“T(/T}”. The begin-text mark “T{” may start a line (if the text is in the first
column of a row) or follow a tab (if the text is in a subsequent column); it is
then always followed by a carriage return:
Maryland(tab)2.3(tab)6
Massachusetts(tab)106.5(tab)34.4

T{
or

Maryland(tab)2.3(tab)6
Massachusetts(tab)106.5(tab)34.4
Michigan(tab)T{
The text follows, on as many lines as are needed; right adjustment is default,
so if you wish a ragged-right margin, a “.na’”’ command should precede the text:
Maryland(tab)2.3(tab)6
Massachusetts(tab)106.5(tab)34.4
Michigan(tab)T{
na
Lincoln’s figures for Michigan were called into question
recently, but 88,480 is probably a reasonably
accurate number.

Finally, at the end of the text, there is another carriage return, and the finishtext mark (‘T}’) begins a new line. If there is more material in that row, a tab
can follow and the line can continue as usual:

Maryland(tab)2.3(tab)6
Massachusetts(tab)106.5(tab)34.4
Michigan(tab)T{

na
Lincoln’s figures for Michigan were called into question
recently, but 88,480 is probably a reasonably
accurate number.

T}(tab)65.1

This is awkward, but the only really tricky part of the process is remembering

the bracket marks “T{” and “T}” [the “T” always goes first] and where they fit
in relation to the line.
Since a text needs a broader column than tbl normally calculates (based
on the widest element in the column), you are advised to take advantage of an

option to increase the horizontal size of a column: “w(M)” lin which Mis any

measurement], placed next to that column’s format letter; for example, “lw(2i)”
means that this column will be left-justified and two inches wide. This is not
necessary, mind you.” In its total assembled form, therefore, a typical text5Or perhaps you get pleasure out of the roffs squeezing text into impossible places.

Just when you weren't afraid to try tbl, you learn of options which should

in alphabetic order; this is not to be construed as an order of priority (it seldom
can be). The most important are, in fact, the globals.

Alphabetics
The key-letter ‘a’ generates alphabetic-style lists. This finds the longest
word in the column and centers it, which then establishes the left margin. For
lists of terms and names, this represents a more pleasing display. Because the
means for calculating the columns for alphabetics (‘a’) and numerics (‘n’) are
so different, the two key-letters cannot be applied to the same column.
Boxes

The commands for the various kinds of boxes available for your table are
given below in the section ‘Globals affecting the entire table.’ Basically, you
can demand a single-line box around an entire table |‘box’], a double-line box
around the table |‘doublebox’|, and a single-lined separation of each heading
and cell as well |‘allbox’|. If the table will carry beyond a single page, nroff

and troff offer a handy macro |“.T#’’| which will draw in the bottom and side
lines of that portion of the table. The best approach is to make the macro part
of your footer definition, like so:
.de Fo
TH
‘bp

-ms and -me do this automatically.
Column width

The width of a column can be varied by placing a “w(M)”—in which Mis
any measurement |default n’s]—next to the key-letter for that column. In this
it behaves like several other column options I|the troff font, point size, vertical
spacing, and the equal width and column-space!:

.TS
cs
1 rw(1i)
The Peninsular Campaign
McClelland(tab)Fort Monroe, Yorktown
.TE

This option is usually requisite for text blocks, establishing sufficient space
for
the text; and for that reason it has been discussed in the section on texts
in
tables, above. If by accident there is a longer element in that column
than the

width you've given, that longer width overrides the “w(M)”.

Macros

Tables | Various Options| 257
Equalizing column width

The columns can be made the same width across a table by inserting an
‘e’ after the key-letter, like so:
celeleccce.

This would make the first three columns equal, that width determined by the

widest single element of data among the three.
Globals affecting the entire table
Eight globals exist in tbl: two relating to the placement of the entire
table, four to its general appearance, one offering the opportunity to use
another character instead of the tab for separating elements, and finally one
relating to the equation-preprocessor eqn. All of these specifications are
entered in a general area immediately after the “.TS” but before the format
instructions; the entire section of globals is ended with a semicolon (‘;’).

The two globals which affect placement are “center” and “expand,” the
former positioning the table in the middle of the page, the latter across the
entire page-width.

The four globals governing the appearance of a table for the most part
define boxes: 1) “box” draws lines around the entire table; 2) “allbox” draws
lines around every heading and cell; 3) “doublebox” draws double lines around

the table; and 4) “linesize (N)” allows you to set the point size (N) for line
drawing, and for the horizontal and vertical lines described below.
The third global option allows you to define individual cells with alternate characters. Instead of a tab, you may choose any letter, number or punc-

tuation to separate elements in their cells with the “tab (c)”, in which c is the
alternate character. For instance, the request “tab (%)” will allow data to be
entered thus: 90,000%111,000%72,703. This may help.

The manual describes a final global that relates to eqn, stipulating the
delimiters which are needed for equations within the table. I presume the
basic logic was that the conventional marks for eqn, dollar-signs, would interfere with literal dollar-signs in the table; unfortunately, I have had no success
in getting the specified command, “delim (cd)” to work. The manual, in its
example, uses the conventional eqn definition for delimiters, and this does
work. In any case, you should not use the sharp-sign (#), the asterisk (*), or
the tilde (").
The following example illustrates a fair number of these globals:

.TS
center allbox tab(%);

C8.s
CiCee
Inn.

Border States
State%Lincoln%=roman McClelland sub alpha=
West-Virginia%
23,437% 10,437
Kansas %14,000% 4,000
.TE

Lines (Horizontal)

Horizontal lines drawn in a table may be either single or double; lines can
be produced to extend (1) over the width of the table, (2) over two or more
columns, (3) over the width of a single column, or (4) within a cell.
(1) The first, for the width of the table, takes a simple form: a single horizontal line, the underscore (‘_’), or the double line, an ‘equals’ sign (‘=’) can be
placed on its own line within the material section of the table, like so:

(4) The fourth kind of line, the shorter one within a cell, is created by

cell, but no longer. And, like the other horizontal lines just discussed, it will
be drawn midway through the cell.
Lines (vertical)

Like horizontal lines, vertical lines may be requested globally or locally:
(a) the length of the column or (b) for a number of consecutive cells in a
column or for an individualized cell. All of these can link with horizontal lines
to form boxes great and small.

(a) To produce a line down an entire column, you would enter a vertical
bar (‘|’) to the right or left of each key-letter belonging to that column:

Any reformatting (using “.T&”) would naturally have to reflect those same
lines in its instruction section.
(b) By extension, a vertical line both for a section of a column and for
individual cells is included among the key-letter instructions, either in the
first

Multipage headings

-ms provides a means to repeat the same header at the top of a table
which continues for more than one page. There are six steps involved: (1) the

start table command (“.TS”) is given an argument, ‘H,’ so that the ‘H’ follows

immediately after the command; (2) any global options are then entered, ending with a semicolon; (3) then there is a series of instruction lines which
define the format for the heading, ending with a period; (4) next is the text of
the repeated heading; (5) then, on it own line, the macro command “.TH” is
inserted; (6) finally, a reformat command (“.T&”) is entered, followed as usual
by a set of key-letters which will determine the layout of the material in the
table itself. Unless changed with another “.T&”, this last will remain in effect
throughout the entire table. The heading will be fixed, but it is possible to
create variable secondary headings at the top of subsequent pages through the
“bp,” followed by reformat. The following will serve as a short example of a
multipage heading; a new page has been forced early, in order to show the way
in which a box can be first closed off:
“TSH

Again, this sort of formatting often works much better in troff than in nroff:
in typewriting the different printers may not be able to close boxes and so
forth without piping the entire process through col:

tbl filename | nroff -ms | col
This should be standard procedure when previewing tables on the terminal.

Numbers

Numbers present a specific problem for tables (as they do elsewhere), in
that decimal figures need to line up on the decimal, no matter how many digits
they carry to the left or right; and, contrariwise, there has to be a provision for
preventing this in cases such as ‘12.28.1970’ and ‘after 1976.’ This problem is
solved through the key-letter ‘n,’ which arranges all the numbers in the
column on the decimal, whole numbers a little to the left, as if there were a

decimal point there:
.TS
cs
Cc
nn.
Votes

4,676,853(tab) 100.00
.TE

Any words or characters will right-justify on the decimal position, so that the
rightmost character will rest right under it. Because of the complicated way
the width is calculated for a number column, you should not use the ‘a’ key-letter
in the same column, even in reformat. Well, you will need to get around this,

and for those situations the general-purpose turn-off request “\&’’®° is
employed: the character will force the string to the right or left, so that whatever follows it sits under the line of decimal points. If nothing comes after it,
the string is over by a smidgen from the decimal column. A typical use of the
zero-width character would be partial numbers, or mixed abbreviations and
numbers:

minus sign in typesetting, and you should use “\-” for a longer, more negative
one.
Repeated characters in cells
The option of repeating characters in cells is meant for cells with missing
data or ones that simply have to have a row of characters as wide as the widest
entry in the column. It is requested through a “\Rc”, the c standing for the
character you want. To put a row of periods in a cell, therefore, you would
enter the following:

.TS
Levan
Buchanan(tab)Democrat(tab)65
Lincoln(tab)Republican(tab)52

Johnson(tab)\R.(tab)56
Grant (tab)Republican(tab)46
.TE
Again, this row of dots will not run to the edges of the cell.
Extra space between columns
Normally, a space of three n’s is provided between each column in a table.
(An n is equal to one half the point size, so that an n in ten-point type is five
points, or five seventy-seconds of an inch, which can be rounded to a sixteenth
of an inch.) Naturally, using the equal-width option or the global option
“expand” would change this. Another way to increase or decrease this gap is
to place a number (default n’s) after any key-letter for that column; this
number will determine the space from the end of one column to the beginning
of the next [these two positions are basically determined by the widest element
in the column]. So, to produce a four-n gap between two columns, you would
enter the following:
TS
css
l4n4n.
States
California(tab)
Connecticut(tab)
Delaware(tab)
.TE

Vertical lines, if requested, will attempt to bisect the space between the
columns.

Macros

Tables | Various Options| 267

The troff commands that affect vertical spacing, such as “.sp”, “bp”, and

It is possible to change

tbl really outdoes itself, however, in offering all sorts of options if you
wish to change the font, point size, or vertical spacing for a column or section
of column. These can be used separately or in combination; in every case, however, the signals follow directly after the key-letter for that column.

Font can be redefined for a column with an ‘fB’ |boldface] or ‘fI’ litalic|
after the key-letter, so that a request for boldface might look like this:

.TS

Disputed Votes

Each one of the numbers will be in italics. To turn this off for the rest of the
column, you must reformat the table (the key-letter by itself specifies the
default font); for an individual cell, you simply place a “\fR” before the contents of the cell. It will return to the key-letter font for the rest of the column.
There are a number of other ways to request font changes in tables, but as far
as I’m concerned one that works is enough.

The point size can be redefined by adding a ‘pN’ to the key-letter; N can
be either a whole number |“p9” is nine-point] or a plus or minus |“p—2” means
lessen the present size by two points].

For text (“T{/T}”) within a table, you can vary the vertical spacing using
a‘vN after the appropriate key-letter. And again this can be incremented or
decremented with a plus or minus. If, then, you need a column that is italic,
two points smaller in point size, and a block of text within that column three

268 Tables | Various Options|

Macros

The vertical spacing addition will only affect text within the table, which
makes sense because nothing else needs to be affected. The font and point size
options, on the other hand, will determine the form of the entire column.

Width
If nothing is done about the width of the table or of the individual
columns, tbl will default to a left-adjusted table, moving as far to the right as
is required by the width of the longest element in each column plus the three-n

space between. To widen the entire table (and the individual columns proportionally), the global option ‘expand’ can be used to draw the table out to the
current line length; the key-letter option for space between the columns will
widen or narrow the table. For groups of individual columns, there is the
equal-width option. Finally, tbl supplies an option which permits you to
specify the width of an individual column, the “w(M)”, which is described
immediately above, under ‘Equalizing column width.’

Tables Summarized

Appendixes VI and VII represent a table constructed with tbl, and are
meant to be more of an indication of approach than a comparison of convenience. tbl is extremely daunting when viewed in its entirety, but when perceived in its constituent parts, it makes a little more sense. The most important consideration is the column, and most of the commands affect the alignment, position, internal characteristics of the column. Rows are defined as a
horizontal series of cells within columns that have begun anew; this allows an

infinite number of rows, but it does take some getting used to. The disadvantages of tbl are the disadvantages of nroff/troff |batch mode, arbitrary coding|
plus some device dependency; the advantage is that it works.

ULTIMATE

ISSUES: SAFEGUARDING

THE TEXT

Final Matters

No text is absolutely perfect, but there are ways to make it less imperfect.
Three problems consistently bedevil UNIX text formatting: (1) faulty characters, including unwanted spaces at the beginning or ending of a line; (2)
‘bracketing’ macros in which the ending command has been forgotten; and (3)
leftover material [like footnotes overflowing] after the last page. For each of
these, safeguards can be developed: the first can be addressed through the
UNIX editors, the second through programs in the ‘shell, and the third in
macro definitions, especially through the end-of-file trap “.em XX”. Each solution has its individual strengths and weaknesses, so it’s best to use all of them
if you have the time and energy. Below, I have listed a few representative
examples of each kind of problem, but cannot exhaust the potential for fallibility; this you will have to do on your own.

Error Checking with the Editors
The UNIX editors ed, edit, ex, and vi offer a general means to check for
format errors, as well as the specific advantage of globally correcting and
printing faulty characters: control characters, for instance, or text lines that

begin or end with a space (or tab), begin with an apostrophe, or begin with a
period. The command to print such lines is identical in all four editors; the
only hitch might be that the period is a special character, and so must be preceded by a backslash, like so:

269

Macros

270 Ultimate Issues |Error Checking with the Editors]
:g/*\./p<er>

In edit, ex, and newer versions of vi, moreover, you may establish just such a
global command as a separate file, which then can be invoked through the
“so filename” function. This function is analogous to the “.so” request in
nroff/troff, but in this case the outside file is an editor command, pure and

simple. The “so” and file name, entered after the editor’s colon prompt, executes that command, just as if you typed it in. For example, the “g/°\./p” command can be written as its own file, called, say, ‘globe’:

$ ex globe<cr>
"globe": New file
:a<cr>

g/\./p<er>
:wq<cr>

"globe" |New file] 1 line, 8 characters

$
edit, ex, or vi will then execute the command
ment file. You simply type in

when you are back in your docu-

:so globe<er>
The advantage lies in consistency and ease, since a very complicated command
can be perfected and stored in this way, nor are you limited to a single command per outside file; any number may follow each other, one to a line, like so:

g/(space)/p
g/(tab)/p
If you do not have access to this function, the stream editor sed can be made to
behave in a similar—but much more clumsy—way. Such outside files are more
a convenience than a necessity in the process of checking and correcting; it is
important, though, that there be an established routine for detecting the most
common errors before sending the text off to the processor.
Control characters in the text

The most common control character to sneak into a text is the backspace,
control-h (appearing as a ‘H’), usually generated by backspacing over a
backslash. Since this is the way the character entered the document, this is
also one of the best ways to remove it. You begin, as usual, with the globalsearch command, “g/”’, then enter a backslash, then backspace over it, then
continue with the rest of the command, substituting nothing for each instance
that the global found. This looks a little strange in print:

:g/\(backspace)/s///gp<er >
Since you can be certain that these characters are unwanted, the global is justified; but it’s still a good idea to print the affected lines, as I have here.

In

edit, ex, and vi, a ‘#’ instead of a ‘p’ will print the line number as well, so that
you may more easily retrieve a specific line if it turns out to be an error.

Macros

Ultimate Issues |Error Checking with the Editors] 271
Blank spaces at the beginning of a line
A blank space accidentally appearing at the beginning of a line should be
removed; if it is not, the roffs will break to a new line and will indent that
number of spaces. In the various UNIX editors, the global command to delete
these unwanted beginning spaces is the following:

:g/"(space)(space)*/s///#
<er>
This will delete a single space, or any multiple of spaces at the beginning of a
line, and print the changed line for your inspection. If tabs have been used,
they can be removed in the same way.

Blank spaces at the end of a line
Similarly, blank spaces or tabs at the end of a line will be taken literally,
and included in the text. And again, since such spaces are always unneeded,
they can be removed globally:
:g/(space)(space)* $/s///#
<er >
In very large files, this command may produce an error message to the effect
that the ‘Tmp file is too large.’ If this is the case, you'll have to exit the editor,
and then return to limit your global command to a certain range of lines, like

:1,1000g/(space) (space)* $/s///#
Otherwise it might be a good idea to divide the file and rejoin it only when
you're ready for formatting.
Apostrophes at the beginning of lines
An apostrophe at the beginning of a line will also cause trouble for nroff
and troff; as you may remember, commands beginning with an apostrophe
allow the formatter to finish composing the line before taking effect. If an
ordinary text line begins with an apostrophe, the entire line will be read as a
command and then rejected, so that the line will disappear, and without any
obvious hole in the formatted document. Bell Laboratories must have figured
that no one would start off a line with it. But, as we have seen, this is what
we need to do to get a proper single quote on the typewriter. Each instance
must now be found and ‘covered’ in one of two ways. The obvious but timeconsuming way is to take the last word from the previous line and place it in
front of the quotation mark. The following two lines in a source file

40
41

Mr. B. remarked that the part about
our one common country” related

might be rewritten as
40
41

Mr. B. remarked that the part
about ’our one common country” related

Or you may ‘protect’ your quotation mark at the beginning of a line with these
two symbols: “\&”.! Hence line 41 above would look like this: \&”our one. The
1 The zero-width nonprinting character (again).

Macros

272 Ultimate Issues |Error Checking with the Editors]

two symbols would not appear in print. If you choose this second way, you can
again use a global search and substitute:

:g/°/s//\\\&'/#<er>
(The third backslash is necessary because the ampersand is a ‘magic’ character

in editorial substitutions.) The ‘#’ lor ‘p’ in ed] will indicate the lines that

have been changed, a necessary safeguard, though the apostrophe command is
extremely rare in most formatting and seldom appears in the text.
Errors with beginning periods
It is also extremely unlikely that any of your text will start with a
period; the only cases I can imagine would be a passage with ellipses, or a line
beginning with a decimal. If there are such lines, you must cover the period
just as you did with initial quote marks, or nroff will believe the line is a formatting command. The same two characters (‘\&’) operate; naturally, you cannot use a global search and substitute, since it would disable all format commands. In all the editors except ed, however, it is possible to add a ‘c’ to the
end of the substitute command, so that the editor will locate each instance, and
then prompt you for permission to change it:

:g/°\./s//\\\&./pe<er>
This places circumflexes below the affected string, followed by the cursor. A ‘y’
and carriage return will make and print the substitution, an ‘n’ and carriage
return will not. In this way you can change lines that do not belong among
legitimate commands.

Errors with ending periods
By default, the roffs will interpret a period ending a line as ending a sentence and automatically add two spaces when filling in subsequent text. If
your document has a great many abbreviations, then, this may produce
unesthetic space throughout. These three precautions, involving incipient
spaces, single quotes, and periods, are not always required, but they may well
save you from destroying the terminal in a fit of fury.
It is possible, too, to use such globals to print all footnote starts and ends,
or other such ‘bracketing’ macros, but the resultant list is difficult to read
without getting a headache. The next section suggests a few techniques that
are easier on the eyes and brain.

Error Detection from the Shell

You may utilize several UNIX shell programs on the level of the shell to
detect errors, notably of the bracketing type. The easiest to employ is grep,
which not only locates patterns but also counts them. You could, then, use
grep to count all beginning and ending footnote macros with the shell command

$ grep -c ""\.FS" sourcefile<er>
$ grep -c ""\.FE" sourcefile<er>
It is most likely that there will be fewer .FE’s.

Both of these commands

can be

Macros

Ultimate Issues |Error Detection from the Shell| 273
placed in a file created with some simple name (like ‘count’):
$ ex count<er>
"count": No such file or directory
‘a<cr>

grep -c”" \.FS" sourcefile<er>
grep -c" \.FE" sourcefile<er>
:wq<cr>
"count" |New file] 2 lines, 54 characters

$
The file can then be made executable with the shell command chmod
$ chmod

+x:

+x count<er>

so that simply typing the command ‘count’ will produce counts of footnote commands for ‘sourcefile.’ The program can be made a little bit more general by
substituting a ‘$1’ for the sourcefile name within the file, and then giving that
file name when ‘count’ is invoked:

$ ex count<er>
"count":

2 lines, 54 characters

:g/sourcefile/s//$1/p<er>

grep -c" \.FS" $1
grep -c" \.FE” $1
:wq<cr>
"count” 2 lines, 38 characters
$ count chapterl<ecr>

Finally, since so many bracketing commands in -ms are of the ‘start/end’
(“xS/xE”) variety, a second string can be added to check many different commands:

grep -c" \.$2S" $1
grep -c" \.$2E" $1
so that

$ count sourcefile D<ecr>

will count displays (“.DS”/“.DE”), ‘F’ will count footnotes, ‘K’ will count keeps
(“ KS”/“ KE”), ‘Q’ will count quotations (“.QS’/“.QE”’), “T’ will count tables
(“ TS” /“.TE”), and ‘X’ will count indexed items (“.XS”/“.XE”’).

Error detection with awk
awk is a complex and powerful programming language that, you may
remember, was used to combine alphabetized items in the section on Indexes.
It can function in error checking to actually give the line where there is an
unmatched command. The representative program given below has been written as a self-contained file called, in this example, ‘bracket.’ After the file has
been created, it must be made executable, just as ‘count’ was, above:

274 Ultimate Issues |Error Detection from the Shell|

Macros

$ chmod +x bracket <ecr>
‘bracket’ can then simply be used like any UNIX utility:
$ bracket sourcefile<cr>

Messages indicating the incomplete bracketing commands will then appear on
the screen. The following example keeps track of footnote commands. awk
automatically stores the line numbers (‘NR’), and can be made to test for certain strings. Here, when it meets two “.FS’s in a row, it gives the line number
for the first; if it meets two “.FE”s, it gives the number for the second:

awk ’BEGIN {c = 1}

{
if ($0 — /\:FS/)

{
ifeles!= 71)

{
printf("Footnote start incomplete at %d\n", s);
s = NR;
c = 0;

if '($0.> 7. ER)
if (c != 0)
printf("Footnote end incomplete at %d\n", NR);
c=1;

END { printf("\n")}’ <$1

Similar awk programs may be designed for the other bracketing macros, and a
slightly more complicated one may be used for italic and roman fonts.

Error Correction in the Ending Macro
The various diversions used for displays, footnotes, and keeps raise a problem that does not happen often but that can be frustrating when it occurs: if
the text ends but all the diversions are still full, the appropriate trap may not
be triggered to disgorge those contents. Luckily, there is a single trap that is
automatically invoked when all regular processing is finished: “.em macro”. If
you've been designing your own footnotes and so forth, the macro sprung by
“em” should in turn invoke each storage area. To accomplish this, -ms uses a
macro called ‘EM,’ which tests for the presence of diversions. If they exist the
header macro (contained in the all-purpose “.NP”, new page command) is
called, and individual diversions are printed just as they would be in the normal formatting of text:

Macros

Ultimate Issues |Error Detection from the Shell| 275
.de EM
-br

if \\n(TB=0 .if t .wh -—lp CM
if \\n(TB \&\c

if \\n(TB ’bp

if \\n(TB .NP

if \\n(TB .ch CM 160
.em EM

Here ‘TB’ is a number register acting as a ‘toggle’ that is always zeroed out
when footnote or keep storage areas are emptied. If TB is zero, the macro simply prints the cut marks (‘CM’) one point from the bottom of the page. Otherwise, the formatter crosses over to a new page, the “.NP” is reinvoked, and the
trap for the cut mark changed to 160 vertical spaces, off into the distance, so
that the cycle can begin over if necessary.

The following nine appendixes provide a summary of functions found in Joseph Ossanna’s
NROFF/TROFF User’s Manual and in this Guide. The appendixes have been arranged in this
order:

ide

\fBeqn\fP, forming with \fBtbl\fP (and a few others) an
exclusive group of preprocessors
to \fBtroff\fP, produces a wide variety of mathematical

symbols and equations.\* +
.FS
sp .5V

\*#The standard work on \fBeqn\fP is Brian W. Kernighan and Lorinda Cherry’s
“Typesetting Mathematics\(emUser’s Guide,” 2nd ed., Bell Laboratories,
March 26, 1980. Further symbols have been developed by
Carmela Scrocca and are described in “New Graphic Symbols for EQN and
NEQN,”

Bell Laboratories, ca. 1982.

.FE
The preprocessor name is apt because in order to
use it, \fBeqn\fP is placed before the file name
and the results are then \%piped through \fBtroff\fP.
the command would look like this:
in 2.251

Typically,

nf

$ eqn \flfilename\fR \s-2\v’-0.2m’\(bv\v’ +0.2m’\s0 troff
sp
fi
din li

The \fBtroff\fP part can contain all the command-line options
Nee 2ol
sp
ne 3
nf

-o\fIN\fR, -s\fIN\fR, -n\fIN\fR

sp
an
in li

and \%\fB-me\fP or \%\fB-ms\fP as well. Without the macro packages,
the equation will be placed against the left margin; with them,
it will be automatically centered.

If you’re using both

\fBeqn\fP and \fBtbl\fP together (work for the computer but sometimes
necessary), it is advisable to have the \fBtbl\fP command go

first, followed by the filename, pipe, \fBeqn\fP, pipe, and
\fBtroff\fP command:
in, 2.251

sp

ene 2
As Appendixes VI and VII indicate, however, the conjunction of

\fBtbl\fP and \fBeqn\fP is not always the simplest of matters.\*+
FS
sp .5v
\*#For example, nearly all the special characters are reserved
in one way or another, and because both \fBtbl\fP and \fBtroff\fP reinterpret
two backslashes as one, you must enter four in the original
file in order to end up with a single literal backslash.

.FE
ee
In fact, even when used individually
both \fBtbl\fP and \fBeqn\fP tend to need special care
(that is, frequent tinkering). It is a very good idea, then, to
reserve text with tables or equations in files separate
from the source document.\* +

FS
sp .5v
\*#In such cases it is extremely important that all general
formatting commands (page length, line length) be placed
in a common macros file that both the source document

and the special page access through the “.so” command.
This insures that each will be consistent with the other.
FE
With a “.bp\ +2”
in the source file at the place where the table or
equation is to appear,
you can instruct the processor to skip the page.
After the entire
text has been printed, the specially formatted
page can then be assigned the skipped page number with the

\%‘-n’ option, as, for example:
ain 2.251

If the table does not go on its own page,

ne 2

of these preprocessors.
[ee

I have here spoken only of \fBtroff\fP on purpose; \fBnroff\fP does have
a version of \fBeqn\fP called \fBneqn\fP, which attempts to produce
the various symbols, given the limitations of line printers and
most automatic typewriters. ‘Attempts’ is extremely accurate
in this case, since the combination of poor vertical resolution,
missing symbols, overstrikes, and offstrikes
makes the equation extremely difficult to read correctly; the common

wisdom
is to use \fBneqn\fP for inexpensive and quick production at a proofreading
stage. The best use of \fBneqn\fP is in fact on the terminal (where the
output looks very strange indeed) for the very first test of an

equation, since it can plaster the screen with

error messages just as well as \fBeqn\fP does on paper.
If you’re using \fBneqn\fP with an automatic typewriter,
and especially if you’re doing any movement within the equation,
you will need to specify the terminal type:

in 2.251
sp
ne 3
nf
$ neqn \fIfilename\fR \s-2\v’-0.2m’\(bv\v’ +0.2m’\s0 nroff -T450

.sp

By necessity and design, \fBeqn\fP utilizes a beginning and an ending command
to bracket requests for special symbols and specific placement.
The equation may be physically set off from the text, or it
ne 2
may exist within the line; for each of these options the
beginning and ending commands are different.
sp
ne 5
.B
Equations apart from the text
a
XS x
Equations apart from the text
XE
ne 2
PP
For the first, functioning to separate the
equation from the rest of the document,
the bracketing commands
are \%‘‘.EQ/.EN” on lines by themselves;
the symbols and instructions for

the equation are placed between the \%‘.EQ” and \%‘.EN”,
like so:
an 2.251
sp
ne 3
nf

\&.EQ
symbols instructions more symbols
\&.EN
sp
fi
anh

you must create multiple \%".EQ/.EN’s:
ain 2.251
sp
ne 6
nf
\&.EQ
symbols instructions symbols
\&.EN
\&.EQ
instructions symbols instructions
\&.EN
sp

Note: In the summary below, N will represent any number, M any measurement, c any character, and x, xx, or yy any
register, macro, or string name. The descriptions and abstract forms of the registers and commands are largely
taken from the NROFF/TROFF User’s Manual in order to avoid confusion. The term ‘Default’ is meant to
extend information in the manual’s ‘Initial value’ column to the various predefined number registers.

abort: prints ‘text’ on the message output and terminates without further processing.

IV. -ms FUNCTIONAL SUMMARY

Note: In the command summary below, N represents any number, M any measurement, and c any character.
venience, some of the more common nroff/troff basic commands have been included in this list.

adjust.

For con-

Both nroff and troff automatically right-justify (“adjust”). You would use the adjust
command to start it up again after a “no adjust” command (below).
.ad

begin new page.
After the begin-page command, the page is immediately scrolled out and
the text following appears at the top of the next page.
-bp

bibliographic format.
This precedes a bibliographic record, giving first a blank line, then
the following line unindented, then all subsequent lines indented five spaces.
.BR

bibliographic indent.
The ‘BI’ register determines the amount of indent for the bibliographic
record macro “.BR’”. [default three ‘“‘n’s
6“

’

|

-nr BI M

boldface font [troff].

This precedes the letters or words, producing bold letters in typesetting.

The material is underlined in nroff. Turned off with a “\fR”’.

\fB
boldface font [troff].

This stands on a separate line above the letters or words, producing

bold letters in typesetting.

The material is underlined in typewriting.

.B
bottom running foot.
.BT

It can be redefined with “.de BT/..” .

(boxed word and text | 301

boxed word and text.
Either a word or a whole text can be placed in a box with these commands; the word is placed to the right of “BX”, the text is bracketed by “.B1,.B2”.

This command interrupts “fill”, causing text following to appear on a new line.

Usually empty, it can be redefined with “.ds CF text”.

control character is a literal.
This is used when you cannot avoid putting a period or apostrophe at the beginning of the line: “\&’Ow are you, ’e says.”

\&
define macro.
define strings.

Used commonly with “PT” and “BT” for headers and footers.
.de
Used commonly with “LH”, “CH”, “RH”, “LF”, “CF”, and “RF”.

.ds
diacritical marks.
There are currently seven foreign accents on the system: \*, (cedilla); \«:
(umlaut); \** (circumflex); \** (grave); \*’ (acute); \*~ (tilde); \*C (hacek). These
sequences precede the letter to be accented thus: \*°age = age. See the discussion of
diacritics in Basic Functions.

\*e
display (keep).

The keep-display command, given various ‘arguments’ (L, C, B, I) prints a

text exactly as it has been entered, until turned off with a “.DE”, saving the text for

the next page if it doesn’t completely fit on the current page.
.DS,.DE

display (non-keep).

There are four different display macros that do not ‘keep’ text.

All print

the text exactly as it appears in the source file, differing in format: .BD = centered
block, .CD = each line centered, .ID = indented, and .LD = left-justified.

The text

must finish with a .DE.

These bracket a mathematical equation that will be interpreted by the preprocesequation.
sor “eqn”.

In “fill,” the normal condition for the formatter, long lines are split and short lines

joined to make up line length.

“PS”] There are at least fifteen sizes, from six point to thirty-six, and in newer

hyphenated words.
A list of words follow this command, with indications of proper hyphenation. The command appears before “.GO”.
-hw
hyphenation break here. __ Placed inside a word, it directs nroff and troff to insert a hyphen
correctly in that word, should the need arise; to avoid hyphenation, the “\%” must
immediately precede the word: “\%printout”.

hyphenation.

is most common. The command appears before the “.GO”.
